Matrix Decomposition and Applications
Jun Lu

Abstract




\nopagecolor

Preface

The realm of matrices is as vast as it is indispensable, with applications spanning from the minutiae of quantum systems to the expansive challenges of large-scale data analytics. At the heart of matrix analysis lies the transformative process of matrix decomposition—a method of reducing a complex matrix into simpler, constituent parts that illuminate its structure and utility. Far from being merely an abstract mathematical concept, matrix decomposition has become a cornerstone in fields as diverse as computer science, engineering, physics, and economics.

At its essence, matrix decomposition simplifies the representation and manipulation of matrices by breaking them down into manageable components. This process enables efficient solutions to linear systems, reduces computational complexity, and provides insights into data’s inherent structure. Its applications are far-reaching, influencing everything from machine learning and optimization to image processing and recommender systems.

The historical roots of matrix decomposition trace back to the foundational work of Alston S. Householder in the mid-20th century, which set the stage for modern numerical analysis. Over the decades, the field has seen tremendous advancements, including innovations like backpropagation for neural networks, dimensionality reduction techniques in machine learning, and the utilization of low-rank matrices in natural language processing and large language models.

Today, matrix decomposition underpins technologies in statistics, optimization, and artificial intelligence. It is fundamental to the functioning of algorithms in deep neural networks, recommendation systems, and high-dimensional data analysis, among others. These applications not only underscore its practical significance but also highlight the evolving complexity of its theoretical underpinnings.

This book seeks to serve as a comprehensive and accessible introduction to matrix decomposition, offering readers a bridge between theoretical concepts and practical applications. It is designed for readers with a foundational understanding of linear algebra and aims to achieve the following objectives:

•

Explore core principles.
Present the mathematical foundations of matrix decomposition, ranging from basic methods such as LU, Cholesky, and QR decomposition to advanced techniques like SVD, eigenvalue decomposition, and their modern extensions.

•

Highlight practical applications.
Demonstrate the relevance of decomposition methods in diverse fields, including optimization, machine learning, neural network compression, and data interpretation.

•

Facilitate problem-solving.
Equip readers with the tools to understand and solve problems involving matrices, emphasizing how decomposition can simplify complex tasks and provide deeper insights.

Keywords

Existence and computing of matrix decompositions, Low-rank approximation, Pivot, LU decomposition for nonzero leading principal minors, Data distillation, CR decomposition, CUR/Skeleton decomposition, Interpolative decomposition, Biconjugate decomposition, Coordinate transformation, Hessenberg decomposition, ULV decomposition, URV decomposition, Rank decomposition, Gram-Schmidt process, Householder reflector, Givens rotation, Rank-revealing decomposition, Cholesky decomposition and update/downdate, Eigenvalue problems, Alternating least squares.

Introduction and Background

Matrix decomposition is a cornerstone of modern numerical linear algebra, with applications in diverse fields such as statistics
(
Banerjee and Roy 2014
;
Gentle 1998
)
, optimization
(
Gill et al. 2021
)
, and machine learning
(
Goodfellow et al. 2016
;
Bishop 2006
)
, particularly in deep learning.
As an essential computational framework, it simplifies complex matrix operations by breaking a matrix into more manageable components. This approach is critical not only for theoretical insights but also for practical implementations, enabling efficient algorithms and enhancing interpretability.

The prominence of matrix decomposition techniques is largely due to advances like the backpropagation algorithm for neural network training and the use of low-rank neural networks in efficient deep learning architectures.
The primary goal of this book is to provide a self-contained introduction to the concepts and mathematical tools of linear algebra and matrix analysis, laying a solid foundation for understanding matrix decomposition techniques and their applications in subsequent sections.
This book explores the fundamental techniques and applications of matrix decomposition. It begins with foundational methods such as LU and Cholesky decomposition, which are integral to solving linear systems and understanding positive definiteness. It then delves into more advanced topics, including QR decomposition, spectral decomposition, and singular value decomposition (SVD), which have broad applications ranging from eigenvalue problems to low-rank approximations and data compression.
This introduction is designed for readers with a foundational knowledge of linear algebra and aims to bridge the gap between theory and application, equipping them with the necessary tools to navigate this critical area of numerical mathematics.

However, we clearly realize our inability to cover all the useful and interesting results concerning matrix decomposition. Given the scope limitations, topics such as the analysis of Euclidean space, Hermitian space, and Hilbert space are not addressed in detail here. For a more comprehensive introduction to these areas, readers are encouraged to consult the literature on linear algebra, including works such as
Trefethen and Bau III 1997
;
Strang 2009
;
Stewart 2000
;
Gentle 2007
;
Higham 2002
;
Quarteroni et al. 2010
;
Golub and Van Loan 2013
;
Beck 2017
;
Gallier and Quaintance 2019
;
Boyd and Vandenberghe 2018
;
Strang 2019
;
van de Geijn and Myers 2020
;
Strang 2021
.
It is important to note that this book specifically focuses on providing compact proofs for the existence of various matrix decomposition methods. For deeper exploration of topics such as reducing computational complexity, more rigorous discussions of applications, and insights into tensor decomposition, readers are encouraged to refer to
Lu 2021c
.

A matrix decomposition involves breaking down a complex matrix into its constituent parts, simplifying its representation.
The underlying principle of this approach is that, rather than solving specific problems directly, matrix algorithms focus on simplifying more complex matrix operations. These operations can be performed on the decomposed components, rather than the original matrix itself. At a general level, a matrix decomposition task for a matrix
\bm{A}
can be formulated as follows:

•

\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{U}
: Here,
\bm{Q}
is an orthogonal matrix that contains the same column space as
\bm{A}
, while
\bm{U}
is a relatively simple and sparse matrix used to reconstruct
\bm{A}
.

•

\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{T}\bm{Q}^{\top}
: In this case,
\bm{Q}
is orthogonal such that
\bm{A}
and
\bm{T}
are
similar matrices
that share essential properties such as eigenvalues and sparsity. Additionally, working with
\bm{T}
is computationally simpler than working with
\bm{A}
.

•

\bm{A}=\bm{U}\bm{T}\bm{V}
: In this formulation,
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
are orthogonal matrices such that the columns of
\bm{U}
and the rows of
\bm{V}
form orthonormal bases for the column space and row space of
\bm{A}
, respectively.

•

\underset{m\times n}{\bm{A}}=\underset{m\times r}{\bm{B}}\,\,\,\underset{r\times n}{\bm{C}}
: Here,
\bm{B}
and
\bm{C}
are full-rank matrices capable of reducing the memory storage requirements for
\bm{A}
.
In practical applications,
a low-rank approximation,
\underset{m\times n}{\bm{A}}\approx\underset{m\times k}{\bm{D}}\,\,\,\underset{k\times n}{\bm{F}}
, where
k<r
is the
numerical rank
of the matrix, proves beneficial. This approximation allows for more efficient storage of the matrix
\bm{A}
, requiring only
k(m+n)
floats instead of
mn
numbers. Additionally, it facilitates the efficient computation of matrix-vector products,
\bm{b}=\bm{A}\bm{x}
, through intermediate steps involving
\bm{c}=\bm{F}\bm{x}
and
\bm{b}=\bm{D}\bm{c}
. This approximation method is also valuable for data interpretation and other computational tasks.

•

Although typically computationally demanding, a matrix decomposition can be leveraged to solve new problems related to the original matrix in various contexts. For instance, once the factorization of
\bm{A}
is obtained, it can be reused to solve a set of linear systems,
\{\bm{b}_{1}=\bm{A}\bm{x}_{1},\bm{b}_{2}=\bm{A}\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{k}=\bm{A}\bm{x}_{k}\}
.

•

More generally, matrix decomposition aids in understanding the internal structure and logic of operations involving matrix multiplication. Each component of the decomposition contributes to a geometrical transformation, as discussed in Section
.

Matrix decomposition algorithms can be classified into several categories. Below are six fundamental types:

1.

Factorizations based on Gaussian elimination, such as LU decomposition and its positive definite counterpart, Cholesky decomposition.

2.

Factorizations achieved by orthogonalizing either the columns or rows of a matrix, enabling effective data representation in an orthonormal basis.

3.

Factorizations involving skeleton matrices, where a subset of columns or rows can sufficiently represent the entire dataset with minimal reconstruction error, while preserving sparsity and nonnegativity.

4.

Reduction to Hessenberg, tridiagonal, or bidiagonal forms, allowing the properties of the matrix (such as rank and eigenvalues) to be explored within these reduced forms.

5.

Factorizations derived from the computation of matrix eigenvalues.

6.

Other specialized methods, which involve optimization techniques and high-level concepts. These may not fit neatly into the categories above but still represent important classes of decompositions.

The visual representations of matrix decomposition in Figures
and
, illustrating the connections between various decomposition methods based on their internal relationships. These figures also differentiate the methods according to specific criteria or prerequisites. Further details about these visualizations are provided in the accompanying text.

Objectives of This Work

This book aims to provide a comprehensive yet accessible introduction to the principles, methods, and applications of matrix decomposition. Designed for readers with a foundational understanding of linear algebra, it bridges the gap between theoretical rigor and practical applications. The goals include:

1.

Presenting core concepts: Introducing the mathematical foundations of matrix decomposition, including LU, Cholesky, QR, and SVD, along with more advanced methods like eigenvalue and Jordan decompositions.

2.

Highlighting applications: Demonstrating the relevance of these techniques in various domains, such as optimization, machine learning, and signal processing.

3.

Providing rigorous proofs: Ensuring that the presented methods are mathematically rigorous, with proofs and derivations to deepen understanding.

Notation and preliminaries.

In the following section, we will introduce and review fundamental concepts in linear algebra. We will also introduce additional important notions as necessary to ensure clarity. Readers with a strong background in matrix analysis may choose to skip this section. Throughout the text, our focus will be on real matrices. Unless specified otherwise, the eigenvalues of the matrices under discussion are assumed to be real. Furthermore, we maintain the assumption that
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert=\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2}
, meaning the default matrix or vector norm is the Frobenius norm or
\ell_{2}
norm.

Scalars will be represented in a non-bold font, potentially with subscripts (e.g.,
a
,
\alpha
,
\alpha_{i}
).
Vectors will be denoted using
boldface
lowercase letters, possibly with subscripts (e.g.,
\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
\bm{x}
,
\bm{x}_{n}
,
\bm{z}
), while matrices will be represented by
boldface
uppercase letters, possibly with subscripts (e.g.,
\bm{A}
,
\bm{L}_{j}
).
The
i
-th element of a vector
\bm{z}
will be denoted by
z_{i}
in the non-bold font.
For matrix
\bm{A}
, the value in the
i
-th row and
j
-th column is represented as
a_{ij}
.
Additionally, we will use
Matlab-style notation
; the submatrix of matrix
\bm{A}
from the
i
-th to
j
-th rows and
k
-th to
m
-th columns will be denoted by
\bm{A}_{i:j,k:m}=\bm{A}[i:j,k:m]
.
When the indices are not continuous, with ordered subindex sets
I
and
J
,
\bm{A}[I,J]
indicates the submatrix of
\bm{A}
obtained by extracting the rows and columns indexed by
I
and
J
, respectively. Similarly,
\bm{A}[:,J]
denotes the submatrix of
\bm{A}
obtained by extracting the columns of
\bm{A}
indexed by
J
.

Vectors are represented in column format rather than row format. A row vector is indicated by the transpose of a column vector, e.g., denoted by
\bm{a}^{\top}
.
A specific column vector with values is delineated by the semicolon symbol
``;"
, for example,
\bm{x}=[1;2;3]
is a column vector in
3
.
Similarly, a row vector with specific values is separated by commas, e.g.,
\bm{y}=[1,2,3]
is a row vector with three values.
Furthermore, a column vector can be expressed as the transpose of a row vector, for instance,
\bm{y}=[1,2,3]^{\top}
is a column vector.

The transpose of a matrix
\bm{A}
is denoted by
\bm{A}^{\top}
, and its inverse is denoted by
\bm{A}^{-1}
.
The
p\times p
identity matrix is represented by
\bm{I}_{p}
.
A vector or matrix consisting entirely of zeros is denoted by a
boldface
zero,
\mathbf{0}
, with its size inferred from context.
For specificity,
\mathbf{0}_{p}
signifies a vector of all zeros with
p
entries, and
\mathbf{0}_{p\times q}
represents a matrix of all zeros with dimensions
p\times q
.

Definition 1
(Eigenvalue).

Given any vector space
{\mathbb{E}}
and any linear map
\bm{A}:{\mathbb{E}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{E}}
, a scalar
\lambda\in{\mathbb{K}}
is called an eigenvalue, or proper value, or characteristic value of
\bm{A}
if there exists a nonzero vector
\bm{u}\in{\mathbb{E}}
such that

\bm{A}\bm{u}=\lambda\bm{u}.

Definition 2
(Spectrum and Spectral Radius).

The set of all eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
is called the spectrum of
\bm{A}
and is denoted by
\Lambda(\bm{A})
. The largest magnitude of the eigenvalues is known as the spectral radius
\rho(\bm{A})
:

\rho(\bm{A})=\mathop{\max}_{\lambda\in\Lambda(\bm{A})}|\lambda|.

Definition 3
(Eigenvector).

A vector
\bm{u}\in{\mathbb{E}}
is called an eigenvector, or proper vector, or characteristic vector of
\bm{A}
if
\bm{u}\neq 0
and if there exists some
\lambda\in{\mathbb{K}}
such that

\bm{A}\bm{u}=\lambda\bm{u},

where the scalar
\lambda
is then an eigenvalue. And we say that
\bm{u}
is an eigenvector associated with
\lambda
.

Furthermore, the pair
(\lambda,\bm{u})
mentioned above is said to be an
eigenpair
. Intuitively, the definitions above imply that multiplying matrix
\bm{A}
by the vector
\bm{u}
yields a new vector in the same direction as
\bm{u}
but scaled by a factor
\lambda
.
For any eigenvector
\bm{u}
, it can be scaled by a scalar
s
such that
s\bm{u}
remains an eigenvector of
\bm{A}
.
This is why we refer to
\bm{u}
as an eigenvector of
\bm{A}
associated with the eigenvalue
\lambda
.
To avoid any ambiguity, we usually assume that the eigenvector is normalized to have a length of 1, and its first entry is positive (or negative), as both
\bm{u}
and
-\bm{u}
are eigenvectors.

In the study of linear algebra, every vector space has a basis, and every vector in the space can be expressed as a linear combination of the basis elements. Using this concept, we define the span and dimension of a subspace through its basis.

Definition 4
(Subspace).

A nonempty subset
\mathcal{V}
of
n
is called a subspace if for every
\bm{a},\bm{b}\in\mathcal{V}
and every
x,y\in\real
, the combination
x\bm{a}+y\bm{a}
also belongs to
\mathcal{V}
.

Definition 5
(Span).

If every vector
\bm{v}
in a subspace
\mathcal{V}
can be expressed as a linear combination of the vectors
\{\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,

\bm{a}_{m}\}
, then the set
\{\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{m}\}
is said to span
\mathcal{V}
.

In linear algebra, the concept of linear independence is fundamental when studying sets of vectors. Two equivalent definitions are provided below.

Definition 6
(Linearly Independent).

A set of vectors
\{\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{m}\}
is called linearly independent if the equation
x_{1}\bm{a}_{1}+x_{2}\bm{a}_{2}+\ldots+x_{m}\bm{a}_{m}=\mathbf{0}
has only the trivial solution where all scalars
x_{i}=0
.
An equivalent definition is that
\bm{a}_{1}\neq\mathbf{0}
, and for every
k>1
, the vector
\bm{a}_{k}
does not belong to the span of the preceding vectors
\{\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{k-1}\}
.

Definition 7
(Basis and Dimension).

A set of vectors
\{\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{m}\}
is called a basis of a subspace
\mathcal{V}
if they are linearly independent and span
\mathcal{V}
. All bases of a given subspace contain the same number of vectors, and the number of vectors in any basis is called the dimension of the subspace
\mathcal{V}
. By convention, the subspace
\{\mathbf{0}\}
is defined to have dimension zero. Furthermore, every nonzero subspace has a basis that can be chosen to be orthogonal (the vectors in the basis are mutually perpendicular).

Definition 8
(Column Space (Range)).

For an
m\times n
real matrix
\bm{A}
, the column space (or range) of
\bm{A}
is defined as the set of all linear combinations of its columns:

\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})=\{\bm{y}\in\real^{m}:\exists\bm{x}\in\real^{n},\,\bm{y}=\bm{A}\bm{x}\}.

Similarly, the row space of
\bm{A}
is the set of all linear combinations of its rows, which is equal to the column space of the transpose
\bm{A}^{\top}
:

\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})=\{\bm{x}\in\real^{n}:\exists\bm{y}\in\real^{m},\,\bm{x}=\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{y}\}.

Definition 9
(Null Space (Nullspace, Kernel)).

For an
m\times n
real matrix
\bm{A}
, the null space (also called the kernel or nullspace) of
\bm{A}
is the set of all vectors in
n
that satisfy:

\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})=\{\bm{y}\in\real^{n}:\,\bm{A}\bm{y}=\mathbf{0}\}.

Similarly, the null space of
\bm{A}^{\top}
is defined as

\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top})=\{\bm{x}\in\real^{m}:\,\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}\}.

Both the column space of
\bm{A}
and the null space of
\bm{A}^{\top}
are subspaces of
n
. Moreover, every vector in
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top})
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
, and vice versa; similarly, every vector in
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
is also orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
, and vice versa.

Definition 10
(Rank).

The
rank
of a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
is the dimension of the column space of
\bm{A}
. That is, the rank of
\bm{A}
is equal to the maximum number of linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
, and is also the maximum number of linearly independent rows of
\bm{A}
. The rank of
\bm{A}
is equal to the rank of its transpose,
\bm{A}^{\top}
. Additionally,
\bm{A}
is said to have full rank if its rank equals
\min\{m,n\}
.
Specifically, given a vector
\bm{u}\in\real^{m}
and a vector
\bm{v}\in\real^{n}
, then the
m\times n
matrix
\bm{u}\bm{v}^{\top}
is of rank 1. In short, the rank of a matrix is equal to:

•

number of linearly independent columns;

•

number of linearly independent rows;

•

and remarkably, these quantities are always equal (see Theorem
).

Definition 11
(Orthogonal Complement in General).

The orthogonal complement
\mathcal{V}^{\perp}
of a subspace
\mathcal{V}
consists of all vectors that are perpendicular to
\mathcal{V}
. Formally,

\mathcal{V}^{\perp}=\{\bm{v}:\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{u}=0,\,\,\,\forall\,\bm{u}\in\mathcal{V}\}.

The two subspaces are disjoint (i.e., their intersection is
\{\mathbf{0}\}
) and together span the entire space. The dimensions of
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\perp}
add up to the dimension of the whole space. Additionally, the orthogonal complement of the orthogonal complement returns the original subspace:
(\mathcal{V}^{\perp})^{\perp}=\mathcal{V}
.

For example, we can explicitly define the orthogonal complement of the column space in the following definition.

Definition 12
(Orthogonal Complement of Column Space).

For an
m\times n
real matrix
\bm{A}
, the orthogonal complement of its column space
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
, denoted by
\mathcal{C}^{\bot}(\bm{A})
, is the subspace:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\bot}(\bm{A})

\displaystyle=\{\bm{y}\in\real^{m}:\,\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0},\,\forall\,\bm{x}\in\real^{n}\}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{y}\in\real^{m}:\,\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{v}=\mathbf{0},\,\forall\,\bm{v}\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})\}.

We now introduce the four fundamental subspaces associated with any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
of rank
r
, as outlined in Theorem
.
To establish the fundamental theorem of linear algebra, it is essential first to verify a key result: the equality of the row rank and column rank of a matrix. This result serves as a foundational step for subsequent developments.

Theorem 13
(Row Rank Equals Column Rank).

The dimension of the column space of a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
is equal to the dimension of its
row space. In other words, the row rank and the column rank of a matrix
\bm{A}
are equal.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
First, we observe that the null space of
\bm{A}
is orthogonal complementary to the row space of
\bm{A}
:
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})\bot\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
(where the row space of
\bm{A}
corresponds to the column space of
\bm{A}^{\top}
). That is, vectors in the null space of
\bm{A}
are orthogonal to vectors in the row space of
\bm{A}
. To see this, suppose
\bm{A}
has rows
\{\bm{a}_{1}^{\top},\bm{a}_{2}^{\top},\ldots,\bm{a}_{m}^{\top}\}
and
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1}^{\top};\bm{a}_{2}^{\top};\ldots;\bm{a}_{m}^{\top}]
is the row partition. For any vector
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
, we have
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
, or equivalently,
[\bm{a}_{1}^{\top}\bm{x};\bm{a}_{2}^{\top}\bm{x};\ldots;\bm{a}_{m}^{\top}\bm{x}]=\mathbf{0}
.
Since the row space of
\bm{A}
is spanned by
\{\bm{a}_{1}^{\top},\bm{a}_{2}^{\top},\ldots,\bm{a}_{m}^{\top}\}
, it follows that
\bm{x}
is perpendicular to all vectors in
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
, which means
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})\bot\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
.

Next, suppose the dimension of the row space of
\bm{A}
is
r
.
Let
\{\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{r}\}
be a set of vectors in
n
and form a basis for the row space
. Then the
r
vectors
\{\bm{A}\bm{r}_{1},\bm{A}\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{A}\bm{r}_{r}\}
lie in the column space of
\bm{A}
. We claim that these vectors are linearly independent.
To verify this, suppose there exists a linear combination of the
r
vectors:
x_{1}\bm{A}\bm{r}_{1}+x_{2}\bm{A}\bm{r}_{2}+\ldots+x_{r}\bm{A}\bm{r}_{r}=\mathbf{0}
, that is,
\bm{A}(x_{1}\bm{r}_{1}+x_{2}\bm{r}_{2}+\ldots+x_{r}\bm{r}_{r})=\mathbf{0}
, and the vector
\bm{v}=x_{1}\bm{r}_{1}+x_{2}\bm{r}_{2}+\ldots+x_{r}\bm{r}_{r}
belongs to the null space of
\bm{A}
. But since
\{\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{r}\}
is a basis for the row space of
\bm{A}
,
\bm{v}
must also lie in the row space of
\bm{A}
.
We have shown that vectors from the null space of
\bm{A}
is perpendicular to vectors from the row space of
\bm{A}
; thus, it holds that
\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{v}=0
, which implies that
x_{1}=x_{2}=\ldots=x_{r}=0
. Hence,
\bm{A}\bm{r}_{1},\bm{A}\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{A}\bm{r}_{r}
lie in the column space of
\bm{A}
, and they are linearly independent
.
Since these
r
linearly independent vectors are in the column space of
\bm{A}
, the column space must have dimension at least
r
. This proves that
row rank of
\bm{A}\leq
column rank of
\bm{A}
.

Applying the same reasoning to
\bm{A}^{\top}
, we conclude that
column rank of
\bm{A}\leq
row rank of
\bm{A}
.
Combining these results, we obtain the equality of the row rank and column rank of
\bm{A}
. This completes the proof.

Additional insights from this proof reveal that if
\{\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{r}\}
forms a basis for the row space of
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, then
\{\bm{A}\bm{r}_{1},\bm{A}\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{A}\bm{r}_{r}\}
constitutes a basis for the column space of
\bm{A}
.
This result is formalized in the following lemma:

Lemma 14
(Column Basis from Row Basis).

For any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, if
\{\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{r}\}
is a set of vectors in
n
, which forms a basis for the row space of
\bm{A}
, then
\{\bm{A}\bm{r}_{1},\bm{A}\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{A}\bm{r}_{r}\}
forms a basis for the column space of
\bm{A}
.

For any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, it can be easily verified that any vector in the row space of
\bm{A}
is orthogonal to any vector in the null space of
\bm{A}
. Specifically, if
\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
, then
\bm{A}\bm{x}_{n}=\mathbf{0}
, which implies that
\bm{x}_{n}
is perpendicular to every row of
\bm{A}
, thus supporting this assertion.

Similarly, any vector in the column space of
\bm{A}
is orthogonal to any vector in the null space of
\bm{A}^{\top}
. Moreover, the column space of
\bm{A}
together with the null space of
\bm{A}^{\top}
span the entire space
m
. This observation is a key part of the fundamental theorem of linear algebra.

The fundamental theorem has two essential components: the dimension of the subspaces and the orthogonality between pairs of subspaces.
The orthogonality relationships have been demonstrated above.
Additionally, when the row space has dimension
r
, the null space has dimension
n-r
. These relationships are rigorously established in the following theorem.

Figure 3:
Two pairs of orthogonal subspaces in
n
and
m
.
\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top}))+\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}))=n
and
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}))+\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}))=m
. The null space component maps to zero as
\bm{A}\bm{x}_{n}=\mathbf{0}\in\real^{m}
. The row space component maps to the column space as
\bm{A}\bm{x}_{r}=\bm{A}(\bm{x}_{r}+\bm{x}_{n})=\bm{b}\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
.

Theorem 15
(The Fundamental Theorem of Linear Algebra).

Orthogonal Complement and Rank-Nullity Theorem: for any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, we have

•

The null space
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
is orthogonal complement to the row space
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
in
n
:
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}))+\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top}))=n
;

•

The left null space
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top})
is orthogonal complement to the column space
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
in
m
:
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}))+\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}))=m
;

•

For a rank-
r
matrix
\bm{A}
,
\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top}))=\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}))=r
, that is,
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}))=n-r
and
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}))=m-r
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
From the proof of Theorem
, let
\{\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{r}\}
be a set of vectors in
n
that forms a basis for the row space; then
\{\bm{A}\bm{r}_{1},\bm{A}\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{A}\bm{r}_{r}\}
is a basis for the column space of
\bm{A}
. Let
\bm{n}_{1},\bm{n}_{2},\ldots,\bm{n}_{k}\in\real^{n}
form a basis for the null space of
\bm{A}
. Following again from the proof of Theorem
,
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})\bot\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
, thus,
\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{r}
are perpendicular to
\bm{n}_{1},\bm{n}_{2},\ldots,\bm{n}_{k}
. Then,
\{\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{r},\bm{n}_{1},\bm{n}_{2},\ldots,\bm{n}_{k}\}
is linearly independent in
n
.

For any vector
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
,
\bm{A}\bm{x}
lies in the column space of
\bm{A}
, so it can be written as a linear combination of
\bm{A}\bm{r}_{1},\bm{A}\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{A}\bm{r}_{r}
:
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\bm{A}\bm{r}_{i}
. This implies that
\bm{A}(\bm{x}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\bm{r}_{i})=\mathbf{0}
, and
\bm{x}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\bm{r}_{i}
is thus in
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
. Since
\{\bm{n}_{1},\bm{n}_{2},\ldots,\bm{n}_{k}\}
is a basis for the null space of
\bm{A}
,
\bm{x}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\bm{r}_{i}
can be represented as a linear combination of
\bm{n}_{1},\bm{n}_{2},\ldots,\bm{n}_{k}
:
\bm{x}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\bm{r}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}b_{j}\bm{n}_{j}
, i.e.,
\bm{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\bm{r}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}b_{j}\bm{n}_{j}
. That is, any vector
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
can be represented by
\{\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{r},\bm{n}_{1},\bm{n}_{2},\ldots,\bm{n}_{k}\}
, and the set forms a basis for
n
. Thus, the dimensions satisfy:
r+k=n
, i.e.,
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}))+\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top}))=n
. Similarly, we can prove that
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}))+\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}))=m
.

Figure
illustrates two pairs of such orthogonal subspaces and demonstrates how
\bm{A}
maps
\bm{x}
into the column space. The dimensions of the row space of
\bm{A}
and the null space of
\bm{A}
sum to
n
. And the dimensions of the column space of
\bm{A}
and the null space of
\bm{A}^{\top}
sum to
m
. The null space component is mapped to zero, as
\bm{A}\bm{x}_{\bm{n}}=\mathbf{0}\in\real^{m}
, which is the intersection of the column space of
\bm{A}
and the null space of
\bm{A}^{\top}
.
Conversely, the row space component is mapped to the column space, as
\bm{A}\bm{x}_{\bm{r}}=\bm{A}(\bm{x}_{\bm{r}}+\bm{x}_{\bm{n}})=\bm{b}\in\real^{m}
.

Definition 16
(Orthogonal Matrix).

A real square matrix
\bm{Q}
is called an orthogonal matrix if its inverse equals its transpose, that is,
\bm{Q}^{-1}=\bm{Q}^{\top}
and
\bm{Q}\bm{Q}^{\top}=\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{Q}=\bm{I}
. Equivalently, suppose
\bm{Q}=[\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{n}]
, where
\bm{q}_{i}\in\real^{n}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. Then,
\bm{q}_{i}^{\top}\bm{q}_{j}=\delta(i,j)
, where
\delta(i,j)
is the Kronecker delta function. If
\bm{Q}
contains only
\gamma
of these columns with
\gamma<n
, the relation
\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{Q}=\bm{I}_{\gamma}
stills holds, where
\bm{I}_{\gamma}
is the
\gamma\times\gamma
identity matrix. However, in this case,
\bm{Q}\bm{Q}^{\top}=\bm{I}
will no longer be valid.
An orthogonal matrix also preserves the length of any vector
\bm{x}
, i.e.,
\left\lVert\bm{Q}\bm{x}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert
.

Definition 17
(Permutation Matrix).

A permutation matrix
\bm{P}
is a square binary matrix with exactly one entry of 1 in each row and each column, and all other entries equal to 0.

Row perspective.

A permutation matrix
\bm{P}
can be viewed as having the rows of the identity matrix
\bm{I}
arranged in a specific order.
This order determines the sequence of row permutations. To permute the rows of a matrix
\bm{A}
, multiply
\bm{A}
on the left by
\bm{P}
, yielding
\bm{P}\bm{A}
.

Column perspective.

Alternatively,
\bm{P}
can be viewed as having the columns of the identity matrix
\bm{I}
rearranged. This order determines the sequence of column permutations. To permute the columns of
\bm{A}
, multiply
\bm{A}
on the right by
\bm{P}
, yielding
\bm{A}\bm{P}
.

The permutation matrix
\bm{P}
can also be efficiently represented using a vector
J\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{n}
of indices, such that
\bm{P}=\bm{I}[:,J]
, where
\bm{I}
is the
n\times n
identity matrix. Notably, the sum of the elements in
J
equals
1+2+\ldots+n=\frac{n^{2}+n}{2}
.

Example 18
(Permutation).

Suppose,

\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}1&2&3\\
4&5&6\\
7&8&9\end{bmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{P}=\begin{bmatrix}&1&\\
&&1\\
1&&\end{bmatrix}.

The row permutation is given by

\bm{P}\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}4&5&6\\
7&8&9\\
1&2&3\\
\end{bmatrix},

where the order of the rows of
\bm{A}
appearing in
\bm{P}\bm{A}
matches the order of the rows of
\bm{I}
in
\bm{P}
. And the column permutation is given by

\bm{A}\bm{P}=\begin{bmatrix}3&1&2\\
6&4&5\\
9&7&8\end{bmatrix},

where the order of the columns of
\bm{A}
appearing in
\bm{A}\bm{P}
matches the order of the columns of
\bm{I}
in
\bm{P}
.

Definition 19
(Determinant: Laplace Expansion by Minors).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
be any square matrix, and let
\bm{A}_{ij}\in\real^{(n-1)\times(n-1)}
denote the submatrix of
\bm{A}
obtained by deleting the
i
-th row and
j
-th column.
The determinant of
\bm{A}
can be computed recursively using the following equations:

\det(\bm{A})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{i+k}a_{ik}\det(\bm{A}_{ik})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k+j}a_{kj}\det(\bm{A}_{kj}),

(0.1)

where the first equation is the Laplace expansion by minors along row
i
, and the second equation is the Laplace expansion by minors along column
j
.
Equivalently, given a cardinality
r
, and consider an index set
J\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
with cardinality
r
(
\left\lvert J\right\rvert=r
) and its complementary set
J^{C}=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}\backslash J
, we have:

\displaystyle\det(\bm{A})=\sum_{I}(-1)^{\gamma}\det(\bm{A}[I,J])\det(\bm{A}[I^{C},J^{C}])=\sum_{I}(-1)^{\gamma}\det(\bm{A}[J,I])\det(\bm{A}[J^{C},I^{C}]),

where
\gamma=\sum_{i\in I}i+\sum_{j\in J}j
, and the sum is taken over all the index sets
I\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
with cardinality
r
.
When
r=1
, this reduces to (
).

Remark 20
(Determinant: Alternating Sums and Permutations).

Let the function
p:\{1,2,\ldots,n\}\rightarrow\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
be a one-to-one function of permutations, i.e.,
p(i)=i
in the identity case.
Then, there are
n!
distinct permutations of the set
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. For a given permutation, let
\text{sgn}(p)=1
if the minimum number of transpositions to achieve this permutation is even; and
\text{sgn}(p)=-1
otherwise.
Then, the determinant can be equivalently defined as

\det(\bm{A})=\sum_{p}\left(\text{sgn}(p)\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{ip(i)}\right).

(0.2)

Definition 21
(Adjugate).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
be any square matrix. Then, the adjugate of
\bm{A}
is an
n\times n
matrix whose
(i,j)
-th element is given by

\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})_{ij}=(-1)^{i+j}\det\left(\bm{A}\big[\{j\}^{C},\{i\}^{C}\big]\right),

(0.3)

where
\{i\}^{C}
is the complementary set of
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
:
\{i\}^{C}=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}\backslash i
.
Comparing this with the definition of determinants, we have

\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})\bm{A}=\bm{A}\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})=\det(\bm{A})\bm{I}.

(0.4)

This shows that
\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})
is nonsingular (resp. upper triangular, diagonal) if
\bm{A}
is nonsingular (resp. upper triangular, diagonal):

\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})=\det(\bm{A})\bm{A}^{-1}.

(0.5)

If
\bm{A}
is singular, then every column of
\bm{A}
lies in the null space of
\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})
: the dimension of the null space of
\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})
is at least the rank of
\bm{A}
.

For example,
\mathrm{adj}\big(\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\normalsize\big)=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}d&-b\\
-c&a\end{bmatrix}
.

Exercise
\thetheo
(Properties of Adjugate)

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
. Show that

•

\mathrm{adj}(c\bm{A})=c^{n-1}\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})\implies\mathrm{adj}(c\bm{I})=c^{n-1}\bm{I}
.

•

\det(\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A}))=(\det(\bm{A}))^{n-1}
.

•

If
\bm{A}
is nonsingular,
\bm{A}^{-1}=(\det(\bm{A}))^{-1}\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})
.

•

If
\bm{A}
is nonsingular,
\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A}^{-1})=\bm{A}/\det(\bm{A})
.

•

If
\bm{A}
is singular and
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})\leq n-2
,
\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})=\mathbf{0}
.

•

If
\bm{A}
is singular and
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=n-1
,
\mathrm{rank}(\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A}))=1
.

•

If
\bm{A},\bm{B}
are nonsingular,
\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A}\bm{B})=\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})\mathrm{adj}(\bm{B})
. (This actually can be extended to all
\bm{A},\bm{B}
due to continuity.)

•

If
\bm{A}
is nonsingular,
\mathrm{adj}(\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A}))=(\det(\bm{A}))^{n-2}\bm{A}
. (This actually can be extended to all
\bm{A}
due to continuity.)

•

If
\bm{A}
is nonsingular,
\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A}^{\top})=\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})^{\top}
. (This actually can be extended to all
\bm{A}
due to continuity.)

•

The adjugate is the transpose of the gradient of
\det(\bm{A})
:
\mathrm{adj}(\bm{A})_{ij}=\frac{\partial}{\partial a_{ji}}\det(\bm{A})
.

From an introductory linear algebra course, we observe the following equivalences regarding nonsingular matrices.

Remark 22
(List of Equivalence of Nonsingularity for a Matrix).

For a square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, the following claims are equivalent:

•

\bm{A}
is nonsingular;

•

\bm{A}
is invertible, i.e.,
\bm{A}^{-1}
exists;

•

\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{b}
has a unique solution
\bm{x}=\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{b}
;

•

\bm{A}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
has a unique, trivial solution:
\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
;

•

Columns of
\bm{A}
are linearly independent;

•

Rows of
\bm{A}
are linearly independent;

•

\det(\bm{A})\neq 0
;

•

\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}))=0
;

•

\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})=\{\mathbf{0}\}
, i.e., the null space is trivial;

•

\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})=\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})=\real^{n}
, i.e., the column space or row space span the whole
n
;

•

\bm{A}
has full rank
r=n
;

•

The reduced row echelon form is
\bm{R}=\bm{I}
;

•

\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is symmetric positive definite;

•

\bm{A}
has
n
nonzero (positive) singular values;

•

All eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
are nonzero.

It is important to keep the above equivalences in mind, as failing to do so can easily lead to confusion. On the other hand, the following remark outlines the equivalent claims for singular matrices.

Remark 23
(List of Equivalence of Singularity for a Matrix).

For a square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
with an eigenpair
(\lambda,\bm{u})
, the following claims are equivalent:

•

(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})
is singular;

•

(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})
is not invertible;

•

(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
has nonzero
\bm{x}\neq\mathbf{0}
solutions, and
\bm{x}=\bm{u}
is one of such solutions;

•

(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})
has linearly dependent columns;

•

\det(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})=0
;

•

\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I}))>0
;

•

Null space of
(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})
is nontrivial;

•

Columns of
(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})
are linearly dependent;

•

Rows of
(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})
are linearly dependent;

•

(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})
has rank
r<n
;

•

Dimension of column space = dimension of row space =
r<n
;

•

(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})^{\top}(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})
is symmetric semidefinite;

•

(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})
has
r<n
nonzero (positive) singular values;

•

Zero is an eigenvalue of
(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})
.

Part I
Gaussian Elimination

\nopagecolor

Chapter 1
LU Decomposition

1.1
LU Decomposition

One of the most well-known and foundational matrix decompositions is the LU decomposition. The details are outlined in the following theorem, and the proof of its existence will be discussed in subsequent sections.

Let
\bm{A}
be a nonsingular
n\times n
square matrix. Then, it can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U},

where
\bm{P}
is a permutation matrix,
\bm{L}
is a unit lower triangular matrix (i.e., a lower triangular matrix with all 1’s on the diagonal), and
\bm{U}
is a
nonsingular
upper triangular matrix.

Note that, in the subsequent text, decomposition-related results will be presented in blue boxes, while other claims and theorems will be in gray boxes. This convention will be consistently applied throughout the remainder of the book without further notification.

In certain cases, the use of the permutation matrix is unnecessary. This decomposition depends on the leading principal minors. We provide a precise definition, which is crucial for the subsequent illustration.

Definition 24
(Principal Minors).

Let
\bm{A}
be an
n\times n
square matrix. A
k\times k
submatrix of
\bm{A}
obtained by deleting any
n-k
columns and the same
n-k
rows from
\bm{A}
is called a
k
-th order principal submatrix of
\bm{A}
. The determinant of a
k\times k
principal submatrix is called a
k
-th order principal minor of
\bm{A}
.

Definition 25
(Leading Principal Minors).

Let
\bm{A}
be an
n\times n
square matrix. A
k\times k
submatrix of
\bm{A}
obtained by deleting the
last

n-k
columns and the
last

n-k
rows from
\bm{A}
is called the
k
-th order leading principal submatrix of
\bm{A}
, that is, the
k\times k
submatrix taken from the top-left corner of
\bm{A}
. The determinant of the
k\times k
leading principal submatrix is called the
k
-th order leading principal minor of
\bm{A}
.

If the leading principal minors of matrix
\bm{A}
satisfy mild conditions, the LU decomposition does not require a permutation matrix.

Let
\bm{A}
be an
n\times n
square matrix with nonzero leading principal minors, i.e.,
\det(\bm{A}_{1:k,1:k})\neq 0
, for all
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. Then,
\bm{A}
can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U},

where
\bm{L}
is a unit lower triangular matrix (i.e., a lower triangular matrix with all 1’s on the diagonal), and
\bm{U}
is a
nonsingular
upper triangular matrix.
Specifically, this decomposition is
unique
. See Corollary
.

Remark 26
(Other Forms of the LU Decomposition without Permutation).

In Theorem
, we assume that the leading principal minors are nonzero, implying that the leading principal submatrices and the matrix
\bm{A}
are nonsingular.

Singular
\bm{A}
.

In the previous theorem, we assume
\bm{A}
is nonsingular as well. However, it is important to note that the LU decomposition can exist for singular matrices
\bm{A}
as well.
This can be shown in the following section that, if matrix
\bm{A}
is singular, some pivots will be zero, and the corresponding diagonal values of
\bm{U}
will be zero.

Singular leading principal submatrices.

Even when
\bm{A}
is nonsingular, the leading principal submatrices might be singular. Moreover, if certain leading principal minors are zero, the LU decomposition is still possible, but it loses its uniqueness under these conditions.

We will explore the origin of this decomposition in the next section. There are also generalizations of the LU decomposition for non-square or singular matrices, such as the rank-revealing LU decomposition. Interested readers are encouraged to consult
Pan 2000
;
Miranian and Gu 2003
;
Dopico et al. 2006
for further discussion, or see Section
for a brief overview.

1.2
Relation to Gaussian Elimination

Solving the linear system equation
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{b}
is a fundamental problem in linear algebra.

Gaussian elimination
simplifies a linear system, transforming it into an upper triangular form through
elementary row transformations
. This process unfolds over
n-1
stages for a square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
.
As a result, it becomes much easier to solve using
backward substitution
. The elementary transformations are defined rigorously as follows.

Definition 27
(Elementary Transformation).

Given a square matrix
\bm{A}
, the following three transformations are referred to as elementary row/column transformations:

1.

Interchanging two rows (or columns) of
\bm{A}
;

2.

Multiplying all elements of a row (or a column) of
\bm{A}
by some nonzero values;

3.

Adding any row (or column) of
\bm{A}
multiplied by a nonzero number to any other row (or column);

Specifically, the elementary row transformations of
\bm{A}
are unit lower triangular matrices that multiply on the left of
\bm{A}
(e.g.,
\bm{E}\bm{A}
), and the elementary column transformations of
\bm{A}
are unit upper triangular matrices that multiply on the right of
\bm{A}
(e.g.,
\bm{A}\bm{E}
).

The Gaussian elimination is then described by the third type (elementary row transformation) mentioned above. Suppose the upper triangular matrix obtained by Gaussian elimination is given by
\bm{U}=\bm{E}_{n-1}\bm{E}_{n-2}\ldots\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}
(
n-1
steps), and at the
k
-th stage, the
k
-th column of
\bm{E}_{k-1}\bm{E}_{k-2}\ldots\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}
is
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
. Gaussian elimination aims to introduce zeros below the diagonal of
\bm{x}
by

\bm{E}_{k}=\bm{I}-\bm{z}_{k}\bm{e}_{k}^{\top},

(1.1)

where
\bm{e}_{k}\in\real^{n}
represents the
k
-th unit basis vector, and
\bm{z}_{k}\in\real^{n}
is defined as

\bm{z}_{k}=[0,\ldots,0,z_{k+1},\ldots,z_{n}]^{\top},\qquad z_{i}=\frac{x_{i}}{x_{k}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,i\in\{k+1,\ldots,n\}.

We observe that
\bm{E}_{k}
is a unit lower triangular matrix (with
1
’s on the diagonal) with only the
k
-th column of the lower submatrix being nonzero,

\bm{E}_{k}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&0&0&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\ldots&1&0&\ldots&0\\
0&\ldots&-z_{k+1}&1&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\ldots&-z_{n}&0&\ldots&1\end{bmatrix}.

Multiplying on the left by
\bm{E}_{k}
will introduce zeros below the diagonal:

\bm{E}_{k}\bm{x}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&0&0&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\ldots&1&0&\ldots&0\\
0&\ldots&-z_{k+1}&1&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\ldots&-z_{n}&0&\ldots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{k}\\
x_{k+1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{k}\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

For example, we outline the Gaussian elimination steps for a
4\times 4
matrix. For simplicity, we assume no row permutations.
In the following matrix,
\boxtimes
represents a value that may not be zero, and
boldface
indicates the value has just been changed.

A Trivial Gaussian Elimination For a
4\times 4
Matrix:

\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{3}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{3}\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}},

(1.2)

where
\bm{E}_{1},\bm{E}_{2}
, and
\bm{E}_{3}
are lower triangular matrices. Specifically, as discussed above, Gaussian transformation matrices
\bm{E}_{i}
’s are unit lower triangular matrices with
1
’s on the diagonal. This can be explained that for the
k
-th transformation
\bm{E}_{k}
, working on the matrix
\bm{E}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}
, the transformation subtracts multiples of the
k
-th row from rows
\{k+1,k+2,\ldots,n\}
to get zeros below the diagonal in the
k
-th column of the matrix, without using rows
\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\}
.

To be more concrete, for the transformation example above at stage
1
, we multiply on the left by
\bm{E}_{1}
so that multiples of the first row are subtracted from rows
2,3,4
, and the first entries of rows
2,3,4
are set to zero. Similar situations apply for step 2 and step 3.
By defining
\bm{L}=\bm{E}_{1}^{-1}\bm{E}_{2}^{-1}\bm{E}_{3}^{-1}
and denoting the matrix after elimination as
\bm{U}
, we get
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U}
. Thus, we obtain an LU decomposition for this
4\times 4
matrix
\bm{A}
.

Definition 28
(Pivot).

The first nonzero entry in the row after each elimination step is referred to as the pivot. For example, the
blue
crosses in Equation (
) represent pivots.

However, it is possible for
a_{11}
(the entry (1,1) of matrix
\bm{A}
) to be zero on occasion.
In such cases, no such matrix
\bm{E}_{1}
can make the next elimination step successful.
Therefore, we need to interchange the first row and the second row via a permutation matrix
\bm{P}_{1}
. This is known as the
pivoting
, or simply
permutation
.

Gaussian Elimination With a Permutation in the Beginning:

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{A}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{P}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{P}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{1}\bm{P}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{P}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{3}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{3}\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{P}_{1}\bm{A}}.

By defining
\bm{L}=\bm{E}_{1}^{-1}\bm{E}_{2}^{-1}\bm{E}_{3}^{-1}
and
\bm{P}=\bm{P}_{1}^{-1}
, the expression
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U}
represents a comprehensive LU decomposition with permutation for the
4\times 4
matrix
\bm{A}
.

In some circumstances, other permutation matrices
\bm{P}_{2},\bm{P}_{3},\ldots
will appear in between the lower triangular
\bm{E}_{i}
’s. An illustration is provided below.

Gaussian Elimination With a Permutation in Between:

\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{P}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{P}_{1}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&\boxtimes\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{2}\bm{P}_{1}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}.

In this scenario, we find
\bm{U}=\bm{E}_{2}\bm{P}_{1}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}
. In Section
or Section
, we will show that the interleaved permutations lead to the same form
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U}
, where
\bm{P}
accounts for all permutations.

The provided examples can be easily extended to any
n\times n
matrix, assuming there are no row permutations in the process. We will have
n-1
such lower triangular transformations.
The
k
-th transformation
\bm{E}_{k}
introduces zeros below the diagonal in the
k
-th column of
\bm{A}
by subtracting multiples of the
k
-th row from rows
\{k+1,k+2,\ldots,n\}
.
Finally, by defining
\bm{L}=\bm{E}_{1}^{-1}\bm{E}_{2}^{-1}\ldots\bm{E}_{n-1}^{-1}
, we obtain the LU decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U}
without permutation.

From the above examples for row elementary operations, we conclude the following result.

Proposition 29
(Row Space after Row Operations).

Given a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, operated by a sequence of row transformations
\bm{E}_{1},\bm{E}_{2},\ldots,\bm{E}_{k}
and let
\bm{E}=\bm{E}_{k}\bm{E}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{E}_{1}
. Then, the row space of
\bm{B}=\bm{E}\bm{A}
is identical to the row space of
\bm{A}
.

Proof
[of Proposition
]
Since the rows of
\bm{B}
are linear combinations of the rows of
\bm{A}
, it follows that
\mathcal{C}(\bm{B}^{\top})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
.
Moreover, since the row transformations are invertible,
\bm{E}=\bm{E}_{k}\bm{E}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{E}_{1}
is also invertible. Therefore, we can write:
\bm{A}={\bm{E}}^{-1}\bm{B}
. This implies that the rows of
\bm{A}
are also linear combinations of the rows of
\bm{B}
:
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\bm{B}^{\top})
.
Combining the two results, we conclude that
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})=\mathcal{C}(\bm{B}^{\top})
.

Note, however, the column space of
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
may be different. But since the dimension of the row space is equal to that of the column space, the dimensions of the column spaces of
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
are the same.

1.3
Existence of LU Decomposition without Permutation

The Gaussian elimination, or Gaussian transformation, elucidates the basis of the LU decomposition.
We then prove Theorem
rigorously, i.e., the existence of the LU decomposition without permutation by induction.

Proof
[of Theorem
: LU Decomposition without Permutation]
We will prove by induction that every
n\times n
square matrix
\bm{A}
with nonzero leading principal minors possesses the LU decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U}
. The
1\times 1
case is trivial by setting
L=1,U=A
, thus,
A=LU
.

Assuming that any
k\times k
matrix
\bm{A}_{k}
with all leading principal minors being nonzero has an LU decomposition without permutation, our goal is to establish that any
(k+1)\times(k+1)
matrix
\bm{A}_{k+1}
can also be expressed in this LU decomposition form without permutation.
This, once proven, completes the overall demonstration.

For any
(k+1)\times(k+1)
matrix
\bm{A}_{k+1}
, suppose the
k
-th order leading principal submatrix of
\bm{A}_{k+1}
is
\bm{A}_{k}
with size
k\times k
. Then
\bm{A}_{k}
can be factored as
\bm{A}_{k}=\bm{L}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}
, where
\bm{L}_{k}
is a unit lower triangular matrix and
\bm{U}_{k}
is a nonsingular upper triangular matrix, as per the assumption. Express
\bm{A}_{k+1}
as

\bm{A}_{k+1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{k}&\bm{b}\\
\bm{c}^{\top}&d\end{bmatrix}.

Then it admits the following factorization:

\bm{A}_{k+1}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{k}&\bm{b}\\
\bm{c}^{\top}&d\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{k}&\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{x}^{\top}&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{y}\\
\mathbf{0}&z\end{bmatrix}=\bm{L}_{k+1}\bm{U}_{k+1},

where
\bm{b}=\bm{L}_{k}\bm{y}
,
\bm{c}^{\top}=\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{U}_{k}
,
d=\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{y}+z
,
\bm{L}_{k+1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{k}&\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{x}^{\top}&1\end{bmatrix}
, and
\bm{U}_{k+1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{y}\\
\mathbf{0}&z\end{bmatrix}
.
From the assumption,
\bm{L}_{k}
and
\bm{U}_{k}
are nonsingular. Therefore,

\bm{y}=\bm{L}_{k}^{-1}\bm{b},\qquad\bm{x}^{\top}=\bm{c}^{\top}\bm{U}_{k}^{-1},\qquad z=d-\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{y}.

If, further, we could prove that
z
is nonzero such that
\bm{U}_{k+1}
is nonsingular, we complete the proof.

Because all the leading principal minors of
\bm{A}_{k+1}
are nonzero,
we have
\det(\bm{A}_{k+1})=

\det(\bm{A}_{k})\cdot

\det(d-\bm{c}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}^{-1}\bm{b})=\det(\bm{A}_{k})\cdot(d-\bm{c}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}^{-1}\bm{b})\neq 0
since
d-\bm{c}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}^{-1}\bm{b}
is a scalar.
As
\det(\bm{A}_{k})\neq 0
from the assumption, we conclude
d-\bm{c}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}^{-1}\bm{b}\neq 0
.
By substituting
\bm{b}=\bm{L}_{k}\bm{y}
and
\bm{c}^{\top}=\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{U}_{k}
into the formula, we have
d-\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}^{-1}\bm{L}_{k}\bm{y}=d-\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{U}_{k}(\bm{L}_{k}\bm{U}_{k})^{-1}\bm{L}_{k}\bm{y}=d-\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{y}\neq 0
, which exactly matches the form of
z\neq 0
. Thus, we find
\bm{L}_{k+1}
with all the values on the diagonal being 1, and
\bm{U}_{k+1}
with all the values on the diagonal being nonzero, which means
\bm{L}_{k+1}
and
\bm{U}_{k+1}
are nonsingular.
This completes the proof.

We further demonstrate that if no permutation is involved, the LU decomposition is unique.

Corollary 30
(Uniqueness of the LU Decomposition without Permutation).

Let
\bm{A}
be an
n\times n
square matrix with nonzero leading principal minors. Then, the LU decomposition of
\bm{A}
is unique.

Proof
[of Corollary
]
Assume the LU decomposition is not unique. Then, there exist two decompositions,
\bm{A}=\bm{L}_{1}\bm{U}_{1}=\bm{L}_{2}\bm{U}_{2}
, which implies
\bm{L}_{2}^{-1}\bm{L}_{1}=\bm{U}_{2}\bm{U}_{1}^{-1}
. The left-hand side of the equation is a unit lower triangular matrix, while the right-hand side is an upper triangular matrix.
Consequently, both sides must be diagonal matrices. Since the inverse of a unit lower triangular matrix is also a unit lower triangular matrix, and the product of unit lower triangular matrices remains a unit lower triangular matrix, we deduce that
\bm{L}_{2}^{-1}\bm{L}_{1}=\bm{I}
.
The equality implies that both sides are identity matrices such that
\bm{L}_{1}=\bm{L}_{2}
and
\bm{U}_{1}=\bm{U}_{2}
, resulting in a contradiction.

In the proof of Theorem
, we showed that the diagonal values of the upper triangular matrix are all nonzero if the leading principal minors of
\bm{A}
are all nonzero. Dividing each row of
\bm{U}
by its corresponding diagonal entry leads to an equivalent decomposition known as the
LDU decomposition
.

Let
\bm{A}
be an
n\times n
square matrix with nonzero leading principal minors, i.e.,
\det(\bm{A}_{1:k,1:k})\neq 0
, for all
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. Then,
\bm{A}
can be
uniquely
decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{D}\bm{U},

where
\bm{L}
is a unit lower triangular matrix,
\bm{U}
is a
unit
upper triangular matrix, and
\bm{D}
is a diagonal matrix.

The result follows directly from the LU decomposition of
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{L}
is unit lower triangular and
\bm{R}
is upper triangular. Define
\bm{D}=\mathrm{diag}(r_{11},r_{22},\ldots,r_{nn})
, where
r_{ii}
are the diagonal entries of
\bm{R}
. Then,
\bm{D}^{-1}\bm{R}=\bm{U}
is a unit upper triangular matrix.
Uniqueness follows from the uniqueness of the LU decomposition.

1.4
Existence of LU Decomposition with Permutation

In Theorem
, we require that
\bm{A}
has nonzero leading principal minors. However, this condition is not necessary. Even when the leading principal minors are zero, nonsingular matrices still admit an LU decomposition, provided row permutations are allowed. The proof still uses induction.
To formalize this, we first introduce the concept of the
Schur complement
.

Definition 31
(Schur Complement).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
be a matrix, and suppose its (1,1) entry,
a_{11}
, is not zero. Then, the Schur complement of
a_{11}
in
\bm{A}
is
\bm{A}_{2}=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{1,2:n}
.

Proof
[of Theorem
: LU Decomposition with Permutation]
We start with the base case: any
1\times 1
nonsingular matrix has a full LU decomposition
A=PLU
by taking
P=1
,
L=1
, and
U=A
.
Assume that every
(n-1)\times(n-1)
nonsingular matrix admits an LU decomposition with permutation. We now show this holds for any
n\times n
nonsingular matrix
\bm{A}
.

We will formulate the proof in the following order.
If
\bm{A}
is nonsingular, then its row-permuted matrix
\bm{B}
is also nonsingular
. And the
Schur complement
of
b_{11}
in
\bm{B}
is also nonsingular. Finally, we formulate the decomposition of
\bm{A}
by
\bm{B}
from this property.

At least one element in the first column of
\bm{A}
must be nonzero; otherwise,
\bm{A}
would be singular. We can then apply a row permutation that makes the element in entry
(1,1)
to be nonzero.
Let
\bm{P}_{1}
be a permutation matrix that moves a nonzero entry to the (1,1) position. Define
\bm{B}=\bm{P}_{1}\bm{A}
, so
b_{11}\neq 0
. Since both
\bm{A}
and
\bm{P}_{1}
are nonsingular, their product
\bm{B}
is also nonsingular.

Schur complement of
\bm{B}
is also nonsingular.

The Schur complement of
b_{11}
in
\bm{B}
, which has dimensions
(n-1)\times(n-1)
:

\widehat{\bm{B}}=\bm{B}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{b_{11}}\bm{B}_{2:n,1}\bm{B}_{1,2:n}.

To prove
\widehat{\bm{B}}
is nonsingular, assume there exists an
(n-1)
-vector
\bm{x}
such that

\widehat{\bm{B}}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}.

Then,
\bm{x}
and
y=-\frac{1}{b_{11}}\bm{B}_{1,2:n}\cdot\bm{x}
satisfy

\bm{B}\left[\begin{matrix}\bm{x}\\
y\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}b_{11}&\bm{B}_{1,2:n}\\
\bm{B}_{2:n,1}&\bm{B}_{2:n,2:n}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}y\\
\bm{x}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right].

Since
\bm{B}
is nonsingular,
\bm{x}
and
y
must be zero. Therefore,
\widehat{\bm{B}}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
holds only if
\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
, which implies that the null space of
\widehat{\bm{B}}
has dimension 0. Hence,
\widehat{\bm{B}}
is nonsingular.

By the induction assumption, any
(n-1)\times(n-1)
nonsingular matrix can be written in the full LU decomposition form:

\widehat{\bm{B}}=\bm{P}_{2}\bm{L}_{2}\bm{U}_{2}.

We then factor
\bm{A}
as

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{P}_{1}^{\top}\left[\begin{matrix}b_{11}&\bm{B}_{1,2:n}\\
\bm{B}_{2:n,1}&\bm{B}_{2:n,2:n}\end{matrix}\right]=\bm{P}_{1}^{\top}\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\bm{P}_{2}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}b_{11}&\bm{B}_{1,2:n}\\
\bm{P}_{2}^{\top}\bm{B}_{2:n,1}&\bm{P}_{2}^{\top}\bm{B}_{2:n,2:n}\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\bm{P}_{1}^{\top}\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\bm{P}_{2}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}b_{11}&\bm{B}_{1,2:n}\\
\bm{P}_{2}^{\top}\bm{B}_{2:n,1}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{L}_{2}\bm{U}_{2}}+\bm{P}_{2}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\frac{1}{b_{11}}\bm{B}_{2:n,1}\bm{B}_{1,2:n}}\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\bm{P}_{1}^{\top}\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\bm{P}_{2}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\frac{1}{b_{11}}\bm{P}_{2}^{\top}\bm{B}_{2:n,1}&\bm{L}_{2}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}b_{11}&\bm{B}_{1,2:n}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{U}_{2}\end{matrix}\right].

Thus, the full LU decomposition of
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U}
is given by:

\bm{P}=\bm{P}_{1}^{\top}\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&\bm{P}_{2}\end{matrix}\right],\qquad\bm{L}=\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\frac{1}{b_{11}}\bm{P}_{2}^{\top}\bm{B}_{2:n,1}&\bm{L}_{2}\end{matrix}\right],\qquad\bm{U}=\left[\begin{matrix}b_{11}&\bm{B}_{1,2:n}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{U}_{2}\end{matrix}\right].

This completes the proof.

1.5
Bandwidth Preserving in LU Decomposition without Permutation

The bandwidth of any matrix is defined as follows.

Definition 32
(Matrix Bandwidth).

For any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
with the entry at position
(i,j)
denoted as
a_{ij}
, the matrix
\bm{A}
has an upper bandwidth
q
if
a_{ij}=0
for
j>i+q
, and a lower bandwidth
p
if
a_{ij}=0
for
i>j+p
.

An example of a
6\times 6
matrix with an upper bandwidth of
2
and a lower bandwidth of
3
is illustrated below:

\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&0&0&0\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&0&0\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&0\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\end{bmatrix}.

We now prove that the bandwidth of a matrix is preserved after LU decomposition, provided no row permutations are involved.

Lemma 33
(Bandwidth Preserving).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
have an upper bandwidth of
q
and a lower bandwidth of
p
. If
\bm{A}
admits the LU decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U}
, then
\bm{L}
has a lower bandwidth of
p
, and
\bm{U}
has an upper bandwidth of
q
.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
The LU decomposition without permutation can be obtained as follows:

\bm{A}=\left[\begin{matrix}a_{11}&\bm{A}_{1,2:n}\\
\bm{A}_{2:n,1}&\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}&\bm{I}_{n-1}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}a_{11}&\bm{A}_{1,2:n}\\
0&\bm{S}\end{matrix}\right]=\bm{L}_{1}\bm{U}_{1},

where
\bm{S}=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{1,2:n}
is the Schur complement of
a_{11}
in
\bm{A}
.
This process is referred to as the
s
-decomposition of
\bm{A}
.
The first column of
\bm{L}_{1}
and the first row of
\bm{U}_{1}
retain the required bandwidth properties (bandwidth
p
and
q
, respectively). The Schur complement
\bm{S}
of
a_{11}
has an upper bandwidth of
q-1
and a lower bandwidth of
p-1
, respectively.
By applying induction on the
s
-decomposition to
\bm{S}
, the result follows.

1.6
Block LU Decomposition

Another form of LU decomposition involves factoring the matrix into block triangular matrices.

For any
n\times n
square matrix
\bm{A}
, if the first
m
leading principal block submatrices are nonsingular, then
\bm{A}
can be uniquely factored as

\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{I}&&&\\
\bm{L}_{21}&\bm{I}&&\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\\
\bm{L}_{m1}&\ldots&\bm{L}_{m,m-1}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{11}&\bm{U}_{12}&\ldots&\bm{U}_{1m}\\
&\bm{U}_{22}&&\vdots\\
&&\ddots&\bm{U}_{m-1,m}\\
&&&\bm{U}_{mm}\\
\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{L}_{i,j}
’s and
\bm{U}_{ij}
’s are some block matrices.

It is important to note that
\bm{U}
in the above theorem is not necessarily upper triangular. For example:

\bm{A}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc;{2pt/2pt}cc}0&1&1&1\\
-1&2&-1&2\\
\hline\cr[2pt/2pt]2&1&4&2\\
1&2&3&3\\
\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc;{2pt/2pt}cc}1&0&&\\
0&1&&\\
\hline\cr[2pt/2pt]5&-2&1&\\
4&-1&&1\\
\end{array}\right]\left[\begin{array}[]{cc;{2pt/2pt}cc}0&1&1&1\\
-1&2&-1&2\\
\hline\cr[2pt/2pt]0&0&-3&1\\
0&0&-2&1\\
\end{array}\right].

The standard non-block LU decomposition fails for
\bm{A}
since the
(1,1)
entry is zero. However, the block LU decomposition remains applicable.

1.7
Application: Linear System via the LU Decomposition

For a well-determined linear system
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{b}
, where
\bm{A}
is an
n\times n
nonsingular matrix, directly computing
\bm{A}^{-1}
is inefficient. Instead, the system can be solved using LU decomposition. If
\bm{A}
admits the LU decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U}
, the solution can be obtained through the following algorithm:

Algorithm 1

Solving Linear Equations by LU Decomposition

1:

\bm{A}
is a nonsingular
n\times n
matrix; solve
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{b}
;

2:

LU Decomposition: factor
\bm{A}
as
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U}
;
\triangleright
(2/3)
n^{3}
flops

3:

Apply permutation: compute
\bm{w}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{b}
;
\triangleright
0 flops

4:

Solve
\bm{L}\bm{v}=\bm{w}
using forward substitution;
\triangleright

n^{2}
flops

5:

Solve
\bm{U}\bm{x}=\bm{v}
using backward substitution;
\triangleright

n^{2}
flops

LU decomposition requires a complexity of
(2/3)n^{3}
floating point operations (flops)
(
Lu 2021c
)
, the backward and forward substitution steps both cost
1+3+\ldots+(2n-1)=n^{2}
flops. Thus, the total cost is approximately
(2/3)n^{3}+2n^{2}
flops. For large
n
, the complexity is dominated by the LU decomposition step, which requires
(2/3)n^{3}
flops. Additionally, for a block LU decomposition of
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U}
, solving
\bm{L}\bm{v}=\bm{w}
and
\bm{U}\bm{x}=\bm{v}
involves additional computations since
\bm{U}
is generally not triangular. This introduces extra complexity compared to standard LU decomposition.

1.8
Application: Computing the Inverse of Nonsingular Matrices

By Theorem
, any nonsingular matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
admits a full LU factorization
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U}
. The inverse of
\bm{A}
can be obtained by solving the matrix equation:

\bm{A}\bm{X}=\bm{I},

which involves solving
n
linear systems of the form
\bm{A}\bm{x}_{i}=\bm{e}_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, where
\bm{x}_{i}
is the
i
-the column of
\bm{X}
and
\bm{e}_{i}
represents the
i
-th column of
\bm{I}
(i.e., the
i
-th unit vector).

Theorem 34
(Inverse of Nonsingular Matrix by Linear System).

Computing the inverse of a nonsingular matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
using
n
linear systems requires
\sim(2/3)n^{3}+n(2n^{2})=(8/3)n^{3}
flops, where
(2/3)n^{3}
is the cost of the LU decomposition of
\bm{A}
.

This result follows directly from Algorithm
.
However, computational efficiency can be improved by leveraging the triangular structure of
\bm{U}
and
\bm{L}
.
Specifically, the inverse of
\bm{A}
can be expressed as
\bm{A}^{-1}=\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{L}^{-1}\bm{P}^{-1}=\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{L}^{-1}\bm{P}^{T}
. Using this approach, the computational complexity can be reduced from
(8/3)n^{3}
to
2n^{3}
flops
(
Lu 2021c
)
.

1.9
Application: Computing the Determinant

The LU decomposition also simplifies the computation of the determinant of a matrix.
If
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U}
, then
\det(\bm{A})=\det(\bm{L}\bm{U})=\det(\bm{L})\det(\bm{U})=u_{11}u_{22}\ldots u_{nn}
, where
u_{ii}
is the
i
-th diagonal element of
\bm{U}
(
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
).

Further, for an LU decomposition with permutation,
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U}
, the determinant of
\bm{A}
becomes
\det(\bm{A})=\det(\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U})=\det(\bm{P})u_{11}u_{22}\ldots u_{nn}
.
The determinant of a permutation matrix is either 1 or –1 because after
changing rows around (which changes the sign of the determinant ), a permutation matrix
becomes the identity matrix
\bm{I}
, whose determinant is one.

1.10
Pivoting

1.10.1
Partial Pivoting

In practice, it is often advantageous to apply pivoting even when it is not strictly necessary. When solving a linear system using LU decomposition, as described in Algorithm
, selecting the pivot as the largest entry—especially when the diagonal elements of
\bm{U}
are small—helps mitigate inaccuracies in the solutions. This technique, known as
partial pivoting
, is widely adopted to enhance numerical stability.
For example,

Partial Pivoting For a
4\times 4
Matrix:

\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\bm{2}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{5}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{7}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{P}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}7}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}\\
0&5&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}2}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{P}_{1}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&7&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{2}\bm{P}_{1}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}.

(1.3)

In this example, after the transformation by
\bm{E}_{1}
, the element 7 is chosen as the pivot. Although this pivot is not strictly necessary, it ensures that no multiplier exceeds an absolute value of 1 during Gaussian elimination.
The general procedure for calculating LU decomposition with partial pivoting for a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is detailed in Algorithm
.

Algorithm 2

LU Decomposition with Partial Pivoting

1:

Matrix
\bm{A}
of size
n\times n
;

2:

Set
\bm{U}=\bm{A}
;

3:

for

k=1
to
n-1

do

\triangleright
i.e., get the
k
-th column of
\bm{U}

4:

Find a row permutation
\bm{P}_{k}
that swaps
u_{kk}
with the largest element in
|\bm{U}_{k:n,k}|
;

5:

\bm{U}=\bm{P}_{k}\bm{U}
;

6:

Compute the Gaussian transformation
\bm{E}_{k}
to zero out elements below the diagonal in the
k
-th column of
\bm{U}
;

7:

\bm{U}=\bm{E}_{k}\bm{U}
;

8:

end

for

9:

Output
\bm{U}
;

The computational cost of this algorithm is approximately
\sim 2/3(n^{3})
flops and
(n-1)+(n-2)+\ldots+1\sim\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
comparisons due to the pivoting process.
The result is an upper triangular matrix
\bm{U}
, expressed as:

\bm{U}=\bm{E}_{n-1}\bm{P}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{E}_{2}\bm{P}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{P}_{1}\bm{A}.

(1.4)

Computing the final
\bm{L}
.

To reconstruct the LU decomposition of the following form:

\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U},

where
\bm{P}=\bm{P}_{1}\bm{P}_{2}\ldots\bm{P}_{n-1}
represents the overall permutation matrix,
\bm{U}
is the upper triangular matrix obtained directly from the algorithm, and
\bm{L}
is a unit lower triangular matrix with
|l_{ij}|\leq 1
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
.
The submatrix
\bm{L}_{k+1:n,k}
is a permuted version of
\bm{E}_{k}
’s multipliers. To see this, we notice that the permutation matrices used in the algorithm involve only swaps of two rows.
This implies each
\bm{P}_{k}
is symmetric and satisfies
\bm{P}_{k}^{2}=\bm{I}
, for
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
.
Let

\bm{M}_{k}=(\bm{P}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{P}_{k+1})\bm{E}_{k}(\bm{P}_{k+1}\ldots\bm{P}_{n-1}).

Substituting this into Equation (
),
\bm{U}
can be expressed as

\bm{U}=\bm{M}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{M}_{2}\bm{M}_{1}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{A}.

To analyze
\bm{M}_{k}
, recall that each
\bm{P}_{k+1}
is a permutation matrix with an identity submatrix in the upper-left
k\times k
. Thus,

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{k}

\displaystyle=(\bm{P}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{P}_{k+1})(\bm{I}_{n}-\bm{z}_{k}\bm{e}_{k}^{\top})(\bm{P}_{k+1}\ldots\bm{P}_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\bm{I}_{n}-(\bm{P}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{P}_{k+1})(\bm{z}_{k}\bm{e}_{k}^{\top})(\bm{P}_{k+1}\ldots\bm{P}_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\bm{I}_{n}-(\bm{P}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{P}_{k+1}\bm{z}_{k})(\bm{e}_{k}^{\top}\bm{P}_{k+1}\ldots\bm{P}_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\bm{I}_{n}-(\bm{P}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{P}_{k+1}\bm{z}_{k})\bm{e}_{k}^{\top}.\qquad

\displaystyle\text{(since $\bm{e}_{k}^{\top}\bm{P}_{k+1}\ldots\bm{P}_{n-1}=\bm{e}_{k}^{\top}$)}

This shows that
\bm{M}_{k}
is unit lower triangular, with its
k
-th column being a permuted version of
\bm{E}_{k}
. Consequently, the final lower triangular matrix is:

\bm{L}=\bm{M}_{1}^{-1}\bm{M}_{2}^{-1}\ldots\bm{M}_{n-1}^{-1}.

We obtain the full LU decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U}
.

1.10.2
Complete Pivoting

In partial pivoting, zeros below the diagonal in the
k
-th column of
\bm{U}
are introduced by selecting the pivot as the largest absolute value in the subcolumn
\bm{U}_{k:n,k}
. In contrast,
complete pivoting
identifies the pivot as the largest absolute entry in the current submatrix
\bm{U}_{k:n,k:n}
, which is then moved to the
(k,k)
position in
\bm{U}
. This requires applying an additional
column permutation

\bm{Q}_{k}
at each step. The final upper triangular matrix
\bm{U}
is expressed as

\bm{U}=\bm{E}_{n-1}\bm{P}_{n-1}\ldots(\bm{E}_{2}\bm{P}_{2}(\bm{E}_{1}\bm{P}_{1}\bm{A}\bm{Q}_{1})\bm{Q}_{2})\ldots\bm{Q}_{n-1}.

The complete pivoting procedure is detailed in Algorithm
.

Algorithm 3

LU Decomposition with Complete Pivoting

1:

Matrix
\bm{A}
with size
n\times n
;

2:

Set
\bm{U}=\bm{A}
;

3:

for

k=1
to
n-1

do

\triangleright
the value
k
is to get the
k
-th column of
\bm{U}

4:

Find a row permutation matrix
\bm{P}_{k}
and a column permutation
\bm{Q}_{k}
that swaps
u_{kk}
with the largest element in
|\bm{U}_{k:n,k:n}|
, say
u_{ab}=\max{|\bm{U}_{k:n,k:n}|}
;

5:

\bm{U}=\bm{P}_{k}\bm{U}\bm{Q}_{k}
;

6:

Determine the Gaussian transformation
\bm{E}_{k}
to introduce zeros below the diagonal in column
k
of
\bm{U}
;

7:

\bm{U}=\bm{E}_{k}\bm{U}
;

8:

end

for

9:

Output
\bm{U}
;

The algorithm requires
\penalty\ 2/3(n^{3})
flops and
(n^{2}+(n-1)^{2}+\ldots+1^{2})\sim\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
comparisons due to pivoting. With
\bm{P}=\bm{P}_{1}\bm{P}_{2}\ldots\bm{P}_{n-1}
,
\bm{Q}=\bm{Q}_{1}\bm{Q}_{2}\ldots\bm{Q}_{n-1}
,

\bm{M}_{k}=(\bm{P}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{P}_{k+1})\bm{E}_{k}(\bm{P}_{k+1}\ldots\bm{P}_{n-1}),\qquad\text{for all $k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}$}

and

\bm{L}=\bm{M}_{1}^{-1}\bm{M}_{2}^{-1}\ldots\bm{M}_{n-1}^{-1},

the final decomposition is
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{U}\bm{Q}^{\top}
, or equivalently,
\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Q}=\bm{L}\bm{U}
.

1.10.3
Rook Pivoting

Rook pivoting
provides an alternative to partial and complete pivoting. Instead of selecting the largest absolute value in
|\bm{U}_{k:n,k:n}|
at the
k
-th step, it identifies an element in the submatrix that is
maximal in both its row and column
. This method is non-unique; multiple elements may satisfy the criteria.
For example, given:

\bm{U}_{k:n,k:n}=\begin{bmatrix}1&2&3&4\\
2&3&7&3\\
5&2&1&2\\
2&1&2&1\\
\end{bmatrix},

complete pivoting would select
7
. However, rook pivoting could choose
5,4,
or
7
, as all are row- and column-maximal.

1.11
Rank-Revealing LU Decomposition

In many applications, Gaussian elimination with pivoting applied to a matrix
\bm{A}
of rank
r
produces a factorization that reveals the rank in the following form:

\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{Q}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{L}_{21}^{\top}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{11}&\bm{U}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{L}_{11}\in\real^{r\times r}
and
\bm{U}_{11}\in\real^{r\times r}
are nonsingular,
\bm{L}_{21},\bm{U}_{21}\in\real^{r\times(n-r)}
, and
\bm{P}
and
\bm{Q}
are permutation matrices. Gaussian elimination with rook pivoting or complete pivoting can generate such factorizations
(
Hwang et al. 1992
;
Higham 2002
)
.

æ
Chapter 1 Problems
6

1.3.

Solve the following system of equations using row reduction:

\displaystyle 2x_{1}+3x_{2}+4x_{3}

\displaystyle=9,

\displaystyle x_{1}+2x_{2}+3x_{3}

\displaystyle=5,

\displaystyle 3x_{1}+4x_{2}+5x_{3}

\displaystyle=7.

1.3.

Two matrices
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
are said to be
row equivalent
(denoted by
\bm{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\sim}}\bm{B}
)
if
\bm{A}
can be transformed into
\bm{B}
using a sequence of elementary row operations.

•

Show that
\bm{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\sim}}\bm{B}
if and only if
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{B}
for some nonsingular matrix
\bm{P}
.

•

Show that if
\bm{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\sim}}\bm{C}
and
\bm{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\sim}}\bm{C}
, then
\bm{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\sim}}\bm{B}
.

•

Show that if
\bm{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\sim}}\bm{B}
and
\bm{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\sim}}\bm{C}
, then
\bm{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\sim}}\bm{C}
.

•

Show that if
\bm{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\sim}}\bm{B}
, then
\bm{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\sim}}\bm{A}
.

•

Show that
\bm{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\sim}}\bm{B}
if both
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
are nonsingular.

Given two matrices
\bm{E}
and
\bm{F}
obtained from the identity matrix by adding multiples of row
i
to rows
j
and
k
with
i\neq j
and
i\neq k
respectively, show that
\bm{E}\bm{F}=\bm{F}\bm{E}
.

does not exist.

\bm{L}_{1}
and

Prove that
