.

1.3.

We know that elementary row operations on a matrix can be represented by left-multiplying the matrix by a corresponding transformation matrix (Definition
).
Describe the transformation matrices for:

•

Interchanging two rows,

•

Multiplying all elements of a row by a scalar,

•

Adding a scalar multiple of one row to another row.

Extend this discussion to the three elementary column transformations.

1.3.

Consider the three types of elementary row transformation defined in Definition
.
Show the following effects on the determinant:

•

Type-1 (row interchange): Multiplies the determinant by
-1
.

•

Type-2 (row scaling): Multiplies the determinant by the scaling factor.

•

Type-3 (row replacement): Leaves the determinant unchanged.

1.3.

Complexity of vector inner product.

Given two vectors
\bm{v},\bm{w}\in\real^{n}
, the inner product of the two vectors
\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{w}
is calculated as
\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{w}=v_{1}w_{1}+v_{2}w_{2}+\ldots v_{n}w_{n}
. Show that the computational complexity of evaluating the inner product is
2n-1
floating-point operations (flops).

1.3.

Complexity of matrix multiplication.
Given two matrices
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
and
\bm{B}\in\real^{n\times k}
, show that the computational complexity of their product,
\bm{A}\bm{B}
, is
mk(2n-1)
flops.

1.3.

Discuss the algorithms used to compute the LU decomposition of a matrix. Use the results from Problems
and
to determine the computational complexity of the decomposition.

Chapter 2
Cholesky Decomposition

2.1
Cholesky Decomposition

The property of positive definiteness or positive semidefiniteness is a significant characteristic of matrices. It not only provides insights into a matrix’s fundamental nature but also underpins various mathematical and computational applications.
In this section, we introduce decomposition methods for two special types of matrices, examining their unique properties and applications. We begin with the widely recognized Cholesky decomposition, a powerful technique for revealing the positive definiteness of a matrix by factoring it into the product of a lower triangular matrix and its transpose. This decomposition facilitates numerical computations and is indispensable in optimization, statistical modeling, and other fields where ensuring positive definiteness is essential.

Every
positive definite
(PD) matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R},

where
\bm{R}\in\real^{n\times n}
is an upper triangular matrix
with positive diagonal elements
. This factorization is known as the
Cholesky decomposition
of
\bm{A}
, and
\bm{R}
is referred to as the
Cholesky factor
or
Cholesky triangle
of
\bm{A}
.

Alternatively,
\bm{A}
can be expressed as
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{L}^{\top}
, where
\bm{L}=\bm{R}^{\top}
is a lower triangular matrix
with positive diagonals
.

Specifically, the Cholesky decomposition is unique (Corollary
).

The Cholesky decomposition derives its name from the French military officer and mathematician,
André-Louis Cholesky
(1875-1918), credited with its development during his surveying work.
Similar to the LU decomposition, the Cholesky decomposition is primarily used to solve linear systems involving positive definite matrices. The approach for solving such systems parallels that of the LU decomposition, as discussed in Section
, and will not be repeated here.

2.2
Existence of Cholesky Decomposition via Recursive Calculation

In this section, we demonstrate the existence of the Cholesky decomposition using recursive calculations. In Section
, we will present an alternative proof of its existence via QR decomposition and spectral decomposition.
Before establishing the existence of the Cholesky decomposition, we introduce the following definitions and lemmas.

Definition 35
(Positive Definite and Positive Semidefinite).

A matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is positive definite (PD) if
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}>0
for all nonzero
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
, denoted as
\bm{A}\succ\mathbf{0}
.
And a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is positive semidefinite (PSD) if
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}\geq 0
for all
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
, denoted as
\bm{A}\succeq\mathbf{0}
.

One prerequisite for the Cholesky decomposition is the concept of positive definiteness. Several properties of positive definite matrices are outlined below:

Positive Definite Matrix Property 1 of 5

A matrix
\bm{A}
is positive definite if and only if all its eigenvalues are positive. Similarly,
\bm{A}
is positive semidefinite if and only if all its eigenvalues are nonnegative.
A detailed proof of this equivalence is presented in Section
using the spectral theorem.

While not all components of a positive definite matrix need to be positive, it is assured that the diagonal components of such a matrix are positive, as asserted by the following result.

Positive Definite Matrix Property 2 of 5

Lemma 36
(Positive Diagonals of Positive Definite Matrices).

The diagonal elements of a positive definite matrix
\bm{A}
are all positive. Likewise, the diagonal elements of a positive semidefinite matrix
\bm{B}
are all nonnegative.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
From the definition of positive definite matrices,
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}>0
for all nonzero
\bm{x}
. In particular, let
\bm{x}=\bm{e}_{i}
, where
\bm{e}_{i}
is the
i
-th unit basis vector with 1 at the
i
-th position and 0 elsewhere. Then:

\bm{e}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{e}_{i}=a_{ii}>0,\qquad\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\},

where
a_{ii}
represents the
i
-th diagonal component. A similar argument applies to PSD matrices, where
a_{ii}\geq 0
. Thus, the proof is complete.

Like the LU decomposition, the existence of the Cholesky decomposition also depends on the Schur complement.

Positive Definite Matrix Property 3 of 5

Lemma 37
(Schur Complement of Positive Definite Matrices).

For a positive definite matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, the Schur complement of
a_{11}
is given by
\bm{S}_{n-1}=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}
. The Schur complement
\bm{S}_{n-1}
is also positive definite.

A word on the notation.

Note that the subscript
n-1
indicates that
\bm{S}_{n-1}
is of size
(n-1)\times(n-1)
, derived from an
n\times n
positive definite matrix. This notation will be used throughout subsequent sections.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Let
\bm{v}\in\real^{n-1}
be any nonzero vector. Construct
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
as follows:

\bm{x}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\bm{v}\\
\bm{v}\end{bmatrix},

which is nonzero. Now compute:

\displaystyle\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\left[-\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\qquad\bm{v}^{\top}\right]\begin{bmatrix}a_{11}&\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\\
\bm{A}_{2:n,1}&\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\bm{v}\\
\bm{v}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left[-\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\qquad\bm{v}^{\top}\right]\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\bm{S}_{n-1}\bm{v}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{S}_{n-1}\bm{v}.

Since
\bm{A}
is positive definite,
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{S}_{n-1}\bm{v}>0
for all nonzero
\bm{v}
. Thus,
\bm{S}_{n-1}
is positive definite as well.

This argument extends to PSD matrices as well: if
\bm{A}
is PSD, then the Schur complement
\bm{S}_{n-1}
is also PSD.

A word on the Schur complement.

In the proof of Theorem
, we showed that the Schur complement
\bm{S}_{n-1}=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}
is nonsingular if
\bm{A}
is nonsngular and
a_{11}\neq 0
. Similarly, the Schur complement of
a_{nn}
in
\bm{A}
is
\widehat{\bm{S}}_{n-1}=\bm{A}_{1:n-1,1:n-1}-\frac{1}{a_{nn}}\bm{A}_{1:n-1,n}\bm{A}_{1:n-1,n}^{\top}
, which is also positive definite if
\bm{A}
is positive definite.This property is critical in proving that the leading principal minors of a PD matrix are all positive; further details are available in Section
.

We now illustrate the existence of Cholesky decomposition using these lemmas.

Proof
[of Theorem
: Existence of Cholesky Decomposition Recursively]
For any positive definite matrix
\bm{A}
, note that
a_{11}>0
by Lemma
. We can express
\bm{A}
as:

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}a_{11}&\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\\
\bm{A}_{2:n,1}&\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{a_{11}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{a_{11}}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{a_{11}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{a_{11}}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{a_{11}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{a_{11}}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{a_{11}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{a_{11}}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{S}_{n-1}\end{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{1},

where

\bm{R}_{1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{a_{11}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{a_{11}}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}.

By Lemma
,
\bm{S}_{n-1}
is positive definite. Thus, we can factor it similarly:

\bm{S}_{n-1}=\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}^{\top}\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{S}_{n-2}\end{bmatrix}\normalsize\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}
,
where
\bm{S}_{n-2}
is also positive definite.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{S}_{n-2}\end{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}.\end{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{1}=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{S}_{n-2}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{1}=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\bm{R}_{2}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{S}_{n-2}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{2}\bm{R}_{1}.

Repeating this process recursively, we express
\bm{A}
as:

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\bm{R}_{2}^{\top}\ldots\bm{R}_{n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{n}\ldots\bm{R}_{2}\bm{R}_{1}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R},

where
\bm{R}_{1},\bm{R}_{2},\ldots,\bm{R}_{n}
are upper triangular matrices with positive diagonal elements, and
\bm{R}=\bm{R}_{1}\bm{R}_{2}\ldots\bm{R}_{n}
is also an upper triangular matrix with positive diagonal elements, from which the result follows.

The above process can also be used to compute the Cholesky decomposition and analyze the algorithm’s computational complexity.

Lemma 38
(
\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
is PD).

Given any upper triangular matrix
\bm{R}
with positive diagonal elements, the matrix

\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}

is positive definite.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Since
\bm{R}
has positive diagonals, it has full column rank, and its null space is of dimension 0 by the fundamental theorem of linear algebra (Theorem
).
Consequently,
\bm{R}\bm{x}\neq\mathbf{0}
for any nonzero vector
\bm{x}
. Therefore,
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\left\lVert\bm{R}\bm{x}\right\rVert^{2}>0
for any nonzero vector
\bm{x}
.

This lemma extends to any
\bm{R}
with linearly independent columns.

A word on the two claims.

Combining Theorem
and Lemma
, we conclude that a matrix
\bm{A}
is positive definite if and only if
\bm{A}
can be factored as
\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{R}
is an upper triangular matrix with positive diagonals.

2.3
Sylvester’s Criterion: Leading Principal Minors of PD Matrices

In Lemma
, we proved that for any positive definite matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, the Schur complement of
a_{11}
is given by
\bm{S}_{n-1}=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{a_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}
, which is also positive definite.
Similarly, the Schur complement of
a_{nn}
,
\bm{S}_{n-1}^{\prime}=\bm{A}_{1:n-1,1:n-1}-\frac{1}{a_{nn}}\bm{A}_{1:n-1,n}\bm{A}_{1:n-1,n}^{\top}
, is also positive definite.

We now claim that all leading principal minors (Definition
) of a positive definite matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
are positive, a result known as
Sylvester’s criterion

(
Swamy 1973
;
Gilbert 1991
)
. Recall that these positive leading principal minors imply the existence of the LU decomposition for any positive definite matrix, as established in Theorem
.

To prove Sylvester’s criterion, we first establish the following lemma:

Positive Definite Matrix Property 4 of 5

Lemma 39
(Quadratic PD).

Let
\bm{E}
be any invertible matrix. Then
\bm{A}
is positive definite if and only if
\bm{E}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{E}
is also positive definite.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
If
\bm{A}
is positive definite, then for any nonzero vector
\bm{x}
,
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{E}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{E}\bm{x}=\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{y}>0
, since
\bm{E}
is invertible such that
\bm{E}\bm{x}
is nonzero. Thus,
\bm{E}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{E}
is PD.

Conversely, if
\bm{E}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{E}
is positive definite, for any nonzero
\bm{x}
,
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{E}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{E}\bm{x}>0
. For any nonzero
\bm{y}
, there exists a nonzero
\bm{x}
such that
\bm{y}=\bm{E}\bm{x}
, since
\bm{E}
is invertible. Hence,
\bm{A}
is also PD.

We then provide a rigorous proof for Sylvester’s criterion.

Positive Definite Matrix Property 5 of 5

Theorem 40
(Sylvester’s Criterion).

A real symmetric matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is positive definite if and only if all of its leading principal minors are positive.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
We prove the forward implication by induction. Base case (
n=1
): Since all the components on the diagonal of positive definite matrices are positive (Lemma
), for a scalar matrix
\bm{A}
,
\det(\bm{A})>0
if
\bm{A}
is positive definite.

Assume all leading principal minors of any
k\times k
positive definite matrix are positive. For a
(k+1)\times(k+1)
positive definite matrix
\bm{M}
, expressed in block form as
\bm{M}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}&\bm{b}\\
\bm{b}^{\top}&d\end{bmatrix}
, where
\bm{A}
is a
k\times k
positive definite submatrix. Its Schur complement of
d
,
\bm{S}_{k}=\bm{A}-\frac{1}{d}\bm{b}\bm{b}^{\top}
, is also positive definite, and its determinant is positive by the inductive hypothesis. Therefore,
\det(\bm{M})=\det(d)\det(\bm{A}-\frac{1}{d}\bm{b}\bm{b}^{\top})
=

d\cdot\det(\bm{A}-\frac{1}{d}\bm{b}\bm{b}^{\top})>0
, establishing the result for
(k+1)\times(k+1)
matrices.

Conversely, if all the leading principal minors of
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
are positive, then all leading principal submatrices are nonsingular. Denote the
(i,j)
-th entry of
\bm{A}
as
a_{ij}
. By assumption,
a_{11}>0
.
To simplify
\bm{A}
, subtract appropriate multiples of its first row from the rows below to zero out the entries in the first column beneath the diagonal element
a_{11}
. This operation can be expressed as:

\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}a_{11}&a_{12}&\ldots&a_{1n}\\
a_{21}&a_{22}&\ldots&a_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{n1}&a_{n2}&\ldots&a_{nn}\\
\end{bmatrix}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\begin{bmatrix}a_{11}&a_{12}&\ldots&a_{1n}\\
0&a_{22}&\ldots&a_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&a_{n2}&\ldots&a_{nn}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Next, subtract appropriate multiples of the first column of
\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}
from the other columns to zero out the entries in the first row to the right of the diagonal element
a_{11}
. Due to the symmetry of
\bm{A}
, this operation can also be performed by multiplying
\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}
on the right by
\bm{E}_{1}^{\top}
. The result is:

\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}a_{11}&a_{12}&\ldots&a_{1n}\\
a_{21}&a_{22}&\ldots&a_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{n1}&a_{n2}&\ldots&a_{nn}\\
\end{bmatrix}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\begin{bmatrix}a_{11}&a_{12}&\ldots&a_{1n}\\
0&a_{22}&\ldots&a_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&a_{n2}&\ldots&a_{nn}\\
\end{bmatrix}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}\bm{E}_{1}^{\top}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\begin{bmatrix}a_{11}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&a_{22}&\ldots&a_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&a_{n2}&\ldots&a_{nn}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

This operation preserves the principal minors of
\bm{A}
. Consequently, the leading principal minors of
\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}\bm{E}_{1}^{\top}
are identical to those of
\bm{A}
.

By repeating this process iteratively, we transform
\bm{A}
into a diagonal matrix of the form
\bm{E}_{n}\ldots\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}\bm{E}_{1}^{\top}\ldots\bm{E}_{n}^{\top}
, where the diagonal entries match the diagonal entries of
\bm{A}
and are positive.
Let
\bm{E}=\bm{E}_{n}\ldots\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}
, which is an invertible matrix. Clearly,
\bm{E}\bm{A}\bm{E}^{\top}
is PD, which implies that
\bm{A}
is also PD, as per Lemma
.

2.4
Existence of Cholesky Decomposition via LU without Permutation

By Theorem
on Sylvester’s criterion and Theorem
regarding the existence of LU decomposition without permutation, a unique LU decomposition exists for a positive definite matrix
\bm{A}
of the form
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U}_{0}
, where
\bm{L}
is a unit lower triangular matrix and
\bm{U}_{0}
is an upper triangular matrix.
It is also established that
the signs of the pivots of a symmetric matrix are the same as the signs of the eigenvalues

(
Strang 2009
)
:

number of positive pivots = number of positive eigenvalues.

The decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U}_{0}
can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U}_{0}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&\ldots&0\\
l_{21}&1&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
l_{n1}&l_{n2}&\ldots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}u_{11}&u_{12}&\ldots&u_{1n}\\
0&u_{22}&\ldots&u_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&u_{nn}\end{bmatrix}.

Here, the diagonal entries of
\bm{U}_{0}
correspond to the pivots of
\bm{A}
. Moreover, as all eigenvalues of PD matrices are positive (by Lemma
, a consequence of spectral decomposition), it follows that the diagonal entries of
\bm{U}_{0}
are also positive.

Let us now arrange the diagonal entries of
\bm{U}_{0}
into a diagonal matrix
\bm{D}
such that
\bm{U}_{0}=\bm{D}\bm{U}
. Substituting this expression, we have:

\displaystyle\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{U}_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&\ldots&0\\
l_{21}&1&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
l_{n1}&l_{n2}&\ldots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}u_{11}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&u_{22}&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&u_{nn}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&u_{12}/u_{11}&\ldots&u_{1n}/u_{11}\\
0&1&\ldots&u_{2n}/u_{22}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&1\end{bmatrix}=\bm{L}\bm{D}\bm{U}.

This simplifies to
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{D}\bm{U}
, where
\bm{U}
is a
unit
upper triangular matrix.
By the uniqueness of the LU decomposition without permutation in Corollary
and the symmetry of
\bm{A}
, we conclude that
\bm{U}=\bm{L}^{\top}
, and hence
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{D}\bm{L}^{\top}
.
Since the diagonal entries of
\bm{D}
are positive, we can define
\bm{R}=\bm{D}^{1/2}\bm{L}^{\top}
, where
\bm{D}^{1/2}=\mathrm{diag}(\sqrt{u_{11}},\sqrt{u_{22}},\ldots,\sqrt{u_{nn}})
.
Thus,
\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
, which represents the Cholesky decomposition of
\bm{A}
. The matrix
\bm{R}
is upper triangular with positive diagonal entries.

2.4.1
Diagonal Values of the Upper Triangular Matrix

Assume that
\bm{A}
is a positive definite matrix. We represent
\bm{A}
as a block matrix
\bm{A}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{k}&\bm{A}_{12}\\
\bm{A}_{21}&\bm{A}_{22}\end{bmatrix}
, where
\bm{A}_{k}\in\real^{k\times k}
. Its block LU decomposition is given by

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{k}&\bm{A}_{12}\\
\bm{A}_{21}&\bm{A}_{22}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{L}\bm{U}_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{k}&\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{L}_{21}&\bm{L}_{22}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{U}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{U}_{22}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{L}_{k}\bm{U}_{12}\\
\bm{L}_{21}\bm{U}_{k11}&\bm{L}_{21}\bm{U}_{12}+\bm{L}_{22}\bm{U}_{22}\end{bmatrix}.

The
k
-th order leading principal minor of
\bm{A}
is defined as
\Delta_{k}=\det(\bm{A}_{1:k,1:k})=\det(\bm{A}_{k})
(Definition
). From the block LU decomposition, we have:

\Delta_{k}=\det(\bm{A}_{k})=\det(\bm{L}_{k}\bm{U}_{k})=\det(\bm{L}_{k})\det(\bm{U}_{k}).

Since
\bm{L}_{k}
is a unit lower triangular matrix, its determinant is 1. By the fact that the determinant of a lower triangular matrix (or an upper triangular matrix) is equal to the product of the diagonal entries, we obtain:

\Delta_{k}=\det(\bm{U}_{k})=u_{11}u_{22}\ldots u_{kk},

i.e., the
k
-th order leading principal minor of
\bm{A}
is equal to the determinant of the
k\times k
submatrix of
\bm{U}_{0}
, which is also the product of the first
k
diagonal entries of
\bm{D}
(from the decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{L}\bm{D}\bm{L}^{\top}
). Let
\bm{D}=\mathrm{diag}(d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{n})
; we then have:

\Delta_{k}=d_{1}d_{2}\ldots d_{k}=\Delta_{k-1}d_{k}.

The entries of
\bm{D}
can also be expressed in terms of the leading principal minors of
\bm{A}
as:

\bm{D}=\mathrm{diag}\left(\Delta_{1},\frac{\Delta_{2}}{\Delta_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\Delta_{n}}{\Delta_{n-1}}\right),

where
\Delta_{k}
is the
k
-th order leading principal minor of
\bm{A}
, for all
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. Consequently, the diagonal entries of
\bm{R}
(from the Cholesky decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
) are:

\mathrm{diag}\left(\sqrt{\Delta_{1}},\sqrt{\frac{\Delta_{2}}{\Delta_{1}}},\ldots,\sqrt{\frac{\Delta_{n}}{\Delta_{n-1}}}\right).

2.4.2
Block Cholesky Decomposition

Building on the above, let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
be a PD matrix represented as:
\bm{A}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{k}&\bm{A}_{12}\\
\bm{A}_{21}&\bm{A}_{22}\end{bmatrix}
, where
\bm{A}_{k}\in\real^{k\times k}
. Its block LU decomposition is given by:

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{k}&\bm{A}_{12}\\
\bm{A}_{21}&\bm{A}_{22}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{L}\bm{U}_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{k}&\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{L}_{21}&\bm{L}_{22}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{U}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{U}_{22}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{L}_{k}\bm{U}_{12}\\
\bm{L}_{21}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{L}_{21}\bm{U}_{12}+\bm{L}_{22}\bm{U}_{22}\end{bmatrix}.

The
k
-th order leading principal submatrix
\bm{A}_{k}
of
\bm{A}
also admits its own LU decomposition:
\bm{A}_{k}=\bm{L}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}
. This implies that the Cholesky decomposition of an
n\times n
matrix
\bm{A}
contains
n-1
smaller Cholesky decompositions for its leading principal submatrices:
\bm{A}_{k}=\bm{R}_{k}^{\top}\bm{R}_{k}
, for all
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
. This is particularly true because any leading principal submatrix
\bm{A}_{k}
of a positive definite matrix
\bm{A}
is also positive definite. To see this, for a PD matrix
\bm{A}_{k+1}\in\real^{(k+1)\times(k+1)}
, consider a vector
\bm{x}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
extended by a zero element,
\bm{x}_{k+1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{x}_{k}\\
0\end{bmatrix}
.
Then,

\bm{x}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}=\bm{x}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k+1}\bm{x}_{k+1}>0.

Thus,
\bm{A}_{k}
is positive definite.
By recursively applying this argument starting from
\bm{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, we demonstrate that
\bm{A}_{n-1},\bm{A}_{n-2},\ldots,\bm{A}_{1}
are all positive definite. Consequently, each of these matrices admits a Cholesky decomposition.

2.5
Existence of Cholesky Decomposition via Induction

In the previous section, we demonstrated the existence of the Cholesky decomposition through the LU decomposition without permutation. Building on the proof of the LU decomposition in Section
, we observe that the existence of the Cholesky decomposition can also be established directly using induction.

Proof
[of Theorem
: Existence of Cholesky Decomposition by Induction]
We will use induction to demonstrate that every
n\times n
positive definite matrix
\bm{A}
can be decomposed as
\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
.
The based case for
1\times 1
matrices is straightforward; setting
R=\sqrt{A}
renders
A=R^{2}
.

Assume that for any
k\times k
positive definite matrix
\bm{A}_{k}
, a Cholesky decomposition exists. We need to show that any
(k+1)\times(k+1)
PD matrix
\bm{A}_{k+1}
can also be factored as this Cholesky decomposition. Write
\bm{A}_{k+1}
as a block matrix

\bm{A}_{k+1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{k}&\bm{b}\\
\bm{b}^{\top}&d\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{A}_{k}
is a
k\times k
PD matrix. By the inductive hypothesis,
\bm{A}_{k}
admits a Cholesky decomposition:
\bm{A}_{k}=\bm{R}_{k}^{\top}\bm{R}_{k}
.
Construct the upper triangular matrix

\bm{R}_{k+1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{k}&\bm{r}\\
0&s\end{bmatrix}.

Then,

\bm{R}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{R}_{k+1}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{k}^{\top}\bm{R}_{k}&\bm{R}_{k}^{\top}\bm{r}\\
\bm{r}^{\top}\bm{R}_{k}&\bm{r}^{\top}\bm{r}+s^{2}\end{bmatrix}.

Therefore, if we can prove
\bm{R}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{R}_{k+1}=\bm{A}_{k+1}
is the Cholesky decomposition of
\bm{A}_{k+1}
(which requires the value
s
to be positive), then we complete the proof. That is, we need to prove

\displaystyle\bm{b}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{k}^{\top}\bm{r}\qquad\text{and}\qquad d=\bm{r}^{\top}\bm{r}+s^{2}.

Since
\bm{R}_{k}
is nonsingular, we have a unique solution for
\bm{r}
and
s
that

\displaystyle\bm{r}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{k}^{-\top}\bm{b}\qquad\text{and}\qquad s=\sqrt{d-\bm{r}^{\top}\bm{r}}=\sqrt{d-\bm{b}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}^{-1}\bm{b}},

where we assume
s
is nonnegative. To ensure
s>0
, note that since
\bm{A}_{k}
is PD, from Sylvester’s criterion and the fact that if matrix
\bm{M}
has a block formulation:
\bm{M}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}&\bm{B}\\
\bm{C}&\bm{D}\end{bmatrix}
, then
\det(\bm{M})=\det(\bm{A})\det(\bm{D}-\bm{C}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{B})
, we have

\det(\bm{A}_{k+1})=\det(\bm{A}_{k})\det(d-\bm{b}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}^{-1}\bm{b})=\det(\bm{A}_{k})(d-\bm{b}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}^{-1}\bm{b})>0.

Since
\det(\bm{A}_{k})>0
, it follows that
(d-\bm{b}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}^{-1}\bm{b})>0
. Thus,
s>0
, and this completes the proof.

2.6
Uniqueness of the Cholesky Decomposition

This uniqueness of the Cholesky decomposition follows directly from the uniqueness of the LU decomposition without permutation. Alternatively, a detailed proof of this uniqueness is presented below.

Corollary 41
(Uniqueness of Cholesky Decomposition).

The Cholesky decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
for any positive definite matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is unique.

Proof
[of Corollary
]
Suppose the Cholesky decomposition is not unique. Then, there exist two distinct decompositions such that
\bm{A}=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\bm{R}_{1}=\bm{R}_{2}^{\top}\bm{R}_{2}
. This implies

\bm{R}_{1}\bm{R}_{2}^{-1}=\bm{R}_{1}^{-\top}\bm{R}_{2}^{\top}.

From the fact that the inverse of an upper triangular matrix is also an upper triangular matrix, and the product of two upper triangular matrices is also an upper triangular matrix, we realize that the left-hand side of the above equation is an upper triangular matrix, while the right-hand side is a lower triangular matrix. The only way both sides can be equal is if
\bm{R}_{1}\bm{R}_{2}^{-1}=\bm{R}_{1}^{-\top}\bm{R}_{2}^{\top}
is a diagonal matrix, and
\bm{R}_{1}^{-\top}\bm{R}_{2}^{\top}=(\bm{R}_{1}^{-\top}\bm{R}_{2}^{\top})^{\top}=\bm{R}_{2}\bm{R}_{1}^{-1}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\bm{R}_{1}\bm{R}_{2}^{-1}=\bm{R}_{2}\bm{R}_{1}^{-1}
be the diagonal matrix. We notice that each diagonal value of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is the product of the corresponding diagonal values of
\bm{R}_{1}
and
\bm{R}_{2}^{-1}
(or
\bm{R}_{2}
and
\bm{R}_{1}^{-1}
). Suppose

\bm{R}_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}r_{11}&r_{12}&\ldots&r_{1n}\\
0&r_{22}&\ldots&r_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&r_{nn}\end{bmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{R}_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}s_{11}&s_{12}&\ldots&s_{1n}\\
0&s_{22}&\ldots&s_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&s_{nn}\end{bmatrix}.

We have

\displaystyle\bm{R}_{1}\bm{R}_{2}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{r_{11}}{s_{11}}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&\frac{r_{22}}{s_{22}}&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&\frac{r_{nn}}{s_{nn}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{s_{11}}{r_{11}}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&\frac{s_{22}}{r_{22}}&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&\frac{s_{nn}}{r_{nn}}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{R}_{2}\bm{R}_{1}^{-1}.

Given that both
\bm{R}_{1}
and
\bm{R}_{2}
have positive diagonals, it follows that
r_{11}=s_{11},r_{22}=s_{22},\ldots,r_{nn}=s_{nn}
. And
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\bm{R}_{1}\bm{R}_{2}^{-1}=\bm{R}_{2}\bm{R}_{1}^{-1}=\bm{I}
.
In other words,
\bm{R}_{1}=\bm{R}_{2}
, which contradicts the assumption that the decomposition is not unique.

Building upon the previous discussion, if the diagonal elements of
\bm{R}_{1}
and
\bm{R}_{2}
are not restricted to positive values, the diagonal elements satisfy
r_{ii}=\pm s_{ii}
for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. In such cases, the factorization
\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
is
not unique
.

2.7
Last Words on Positive Definite Matrices

In Section
, we will prove that a matrix
\bm{A}
is PD if and only if
\bm{A}
can be factored as
\bm{A}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{P}
, where
\bm{P}
is nonsingular. Additionally, in Section
, we will demonstrate that a PD matrix
\bm{A}
can be uniquely factored as
\bm{A}=\bm{B}^{2}
, where
\bm{B}
is also PD.
Both results stem from the spectral decomposition of PD matrices.
To summarize, for a PD matrix
\bm{A}
, we can factor it into

•

\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{R}
is an upper triangular matrix with positive diagonals, as established in Theorem
by Cholesky decomposition;

•

\bm{A}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{P}
, where
\bm{P}
is nonsingular in Theorem
;

•

and
\bm{A}=\bm{B}^{2}
, where
\bm{B}
is PD in Theorem
.

For a comprehensive overview, these factorizations of a positive definite matrix
\bm{A}
are summarized in Figure
.

.

2.8
Decomposition for Semidefinite Matrices

For positive semidefinite matrices, the Cholesky decomposition also exists with slight modifications.

Every positive semidefinite matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
can be factored as

\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R},

where
\bm{R}\in\real^{n\times n}
is an upper triangular matrix. The diagonal elements of
\bm{R}
may be zero, and it’s important to note that the factorization is
not unique
in general.

In such a decomposition, the diagonal elements of
\bm{R}
may not accurately indicate the rank of
\bm{A}
.
(
Higham 2009
)
.

Example 42
(
(
Higham 2009
)
).

Consider the matrix

\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}1&-1&1\\
-1&1&-1\\
1&-1&2\end{bmatrix}.

The semidefinite decomposition is given by

\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
-1&0&0\\
1&1&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1&1\\
0&0&1\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}.

The matrix
\bm{A}
has a rank of 2, while
\bm{R}
only has one nonzero diagonal element.

It is worth noting that all PD matrices inherently have full rank, a property that plays a crucial role in many of the proofs discussed earlier. Sylvester’s criterion (Theorem
) guarantees this by stating that all leading principal minors of PD matrices are positive.
Alternatively, one can argue that if a PD matrix
\bm{A}
were rank-deficient, the null space of
\bm{A}
would have a positive dimension. This would imply the existence of a vector
\bm{x}
in the null space such that
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
, contradicting the definition of positive definiteness.

However, this property does not hold for PSD matrices, where the null space may have a dimension greater than zero. To address this, a more general rank-revealing decomposition for semidefinite matrices is introduced.

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
be a positive semidefinite matrix with rank
r
. Then, it can be decomposed as

\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R},\qquad\mathrm{with}\qquad\bm{R}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\in\real^{n\times n},

where
\bm{R}_{11}\in\real^{r\times r}
is an upper triangular matrix with positive diagonal elements, and
\bm{R}_{12}\in\real^{r\times(n-r)}
.

The proof of this rank-revealing decomposition for semidefinite matrices will be presented in Section
, relying on the spectral decomposition (Theorem
) and the column-pivoted QR decomposition (Theorem
).
Whereas, the proof for the trivial semidefinite decomposition Theorem
can be derived directly from the spectral decomposition and the standard QR decomposition (Theorem
).

When the matrix
\bm{A}
is symmetric and indefinite, we can employ a
symmetric indefinite decomposition
or
Bunch Kaufman decomposition

(
Bunch and Kaufman 1977
)
.

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
be a symmetric (indefinite) matrix. Then, it can be factored as

\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{L}\bm{B}\bm{L}^{\top},

where
\bm{P}
is a permutation matrix,
\bm{L}
is an unit lower triangular matrix, and
\bm{B}
is a block-diagonal matrix with each diagonal block of
\bm{B}
being either a
1\times 1
or a
2\times 2
matrix.

This type of decomposition is sometimes referred to as an

\bm{L}\bm{B}\bm{L}^{\top}
decomposition.
It is particularly useful in practical applications, such as solving linear systems and computing the eigenvalues of matrices, especially in cases where a direct Cholesky decomposition cannot be applied (e.g., when the matrix is not positive definite)
(
Dumas and Pernet 2018
)
.

2.9
Application: Rank-One Update/Downdate

Updating linear systems after low-rank modifications of the system matrix is a common procedure in fields such as machine learning, statistics, and more. However, it is well-known that these updates can become numerically unstable in the presence of round-off errors
(
Seeger 2004
)
. If the system matrix is positive definite, a more numerically stable approach involves using a representation based on the Cholesky decomposition. In this section, we will provide a proof of the rank-one update/downdate using Cholesky decomposition.

2.9.1
Rank-One Update

A rank-one update
\bm{A}^{\prime}
of a matrix
\bm{A}
by a vector
\bm{v}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}+\bm{v}\bm{v}^{\top};

\displaystyle\bm{R}^{\prime\top}\bm{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}+\bm{v}\bm{v}^{\top}.

If we have already calculated the Cholesky factor
\bm{R}
of
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, then the Cholesky factor
\bm{R}^{\prime}
of
\bm{A}^{\prime}
can be calculated efficiently.
This avoids recalculating the decomposition from scratch, reducing the computational cost from
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
to
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
.
Specifically,
\bm{R}^{\prime}
is obtained via a rank-one Cholesky update, leveraging the fact that
\bm{A}^{\prime}
differs from
\bm{A}
only by a symmetric rank-one matrix.
To derive
\bm{R}^{\prime}
,
consider a set of orthogonal matrices
\bm{Q}_{n}\bm{Q}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{Q}_{1}
such that:

\bm{Q}_{n}\bm{Q}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{Q}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{R}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}.

The Cholesky factor
\bm{R}^{\prime}
can be determined by analyzing the above transformation.
Specifically, the left-hand side of the equation, when multiplied by its transpose, yields:

\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}&\bm{R}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\bm{Q}_{1}\ldots\bm{Q}_{n-1}\bm{Q}_{n}\bm{Q}_{n}\bm{Q}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{Q}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}+\bm{v}\bm{v}^{\top}.

Similarly, the right-hand side, when multiplied by its transpose, results in:

\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\bm{R}^{\prime\top}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{R}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{R}^{\prime\top}\bm{R}^{\prime},

which agrees with the left-hand side equation.
Givens rotations
are such orthogonal matrices that can transfer
\bm{R}
and
\bm{v}
into
\bm{R}^{\prime}
. The fundamental significance of Givens rotations, crucial for proving the existence of the QR decomposition, will be discussed shortly in Section
.

Definition 43
(
n
-th Order Givens Rotation).

An
n
-th order Givens rotation is a matrix
\bm{G}_{kl}
of the following form:

\bm{G}_{kl}=\bm{I}+(c-1)(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\delta}_{l}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{l}^{\top})+s(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{l}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\delta}_{l}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}^{\top}),

where
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}\in\real^{n}
is the
k
-th unit basis.
The subscripts
k
and
l
indicate that the
rotation occurs in the plane defined by the
k
-th and
l
-th dimensions
.
In other words, we have

\bm{G}_{kl}=\begin{bmatrix}1&&&&&&&&&\\
&\ddots&&&&&&&&\\
&&1&&&&&&&\\
&&&c&&&&s&&\\
&&&&1&&&&&\\
&&&&&\ddots&&&&\\
&&&&&&1&&&\\
&&&-s&&&&c&&\\
&&&&&&&&1&\\
&&&&&&&&&\ddots\end{bmatrix}_{n\times n},

where the
(k,k),(k,l),(l,k),(l,l)
entries are
c,s,-s,c
respectively, and
s=\cos\theta
and
c=\cos\theta
for some
\theta
.
Specifically, one can also define the
n
-th order Givens rotation, where
(k,k),(k,l),(l,k),
and
(l,l)
entries are
c,{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}-s,s},
and
c
, respectively. The ideas are the same.

It can be easily verified that the
n
-th order Givens rotation is an orthogonal matrix, and its determinant is 1. For any vector
\bm{x}=[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}]^{\top}\in\real^{n}
,
the effect of
\bm{G}_{kl}
on
\bm{x}
is as follows:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &y_{k}=c\cdot x_{k}+s\cdot x_{l},\\
&y_{l}=-s\cdot x_{k}+c\cdot x_{l},\\
&y_{j}=x_{j},&(j\neq k,l)\end{aligned}\right.

In other words, a Givens rotation applied to
\bm{x}
rotates the components
x_{k}
and
x_{l}
of
\bm{x}
by an angle
\theta
, leaving all other components unchanged.

Now, suppose we have an
(n+1)
-th order Givens rotation indexed from
0
to
n
:

\bm{G}_{k}=\bm{I}+(c_{k}-1)(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{0}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{0}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}^{\top})+s_{k}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{0}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{0}^{\top}),

where
c_{k}=\cos\theta_{k},s_{k}=\sin\theta_{k}
for some
\theta_{k}
,
\bm{G}_{k}\in\real^{(n+1)\times(n+1)}
, and
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}\in\real^{n+1}
is a zero vector except that the
(k+1)
-th entry is 1.

Taking out the
k
-th column of the following transformation

\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}\end{bmatrix}\rightarrow\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{R}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}.

Let the
k
-th element of
\bm{v}
be
v_{k}
, and the
k
-th diagonal of
\bm{R}
be
r_{kk}
.
Since
\sqrt{v_{k}^{2}+r_{kk}^{2}}\neq 0
, we can define
c_{k}=\frac{r_{kk}}{\sqrt{v_{k}^{2}+r_{kk}^{2}}}
,
s_{k}=-\frac{v_{k}}{\sqrt{v_{k}^{2}+r_{kk}^{2}}}
. Then,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{k}\rightarrow c_{k}v_{k}+s_{k}r_{kk}=0;\\
&r_{kk}\rightarrow-s_{k}v_{k}+c_{k}r_{kk}=\sqrt{v_{k}^{2}+r_{kk}^{2}}=r^{\prime}_{kk}.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

In other words,
\bm{G}_{k}
will set the
k
-th element of
\bm{v}
to zero and assign a nonzero value to
r_{kk}
.
This finding above is essential for the rank-one update.
The sequence of Givens rotations
\bm{G}_{n}\bm{G}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{G}_{1}
transforms:

\bm{G}_{n}\bm{G}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{G}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{R}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}.

Each rotation requires
6n
floating-point operations (flops), yielding a total computational cost of
6n^{2}
flops for
n
rotations.
This approach significantly reduces the complexity of calculating the Cholesky factor of
\bm{A}^{\prime}
from
\frac{1}{3}n^{3}
to
6n^{2}
flops, assuming the Cholesky factor of
\bm{A}
is already known
(
Lu 2021c
)
.
The algorithm is particularly useful in reducing the computational complexity of posterior calculations in Bayesian inference for Gaussian mixture models
(
Lu 2021a
)
.
At each stage,
k
new samples are added or removed from an existing cluster, which corresponds to performing
k
rank-one updates.

2.9.2
Rank-One Downdate

Now, suppose the Cholesky factor of
\bm{A}
has been computed, and
\bm{A}^{\prime}
is a downdate of
\bm{A}
, defined as:

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}-\bm{v}\bm{v}^{\top};

\displaystyle\bm{R}^{\prime\top}\bm{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}-\bm{v}\bm{v}^{\top}.

The algorithm for downdating follows a similar procedure:

\bm{G}_{1}\bm{G}_{2}\ldots\bm{G}_{n}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{R}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}.

(2.1)

Again, each transformation,
\bm{G}_{k}=\bm{I}+(c_{k}-1)(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{0}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{0}^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}^{\top})+s_{k}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{0}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{0}^{\top}),
can be constructed as follows.
Taking out the
k
-th column of the following equation

\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{R}\end{bmatrix}\rightarrow\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}.

We realize that
r_{kk}\neq 0
, and let
c_{k}=\frac{\sqrt{r_{kk}^{2}-v_{k}^{2}}}{r_{kk}}
,
s_{k}=\frac{v_{k}}{r_{kk}}
. Then,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &0\rightarrow s_{k}r_{kk}=v_{k};\\
&r_{kk}\rightarrow c_{k}r_{kk}=\sqrt{r_{kk}^{2}-v_{k}^{2}}=r^{\prime}_{kk}.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

To ensure the positive definiteness of
\bm{A}^{\prime}
, it is necessary that
r^{2}_{kk}>v_{k}^{2}
.
If this condition is not met,
c_{k}
as defined above will not exist.
Again, one can check that by multiply the left-hand side of Equation (
) by its transpose, we obtain

\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\bm{R}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\bm{G}_{n}\ldots\bm{G}_{2}\bm{G}_{1}\bm{G}_{1}\bm{G}_{2}\ldots\bm{G}_{n}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{R}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}.

Similarly, multiplying the right-hand side by its transpose gives:

\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}&\bm{R}^{\prime\top}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{v}\bm{v}^{\top}+\bm{R}^{\prime\top}\bm{R}^{\prime}.

This confirms that
\bm{R}^{\prime\top}\bm{R}^{\prime}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}-\bm{v}\bm{v}^{\top}
.

2.10
Application: Indefinite Rank-Two Update

Let
\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
be the Cholesky decomposition of
\bm{A}
,
Goldfarb 1976
;
Seeger 2004
introduced a stable method for performing an indefinite rank-two update in the form

\bm{A}^{\prime}=(\bm{I}+\bm{v}\bm{u}^{\top})\bm{A}(\bm{I}+\bm{u}\bm{v}^{\top}).

Let

\bigg\{\begin{aligned} \bm{z}&=\bm{R}^{-\top}\bm{v},\\
\bm{w}&=\bm{R}\bm{u},\end{aligned}\qquad\implies\qquad\bigg\{\begin{aligned} \bm{v}&=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{z},\\
\bm{u}&=\bm{R}^{-1}\bm{w}.\end{aligned}

Now suppose the LQ decomposition of
\bm{I}+\bm{z}\bm{w}^{\top}
is given by
\bm{I}+\bm{z}\bm{w}^{\top}=\bm{L}\bm{Q}
, where
\bm{L}
is lower triangular and
\bm{Q}
is orthogonal. Then, we can express
\bm{A}^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\bm{I}+\bm{v}\bm{u}^{\top})\bm{A}(\bm{I}+\bm{u}\bm{v}^{\top})=(\bm{I}+\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{z}\bm{w}^{\top}\bm{R}^{-\top})\bm{A}(\bm{I}+\bm{R}^{-1}\bm{w}\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{R})

\displaystyle=\bm{R}^{\top}(\bm{I}+\bm{z}\bm{w}^{\top})(\bm{I}+\bm{w}\bm{z}^{\top})\bm{R}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{L}\bm{Q}\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{L}^{\top}\bm{R}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{L}\bm{L}^{\top}\bm{R}.

Finally, let
\bm{R}^{\prime}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{L}
, which is a lower triangular matrix. This establishes the Cholesky decomposition of
\bm{A}^{\prime}
.

æ

Part II
Triangularization, Orthogonalization, and Gram-Schmidt Process

\nopagecolor

Chapter 3
QR Decomposition

3.1
QR Decomposition

In many applications, the column space of a matrix
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]\in\real^{m\times n}
is of interest. The sequence of subspaces spanned by the columns
\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots
of
\bm{A}
is given by

\mathcal{C}([\bm{a}_{1}])\,\,\,\,\subseteq\,\,\,\,\mathcal{C}([\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2}])\,\,\,\,\subseteq\,\,\,\,\mathcal{C}([\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\bm{a}_{3}])\,\,\,\,\subseteq\,\,\,\,\ldots,

where
\mathcal{C}([\ldots])
denotes the subspace spanned by the vectors enclosed in the brackets.
The principle behind QR decomposition is to construct a sequence of orthonormal vectors
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots
that span the same successive subspaces:

\big\{\mathcal{C}([\bm{q}_{1}])=\mathcal{C}([\bm{a}_{1}])\big\}\subseteq\big\{\mathcal{C}([\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}])=\mathcal{C}([\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2}])\big\}\subseteq\big\{\mathcal{C}([\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\bm{q}_{3}])=\mathcal{C}([\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\bm{a}_{3}])\big\}\subseteq\ldots.

The QR decomposition theorem, stated below, summarizes the result. A detailed discussion of its existence follows in subsequent sections.

Any
m\times n
matrix
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]
(whether it has linearly independent or dependent columns) with
m\geq n
can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R},

where

1.

Reduced
:
\bm{Q}
is an
m\times n
matrix with orthonormal columns, and
\bm{R}
is an
n\times n
upper triangular matrix, known as the
reduced QR decomposition
;

2.

Full
:
\bm{Q}
is an
m\times m
matrix with orthonormal columns, and
\bm{R}
is an
m\times n
upper triangular matrix, known as the
full QR decomposition
. If the upper triangular matrix is further restricted to be square, the full QR decomposition can be expressed as:

\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{0}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{R}_{0}
is an
n\times n
upper triangular matrix.

If
\bm{A}
has full rank, i.e.,
\bm{A}
has linearly independent columns,
\bm{R}
also has linearly independent columns, and
\bm{R}
is nonsingular in the
reduced
case. This implies the diagonals of
\bm{R}
are nonzero. Under the additional condition that the diagonal entries of
\bm{R}
are positive, the reduced QR decomposition is
unique
. However, the full QR decomposition is typically not unique because the rightmost
(m-n)
columns of
\bm{Q}
can be arranged in any order.

3.2
Project a Vector Onto Another Vector

Projecting a vector
\bm{a}
onto another vector
\bm{b}
involves finding the vector closest to
\bm{a}
on the line defined by
\bm{b}
. The projection vector, denoted
\widehat{\bm{a}}
, is a scalar multiple of
\bm{b}
:
\widehat{\bm{a}}=\widehat{x}\bm{b}
. By construction,
\bm{a}-\widehat{\bm{a}}
is perpendicular to
\bm{b}
, as illustrated in Figure
. This relationship yields the following result:

Project Vector
\bm{a}
Onto Vector
\bm{b}

\bm{a}^{\perp}=\bm{a}-\widehat{\bm{a}}
is perpendicular to
\bm{b}
, so
(\bm{a}-\widehat{x}\bm{b})^{\top}\bm{b}=0
:
\widehat{x}
=
\frac{\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{b}}{\bm{b}^{\top}\bm{b}}
and
\widehat{\bm{a}}=\frac{\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{b}}{\bm{b}^{\top}\bm{b}}\bm{b}=\frac{\bm{b}\bm{b}^{\top}}{\bm{b}^{\top}\bm{b}}\bm{a}
.

(a)

Project onto a line.

(b)

Project onto a space.

Figure 3.1:
Project a vector onto a line and a space.

3.3
Project a Vector Onto a Plane

The projection of a vector
\bm{a}
onto a space spanned by
\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{n}
involves finding the vector closest to
\bm{a}
within the column space of
[\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{n}]
. The projection vector, demoted
\widehat{\bm{a}}
, is expressed as a linear combination of
\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{n}
:
\widehat{\bm{a}}=\widehat{x}_{1}\bm{b}_{1}+\widehat{x}_{2}\bm{b}_{2}+\ldots+\widehat{x}_{n}\bm{b}_{n}
. This is a least squares problem, solved by the normal equation:
\bm{B}^{\top}\bm{B}\widehat{\bm{x}}=\bm{B}^{\top}\bm{a}
, where
\bm{B}=[\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{n}]
and
\widehat{\bm{x}}=[\widehat{x}_{1},\widehat{x}_{2},\ldots,\widehat{x}_{n}]
.
The projection onto each vector
\bm{b}_{i}
can be computed individually as:

\widehat{\bm{a}}_{i}=\frac{\bm{b}_{i}\bm{b}_{i}^{\top}}{\bm{b}_{i}^{\top}\bm{b}_{i}}\bm{a},\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}.

The total projection is then the sum of these individual projections:
\widehat{\bm{a}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\bm{a}}_{i}
. Consequently, the residual vector is orthogonal to the column space of
\bm{B}
:

\bm{a}^{\perp}=(\bm{a}-\widehat{\bm{a}})\perp\mathcal{C}(\bm{B}),

as shown in Figure
.

3.4
Existence of the QR Decomposition via the Gram-Schmidt Process

Given three linearly independent vectors
\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},
and
\bm{a}_{3}
spanning the space denoted by
\mathcal{C}{([\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\bm{a}_{3}])}
, which corresponds to the column space of the matrix
[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\bm{a}_{3}]
, we aim to construct three orthogonal vectors
\{\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\bm{b}_{3}\}
such that
\mathcal{C}{([\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\bm{b}_{3}])}
=
\mathcal{C}{([\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\bm{a}_{3}])}
. By normalizing these orthogonal vectors (dividing each by its norm), we obtain three mutually orthonormal vectors:
\bm{q}_{1}=\frac{\bm{b}_{1}}{\left\lVert\bm{b}_{1}\right\rVert}
,
\bm{q}_{2}=\frac{\bm{b}_{2}}{\left\lVert\bm{b}_{2}\right\rVert}
, and
\bm{q}_{2}=\frac{\bm{b}_{2}}{\left\lVert\bm{b}_{2}\right\rVert}
.

The first orthogonal vector is directly chosen as
\bm{b}_{1}=\bm{a}_{1}
. The second vector,
\bm{b}_{2}
, must be perpendicular to the first one. It is obtained by subtracting the projection of
\bm{a}_{2}
onto
\bm{b}_{1}
:

\displaystyle\bm{b}_{2}

\displaystyle=\bm{a}_{2}-\frac{\bm{b}_{1}\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}}{\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}\bm{b}_{1}}\bm{a}_{2}=\left(\bm{I}-\frac{\bm{b}_{1}\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}}{\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}\bm{b}_{1}}\right)\bm{a}_{2}\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{Projection view})

\displaystyle=\bm{a}_{2}-\underbrace{\frac{\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}\bm{a}_{2}}{\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}\bm{b}_{1}}\bm{b}_{1}}_{\widehat{\bm{a}}_{2}}.\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{Combination view})

The first equation shows
\bm{b}_{2}
is computed by applying the matrix
\left(\bm{I}-\frac{\bm{b}_{1}\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}}{\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}\bm{b}_{1}}\right)
to
\bm{a}_{2}
, which projects
\bm{a}_{2}
onto the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{C}{([\bm{b}_{1}])}
.
The second equality expresses
\bm{a}_{2}
as a linear combination of its projection onto
\bm{b}_{1}
and a component orthogonal to
\bm{b}_{1}
:
\bm{b}_{2}\perp\bm{b}_{1}
.
This ensures that
\mathcal{C}([\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2}])=\mathcal{C}([\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2}])
. Figure
illustrates the process, where
the direction of
\bm{b}_{1}
is chosen as the
x
-axis in the Cartesian coordinate system
.
\widehat{\bm{a}}_{2}
is the projection of
\bm{a}_{2}
onto line
\bm{b}_{1}
. From the figure, it is clear that
\bm{b}_{2}=\bm{a}_{2}-\widehat{\bm{a}}_{2}
is the component of
\bm{a}_{2}
orthogonal to
\bm{b}_{1}
.

The third vector,
\bm{b}_{3}
, must be orthogonal to both
\bm{b}_{1}
and
\bm{b}_{2}
. It is computed by subtracting the projections of
\bm{a}_{3}
onto the planes spanned by
\bm{b}_{1}
and
\bm{b}_{2}
:

\displaystyle\bm{b}_{3}

\displaystyle=\bm{a}_{3}-\frac{\bm{b}_{1}\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}}{\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}\bm{b}_{1}}\bm{a}_{3}-\frac{\bm{b}_{2}\bm{b}_{2}^{\top}}{\bm{b}_{2}^{\top}\bm{b}_{2}}\bm{a}_{3}=\left(\bm{I}-\frac{\bm{b}_{1}\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}}{\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}\bm{b}_{1}}-\frac{\bm{b}_{2}\bm{b}_{2}^{\top}}{\bm{b}_{2}^{\top}\bm{b}_{2}}\right)\bm{a}_{3}\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{Projection view})

(3.1)

\displaystyle=\bm{a}_{3}-\underbrace{\frac{\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}\bm{a}_{3}}{\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}\bm{b}_{1}}\bm{b}_{1}}_{\widehat{\bm{a}}_{3}}-\underbrace{\frac{\bm{b}_{2}^{\top}\bm{a}_{3}}{\bm{b}_{2}^{\top}\bm{b}_{2}}\bm{b}_{2}}_{\bar{\bm{a}}_{3}}.\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{Combination view})

The first equation shows that
\bm{b}_{3}
is a multiplication of the matrix
\left(\bm{I}-\frac{\bm{b}_{1}\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}}{\bm{b}_{1}^{\top}\bm{b}_{1}}-\frac{\bm{b}_{2}\bm{b}_{2}^{\top}}{\bm{b}_{2}^{\top}\bm{b}_{2}}\right)
and the vector
\bm{a}_{3}
, i.e., projecting
\bm{a}_{3}
onto the orthogonal complement space of
\mathcal{C}{([\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2}])}
. The second equality expresses
\bm{a}_{3}
as a linear combination of
\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},
and
\bm{b}_{3}
. We will see this property is essential in the idea of the QR decomposition.
Again, it can be shown that the space spanned by
\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\bm{b}_{3}
is the same space spanned by
\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\bm{a}_{3}
. Figure
illustrates this step, where
the direction of
\bm{b}_{2}
is chosen as the
y
-axis in the Cartesian coordinate system
. Here,
\widehat{\bm{a}}_{3}
is the projection of
\bm{a}_{3}
onto
\bm{b}_{1}
, while
\bar{\bm{a}}_{3}
is the projection of
\bm{a}_{3}
onto
\bm{b}_{2}
.
The figure shows that the component of
\bm{a}_{3}
orthogonal to both
\bm{b}_{1}
and
\bm{b}_{2}
is
\bm{b}_{3}=\bm{a}_{3}-\widehat{\bm{a}}_{3}-\bar{\bm{a}}_{3}
.

Finally, each vector is normalized to produce the orthonormal vectors:
\bm{q}_{1}=\frac{\bm{b}_{1}}{\left\lVert\bm{b}_{1}\right\rVert}
,
\bm{q}_{2}=\frac{\bm{b}_{2}}{\left\lVert\bm{b}_{2}\right\rVert}
, and
\bm{q}_{2}=\frac{\bm{b}_{2}}{\left\lVert\bm{b}_{2}\right\rVert}
.

(a)

Project
\bm{a}_{2}
onto the space perpendicular to
\bm{b}_{1}
.

(b)

Project
\bm{a}_{3}
onto the space perpendicular to
\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2}
.

Figure 3.2:
The Gram-Schmidt process.

This process, called the
Gram-Schmidt process
, generalizes to any set of linearly independent vectors. The method triangularizes the matrix
\bm{A}
, yielding its QR decomposition. Named after
Jørgen Pedersen Gram
and
Erhard Schmidt
,
it has origins in the work of
Pierre-Simon Laplace
on Lie group decomposition.

As mentioned previously, the goal of the QR decomposition is to construct a sequence of orthonormal vectors
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots
that span the same successive subspaces.

\big\{\mathcal{C}([\bm{q}_{1}])=\mathcal{C}([\bm{a}_{1}])\big\}\subseteq\big\{\mathcal{C}([\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}])=\mathcal{C}([\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2}])\big\}\subseteq\big\{\mathcal{C}([\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\bm{q}_{3}])=\mathcal{C}([\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\bm{a}_{3}])\big\}\subseteq\ldots.

This implies that any vector
\bm{a}_{k}
lies in the space spanned by
\mathcal{C}([\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{k}])
. Once the orthonormal vectors are determined,
\bm{A}

can be reconstructed as
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{Q}
is orthogonal and
\bm{R}
is upper triangular.

The Gram–Schmidt process is not the only method for performing QR decomposition. Other algorithms, such as
Householder reflections
and
Givens rotations
, are also available and are often preferred due to their greater robustness against round-off errors. Additionally, these alternative methods may process the columns of
\bm{A}
in a different order.

3.5
Orthogonal vs Orthonormal

The vectors
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{n}\in\real^{m}
are considered
mutually orthogonal
if their dot products satisfy
\bm{q}_{i}^{\top}\bm{q}_{j}=0
whenever
i\neq j
. By normalizing each vector to have unit length, these vectors become orthogonal unit vectors. At this point,
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{n}
are said to be
mutually orthonormal
. These orthonormal vectors can be arranged as columns in a matrix
\bm{Q}
:

•

When
m\neq n
: the matrix
\bm{Q}
is easy to work with because
\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{Q}=\bm{I}\in\real^{n\times n}
. Such a matrix
\bm{Q}
with
m>n
is sometimes referred to as a
semi-orthogonal
matrix.

•

When
m=n
: the matrix
\bm{Q}
is square, and the condition
\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{Q}=\bm{I}
implies that
\bm{Q}^{\top}=\bm{Q}^{-1}
, meaning the transpose of
\bm{Q}
is its inverse. Then we also have
\bm{Q}\bm{Q}^{\top}=\bm{I}
, i.e.,
\bm{Q}^{\top}
is the
two-sided inverse
of
\bm{Q}
. In this case,
\bm{Q}
is called an
orthogonal matrix
.

Orthogonal matrices can be interpreted as transformations that change the basis of a vector space while preserving angles (inner products) and lengths. Specifically:

•

The length of a vector is also preserved:
\left\lVert\bm{Q}\bm{u}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert.

•

The angle (inner product) between two vectors remains invariant:
\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{v}=(\bm{Q}\bm{u})^{\top}(\bm{Q}\bm{v}).

In real-valued cases, multiplying a vector by an orthogonal matrix
\bm{Q}
results in a
rotation
(if
\det(\bm{Q})=1
) or a
reflection
(if
\det(\bm{Q})=-1
) in the vector space. Many decomposition algorithms produce two orthogonal matrices, resulting in two such transformations (rotations or reflections).

3.6
Computing Reduced QR Decomposition via CGS and MGS

We express the reduced QR decomposition in the form
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{Q}\in\real^{m\times n}
and
\bm{R}\in\real^{n\times n}
, as follows:

\bm{A}=\left[\begin{matrix}\bm{a}_{1}&\bm{a}_{2}&\ldots&\bm{a}_{n}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}\bm{q}_{1}&\bm{q}_{2}&\ldots&\bm{q}_{n}\end{matrix}\right]\footnotesize\begin{bmatrix}r_{11}&r_{12}&\dots&r_{1n}\\
&r_{22}&\dots&r_{2n}\\
&&\ddots&\vdots\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{4.47778pt}[0.0pt]{\Huge 0}\hfil\lx@intercol&&r_{nn}\end{bmatrix}.

The orthogonal matrix
\bm{Q}
can be easily calculated using the Gram-Schmidt process.
To see why we obtain the upper triangular matrix
\bm{R}
, we explicitly write the equations:

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{1}

\displaystyle=r_{11}\bm{q}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{1}r_{i1}\bm{q}_{1},

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{k}

\displaystyle=r_{1k}\bm{q}_{1}+r_{2k}\bm{q}_{2}+\ldots+r_{kk}\bm{q}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{ik}\bm{q}_{k},

\displaystyle\vdots.

This formulation aligns with the second equation in Equation (
) and confirms the upper triangular structure of
\bm{R}
.
Extending the idea of Equation (
) to the
k
-th term, we obtain:

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(\bm{q}_{i}^{\top}\bm{a}_{k})\bm{q}_{i}+\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(\bm{q}_{i}^{\top}\bm{a}_{k})\bm{q}_{i}+\left\lVert\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}\right\rVert\cdot\bm{q}_{k},

which implies we can gradually orthonormalize
\bm{A}
to obtain an orthonormal set
\bm{Q}=[\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{n}]
by

\left\{\begin{aligned} r_{ik}&=\bm{q}_{i}^{\top}\bm{a}_{k},\,\,\,\,\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\};\\
\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}&=\bm{a}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}r_{ik}\bm{q}_{i};\\
r_{kk}&=\left\lVert\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}\right\rVert;\\
\bm{q}_{k}&=\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}/r_{kk}.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.2)

The procedure is detailed in Algorithm
.

Algorithm 4

Reduced QR Decomposition via Gram-Schmidt Process

1:

Matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
with linearly independent columns, where
m\geq n
;

2:

for

k=1
to
n

do

\triangleright
compute the
k
-th column of
\bm{Q},\bm{R}

3:

for

i=1
to
k-1

do

4:

r_{ik}\leftarrow\bm{q}_{i}^{\top}\bm{a}_{k}
;
\triangleright
entry (
i,k
) of
\bm{R}

5:

end

for

6:

\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}\leftarrow\bm{a}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}r_{ik}\bm{q}_{i}
;

7:

r_{kk}\leftarrow\left\lVert\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}\right\rVert
;
\triangleright
main diagonal of
\bm{R}

8:

\bm{q}_{k}\leftarrow\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}/r_{kk}
;

9:

end

for

10:

Output
\bm{Q}=[\bm{q}_{1},\ldots,\bm{q}_{n}]
and
\bm{R}
with entry
(i,k)
being
r_{ik}
;

Orthogonal projection.

From Equation (
), specifically steps 2 to 6 of Algorithm
, we observe that the first two equalities imply that

\left.\begin{aligned} r_{ik}&=\bm{q}_{i}^{\top}\bm{a}_{k},\,\,\,\,\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\}\\
\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}&=\bm{a}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}r_{ik}\bm{q}_{i}\\
\end{aligned}\right\}\rightarrow\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}=\bm{a}_{k}-\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top}\bm{a}_{k}=(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top})\bm{a}_{k},

(3.3)

where
\bm{Q}_{k-1}=[\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{k-1}]
. This implies
\bm{q}_{k}
can be computed as:

\bm{q}_{k}=\frac{\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}}{\left\lVert\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}\right\rVert}=\frac{(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top})\bm{a}_{k}}{\left\lVert(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top})\bm{a}_{k}\right\rVert}.

The matrix
(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top})
in the above equation is known as an
orthogonal projection matrix
(symmetric and idempotent) that projects
\bm{a}_{k}

along
the column space of
\bm{Q}_{k-1}
, ensuring the projected vector is orthogonal to the column space of
\bm{Q}_{k-1}

(
Lu 2021c
)
.
As a result, the
\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}
or
\bm{q}_{k}
calculated in this manner will be orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}(\bm{Q}_{k-1})
, i.e., it lies in the null space of
\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top}
:
\mathcal{N}(\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top})
, according to the fundamental theorem of linear algebra (Theorem
).

Let
\bm{P}_{1}=(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top})
. We assert that
\bm{P}_{1}=(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top})
is an orthogonal projection matrix, such that
\bm{P}_{1}\bm{v}
projects any vector
\bm{v}
onto the null space of
\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top}
.
Additionally, let
\bm{P}_{2}=\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top}
. Then
\bm{P}_{2}
is also an orthogonal projection matrix, such that
\bm{P}_{2}\bm{v}
projects any vector
\bm{v}
onto the column space of
\bm{Q}_{k-1}
.

Why can the matrices
\bm{P}_{1}
and
\bm{P}_{2}
effectively project vectors onto the corresponding subspaces? It can be shown that the column space of
\bm{Q}_{k-1}
is equal to the column space of
\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top}
:

\mathcal{C}(\bm{Q}_{k-1})=\mathcal{C}(\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top})=\mathcal{C}(\bm{P}_{2}).

Hence,
\bm{P}_{2}\bm{v}
represents a linear combination of the columns of
\bm{P}_{2}
, which lies in the column space of
\bm{P}_{2}
or the column space of
\bm{Q}_{k-1}
.

A
projection matrix

\bm{P}
is formally defined as a matrix that is idempotent satisfying
\bm{P}^{2}=\bm{P}
, which means projecting twice is equivalent to projecting once.
What distinguishes
\bm{P}_{2}=\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top}
is that the projection
\widehat{\bm{v}}
of any vector
\bm{v}
is orthogonal to
\bm{v}-\widehat{\bm{v}}
:

(\widehat{\bm{v}}=\bm{P}_{2}\bm{v})\perp(\bm{v}-\widehat{\bm{v}}).

This property aligns with the definition of an
orthogonal projection matrix
.
In contrast, a non-orthogonal case is referred to as an
oblique projection matrix
.
When
\bm{P}_{2}
is an orthogonal projection matrix, the matrix
\bm{P}_{1}=\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{2}
is also an orthogonal projection matrix, projecting any vector onto the space perpendicular to
\mathcal{C}(\bm{Q}_{k-1})
, i.e.,
\mathcal{N}(\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top})
.
Thus, we conclude that there are two complementary orthogonal projections:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \bm{P}_{1}:&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{project onto $\mathcal{N}(\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top})$, along the column space of $\bm{Q}_{k-1}$;}\\
\bm{P}_{2}:&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{project onto $\mathcal{C}(\bm{Q}_{k-1})$, onto the column space of $\bm{Q}_{k-1}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

An additional noteworthy result arises when the columns of
\bm{Q}_{k-1}
are mutually orthonormal.
In this case, we observe the following decomposition:

{\bm{P}_{1}=\bm{I}-\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top}=(\bm{I}-\bm{q}_{1}\bm{q}_{1}^{\top})(\bm{I}-\bm{q}_{2}\bm{q}_{2}^{\top})\ldots(\bm{I}-\bm{q}_{k-1}\bm{q}_{k-1}^{\top}),}

(3.4)

where
\bm{Q}_{k-1}=[\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{k-1}]
, and each term
(\bm{I}-\bm{q}_{i}\bm{q}_{i}^{\top})
serves to project a vector onto the subspace orthogonal to
\bm{q}_{i}
.
This finding is crucial for advancing towards a
modified Gram-Schmidt process (MGS)
, where projection and subtraction occur iteratively.
To avoid ambiguity, the traditional Gram-Schmidt is referred to as the
classical Gram-Schmidt process (CGS)
.

The primary distinction between CGS and MGS lies in their approach to projection and subtraction. In CGS, the same vector is projected onto all previously computed orthonormal vectors before performing the subtraction. Conversely, in MGS, projection and subtraction are interleaved.
For illustration, consider a three-column matrix
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\bm{a}_{3}]
, as shown in Figure
, where each step is represented in a different color. Below is a summary of the processes for computing
\bm{q}_{k}
from the
k
-th column
\bm{a}_{k}
of
\bm{A}
, given the orthonormalized vectors
\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{k-1}\}
:

\displaystyle\text{(CGS)}:

\displaystyle\text{obtain $\bm{q}_{k}$ by normalizing $\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}=(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}_{k-1}\bm{Q}_{k-1}^{\top})\bm{a}_{k}$;}

\displaystyle\text{(MGS)}:

\displaystyle\text{obtain $\bm{q}_{k}$ by normalizing $\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}=\left\{(\bm{I}-\bm{q}_{k-1}\bm{q}_{k-1}^{\top})\ldots\left[(\bm{I}-\bm{q}_{2}\bm{q}_{2}^{\top})\left((\bm{I}-\bm{q}_{1}\bm{q}_{1}^{\top})\bm{a}_{k}\right)\right]\right\}$},

where the nested parentheses in MGS denote the computation order.

(a)

CGS, step 1:
blue
vector; step 2:
green
vector; step 3:
purple
vector.

(b)

MGS, step 1:
blue
vector; step 2:
purple
vector.

Figure 3.3:
Comparison of CGS and MGS in three-dimensional space. Here,
\bm{q}_{2}^{\prime}
is parallel to
\bm{q}_{2}
, so projecting onto
\bm{q}_{2}
is equivalent to projecting onto
\bm{q}_{2}^{\prime}
.

What’s the difference?

To illustrate, consider a three-column matrix
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\bm{a}_{3}]
. Suppose we have already computed
\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}\}
, where
\mathrm{span}\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}\}=\mathrm{span}\{\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2}\}
, our objective is to compute
\bm{q}_{3}
.

In the CGS algorithm, the orthogonalization of column
\bm{a}_{3}
against
\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}\}
is accomplished by simultaneously projecting the original column
\bm{a}_{3}
of
\bm{A}
onto
\bm{q}_{1}
and
\bm{q}_{2}
, followed by subtracting these projections at once (Figure
):

\left\{\begin{aligned} \bm{a}_{3}^{\perp}&=\bm{a}_{3}-(\bm{q}_{1}^{\top}\bm{a}_{3})\bm{q}_{1}-(\bm{q}_{2}^{\top}\bm{a}_{3})\bm{q}_{2}=\bm{a}_{3}-(\bm{q}_{1}\bm{q}_{1}^{\top})\bm{a}_{3}-\boxed{(\bm{q}_{2}\bm{q}_{2}^{\top}){\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{a}_{3}}};\\
\bm{q}_{3}&=\frac{\bm{a}_{3}^{\perp}}{\left\lVert\bm{a}_{3}^{\perp}\right\rVert}.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.5)

In contrast, the MGS algorithm subtracts the components along
\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}\}
from
\bm{a}_{3}
sequentially as they are computed.
Therefore, the orthogonalization of column
\bm{a}_{3}
against
\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}\}
is not performed by projecting the original column
\bm{a}_{3}
against
\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}\}
as it is in the CGS, but rather against a vector obtained by subtracting from that column
\bm{a}_{3}
of
\bm{A}
the components in the direction of
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}
successively.
This distinction is crucial because it leads to smaller error components of
\bm{q}_{3}
in
\mathrm{span}\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}\}
(further discussed in the following paragraphs).

More precisely, in the MGS algorithm, the orthogonalization of column
\bm{a}_{3}
against
\bm{q}_{1}
is accomplished by subtracting the component of
\bm{q}_{1}
from the vector
\bm{a}_{3}
:

\bm{a}_{3}^{(1)}=(\bm{I}-\bm{q}_{1}\bm{q}_{1}^{\top})\bm{a}_{3}=\bm{a}_{3}-(\bm{q}_{1}\bm{q}_{1}^{\top})\bm{a}_{3},

where
\bm{a}_{3}^{(1)}
represents the component of
\bm{a}_{3}
orthogonal to
\bm{q}_{1}
. The subsequent step is then executed by

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{3}^{(2)}=(\bm{I}-\bm{q}_{2}\bm{q}_{2}^{\top})\bm{a}_{3}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\bm{a}_{3}^{(1)}-(\bm{q}_{2}\bm{q}_{2}^{\top})\bm{a}_{3}^{(1)}=\bm{a}_{3}-(\bm{q}_{1}\bm{q}_{1}^{\top})\bm{a}_{3}-\boxed{(\bm{q}_{2}\bm{q}_{2}^{\top}){\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{a}_{3}^{(1)}}},

(3.6)

where
\bm{a}_{3}^{(2)}
denotes the component of
\bm{a}_{3}^{(1)}
orthogonal to
\bm{q}_{2}
. The distinction from CGS (Equation (
)) is highlighted in
blue
text.
Consequently,
\bm{a}_{3}^{(2)}
corresponds to the component of
\bm{a}_{3}
orthogonal to
\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}\}
, as shown in Figure
.

Main difference and catastrophic cancellation.

The key difference is that the vector
\bm{a}_{3}
can in general have large components in
\mathrm{span}\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}\}
, in which case one starts with large
values and ends up with small values that result in large relative errors in them—a phenomenon known as
catastrophic cancellation
.
In contrast,
\bm{a}_{3}^{(1)}
is already orthogonal to
\bm{q}_{1}
and has only a small “error” component in the direction of
\bm{q}_{1}
.
A comparison of the
boxed
text in Equations (
) and (
) reveals that
(\bm{q}_{2}\bm{q}_{2}^{\top})\bm{a}_{3}^{(1)}
in Equation (
) is more accurate, as argued above.
Due to the reduced error in this projection factor, the MGS method introduces smaller orthogonalization errors at each subtraction step compared to the CGS method. In fact, this difference is quantifiable. It can be shown that the final
\bm{Q}
obtained in the CGS satisfies

\left\lVert\bm{I}-\bm{Q}\bm{Q}^{\top}\right\rVert\leq\mathcal{O}(\epsilon\kappa^{2}(\bm{A})),

where
\kappa(\bm{A})
is a value larger than 1 determined by
\bm{A}
.
Whereas, in the MGS, the corresponding error satisfies

\left\lVert\bm{I}-\bm{Q}\bm{Q}^{\top}\right\rVert\leq\mathcal{O}(\epsilon\kappa(\bm{A})).

That is, the
\bm{Q}
obtained via MGS is “more orthogonal” than that obtained via CGS.

More to go, preliminaries for Householder and Givens methods.

WWhile MGS generally outperforms CGS in practice, it is not entirely immune to the
catastrophic cancellation
issue.
For example, in iteration
k
of the MGS algorithm, if
\bm{a}_{k}
is nearly in the span of
\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{k-1}\}
, then the resulting
\bm{a}_{k}^{\perp}
will have only a small component perpendicular to
\mathrm{span}\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{k-1}\}
. This amplifies the “error” component in
\mathrm{span}\{\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{k-1}\}
, leading to a less orthogonal
\bm{Q}
.
In such scenarios, a more robust approach involves finding a sequence of orthogonal matrices
\{\bm{Q}_{1},\bm{Q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{Q}_{l}\}
such that
\bm{Q}_{l}\ldots\bm{Q}_{2}\bm{Q}_{1}\bm{A}
become triangular. In this case, the resulting
\bm{Q}=(\bm{Q}_{l}\ldots\bm{Q}_{2}\bm{Q}_{1})^{\top}
will be “more” orthogonal than those produced by both CGS and MGS.
These improved techniques will be explored in Section
and Section
using Householder reflectors and Givens rotations.

3.7
Computing Full QR Decomposition via Gram-Schmidt Process

A full QR decomposition of an
m\times n
matrix with linearly independent columns involves extending the decomposition by appending additional
m-n
orthonormal columns to
\bm{Q}
, transforming it into an
m\times m
orthogonal matrix. Simultaneously, rows of zeros are added to
\bm{R}
, making it an
m\times n
upper triangular matrix. The additional columns in
\bm{Q}
are referred to as
silent columns
, while the additional rows in
\bm{R}
are called
silent rows
.
Figure
illustrates the differences between the reduced QR decomposition and the full QR decomposition, where silent columns in
\bm{Q}
are denoted in
gray
, blank entries are zero, and
blue
entries indicate elements that are not necessarily zero.

(a)

Reduced QR decomposition.

(b)

Full QR decomposition.

Figure 3.4:
Comparison between the reduced and full QR decompositions.

3.8
Dependent Columns

Previously, we assumed that the matrix
\bm{A}
has linearly independent columns. However, this assumption is not always necessary.
Suppose in step
k
of the CGS or MGS process, the vector
\bm{a}_{k}
lies in the plane spanned by
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{k-1}
, which is equivalent to the space spanned by
\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{k-1}
, i.e., the vectors
\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{k}
are dependent.
When this dependency occurs, the scalar
r_{kk}
becomes zero (see Equation (
) or Algorithm
), rendering
\bm{q}_{k}
undefined due to division by zero.
To handle such scenarios, one can arbitrarily choose
\bm{q}_{k}
as any normalized vector orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}([\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{k-1}])
and proceed with the Gram-Schmidt process.
For a matrix
\bm{A}
with dependent columns, both reduced and full QR decomposition algorithms are still applicable. The procedure for step
k
in the algorithm is redefined as follows:

\bm{q}_{k}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &(\bm{a}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}r_{ik}\bm{q}_{i})/r_{kk},\qquad r_{ik}=\bm{q}_{i}^{\top}\bm{a}_{k},r_{kk}=\left\lVert\bm{a}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}r_{ik}\bm{q}_{i}\right\rVert,&\mathrm{if\,}r_{kk}\neq 0,\\
&\text{pick one vector in }\mathcal{C}^{\bot}([\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{k-1}]),\text{ and normalize},\qquad&\mathrm{if\,}r_{kk}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

This idea can be further extended such that, when
\bm{q}_{k}
does not exist, we simply skip the current step and add the silent columns at the end of the process.
Consequently, the QR decomposition of a matrix with dependent columns is generally not unique. However, following a consistent procedure ensures the uniqueness of the decomposition for any given matrix.

This framework also serves as a method to determine linear independence. If
r_{kk}=0
at any step in CGS or MGS, the vectors
\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{k}
are reported as dependent, and the algorithm terminates for the purpose of “independence checking.”

3.9
QR with Column Pivoting: Column-Pivoted QR (CPQR)

If the columns of
\bm{A}
are linearly dependent, a column-pivoted QR (CPQR) decomposition can be obtained as follows:

Any
m\times n
matrix
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]
with
m\geq n
and rank
r
can be decomposed as

\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{Q}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{R}_{11}\in\real^{r\times r}
is upper triangular,
\bm{R}_{12}\in\real^{r\times(n-r)}
,
\bm{Q}\in\real^{m\times m}
is an orthogonal matrix, and
\bm{P}
is a permutation matrix. This is known as the
full
CPQR decomposition. Similarly, the
reduced
version is given by

\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{Q}_{r}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{R}_{12}\\
\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{R}_{11}\in\real^{r\times r}
is upper triangular,
\bm{R}_{12}\in\real^{r\times(n-r)}
,
\bm{Q}_{r}\in\real^{m\times r}
contains orthonormal columns, and
\bm{P}
is a permutation matrix.

3.9.1
A Simple CPQR via CGS

The CPQR decomposition can be computed using the classical Gram-Schmidt process.
In the QR decomposition for dependent columns, if
r_{kk}=0
, it indicates that column
k
of
\bm{A}
is dependent on the previous
k-1
columns.
In such cases, a column permutation is performed, moving the dependent column to the last position, and the Gram-Schmidt process is continued.
Here,
\bm{P}
represents the permutation matrix that reorders the dependent columns into the last
n-r
positions.
Suppose the first
r
columns of
\bm{A}\bm{P}
are
[\widehat{\bm{a}}_{1},\widehat{\bm{a}}_{2},\ldots,\widehat{\bm{a}}_{r}]
. The span of these columns is equivalent to the span of
\bm{Q}_{r}
(in the reduced version) or the span of
\bm{Q}_{:,1:r}
(in the full version):

\mathcal{C}([\widehat{\bm{a}}_{1},\widehat{\bm{a}}_{2},\ldots,\widehat{\bm{a}}_{r}])=\mathcal{C}(\bm{Q}_{r})=\mathcal{C}(\bm{Q}_{:,1:r}).

The matrix
\bm{R}_{12}
recovers the dependent
n-r
columns from the column space of
\bm{Q}_{r}
or
\bm{Q}_{:,1:r}
.
Figure
compares the reduced and full CPQR decompositions, where silent columns in
\bm{Q}
are denoted in
gray
, blank entries are zero, and
blue
/
orange
entries denote elements that are not necessarily zero.

(a)

Reduced CPQR decomposition.

(b)

Full CPQR decomposition.

Figure 3.5:
Comparison between the reduced and full CPQR decompositions.

3.9.2
A Practical CPQR via CGS

We observe that the simple CPQR algorithm pivots the first
r
independent columns to the first
r
columns of
\bm{A}\bm{P}
. Let
\bm{A}_{1}
represent the first
r
columns of
\bm{A}\bm{P}
, and
\bm{A}_{2}
represent the remaining columns. In the context of the full CPQR decomposition, we have:

\bm{A}\bm{P}=[\bm{A}_{1},\bm{A}_{2}]=\bm{Q}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}=\left[\bm{Q}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix},\bm{Q}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\right].

It is evident that

\left\lVert\bm{A}_{2}\right\rVert=\left\|\bm{Q}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\right\|=\left\|\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\right\|=\left\|\bm{R}_{12}\right\|,

where the penultimate equality arises from the preservation of the matrix norm under orthogonal transformations. Therefore, the norm of
\bm{R}_{12}
is directly determined by the norm of
\bm{A}_{2}
.
For a well-conditioned CPQR, it is desirable for
\bm{R}_{12}
to have a small norm. A practical CPQR algorithm achieves this by first permuting the columns of
\bm{A}
so that they are ordered in decreasing vector norm:

\widetilde{\bm{A}}=\bm{A}\bm{P}_{0}=[\bm{a}_{j_{1}},\bm{a}_{j_{2}},\ldots,\bm{a}_{j_{n}}],

where
\{j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{n}\}
is a permuted index set of
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, and

\left\lVert\bm{a}_{j_{1}}\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert\bm{a}_{j_{2}}\right\rVert\geq\ldots\geq\left\lVert\bm{a}_{j_{n}}\right\rVert.

Next, the “simple” reduced CPQR decomposition is applied to
\widetilde{\bm{A}}
, resulting in
\widetilde{\bm{A}}\bm{P}_{1}=\bm{Q}_{r}[\bm{R}_{11},\bm{R}_{12}]
. The “practical” reduced CPQR of
\bm{A}
is then recovered as

\bm{A}\underbrace{\bm{P}_{0}\bm{P}_{1}}_{\bm{P}}=\bm{Q}_{r}[\bm{R}_{11},\bm{R}_{12}].

The CPQR algorithm can be further optimized by using the MGS process. This enhanced approach has the added benefit of halting when the factorization encounters a rank-deficient submatrix, allowing the numerical rank to be determined. This method is referred to as
partial factorization
, though we will not delve into its details here
(
Lu 2021c
)
.

3.10
QR with Column Pivoting: Revealing Rank-One Deficiency

Column-pivoted QR (CPQR) is one of several methods for identifying a column permutation when
\bm{A}
is rank-deficient. This process rearranges the first
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
to occupy the first
r
columns of
\bm{A}\bm{P}
.
If
\bm{A}
is nearly rank-one deficient, the goal becomes determining a column permutation of
\bm{A}
that minimizes the pivotal element
r_{nn}
in the resulting QR decomposition. This is known as the
revealing rank-one deficiency
problem.

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
and
\bm{v}\in\real^{n}
be a unit vector (i.e.,
\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert=1
). There exists a permutation matrix
\bm{P}
such that the reduced QR decomposition

\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}

satisfies
r_{nn}\leq\sqrt{n}\epsilon
, where
\epsilon=\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{v}\right\rVert
, and
r_{nn}
is the
n
-th diagonal element of
\bm{R}
. In this decomposition,
\bm{Q}\in\real^{m\times n}
and
\bm{R}\in\real^{n\times n}
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Let
\bm{P}\in\real^{n\times n}
be a permutation matrix such that, if
\bm{w}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{v}
, where

|w_{n}|=\max|v_{i}|,\,\,\,\,\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}.

That is, we swap the entry with the largest magnitude to the last position, ensuring that the last component of
\bm{w}
equals the maximal component of
\bm{v}
in absolute value.
Then we have
|w_{n}|\geq 1/\sqrt{n}
. Suppose the QR decomposition of
\bm{A}\bm{P}
is
\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
. Then,

\epsilon=\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{v}\right\rVert=\left\lVert(\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P})(\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{v})\right\rVert=\left\lVert\bm{R}\bm{w}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}\vdots\\
r_{nn}w_{n}\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert\geq|r_{nn}w_{n}|\geq|r_{nn}|/\sqrt{n},

where the second equality follows from the preservation of length under orthogonal transformations, and
\bm{P}
is an orthogonal matrix satisfying
\bm{P}\bm{P}^{\top}=\bm{I}
. This concludes the proof.

The following discussion uses the singular value decomposition (SVD), which will be introduced in Chapter
. You may skip this section on a first reading.
Suppose the SVD of
\bm{A}
is given by
\bm{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}
, where
\sigma_{i}
’s are singular values with
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\sigma_{n}
, i.e.,
\sigma_{n}
is the smallest singular value, and
\bm{u}_{i}
’s and
\bm{v}_{i}
’s are the left and right singular vectors, respectively. Then, if we let
\bm{v}=\bm{v}_{n}
such that
\bm{A}\bm{v}_{n}=\sigma_{n}\bm{u}_{n}
, we have

\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{v}\right\rVert=\sigma_{n}.

By constructing a permutation matrix
\bm{P}
satisfying

|\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{v}|_{n}=\max|v_{i}|,\,\,\,\,\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\},

we find a QR decomposition of
\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
where the pivotal element
r_{nn}
satisfies
r_{nn}\leq\sqrt{n}\sigma_{n}
. If
\bm{A}
is rank-one deficient (
\sigma_{n}\approx 0
), then
r_{nn}
will also be close to zero, reflecting the deficiency in the matrix’s rank.

3.11
QR with Column Pivoting: Revealing Rank-r Deficiency*

Building on the previous section, we now focus on computing the reduced QR decomposition for a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
that is approximately rank-
r
deficient with
r>1
. Our goal is to find a permutation matrix
\bm{P}
such that:

\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}=\bm{Q}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}&\bm{M}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{N}\end{bmatrix},

(3.7)

where
\bm{N}\in\real^{r\times r}
, and
\left\lVert\bm{N}\right\rVert
is small in some norm.
A recursive algorithm can be employed to achieve this. Suppose we have already isolated a small
k\times k
block
\bm{N}_{k}
. If we can isolate a small
