(k+1)\times(k+1)
block
\bm{N}_{k+1}
, the permutation matrix can be determined recursively.
To reiterate, assume the existence of a permutation
\bm{P}_{k}
such that
\bm{N}_{k}\in\real^{k\times k}
has a small norm:

\bm{A}\bm{P}_{k}=\bm{Q}_{k}\bm{R}_{k}=\bm{Q}_{k}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{k}&\bm{M}_{k}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{N}_{k}\end{bmatrix}.

Now, we aim to find a permutation
\bm{P}_{k+1}
such that
\bm{N}_{k+1}\in\real^{(k+1)\times(k+1)}
also has a small norm:

\bm{A}\bm{P}_{k+1}=\bm{Q}_{k+1}\bm{R}_{k+1}=\bm{Q}_{k+1}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{k+1}&\bm{M}_{k+1}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{N}_{k+1}\end{bmatrix}.

Using the algorithm described earlier, there exists an
(n-k)\times(n-k)
permutation matrix
\widetilde{\bm{P}}_{k+1}
such that
\bm{L}_{k}\in\real^{(n-k)\times(n-k)}
has the QR decomposition
\bm{L}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{P}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{k+1}\widetilde{\bm{L}}_{k}
, where the entry
(n-k,n-k)
of
\widetilde{\bm{L}}_{k}
is small. Construct the following:

\bm{P}_{k+1}=\bm{P}_{k}\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\bm{P}}_{k+1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{Q}_{k+1}=\bm{Q}_{k}\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{k+1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}.

This yields:

{\bm{A}\bm{P}_{k+1}=\bm{Q}_{k+1}\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\bm{L}}_{k}&\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{M}_{k}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{N}_{k}\end{bmatrix}}.

Since the
(n-k,n-k)
-th entry of
\widetilde{\bm{L}}_{k}
is small, proving that the last row of
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{M}_{k}
is also small in norm will reveal the rank-
(k+1)
deficiency of
\bm{A}
(see
(
Chan 1987
)
for a proof).

3.12
Existence of the QR Decomposition via Householder Reflector

Householder matrices
, also referred to as
Householder reflectors
, play a crucial role in numerical linear algebra. They are widely used in solving linear systems, estimating least-squares problems, and obtaining Hessenberg forms. This section demonstrates how Householder reflectors can be employed to establish the existence of QR decomposition.

We begin by formally defining a Householder reflector and then explore its properties.

Definition 44
(Householder Reflector).

Let
\bm{u}\in\real^{n}
be a vector of unit length (i.e.,
\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert=1
). The matrix
\bm{H}=\bm{I}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}
is called a Householder reflector or a Householder transformation. This matrix is associated with the unit vector
\bm{u}
, which is referred to as the Householder vector.
When a vector
\bm{x}
is multiplied by
\bm{H}
, it is reflected across the hyperplane orthogonal to
\mathrm{span}\{\bm{u}\}
(denoted as
\mathrm{span}\{\bm{u}\}^{\perp}
).

If
\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert\neq 1
, the Householder reflector is defined as:
\bm{H}=\bm{I}-2\frac{\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}}{\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{u}}
.

Derived from the definition of the Householder reflector, we obtain the following corollary, indicating that specific vectors remain unaltered when subjected to the Householder reflector.

Corollary 45
(Unreflected by Householder).

Given a unit vector
\bm{u}
, the Householder reflector
\bm{H}=\bm{I}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}
leaves any vector
\bm{v}
perpendicular to
\bm{u}
unchanged. In other words, if
\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{v}=0
, then
\bm{H}\bm{v}=\bm{v}
.

This follows directly from substitution:
(\bm{I}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top})\bm{v}=\bm{v}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{v}=\bm{v}
.

Let
\bm{u}
be a unit vector with
\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert=1
, and let
\bm{v}
be a vector perpendicular to
\bm{u}
. Then any vector
\bm{x}
in the plane can be decomposed into two parts:
\bm{x}=\bm{x}_{\bm{v}}+\bm{x}_{\bm{u}}
, where the first component
\bm{x}_{\bm{u}}
is parallel to
\bm{u}
and the second one
\bm{x}_{\bm{v}}
is perpendicular to
\bm{u}
(i.e., parallel to
\bm{v}
).
Using the projection formula from Section
, the component parallel to
\bm{x}_{\bm{u}}
is
\bm{x}_{\bm{u}}=\frac{\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}}{\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{u}}\bm{x}=\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{x}
.
Applying the Householder reflector
\bm{H}=\bm{I}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}
to
\bm{x}
, we get:
\bm{H}\bm{x}=(\bm{I}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top})(\bm{x}_{\bm{v}}+\bm{x}_{\bm{u}})=\bm{x}_{\bm{v}}-\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{x}=\bm{x}_{\bm{v}}-\bm{x}_{\bm{u}}
. This shows that the Householder reflector flips
\bm{x}
across the hyperplane
\mathrm{span}\{\bm{u}\}^{\perp}
.
In other words, the subspace perpendicular to
\bm{u}
acts as a mirror, reflecting
\bm{x}
. This transformation is illustrated in Figure
.

Figure 3.6:
Illustration of the Householder reflector. The Householder reflector obtained by
\bm{H}=\bm{I}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}
, where
\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert=1
, will reflect a vector
\bm{x}
along the plane perpendicular to
\bm{u}
:
\bm{x}=\bm{x}_{\bm{v}}+\bm{x}_{\bm{u}}\rightarrow\bm{x}_{\bm{v}}-\bm{x}_{\bm{u}}
.

The preceding explanation outlines the process of determining the reflected vector using the Householder reflector.
However, an additional question arises: If given that two vectors undergo a Householder reflection, how can we determine the corresponding Householder reflector?
This property is particularly important in QR decomposition computations, where the goal is to transform a column into a predetermined form.

Corollary 46
(Finding the Householder Reflector).

Suppose
\bm{x}
is reflected to
\bm{y}
by a Householder reflector with
\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert
. The Householder reflector can be computed as:

\bm{H}=\bm{I}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top},\text{ where }\bm{u}=\frac{\bm{x}-\bm{y}}{\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{y}\right\rVert}.

Proof
[of Corollary
]
Starting from the definition of the reflection, we have:

\displaystyle\bm{H}\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\bm{x}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{x}=\bm{x}-2\frac{(\bm{x}-\bm{y})(\bm{x}^{\top}-\bm{y}^{\top})}{(\bm{x}-\bm{y})^{\top}(\bm{x}-\bm{y})}\bm{x}=\bm{x}-(\bm{x}-\bm{y})=\bm{y}.

The condition
\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert
is necessary to ensure the result holds.

Householder reflectors are particularly useful for setting specific components of a vector to zero.
For example, it is often necessary to zero out all elements of a vector
\bm{a}\in\real^{n}
except for the
i
-th element.
In such cases, the Householder vector can be defined as:

\bm{u}=\frac{\bm{a}-r\bm{e}_{i}}{\left\lVert\bm{a}-r\bm{e}_{i}\right\rVert},\qquad\text{where }r=\pm\left\lVert\bm{a}\right\rVert,

which is a reasonable Householder vector since
\left\lVert\bm{a}\right\rVert=\left\lVert r\bm{e}_{i}\right\rVert=|r|
. Specifically, if
r=\left\lVert\bm{a}\right\rVert
, the Householder reflector
\bm{H}=\bm{I}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}
reflects
\bm{a}
to
\left\lVert\bm{a}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{i}
; conversely, if
r=-\left\lVert\bm{a}\right\rVert
, the reflector maps
\bm{a}
to
-\left\lVert\bm{a}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{i}
.

Remark 47
(Householder Properties).

A Householder reflector
\bm{H}
possesses the following properties:

•

\bm{H}\bm{H}=\bm{I}
: reflecting a vector twice yields the original vector.

•

Symmetry:
\bm{H}=\bm{H}^{\top}
.

•

Orthogonality:
\bm{H}^{\top}\bm{H}=\bm{H}\bm{H}^{\top}=\bm{I}
, meaning
\bm{H}
is an orthogonal matrix.

•

\bm{H}\bm{u}=-\bm{u}
, if
\bm{H}=\bm{I}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}
.

In the Gram-Schmidt process, we observe that the QR decomposition employs a triangular matrix to orthogonalize a given matrix
\bm{A}
.
A key insight is that constructing a sequence of orthogonal matrices to iteratively transform
\bm{A}
into triangular form can also yield the QR decomposition.
For example, consider an orthogonal matrix
\bm{Q}_{1}
that introduces zeros into all elements of the first column of
\bm{A}
except for the entry (1,1); similarly, an orthogonal matrix
\bm{Q}_{2}
zeros out all entries of the second column below (2,2); and so forth. This sequence of operations allows us to derive the QR decomposition.
This process utilizes reflections to transform columns relative to the standard basis vector
\bm{e}_{1}
, which has all entries zero except the first.

Let
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]\in\real^{m\times n}
be the column partition of
\bm{A}
. Define:

r_{1}=\left\lVert\bm{a}_{1}\right\rVert,\qquad\bm{u}_{1}=\frac{\bm{a}_{1}-r_{1}\bm{e}_{1}}{\left\lVert\bm{a}_{1}-r_{1}\bm{e}_{1}\right\rVert},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{H}_{1}=\bm{I}-2\bm{u}_{1}\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}.

(3.8)

Here,
\bm{e}_{1}=[1;0;0;\ldots;0]\in\real^{m}
denotes the first unit basis in
m
. Applying
\bm{H}_{1}
to
\bm{A}
yields:
Then

\bm{H}_{1}\bm{A}=[\bm{H}_{1}\bm{a}_{1},\bm{H}_{1}\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{H}_{1}\bm{a}_{n}]=\begin{bmatrix}r_{1}&\bm{R}_{1,2:n}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{B}_{2}\end{bmatrix}.

(3.9)

The operation reflects
\bm{a}_{1}
to
r_{1}\bm{e}_{1}
, zeroing out all entries below the diagonal in the first column.
Notably, we reflect
\bm{a}_{1}
to
\left\lVert\bm{a}_{1}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1}
, where the two vectors have the same length (preserving its length) rather than directly to
\bm{e}_{1}
to ensure numerical stability; and this aligns with the criteria outlined in Corollary
.

Next, we apply the same process to the submatrix
\bm{B}_{2}
in Equation (
), ensuring that entries below (2,2) become zero. This selective application avoids reintroducing nonzero values in the first column, preserving previous progress.
Let
\bm{B}_{2}=[\bm{b}_{2},\bm{b}_{3},\ldots,\bm{b}_{n}]
be the column partition of
\bm{B}_{2}
, and define

r_{2}=\left\lVert\bm{b}_{2}\right\rVert,\qquad\bm{u}_{2}=\frac{\bm{b}_{2}-r_{2}\bm{e}_{1}}{\left\lVert\bm{b}_{2}-r_{2}\bm{e}_{1}\right\rVert},\qquad\qquad\widetilde{\bm{H}}_{2}=\bm{I}-2\bm{u}_{2}\bm{u}_{2}^{\top},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{H}_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\widetilde{\bm{H}}_{2}\end{bmatrix}.

In this context,
\bm{e}_{1}
denotes the first unit basis in
m-1
, and
\bm{H}_{2}
is orthogonal, since
\widetilde{\bm{H}}_{2}
is orthogonal. Applying
\bm{H}_{2}
yields

\bm{H}_{2}\bm{H}_{1}\bm{A}=[\bm{H}_{2}\bm{H}_{1}\bm{a}_{1},\bm{H}_{2}\bm{H}_{1}\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{H}_{2}\bm{H}_{1}\bm{a}_{n}]=\begin{bmatrix}r_{1}&r_{12}&\bm{R}_{1,3:n}\\
0&r_{2}&\bm{R}_{2,3:n}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\bm{C}_{3}\end{bmatrix}.

Repeating this process iteratively triangularizes
\bm{A}=(\bm{H}_{n}\bm{H}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{H}_{1})^{-1}\bm{R}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
. Since each
\bm{H}_{i}
is symmetric and orthogonal,
\bm{Q}=(\bm{H}_{n}\bm{H}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{H}_{1})^{-1}=\bm{H}_{1}\bm{H}_{2}\ldots\bm{H}_{n}
.

For a
5\times 4
matrix, the transformation proceeds as follows, where
\boxtimes
represents a value that is not necessarily zero, and
boldface
indicates the value has just been changed:

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{A}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{H}_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{H}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{H}_{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{H}_{2}\bm{H}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{H}_{3}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{H}_{3}\bm{H}_{2}\bm{H}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{H}_{4}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&\bm{0}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{H}_{4}\bm{H}_{3}\bm{H}_{2}\bm{H}_{1}\bm{A}}.

The Householder algorithm is a method for transforming a matrix into triangular form using a series of orthogonal matrix operations.
In contrast to the Gram-Schmidt process (both CGS and MGS), which employs a triangular matrix to orthogonalize a given matrix, the Householder algorithm uses orthogonal matrices to achieve triangularization. This key distinction can be summarized as follows:

•

Gram-Schmidt algorithm (triangular orthogonalization): Uses projections to orthogonalize vectors, resulting in a triangular matrix.;

•

Householder algorithm (orthogonal triangularization): Applies orthogonal transformations to triangularize the matrix.

Additionally, note that both the Householder algorithm and the Givens algorithm (which we will explore shortly) apply a sequence of orthogonal matrices to compute a
full
QR decomposition. In contrast, the QR decomposition obtained through CGS or MGS is a
reduced
version. While it is possible to append silent columns or rows to extend the reduced QR decomposition to the full version, this step is not inherent to the CGS or MGS methods.

3.13
Existence of the QR Decomposition the Givens Rotation

In Definition
, we introduced the concept of a Givens rotation, particularly in the context of finding the rank-one update or downdate of the Cholesky decomposition. Let us now explore the specific effects of Givens rotations through illustrative examples.
Consider the following
2\times 2
orthogonal matrices:

\bm{F}=\begin{bmatrix}-c&s\\
s&c\end{bmatrix},\qquad\bm{J}=\begin{bmatrix}c&-s\\
s&c\end{bmatrix},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{G}=\begin{bmatrix}c&s\\
-s&c\end{bmatrix},

where
s=\sin\theta
and
c=\cos\theta
for some angle
\theta
. The first matrix has
\det(\bm{F})=-1
and represents a special case of a Householder reflector in two dimensions. It can be expressed as
\bm{F}=\bm{I}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}
, where
\bm{u}=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\frac{1+c}{2}},&\sqrt{\frac{1-c}{2}}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}
or
\bm{u}=\begin{bmatrix}-\sqrt{\frac{1+c}{2}},&-\sqrt{\frac{1-c}{2}}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}
.
This matrix reflects vectors across a specific axis.
The matrices
\bm{J}
and
\bm{G}
have
\det(\bm{J})=\det(\bm{G})=1
and perform rotations rather than reflections. A matrix of this type is referred to as a
Givens rotation
.

(a)

\bm{y}=\bm{J}\bm{x}
, counter-clockwise rotation.

(b)

\bm{y}=\bm{G}\bm{x}
, clockwise rotation.

Figure 3.7:
Illustration of two Givens rotations.

Figure
demonstrate a rotation of a vector
\bm{x}
under the action of
\bm{J}
, yielding
\bm{y}=\bm{J}\bm{x}
with

\left\{\begin{aligned} &y_{1}=c\cdot x_{1}-s\cdot x_{2};\\
&y_{2}=s\cdot x_{1}+c\cdot x_{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

We aim to verify that the angle between vectors
\bm{x}
and
\bm{y}
is indeed
\theta
(indicating a counter-clockwise rotation) under the Givens rotation
\bm{J}
.
Firstly, we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\cos(\alpha)=\frac{x_{1}}{\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}};\\
&\sin(\alpha)=\frac{x_{2}}{\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}},\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\text{and }\qquad\left\{\begin{aligned} &\cos(\theta)=c;\\
&\sin(\theta)=s.\end{aligned}\right.

This implies that
\cos(\theta+\alpha)=\cos(\theta)\cos(\alpha)-\sin(\theta)\sin(\alpha)
.
If we can show that
\cos(\theta+\alpha)=\cos(\theta)\cos(\alpha)-\sin(\theta)\sin(\alpha)
is equal to
\frac{y_{1}}{\sqrt{y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}}}
, then we complete the proof.

For the former one,
\cos(\theta+\alpha)=\cos(\theta)\cos(\alpha)-\sin(\theta)\sin(\alpha)=\frac{c\cdot x_{1}-s\cdot x_{2}}{\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}}
. For the latter one, it can be verified that
\sqrt{y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}}=\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}
, and
\frac{y_{1}}{\sqrt{y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}}}=\frac{c\cdot x_{1}-s\cdot x_{2}}{\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}}
. This completes the proof. Similarly, we can also show that the angle between vectors
\bm{y}=\bm{G}\bm{x}
and
\bm{x}
is also
\theta
in Figure
, and the rotation is clockwise.

It can be easily verified that the
n
-th order Givens rotation (Definition
) is an orthogonal matrix with a determinant of 1. For any vector
\bm{x}=[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}]^{\top}\in\real^{n}
, applying a Givens rotation yields
\bm{y}=\bm{G}_{kl}\bm{x}
(the subscripts
k,l
indicate the rotations occur
in plane
k
and
l
), where

\left\{\begin{aligned} &y_{k}=c\cdot x_{k}+s\cdot x_{l},\\
&y_{l}=-s\cdot x_{k}+c\cdot x_{l},\\
&y_{j}=x_{j}.&(j\neq k,l)\end{aligned}\right.

In other words, a Givens rotation applied to
\bm{x}
rotates two components of
\bm{x}
by an angle
\theta
, leaving all other components unchanged.
When
\sqrt{x_{k}^{2}+x_{l}^{2}}\neq 0
,
let
c=\frac{x_{k}}{\sqrt{x_{k}^{2}+x_{l}^{2}}}
and
s=\frac{x_{l}}{\sqrt{x_{k}^{2}+x_{l}^{2}}}
. Then,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &y_{k}=\sqrt{x_{k}^{2}+x_{l}^{2}},\\
&y_{l}=0,\\
&y_{j}=x_{j}.&(j\neq k,l)\end{aligned}\right.

This result is critical for implementing the QR decomposition using Givens rotations.

Corollary 48
(Basis Transformation Using Forward Givens Rotations).

For any vector
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
, there exist a set of Givens rotations
\{\bm{G}_{12},\bm{G}_{13},\ldots,\bm{G}_{1n}\}
such that
\bm{G}_{1n}\ldots\bm{G}_{13}\bm{G}_{12}\bm{x}=\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1}
, where
\bm{e}_{1}\in\real^{n}
is the first unit basis in
n
.

Proof
[of Corollary
]
From the earlier result, we can construct a sequence of Givens rotations
\bm{G}_{12},\bm{G}_{13},
and
\bm{G}_{14}
such that

\bm{G}_{12}\bm{x}=\left[\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}},0,x_{3},\ldots,x_{n}\right]^{\top},

\bm{G}_{13}\bm{G}_{12}\bm{x}=\left[\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}},0,0,x_{4},\ldots,x_{n}\right]^{\top},

and

\bm{G}_{14}\bm{G}_{13}\bm{G}_{12}\bm{x}=\left[\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}+x_{4}^{2}},0,0,0,x_{5},\ldots,x_{n}\right]^{\top}.

By continuing this process, we eventually obtain:
\bm{G}_{1n}\ldots\bm{G}_{13}\bm{G}_{12}=\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1}
.

Remark 49
(Basis Transformation Using Reverse Givens Rotations).

In Corollary
, he Givens rotations are applied in forward order, introducing zeros from the second entry to the second entry to the
n
-th entry.
However, in some cases, it may be desirable to apply the rotations in reverse order, introducing zeros from the
n
-th entry to the second entry, such that
\bm{G}_{12}\bm{G}_{13}\ldots\bm{G}_{1n}\bm{x}=\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1}
, where
\bm{e}_{1}\in\real^{n}
is the first unit basis in
n
.

The procedure is analogous to the forward case. Specifically, we construct Givens rotations
\bm{G}_{1n},\bm{G}_{1,(n-1)},\bm{G}_{1,(n-2)}
as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{G}_{1n}\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\left[\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{n-1},0\right]^{\top},

\displaystyle\bm{G}_{1,(n-1)}\bm{G}_{1n}\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\left[\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{n-1}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{n-2},0,0\right]^{\top},

\displaystyle\bm{G}_{1,(n-2)}\bm{G}_{1,(n-1)}\bm{G}_{1n}\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\left[\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{n-2}^{2}+x_{n-1}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{n-3},0,0,0\right]^{\top}.

Continuing this process, we ultimately obtain:
\bm{G}_{12}\bm{G}_{13}\ldots\bm{G}_{1n}\bm{x}=\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1}
.

Alternatively, there exists a sequence of rotations
\{\bm{G}_{12},\bm{G}_{23},\ldots,\bm{G}_{(n-1),n}\}
such that
\bm{G}_{12}\bm{G}_{23}\ldots\bm{G}_{(n-1),n}\bm{x}=\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1}
, where

\bm{G}_{(n-1),n}\bm{x}=\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-2},\sqrt{x_{n-1}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}},0\right]^{\top},

\bm{G}_{(n-2),(n-1)}\bm{G}_{(n-1),n}\bm{x}=\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-3},\sqrt{x_{n-2}^{2}+x_{n-1}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}},0,0\right]^{\top},

and

\displaystyle\bm{G}_{(n-3),(n-2)}\bm{G}_{(n-2),(n-1)}\bm{G}_{(n-1),n}\bm{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\bigg[x_{1},

\displaystyle x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-4},\sqrt{x_{n-3}^{2}+x_{n-2}^{2}+x_{n-1}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}},0,0,0\bigg]^{\top}.

By continuing this process, we ultimately obtain:
\bm{G}_{12}\bm{G}_{23}\ldots\bm{G}_{(n-1),n}\bm{x}=\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1}
.

This backward application of Givens rotations will prove useful in the context of rank-one updates to the QR decomposition (Section
).

From Corollary
mentioned earlier, we can introduce zeros by
rotating
the columns of the matrix to align with a basis vector
\bm{e}_{1}
, where all entries except the first entry are zero.
Let
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]\in\real^{m\times n}
be the column partition of
\bm{A}
, and let

\bm{G}_{1}=\bm{G}_{1m}\ldots\bm{G}_{13}\bm{G}_{12},

(3.10)

where
\bm{e}_{1}
here is the first unit basis in
m
, i.e.,
\bm{e}_{1}=[1;0;0;\ldots;0]\in\real^{m}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\bm{G}_{1}\bm{A}

\displaystyle=[\bm{G}_{1}\bm{a}_{1},\bm{G}_{1}\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{G}_{1}\bm{a}_{n}]=\begin{bmatrix}\left\lVert\bm{a}_{1}\right\rVert&\bm{R}_{1,2:n}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{B}_{2}\end{bmatrix},

(3.11)

which rotates
\bm{a}_{1}
to
\left\lVert\bm{a}_{1}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1}
and introduces zeros below the diagonal in the first column.

Next, we apply this process to
\bm{B}_{2}
in Equation (
) to make all entries below the (2,2)-th entry zero.
Suppose
\bm{B}_{2}=[\bm{b}_{2},\bm{b}_{3},\ldots,\bm{b}_{n}]
, and let

\bm{G}_{2}=\bm{G}_{2m}\ldots\bm{G}_{24}\bm{G}_{23},

where
\bm{G}_{2n},\ldots,\bm{G}_{24},\bm{G}_{23}
can be inferred from the context.
Then,

\displaystyle\bm{G}_{2}\bm{G}_{1}\bm{A}

\displaystyle=[\bm{G}_{2}\bm{G}_{1}\bm{a}_{1},\bm{G}_{2}\bm{G}_{1}\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{G}_{2}\bm{G}_{1}\bm{a}_{n}]=\begin{bmatrix}\left\lVert\bm{a}_{1}\right\rVert&r_{12}&\bm{R}_{1,3:n}\\
0&\left\lVert\bm{b}_{2}\right\rVert&\bm{R}_{2,3:n}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\bm{C}_{3}\end{bmatrix}.

This process can continue, and we will ultimately triangularize the matrix
\bm{A}
as
\bm{A}=(\bm{G}_{n}\bm{G}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{G}_{1})^{-1}\bm{R}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
. Since each matrix
\bm{G}_{i}
is orthogonal for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, we have
\bm{Q}=(\bm{G}_{n}\bm{G}_{n-1}\ldots\bm{G}_{1})^{-1}=\bm{G}_{1}^{\top}\bm{G}_{2}^{\top}\ldots\bm{G}_{n}^{\top}
, and

\displaystyle\bm{G}_{1}^{\top}\bm{G}_{2}^{\top}\ldots\bm{G}_{n}^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\bm{G}_{n}\ldots\bm{G}_{2}\bm{G}_{1})^{\top}

(3.12)

\displaystyle=\left\{(\bm{G}_{nm}\ldots\bm{G}_{n,(n+1)})\ldots(\bm{G}_{2m}\ldots\bm{G}_{23})(\bm{G}_{1m}\ldots\bm{G}_{12})\right\}^{\top}.

In practice, the Givens rotation algorithm outperforms the Householder algorithm when
\bm{A}
already contains many zeros below the main diagonal.
Therefore, Givens rotations are particularly suited for rank-one changes in the QR decomposition, as these changes introduce only a small number of nonzero values (Section
).
An example of a
5\times 4
matrix is presented below, where
\boxtimes
represents a value that is not necessarily zero, and
boldface
indicates the value has just been changed.

Givens rotations in
\bm{G}_{1}
.

For a
5\times 4
example, we can express
\bm{G}_{1}=\bm{G}_{15}\bm{G}_{14}\bm{G}_{13}\bm{G}_{12}
. The process is shown below:

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{A}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{12}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{12}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{13}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{13}\bm{G}_{12}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{14}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{14}\bm{G}_{13}\bm{G}_{12}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{15}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{15}\bm{G}_{14}\bm{G}_{13}\bm{G}_{12}\bm{A}}.

Givens rotation as a big picture.

When we consider
\bm{G}_{1},\bm{G}_{2},\bm{G}_{3},\bm{G}_{4}
as a single matrix, we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{A}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{2}\bm{G}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{3}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{3}\bm{G}_{2}\bm{G}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{4}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&\bm{0}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{4}\bm{G}_{3}\bm{G}_{2}\bm{G}_{1}\bm{A}}.

Orders to introduce the zeros.

The use of Givens rotations in the QR decomposition provides flexibility in choosing the order for introducing zeros in
\bm{R}
. In our approach, we introduce zeros column by column; however, an alternative method is to introduce zeros row by row.

3.14
Uniqueness of the QR Decomposition

The results of the QR decomposition using the Gram-Schmidt process, the Householder algorithm, and the Givens algorithm can vary. Even within the Householder algorithm, different approaches exist for selecting the sign of
r_{1}
in Equation (
). As a result, the QR decomposition of a matrix is not necessarily unique.

However, if the procedure described in the Gram-Schmidt process is followed, or if the sign in the Householder algorithm is chosen systematically, the decomposition is unique. The uniqueness of the
reduced
QR decomposition for a full-column-rank matrix
\bm{A}
is guaranteed when the diagonal elements of
\bm{R}
are positive, as shown by inductive analysis
(
Lu 2021c
)
. Here, we provide an alternative proof for the uniqueness of the
reduced
QR decomposition, assuming the diagonal elements of
\bm{R}
are positive. This proof also sheds light on the
implicit Q theorem
in Hessenberg decomposition (Section
) and tridiagonal decomposition (Theorem
).

Corollary 50
(Uniqueness of the reduced QR Decomposition).

Suppose
\bm{A}
be an
m\times n
matrix with full column rank
n
, where
m\geq n
. Then, the reduced QR decomposition is
unique
if the main diagonal values of
\bm{R}
are positive.

Proof
[of Corollary
]
Suppose the
reduced
QR decomposition is not unique. In that case, it can be extended into a
full
QR decomposition, and we can find two such full decompositions, so that
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}_{1}\bm{R}_{1}=\bm{Q}_{2}\bm{R}_{2}
. This implies
\bm{R}_{1}=\bm{Q}_{1}^{-1}\bm{Q}_{2}\bm{R}_{2}=\bm{V}\bm{R}_{2}
, where
\bm{V}=\bm{Q}_{1}^{-1}\bm{Q}_{2}
is an orthogonal matrix. Expanding this equation gives:

\displaystyle\bm{R}_{1}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}r_{11}&r_{12}&\dots&r_{1n}\\
&r_{22}&\dots&r_{2n}\\
&&\ddots&\vdots\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{5.59721pt}[0.0pt]{\Huge 0}\hfil\lx@intercol&&r_{nn}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}v_{11}&v_{12}&\ldots&v_{1m}\\
v_{21}&v_{22}&\ldots&v_{2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
v_{m1}&v_{m2}&\ldots&v_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}mm}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}s_{11}&s_{12}&\dots&s_{1n}\\
&s_{22}&\dots&s_{2n}\\
&&\ddots&\vdots\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{5.59721pt}[0.0pt]{\Huge 0}\hfil\lx@intercol&&s_{nn}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{V}\bm{R}_{2},

This implies

r_{11}=v_{11}s_{11},\qquad v_{21}=v_{31}=v_{41}=\ldots=v_{m1}=0.

Since
\bm{V}
is an orthogonal matrix and its columns are mutually orthonormal, with the first column having a norm of 1, it follows that
v_{11}=\pm 1
.
By assumption,
r_{ii}>0
and
s_{ii}>0
for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, meaning that
r_{11}>0
and
s_{11}>0
, so
v_{11}
must be positive 1. Since
\bm{V}
is an orthogonal matrix, we also have

v_{12}=v_{13}=v_{14}=\ldots=v_{1m}=0.

By applying this reasoning to the submatrices of
\bm{R}_{1},\bm{V},
and
\bm{R}_{2}
, we find that the upper-left submatrix of
\bm{V}
is the identity:
\bm{V}[1:n,1:n]=\bm{I}_{n}
, indicating
\bm{R}_{1}=\bm{R}_{2}
. This implies
\bm{Q}_{1}[:,1:n]=\bm{Q}_{2}[:,1:n]
and leads to a contradiction such that the reduced QR decomposition is unique.

3.15
LQ, RRLQ, QL, RQ Decomposition

We previously established the existence of the QR decomposition via the Gram-Schmidt process, which is applied to the column space of a matrix
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]\in\real^{m\times n}
.
However, in many applications (see
Schilders 2009
), there is also interest in the row space of a matrix

\bm{B}=[\bm{b}_{1}^{\top};\bm{b}_{2}^{\top};\ldots;\bm{b}_{m}^{\top}]\in\real^{m\times n}
, where
\bm{b}_{i}
is the
i
-th row of
\bm{B}
. The successive spaces spanned by the rows
\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots
of
\bm{B}
are

\mathcal{C}([\bm{b}_{1}])\,\,\,\,\subseteq\,\,\,\,\mathcal{C}([\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2}])\,\,\,\,\subseteq\,\,\,\,\mathcal{C}([\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\bm{b}_{3}])\,\,\,\,\subseteq\,\,\,\,\ldots.

The QR decomposition has a counterpart that characterizes the orthogonal row space. When we perform the QR decomposition on
\bm{B}^{\top}=\bm{Q}_{0}\bm{R}
, we obtain the LQ decomposition of the matrix
\bm{B}=\bm{L}\bm{Q}
, where
\bm{Q}=\bm{Q}_{0}^{\top}
and
\bm{L}=\bm{R}^{\top}
.
The LQ decomposition is helpful in demonstrating the existence of the UTV decomposition in the following chapter.

Any
m\times n
matrix
\bm{B}
(whether it has linearly independent or dependent rows) with
n\geq m
can be decomposed as

\bm{B}=\bm{L}\bm{Q},

where

1.
Reduced
:
\bm{L}
is an
m\times m
lower triangular matrix, and
\bm{Q}
is
m\times n
with orthonormal rows, known as the
reduced LQ decomposition
;

2.
Full
:
\bm{L}
is an
m\times n
lower triangular matrix, and
\bm{Q}
is
n\times n
with orthonormal rows, known as the
full LQ decomposition
. If we further restrict the lower triangular matrix to be square, the full LQ decomposition can be written as:

\bm{B}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{Q},

where
\bm{L}_{0}
is an
m\times m
square lower triangular matrix.

Row-pivoted LQ (RPLQ).

Similar to the column-pivoted QR discussed in Section
, there exists a
row-pivoted LQ (RPLQ)
decomposition for a rank-
r
matrix
\bm{B}\in\real^{m\times n}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \text{Reduced RPLQ: }&\qquad\bm{P}\bm{B}&=&\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{11}\\
\bm{L}_{21}\end{bmatrix}}_{m\times r}\underbrace{\bm{Q}_{r}}_{r\times n};\\
\text{Full RPLQ: }&\qquad\bm{P}\bm{B}&=&\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{L}_{21}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}}_{m\times m}\underbrace{\bm{Q}}_{m\times n},\\
\end{aligned}\right.

where
\bm{L}_{11}\in\real^{r\times r}
is lower triangular,
\bm{Q}_{r}
or
\bm{Q}_{1:r,:}
spans the same row space as
\bm{B}
, and
\bm{P}
is a permutation matrix that interchanges independent rows into the upper-most rows.

Exercise
\thetheo
(QL and RQ Decomposition)

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
with
m\geq n
. Show that there exists a permutation matrix
\bm{P}
such that
\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{Q}\bm{L}
, where
\bm{Q}
is orthogonal and
\bm{L}
is lower triangular.
Similarly, let
\bm{B}\in\real^{m\times n}
with
m\leq n
. Show that there exists a permutation matrix
\bm{P}
such that
\bm{P}\bm{B}=\bm{R}\bm{Q}
, where
\bm{Q}
is orthogonal and
\bm{R}
is upper triangular.

3.16
Two-Sided Orthogonal Decomposition

To this end, an immediate consequence of the CPQR and RPLQ decompositions is the
two-sided orthogonal decomposition
, which simultaneously finds the orthonormal row and column bases of a matrix.

Given a square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
with rank
r
, the full CPQR and RPLQ of
\bm{A}
are given by

\bm{A}\bm{P}_{1}=\bm{Q}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{P}_{2}\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{L}_{21}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{Q}_{2}

respectively. Then, we obtain:

\bm{A}\bm{P}\bm{A}=\bm{Q}_{1}\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}\bm{L}_{11}+\bm{R}_{12}\bm{L}_{21}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}}_{\text{rank $r$}}\bm{Q}_{2},

where the first
r
columns of
\bm{Q}_{1}
span the same column space as
\bm{A}
, the first
r
rows of
\bm{Q}_{2}
span the same row space as
\bm{A}
, and
\bm{P}
is a permutation matrix. This decomposition is called the
two-sided orthogonal decomposition
.

This decomposition exhibits a similarity with the singular value decomposition (SVD):
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
, where the first
r
columns of
\bm{U}
span the same column space as
\bm{A}
and the first
r
columns of
\bm{V}
span the same row space as
\bm{A}
(we shall see in Proposition
). Thus, the two-sided orthogonal decomposition can be considered a computationally inexpensive alternative to the SVD.

Proposition 51
(Four Orthonormal Basis).

Given the two-sided orthogonal decomposition of the matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
with rank
r
:
\bm{A}\bm{P}\bm{A}=\bm{U}\bm{F}\bm{V}^{\top}
, where
\bm{U}=[\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{n}]
and
\bm{V}=[\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{n}]
are the column partitions of
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
, respectively, the following properties hold:

•

\{\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{r}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
;

•

\{\bm{v}_{r+1},\bm{v}_{r+2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
;

•

\{\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{r}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
;

•

\{\bm{u}_{r+1},\bm{u}_{r+2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top})
.

3.17
Rank-One Changes

In Section
, we discussed the rank-one update and downdate of the Cholesky decomposition. Similarly, the rank-one change of a matrix
\bm{A}^{\prime}
of a matrix
\bm{A}
in the QR decomposition is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}+\bm{u}\bm{v}^{\top},

\displaystyle\downarrow

\displaystyle\downarrow

\displaystyle\bm{Q}^{\prime}\bm{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bm{Q}\bm{R}+\bm{u}\bm{v}^{\top},

where the downdate form can be recovered by setting
\bm{A}^{\prime}=\bm{A}-(-\bm{u})\bm{v}^{\top}
. This shows that the update and downdate forms in the QR decomposition are essentially equivalent.
To restate, the rank-one update/downdate problem involves determining the QR decomposition of
\bm{A}^{\prime}
given the QR decomposition of
\bm{A}
.
Let
\bm{w}=\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{u}
, then we have

\bm{A}^{\prime}=\bm{Q}(\bm{R}+\bm{w}\bm{v}^{\top}).

Using the second form from Remark
, which introduces zeros in reverse order, a set of Givens rotations
\bm{G}_{12}\bm{G}_{23}\ldots\bm{G}_{(n-1),n}
, exists such that

\bm{G}_{12}\bm{G}_{23}\ldots\bm{G}_{(n-1),n}\bm{w}=\pm\left\lVert\bm{w}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1},

where
\bm{G}_{(k-1),k}
is the Givens rotation in the plane of indices
k-1
and
k
, zeroing out the
k
-th entry of
\bm{w}
. Applying these rotations to
\bm{R}
, we have

\bm{G}_{12}\bm{G}_{23}\ldots\bm{G}_{(n-1),n}\bm{R}=\bm{H}_{0},

where the Givens rotations in this
reverse order
(
backward rotations
) are useful to transform the upper triangular
\bm{R}
into a “simple”
upper Hessenberg matrix
—a nearly triangular matrix (see Definition
) that we will introduce in the Hessenberg decomposition).
Conversely, using forward rotations to transform
\bm{w}
into
\pm\left\lVert\bm{w}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1}
, as demonstrated in Corollary
, does not yield an upper Hessenberg matrix.
Instead, it results in a full matrix.
For example, considering
\bm{R}\in\real^{5\times 5}
, backward rotations preserve many zeros, simplifying the structure, where
\boxtimes
represents a value that is not necessarily zero, and
boldface
indicates the value has just been changed:

\displaystyle\text{\parbox{70.0001pt}{Backward\\
(Right Way)}: }\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{R}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{45}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{45}\bm{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{34}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{34}\bm{G}_{45}\bm{R}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{23}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{23}\bm{G}_{34}\bm{G}_{45}\bm{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{12}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{12}\bm{G}_{23}\bm{G}_{34}\bm{G}_{45}\bm{R}}.

While forward rotations eliminate these zeros, resulting in a
full matrix
:

\displaystyle\text{\parbox{70.0001pt}{Forward\\
(Wrong Way)}: }\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{R}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{12}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{12}\bm{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{23}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{23}\bm{G}_{12}\bm{R}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{34}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&0&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{34}\bm{G}_{23}\bm{G}_{12}\bm{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{45}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{45}\bm{G}_{34}\bm{G}_{23}\bm{G}_{12}\bm{R}}.

In general, backward rotations yield:

\bm{G}_{12}\bm{G}_{23}\ldots\bm{G}_{(n-1),n}(\bm{R}+\bm{w}\bm{v}^{\top})=\bm{H}_{0}\pm\left\lVert\bm{w}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1}\bm{v}^{\top}=\bm{H},

which is also upper Hessenberg. Subsequently, as in the triangularization process via Givens rotation in Section
, there exist a set of rotations
\bm{J}_{12},\bm{J}_{23},\ldots,\bm{J}_{(n-1),n}
such that

\bm{J}_{(n-1),n}\ldots\bm{J}_{23}\bm{J}_{12}\bm{H}=\bm{R}^{\prime}

is upper triangular. Following the example of a
5\times 5
matrix, the sequence of rotations progressively simplifies the matrix, preserving and introducing zeros step by step:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\bm{H}_{0}\pm\left\lVert\bm{w}\right\rVert\bm{e}_{1}\bm{v}^{\top}}_{\bm{H}}=\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{H}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{J}_{12}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{J}_{12}\bm{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{J}_{23}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{J}_{23}\bm{J}_{12}\bm{H}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{J}_{34}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{J}_{34}\bm{J}_{23}\bm{J}_{12}\bm{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{J}_{45}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{J}_{45}\bm{J}_{34}\bm{J}_{23}\bm{J}_{12}\bm{H}}.

The QR decomposition of
\bm{A}^{\prime}
is thus expressed as:

\bm{A}^{\prime}=\bm{Q}^{\prime}\bm{R}^{\prime},

where

\left\{\begin{aligned} \bm{R}^{\prime}&=(\bm{J}_{(n-1),n}\ldots\bm{J}_{23}\bm{J}_{12})(\bm{G}_{12}\bm{G}_{23}\ldots\bm{G}_{(n-1),n})(\bm{R}+\bm{w}\bm{v}^{\top});\\
\bm{Q}^{\prime}&=\bm{Q}\left\{(\bm{J}_{(n-1),n}\ldots\bm{J}_{23}\bm{J}_{12})(\bm{G}_{12}\bm{G}_{23}\ldots\bm{G}_{(n-1),n})\right\}^{\top}.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(3.13)

3.18
Appending or Deleting a Column

In certain applications, such as the
F
-test for least squares via QR decomposition
(
Lu 2021e
)
, it is often necessary to delete or append a column to the observed matrix. The objective is to compute the QR decomposition of the modified matrix efficiently.

Deleting a column.

Suppose the QR decomposition of
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
is given by
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
, where the columns of
\bm{A}
are partitioned as
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]
. If the
k
-th column of
\bm{A}
is deleted, the resulting matrix is denoted by
\bm{A}^{\prime}=[\bm{a}_{1},\ldots,\bm{a}_{k-1},\bm{a}_{k+1},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]\in\real^{m\times(n-1)}
. Our goal is to efficiently compute the QR decomposition of
\bm{A}^{\prime}
.
The matrix
\bm{R}
can be represented in the following block structure:

\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{ccccc}&\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{a}&\bm{R}_{12}&k-1\\
\bm{R}=&\mathbf{0}&r_{kk}&\bm{b}^{\top}&1\\
&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\bm{R}_{22}&m-k\\
&k-1&1&n-k&\\
\end{array},\\
\end{aligned}\quad\text{such that}\quad\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{b}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{R}_{22}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{H}

is upper Hessenberg.
An illustrative example is presented below, demonstrating the scenario of a
6\times 5
matrix. Here,
k=3
, and the column corresponding to
k
has been removed:

\displaystyle\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{R}=\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{H}=\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\prime}}.

To transform
\bm{H}
into a triangular matrix, we apply a sequence of Givens rotations
\bm{G}_{k,k+1}
,
\bm{G}_{k+1,k+2}
,
\ldots
,
\bm{G}_{n-1,n}
to eliminate specific off-diagonal entries
h_{k+1,k}
,
h_{k+2,k+1}
,
\ldots
,
h_{n,n-1}
of
\bm{H}
.The resulting triangular matrix
\bm{R}^{\prime}
is computed as:

\bm{R}^{\prime}=\bm{G}_{n-1,n}\ldots\bm{G}_{k+1,k+2}\bm{G}_{k,k+1}\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\prime}.

The updated orthogonal matrix is:

\bm{Q}^{\prime}=(\bm{G}_{n-1,n}\ldots\bm{G}_{k+1,k+2}\bm{G}_{k,k+1}\bm{Q}^{\top})^{\top}=\bm{Q}\bm{G}_{k,k+1}^{\top}\bm{G}_{k+1,k+2}^{\top}\ldots\bm{G}_{n-1,n}^{\top},

(3.14)

such that
\bm{A}^{\prime}=\bm{Q}^{\prime}\bm{R}^{\prime}
.
The
6\times 5
example is shown as follows, where
\boxtimes
represents a value that is not necessarily zero, and
boldface
indicates the value has just been changed:

\displaystyle\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{R}=\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k=3}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{H}=\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{34}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{34}\bm{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{G}_{45}}}{{\rightarrow}}\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&\bm{0}\\
0&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{G}_{45}\bm{G}_{34}\bm{H}}.

Appending a column.

Similarly, consider the case where a vector
\bm{w}
is appended as the
(k+1)
-th column of
\bm{A}
, resulting in
\widetilde{\bm{A}}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{k},\bm{w},\bm{a}_{k+1},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]
.
The goal is to efficiently compute the QR decomposition of
\widetilde{\bm{A}}
.
Applying the orthogonal transformation
\bm{Q}^{\top}
, we have

\bm{Q}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{A}}=[\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{a}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{a}_{k},\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{w},\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{a}_{k+1},\ldots,\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{a}_{n}]=\widetilde{\bm{H}}.

Next, a sequence of Givens rotations
\bm{J}_{m-1,m},\bm{J}_{m-2,m-1},\ldots,\bm{J}_{k+1,k+2}
can be applied to zero out the elements
\widetilde{h}_{m,k+1}
,
\widetilde{h}_{m-1,k+1}
,
\ldots
,
\widetilde{h}_{k+2,k+1}
in
\widetilde{\bm{H}}
, transforming it into an upper triangular matrix:

\widetilde{\bm{R}}=\bm{J}_{k+1,k+2}\ldots\bm{J}_{m-2,m-1}\bm{J}_{m-1,m}\bm{Q}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{A}}.

To illustrate, suppose
\widetilde{\bm{H}}
is a
6\times 5
matrix, and
k=2
. Below is an example where
\boxtimes
represents a value that is not necessarily zero, and
boldface
indicates the value has just been changed:

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&0&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&0&0\\
0&0&\boxtimes&0&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\widetilde{\bm{H}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{J}_{56}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&0&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&0&0\\
0&0&\bm{0}&0&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{J}_{56}\widetilde{\bm{H}}\rightarrow\widetilde{h}_{63}=0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{J}_{45}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&0&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\bm{0}&0&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{J}_{45}\bm{J}_{56}\widetilde{\bm{H}}\rightarrow\widetilde{h}_{53}=0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{J}_{34}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&0&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{J}_{34}\bm{J}_{45}\bm{J}_{56}\widetilde{\bm{H}}\rightarrow\widetilde{h}_{43}=0}.

Finally, the orthogonal matrix is computed as

\widetilde{\bm{Q}}=(\bm{J}_{k+1,k+2}\ldots\bm{J}_{m-2,m-1}\bm{J}_{m-1,m}\bm{Q}^{\top})^{\top}=\bm{Q}\bm{J}_{m-1,m}^{\top}\bm{J}_{m-2,m-1}^{\top}\ldots\bm{J}_{k+1,k+2}^{\top},

(3.15)

such that
\widetilde{\bm{A}}=\widetilde{\bm{Q}}\widetilde{\bm{R}}
.

Real world application.

This method is particularly valuable for efficient variable selection in least squares problems using QR decomposition. At each step, a column of the data matrix
\bm{A}
is removed, and an
F
-test is performed to evaluate the significance of the corresponding variable. Insignificant variables are eliminated, simplifying the model and improving interpretability
(
Lu 2021e
)
.

3.19
Appending or Deleting a Row

Similarly, we may need to append or delete a row from the observed matrix. The goal is to efficiently compute the QR decomposition of the updated matrix.

Appending a row.

Suppose the full QR decomposition of
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
is given by
\bm{A}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{1}\\
\bm{A}_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{A}_{1}\in\real^{k\times n}
and
\bm{A}_{2}\in\real^{(m-k)\times n}
. Now, if we append a row, the resulting matrix is
\bm{A}^{\prime}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{1}\\
\bm{w}^{\top}\\
\bm{A}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\in\real^{(m+1)\times n}
. The objective is to find the full QR decomposition of
\bm{A}^{\prime}
efficiently. To do this, we construct a permutation matrix

\bm{P}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&1&\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{I}_{k}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\bm{I}_{m-k}\end{bmatrix}\longrightarrow\bm{P}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{1}\\
\bm{w}^{\top}\\
\bm{A}_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{w}^{\top}\\
\bm{A}_{1}\\
\bm{A}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\quad\implies\quad\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\bm{P}\bm{A}^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{w}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{H}

is upper Hessenberg. Similarly, a set of rotations
\bm{G}_{12},\bm{G}_{23},\ldots,\bm{G}_{n,n+1}
can be applied to introduce zeros in the elements
h_{21}
,
h_{32}
,
\ldots
,
h_{n+1,n}
of
\bm{H}
. The triangular matrix
\bm{R}^{\prime}
is given by

\bm{R}^{\prime}=\bm{G}_{n,n+1}\ldots\bm{G}_{23}\bm{G}_{12}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\bm{P}\bm{A}^{\prime}.

The updated orthogonal matrix is computed as

\bm{Q}^{\prime}=\left(\bm{G}_{n,n+1}\ldots\bm{G}_{23}\bm{G}_{12}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\bm{P}\right)^{\top}=\bm{P}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}\end{bmatrix}\bm{G}_{12}^{\top}\bm{G}_{23}^{\top}\ldots\bm{G}_{n,n+1}^{\top},

such that
\bm{A}^{\prime}=\bm{Q}^{\prime}\bm{R}^{\prime}
is the QR decomposition of
\bm{A}^{\prime}
.

Deleting a row.

Suppose
\bm{A}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{1}\\
\bm{w}^{\top}\\
\bm{A}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\in\real^{m\times n}
, where
\bm{A}_{1}\in\real^{k\times n}
,
\bm{A}_{2}\in\real^{(m-k-1)\times n}
, and the full QR decomposition given by
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{Q}\in\real^{m\times m}
is orthogonal, and
\bm{R}\in\real^{m\times n}
is upper triangular. We want to compute the full QR decomposition of
\widetilde{\bm{A}}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{1}\\
\bm{A}_{2}\end{bmatrix}
efficiently (assuming
m-1\geq n
). Analogously, to achieve this, construct a permutation matrix
\bm{P}
as follows:

\bm{P}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&1&\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{I}_{k}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\bm{I}_{m-k-1}\end{bmatrix}\implies\bm{P}\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&1&\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{I}_{k}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\bm{I}_{m-k-1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{1}\\
\bm{w}^{\top}\\
\bm{A}_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{w}^{\top}\\
\bm{A}_{1}\\
\bm{A}_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{P}\bm{Q}\bm{R}=\bm{M}\bm{R},

where
\bm{M}=\bm{P}\bm{Q}
is an orthogonal matrix. Let
\bm{m}^{\top}
denote the first row of
\bm{M}
. A series of Givens rotations,
\bm{G}_{m-1,m},\bm{G}_{m-2,m-1},\ldots,\bm{G}_{1,2}
, can be applied to zero out the elements
m_{m},m_{m-1},\ldots,m_{2}
of
\bm{m}
, resulting in
\bm{G}_{1,2}\ldots\bm{G}_{m-2,m-1}\bm{G}_{m-1,m}\bm{m}=\alpha\bm{e}_{1}
, where
\alpha=\pm 1
. Consequently,

\bm{G}_{1,2}\ldots\bm{G}_{m-2,m-1}\bm{G}_{m-1,m}\bm{R}=\begin{array}[]{cc}\bm{v}^{\top}&1\\
\bm{R}_{1}&m-1\\
\end{array},$$whichisupperHessenbergwith$\bm{R}_1\in\real^{(m-1)\times n}$beinguppertriangular.And$$\bm{M}\bm{G}_{m-1,m}^{\top}\bm{G}_{m-2,m-1}^{\top}\ldots\bm{G}_{1,2}^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}_{1}\end{bmatrix},$$where$\bm{Q}_1\in\real^{(m-1)\times(m-1)}$isanorthogonalmatrix.Thebottom-leftblockoftheabovematrixisazerovectorbecause$\alpha=\pm 1$and$\bm{M}$isorthogonal.Toseethis,let$\bm{G}=\bm{G}_{m-1,m}^\top\bm{G}_{m-2,m-1}^\top$$\ldots\bm{G}_{1,2}^\top$,withitsfirstcolumndenotedas$\bm{g}$.Writing$\bm{M}$astherowpartition$\bm{M}= [\bm{m}^\top; \bm{m}_2^\top; \bm{m}_3^\top; \ldots, \bm{m}_{m}^\top]$,wehave$$\begin{aligned} \bm{m}^{\top}\bm{g}&=\pm 1\qquad\rightarrow\qquad\bm{g}=\pm\bm{m},\\
\bm{m}_{i}^{\top}\bm{m}&=0,\qquad\forall\,i\in\{2,3,\ldots,m\}.\end{aligned}$$Thus,wecanwrite:$$\begin{aligned} \bm{P}\bm{A}&=\bm{M}\bm{R}=(\bm{M}\bm{G}_{m-1,m}^{\top}\bm{G}_{m-2,m-1}^{\top}\ldots\bm{G}_{1,2}\top)(\bm{G}_{1,2}\ldots\bm{G}_{m-2,m-1}\bm{G}_{m-1,m}\bm{R})\\
&=\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}_{1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\alpha\bm{v}^{\top}\\
\bm{Q}_{1}\bm{R}_{1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{w}^{\top}\\
\widetilde{\bm{A}}\end{bmatrix}.\end{aligned}$$Thisshowsthat$\bm{Q}_1\bm{R}_1$isthefullQRdecompositionof$\widetilde{\bm{A}}=\footnotesize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{1}\\
\bm{A}_{2}
\end{bmatrix}$.\par\par\par\par\par\vskip 10.0pt\begin{center}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Chapter\penalty\ 3\penalty\ \penalty\ Problems}\markright{\color[rgb]{0.2344,0.4414,0.7188}\Large{\char 26\relax}\penalty\ Chapter\penalty\ 3\penalty\ \penalty\ Problems\penalty\ {\char 54\relax}}
\end{center}\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@{Adjugate of orthogonal.} Let $\bm{Q}\in\real^{n\times n}$ be orthogonal. Show that $\mathrm{adj}(\bm{Q})=\det(\bm{Q})\bm{Q}^{\top}$ such that $\mathrm{adj}(\bm{Q})$ is also orthogonal (Definition\penalty\ \ref{definition:adjugate}).
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Prove that if $\bm{A}$ is triangular and orthogonal, then $\bm{A}$ must be diagonal.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Let $\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}$ be skew-symmetric ($\bm{A}^{\top}=-\bm{A}$). Show that the matrix $(\bm{I}-\bm{A})^{-1}(\bm{I}+\bm{A})$ is orthogonal.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Let $\bm{u}$ and $\bm{v}$ be two orthogonal unit vectors. Show that $\bm{u}+\bm{v}$ is orthogonal to $\bm{u}-\bm{v}$.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@{Reflector.} Let $\bm{u}\in\real^{n}$ and $\bm{v}\in\real^{n}$ be two orthogonal vectors (not necessarily unit), where $\bm{u}\in\mathcal{V}$ and $\bm{v}\in\mathcal{V}^{\perp}$. Define $\bm{a}=\bm{u}+\bm{v}$ and $\bm{b}=\bm{u}-\bm{v}$. Show that there exists a unique Householder reflector $\bm{H}\in\real^{n\times n}$ (Definition\penalty\ \ref{definition:householder-reflector}) such that $\bm{H}\bm{a}=\bm{b}$.
Moreover, if $\mathcal{V}=\{\bm{w}\}^{\perp}$, show that $\bm{H}=\bm{I}-2\frac{\bm{w}\bm{w}^{\top}}{\bm{w}^{\top}\bm{w}}$.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Let $\bm{u}=[-\sin(\theta),\cos(\theta)]$ be a unit vector. Show that the Householder reflector determined by $\bm{u}$ is $\bm{H}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\cos(2\theta)&\sin(2\theta)\\
\sin(2\theta)&-\cos(2\theta)\end{bmatrix}$.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Let $\bm{Q},\bm{U}\in\real^{m\times n}$ be two semi-orthogonal matrices with $m\geq n$. Show that $\bm{Q}$ and $\bm{U}$ have the same column space if and only if there exists an orthogonal matrix $\bm{P}\in\real^{n\times n}$ such that $\bm{Q}=\bm{U}\bm{P}$.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Let $\bm{Q},\bm{U}\in\real^{n\times n}$ be orthogonal. Show that there exists an orthogonal matrix $\bm{P}$ such that $\bm{Q}=\bm{P}\bm{U}$.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Let $\bm{Q},\bm{U}\in\real^{m\times n}$ be two semi-orthogonal matrices with $m\geq n$. Show that there exists an orthogonal matrix $\bm{P}\in\real^{m\times m}$ such that $\bm{Q}=\bm{P}\bm{U}$. Compare this result to Problems\penalty\ \ref{prob:semi_eq1} and \ref{prob:semi_eq2}. {Hint: Complete the semi-orthogonal matrices into $m\times m$ orthogonal matrices.}
\par\par\par\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Let $\bm{A}$ admit QR decomposition $\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}$. Show that $\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}$ is normal if and only if $\bm{R}\bm{Q}$ is normal.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Consider the partition of an orthogonal matrix
$$
\bm{Q}= \begin{bmatrix}\underset{p\times p}{\bm{A}} &\underset{p\times q}{\bm{B}} \\
\underset{q\times p}{\bm{C}} &\underset{q\times q}{\bm{D}}
\end{bmatrix}\in\real^{n\times n}.
$$
Show that $\mathrm{rank}(\bm{B})=\mathrm{rank}(\bm{C})$ and $\mathrm{rank}(\bm{D})=n+\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})-2p$.
\par\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Consider the rank of matrices:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@Suppose matrices $\bm{A}$ and $\bm{B}$ have full column ranks. Show that $\bm{A}\bm{B}$ has full column rank.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Suppose $\bm{A}\bm{B}$ has full column ranks. Show that $\bm{B}$ also has full column rank, but $\bm{A}$ may not necessarily have full column rank.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Discuss the rank of the upper triangular matrices obtained from the QR decompositions of $\bm{A}\bm{B}$, $\bm{A}$, and $\bm{B}$ in various cases of the matrices involved.
\end{itemize}\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@In Theorem\penalty\ \ref{theorem:qr-decomposition}, we stated that
$\bm{R}$ is nonsingular in the reduced QR decomposition when
$\bm{A}$ has full column rank $n$.
Suppose $\bm{A}$ does not have full column rank. Examine the relationship between the rank of $\bm{A}$ and the number of nonzero entries in $\bm{R}$.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Use the Gram-Schmidt process, Householder transformations, Givens rotations to find an orthonormal basis for the space spanned by the vectors
$$
\begin{aligned} \bm{v}_{1} &= [1,3,7,5]^{\top}, \,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{v}_{2} &= [6,3,6,3]^{\top}, \,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{v}_{3} &= [5,2,7,4]^{\top}.
\end{aligned}
$$
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@{Distance between a vector and a hyperplane.} Given a nonzero vector $\mathbf{0}\neq\bm{a}\in\real^{n}$ and a scalar $\beta$, define the hyperplane $H(\bm{a},\beta)=\{\bm{x}\in\real^{n}:\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{x}+\beta=0\}$. For any $\bm{y}\in\real^{n}$, use the projection along a line (see Section\penalty\ \ref{section:project-onto-a-vector}) to show that the distance between $\bm{y}$ $H(\bm{a},\beta)$ is given by
\begin{equation}d(\bm{y},H(\bm{a},\beta))=\frac{\left\lvert\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{y}+\beta\right\rvert}{\left\lVert\bm{a}\right\rVert_{2}}.\end{equation}{Hint: Choose two random points on the plane and first show that $\bm{a}$ is orthogonal to the plane.}
\par\par\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Although we have used the fact that every orthogonal (or orthonormal) list of vectors is linearly independent throughout our discussions, provide a rigorous proof of this claim. {Hint: Assume the vectors are linearly dependent and derive a contradiction.}
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Let $\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}$ be given with $m\geq n$. Provide an algorithm using Householder reflectors to compute an orthogonal matrix $\bm{Q}\in\real^{m\times m}$ such that $\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{L}$, where $\bm{L}[1:n,1:n]$ is lower triangular and $\bm{L}[n+1:m,:]=\mathbf{0}$.
\par\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Let $\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}$ with rank $r$. Show that $\bm{A}$ is range-symmetric (i.e., $\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})=\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})$) if and only if there exist a nonsingular matrix $\bm{S}\in\real^{n\times n}$ and a nonsingular matrix $\bm{M}\in\real^{r\times r}$ such that
$$
\bm{A}=\bm{S}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{M}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}
\bm{S}^\top.
$$
{Hint: Consider the QR decomposition of $\bm{S}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}$.}
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Let $\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}$ be skew-symmetric (i.e., $\bm{A}^{\top}=-\bm{A}$). Show that $\bm{I}+\bm{A}$ is nonsingular, $\bm{B}=(\bm{I}-\bm{A})(\bm{I}+\bm{A})^{-1}$ is orthogonal, $\bm{I}+\bm{B}=2(\bm{I}+\bm{A})^{-1}$, and $\det(\bm{B})=1$.
\par\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Prove that the following statements about a square matrix $\bm{Q}\in\real^{n\times n}$ are equivalent:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$\bm{Q}$ is orthogonal.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\bm{Q}^{\top}$ is orthogonal.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\bm{Q}$ is nonsingular and $\bm{Q}^{\top}=\bm{Q}^{-1}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@The rows of $\bm{Q}$ are orthogonormal.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@The columns of $\bm{Q}$ are orthonormal.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@For all $\bm{x}\in\real^{n}$, it follows that $\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert\bm{Q}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}$.
\end{itemize}\par\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@{Orthogonal preservation.} Let $\bm{Q}\in\real^{n\times n}$ be orthogonal. Show that $\bm{x},\bm{y}\in\real^{n}$ are orthogonal if and only if $\bm{Q}\bm{x}$ and $\bm{Q}\bm{y}$ are orthogonal.
\par\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@{Orthogonal preservation.} Let $\bm{Q}\in\real^{n\times n}$ be orthogonal, and let $\lambda$ be an eigenvalue of $\bm{Q}$. Show that $\lambda=\pm 1$, and $\bm{x}\in\real^{n}$ is a (right) eigenvector of $\bm{Q}$ associated with $\lambda$ if and only if $\bm{x}$ is a left eigenvector
of $\bm{Q}$ associated with $\lambda$.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Use the results from Problems\penalty\ \ref{prob:compl1} and \ref{prob:compl2} to determine the computational complexity of QR decomposition using the CGS, MGS, Householder,and Givens approaches.
\par\end{enumerate}\par\par

Chapter 4
UTV Decomposition: ULV and URV Decomposition

4.1
UTV Decomposition

The UTV decomposition generalizes the QR factorization of a matrix
\bm{A}
into two orthogonal matrices,
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
, and a (upper or lower) triangular matrix
\bm{T}
, such that
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\bm{T}\bm{V}
.
The triangular matrix
\bm{T}
supports rank estimation.
The decomposition takes different names: if
\bm{T}
is lower triangular, it is called the
ULV decomposition
; if
\bm{T}
is upper triangular, it is known as the URV decomposition.
The UTV decomposition framework resembles the singular value decomposition (SVD; see Chapter
) in structure and serves as a computationally efficient alternative to the SVD. Both methods can be applied to find the least squares solution for rank-deficient matrices (Theorem
).

Any
m\times n
matrix
\bm{A}
with rank
r
can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{U}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{V},

where
\bm{U}\in\real^{m\times m}
and
\bm{V}\in\real^{n\times n}
are orthogonal matrices, and
\bm{L}\in\real^{r\times r}
is a lower triangular matrix with full rank.

The existence of the ULV decomposition follows from those of the QR and LQ decomposition.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
For any rank-
r
matrix
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]
, a column permutation matrix
\bm{P}
(Definition
) can be used to reorder the columns of
\bm{A}
, placing its linearly independent columns in the first
r
positions of
\bm{A}\bm{P}
.
Without loss of generality, let
\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{r}
denote the
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
. Then,

\bm{A}\bm{P}=[\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{r},\bm{b}_{r+1},\ldots,\bm{b}_{n}].

Define
\bm{Z}=[\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{r}]\in\real^{m\times r}
. Since each
\bm{b}_{i}
lies in the column space of
\bm{Z}
, there exists a matrix
\bm{E}\in\real^{r\times(n-r)}
such that

[\bm{b}_{r+1},\bm{b}_{r+2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{n}]=\bm{Z}\bm{E}.

Consequently,

\bm{A}\bm{P}=[\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{r},\bm{b}_{r+1},\ldots,\bm{b}_{n}]=\bm{Z}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{I}_{r}&\bm{E}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{I}_{r}
is the
r\times r
identity matrix. Additionally, the matrix
\bm{Z}\in\real^{m\times r}
has full column rank, allowing its full QR decomposition:
\bm{Z}=\bm{U}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}
, where
\bm{R}\in\real^{r\times r}
is an upper triangular matrix with full rank, and
\bm{U}
is an orthogonal matrix. Substituting this into the previous expression gives:

\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{Z}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{I}_{r}&\bm{E}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{U}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{I}_{r}&\bm{E}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{U}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}&\bm{R}\bm{E}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}.

(4.1)

Since
\bm{R}
has full rank,

\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}&\bm{R}\bm{E}\end{bmatrix}
also has full rank. Its full LQ decomposition is given by:

\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{V}_{0}
, where
\bm{L}\in\real^{r\times r}
is a lower triangular matrix, and
\bm{V}_{0}
is an orthogonal matrix. Substitute this into Equation (
), we have

\bm{A}=\bm{U}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{V}_{0}\bm{P}^{-1}.

Finally, let
\bm{V}=\bm{V}_{0}\bm{P}^{-1}
, which is orthogonal since it is a product of two orthogonal matrices. This completes the proof.

An alternative proof of the ULV decomposition will be discussed in Theorem
using the rank-revealing QR decomposition and the standard QR decomposition.

Now, suppose the ULV decomposition of a matrix
\bm{A}
is

\bm{A}=\bm{U}\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\normalsize\bm{V}
.
Let
\bm{U}_{0}=\bm{U}_{:,1:r}
and
\bm{V}_{0}=\bm{V}_{1:r,:}
, where
\bm{U}_{0}
consists of the first
r
columns of
\bm{U}
, and
\bm{V}_{0}
consists of the first
r
rows of
\bm{V}
. Then,
\bm{A}=\bm{U}_{0}\bm{L}\bm{V}_{0}
. This form is called the
reduced ULV decomposition
.
Similarly, the URV decomposition can be derived analogously.

Any
m\times n
matrix
\bm{A}
with rank
r
can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{U}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{V},

where
\bm{U}\in\real^{m\times m}
and
\bm{V}\in\real^{n\times n}
are two orthogonal matrices, and
\bm{R}\in\real^{r\times r}
is an upper triangular matrix with full rank.

The proof closely resembles that of the ULV decomposition, so we omit the details here.
Additionally, there is a version of the reduced URV decomposition, with the distinction between the full and reduced URV being implied by context.
Collectively, the ULV and URV decompositions are referred to as the UTV decomposition framework
(
Fierro and Hansen 1997
;
Golub and Van Loan 2013
)
.

We will soon observe that the structures of ULV and URV bear a strong resemblance to the singular value decomposition (SVD).
All three decompositions factorize the matrix
\bm{A}
into two orthogonal matrices.
Specifically, ULV and URV provide bases for the four fundamental subspaces of
\bm{A}
as described in the fundamental theorem of linear algebra.
For example, in the ULV decomposition, the first
r
columns of
\bm{U}
form an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
, while the last
(m-r)
columns of
\bm{U}
form an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top})
. Similarly, the first
r
rows of
\bm{V}
form an orthonormal basis for the row space
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
, while the last
(n-r)
rows provide an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
(resembling the two-sided orthogonal decomposition; Theorem
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{span}\{\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{r}\},\qquad\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{span}\{\bm{v}_{r+1},\bm{v}_{r+2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{n}\},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{span}\{\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{r}\},\qquad\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{span}\{\bm{u}_{r+1},\bm{u}_{r+2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{m}\}.

The SVD extends this framework by establishing direct connections between the two pairs of orthonormal bases. It describes transformations between the column space and row space, as well as between the left null space and right null space. These relationships will be discussed in greater detail in the SVD section.

4.2
Complete Orthogonal Decomposition

The UTV decomposition is closely related to the concept of the
complete orthogonal decomposition
, which also involves factoring a matrix into two orthogonal matrices.

Any
m\times n
matrix
\bm{A}
with rank
r
can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{U}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{T}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{V},

where
\bm{U}\in\real^{m\times m}
and
\bm{V}\in\real^{n\times n}
are two orthogonal matrices, and
\bm{T}\in\real^{r\times r}
is a rank-
r
matrix.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Using the column-pivoted QR decomposition (Theorem
), the matrix
\bm{A}
can be decomposed as

\bm{Q}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{R}_{11}\in\real^{r\times r}
is upper triangular,
\bm{R}_{12}\in\real^{r\times(n-r)}
,
\bm{Q}_{1}\in\real^{m\times m}
is an orthogonal matrix, and
\bm{P}
is a permutation matrix. Next, we construct a decomposition that satisfies:

\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}_{12}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{Q}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{S}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix},

(4.3)

where
\bm{Q}_{2}
is an orthogonal matrix, and
\bm{S}
is a rank-
r
matrix. This decomposition is valid because the matrix
\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}_{12}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\in\real^{n\times r}
has rank
r
of which the columns stay in a subspace of
n
. Nevertheless, the columns of
\bm{Q}_{2}
span the entire space
n
, where we can assume that the first
r
columns of
\bm{Q}_{2}
span the same space as that of
\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}_{12}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}
. The matrix
\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{S}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}
serves to map
\bm{Q}_{2}
back to
\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}_{12}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}
.
Finally, substituting this decomposition, we find:

\bm{Q}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}\bm{Q}_{2}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{S}^{\top}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}.

Setting
\bm{U}=\bm{Q}_{1}
,
\bm{V}=\bm{Q}_{2}^{\top}\bm{P}^{\top}
, and
\bm{T}=\bm{S}^{\top}
, we complete the proof.

When Equation (
) is interpreted as the reduced QR decomposition of
\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}^{\top}\\
\bm{R}_{12}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}
, the complete orthogonal decomposition simplifies to the ULV decomposition.

4.3
Application: Row Rank equals Column Rank via UTV

The UTV framework enables the proof of a fundamental theorem in linear algebra: the equality of the row rank and column rank of a matrix.
It is worth noting that when using the UTV decomposition for this proof, a slight adjustment is needed in framing the existence claim.
For instance, in Theorem
, the initial assumption about the matrix
\bm{A}
is that its rank is
r
. Since having rank
r
inherently implies the equality of row rank and column rank, a more precise assertion in this context would be to state that
\bm{A}
has a
column rank
of
r
in Theorem
.
For further details, refer to
Lu 2021b
.

Proof
[of Theorem
, the Second Way]
Any
m\times n
matrix
\bm{A}
with rank
r
can be factored as

\bm{A}=\bm{U}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{V}_{0},

where
\bm{U}_{0}\in\real^{m\times m}
and
\bm{V}_{0}\in\real^{n\times n}
are two orthogonal matrices, and
\bm{L}\in\real^{r\times r}
is a lower triangular matrix . Let
\bm{D}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}
. Clearly, the row rank and column rank of
\bm{D}
are equal. If we can show that the column rank and row rank of
\bm{A}
are equal to those of
\bm{D}
, the proof is complete.

Let
\bm{U}=\bm{U}_{0}^{\top}
and
\bm{V}=\bm{V}_{0}^{\top}
. Then,
\bm{D}=\bm{U}\bm{A}\bm{V}
. The proof can be broken into two parts: first prove that the row rank and column rank of
\bm{A}
are equal to those of
\bm{U}\bm{A}
; then prove that the row rank and column rank of
\bm{U}\bm{A}
are equal to those of
\bm{U}\bm{A}\bm{V}
.

Row and column rank of
\bm{A}
equal those of
\bm{U}\bm{A}
.

Let
\bm{B}=\bm{U}\bm{A}
. Partition
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
by columns:
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]
and
\bm{B}=[\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{n}]
, where
\bm{b}_{i}=\bm{U}\bm{a}_{i}
for all
i
. If a linear combination
x_{1}\bm{a}_{1}+x_{2}\bm{a}_{2}+\ldots+x_{n}\bm{a}_{n}=\mathbf{0}
, then premultiplying by
\bm{U}
gives

\bm{U}(x_{1}\bm{a}_{1}+x_{2}\bm{a}_{2}+\ldots+x_{n}\bm{a}_{n})=x_{1}\bm{b}_{1}+x_{2}\bm{b}_{2}+\ldots+x_{n}\bm{b}_{n}=\mathbf{0}.

Thus, any independent subset of columns in
\bm{A}
corresponds to an independent subset of columns in
\bm{B}
, implying:

\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{B}))\leq\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})).

Similarly, since
\bm{A}=\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{B}
, we have

\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}))\leq\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{B})).

This proves

\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{B}))=\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})).

Applying the same reasoning to
\bm{B}^{\top}
and
\bm{A}^{\top}
shows that

\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{B}^{\top}))=\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})).

This implies the row rank and column rank of
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}=\bm{U}\bm{A}
are the same.

Row and column rank of
\bm{U}\bm{A}
equal those of
\bm{U}\bm{A}\bm{V}
.

Using the same reasoning as above, applied to
\bm{U}\bm{A}
and
\bm{U}\bm{A}\bm{V}
, we conclude that their row and column ranks are equal.

æ

\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
to derive the UTV decomposition of
\mathrm{diag}(\bm{A},\bm{B})
.

Prove rigorously that the four subspaces described in Equation (
) can be spanned using the UTV decomposition.

4.23.

Let
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\bm{B}\bm{V}
be given, where
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
are orthogonal matrices. Prove that
\sum_{i,j}\left\lvert a_{ij}\right\rvert^{2}=\sum_{i,j}\left\lvert b_{ij}\right\rvert^{2}
.

Hint: Verify this by showing that
\mathrm{tr}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})=\mathrm{tr}(\bm{B}^{\top}\bm{B})
.

4.23.

Explain how to compute the UTV decomposition using either Householder reflectors or Givens rotations. Provide insights into the advantages and limitations of each approach.

4.23.

.

4.23.

Let
\bm{A},\bm{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
be given, where
\bm{B}
is nonsingular. Prove that there exist unitary matrices
\bm{U},\bm{V}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
such that
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\bm{T}_{A}\bm{V}
and
\bm{B}=\bm{U}\bm{T}_{B}\bm{V}
, where
\bm{T}_{A}
and
\bm{T}_{B}
are upper triangular matrices. Furthermore, show that the main diagonal entries of
\bm{T}_{B}^{-1}\bm{T}_{A}
are the eigenvalues
\bm{B}^{-1}\bm{A}
.
When these eigenvalues are real, show that all the underlying matrices can be chosen to be real, and
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
are orthogonal.
