(Theorem
) and the QR decomposition for
\bm{B}\bm{U}
.

Part III
Data Interpretation and Information Distillation

\nopagecolor

Chapter 5
CR Decomposition

5.1
CR Decomposition

The CR decomposition of a matrix, introduced in
Strang 2021
;
Strang and Moler 2022
, provides insights into the matrix’s rank and the relationships between its columns and rows. As is customary, we first present the result, deferring the discussion of its existence and derivation to subsequent sections.

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be a matrix with rank-
r
. Then it can be factored as

\underset{m\times n}{\bm{A}}=\underset{m\times r}{\bm{C}}\,\,\,\underset{r\times n}{\bm{R}},

where
\bm{C}
consists of the first
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
, and
\bm{R}
is an
r\times n
matrix that reconstructs the columns of
\bm{A}
from
\bm{C}
. Specifically,
\bm{R}
corresponds to the
reduced row echelon form (RREF)
of
\bm{A}
, excluding zero rows.

The storage required for this decomposition changes from
mn
floating-point numbers to
r(m+n)
floating-point numbers, which may reduce or, in some cases, increase the memory requirements.

The CR decomposition offers several key benefits: It highlights the independent columns and rows of the matrix, which are essential for understanding its rank and the column and row spaces.
For large matrices, the decomposition can approximate the matrix by retaining only the most significant columns and rows, reducing computational complexity.
The CR decomposition can simplify solving linear systems
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{b}
by transforming the problem into a more manageable form using
\bm{C}
and
\bm{R}
.
Since both
\bm{C}
and
\bm{R}
have full rank, the decomposition provides an efficient method to compute the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse:
\bm{A}^{+}=\bm{R}^{+}\bm{C}^{+}
, where
\bm{R}^{+}
and
\bm{C}^{+}
are the pseudo-inverses of
\bm{R}
and
\bm{C}
, respectively (see Problem
).
The CR decomposition is also useful for analyzing the incidence matrix of a graph. It aids in studying conserved quantities, such as current flow in electrical circuits, as described by Kirchhoff’s Current Law
(
Strang and Drucker 2021
)
.

5.2
Existence of CR Decomposition

Given that the matrix
\bm{A}
has rank of
r
, it contains
r
linearly independent columns. These columns can be identified and included in the matrix
\bm{C}
as follows:

•

If column 1 of
\bm{A}
is nonzero, include it in the columns of
\bm{C}
.

•

If column 2 of
\bm{A}
is not a scalar multiple of column 1, include it in the columns of
\bm{C}
.

•

If column 3 of
\bm{A}
is not a linear combination of columns 1 and 2, include it in the columns of
\bm{C}
.

•

Repeat this process until
r
linearly independent columns have been identified (or until all linearly independent columns have been selected, if
r
is unknown in advance).

Once
r
linearly independent columns are extracted from
\bm{A}
, the CR decomposition can be established by interpreting matrix multiplication within the column space.

Matrix multiplication in the column space.

The multiplication of two matrices,
\bm{D}\in\real^{m\times k}
and
\bm{E}\in\real^{k\times n}
, results in the matrix
\bm{A}=\bm{D}\bm{E}
. This can be expressed as
\bm{A}=\bm{D}[\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2},\ldots,\bm{e}_{n}]=[\bm{D}\bm{e}_{1},\bm{D}\bm{e}_{2},\ldots,\bm{D}\bm{e}_{n}]
.
In this interpretation, each column of
\bm{A}
is a linear combination of the columns of
\bm{D}
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Since
\bm{A}
has rank
r
and
\bm{C}
is constructed from
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
, the column space of
\bm{C}
is equivalent to that of
\bm{A}
.
Any other column
\bm{a}_{i}
of
\bm{A}
can be represented as a linear combination of the columns of
\bm{C}
, i.e., there exists a vector
\bm{r}_{i}
such that
\bm{a}_{i}=\bm{C}\bm{r}_{i}
,
\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. By arranging these vectors
\bm{r}_{i}
’s as the columns of a matrix
\bm{R}
, we obtain

\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]=[\bm{C}\bm{r}_{1},\bm{C}\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{C}\bm{r}_{n}]=\bm{C}\bm{R}.

Thus, the decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{R}
is established, completing the proof.

5.3
Reduced Row Echelon Form (RREF)

In Section
on Gaussian elimination, we introduced the elimination matrix (a lower triangular matrix; see (
)) and the permutation matrix to facilitate transforming
\bm{A}
into an upper triangular form.
Let us now revisit the Gaussian elimination process for a
4\times 4
square matrix, where
\boxtimes
represents a value that is not necessarily zero, and
boldface
indicates the value has just been changed:

\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{P}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{P}_{1}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\boxtimes}&\boxtimes\\
0&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{\boxtimes}}\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{2}\bm{P}_{1}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}.

Moreover, Gaussian elimination can also be extended to rectangular matrices. Below, we demonstrate the process for a
4\times 5
matrix:

\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}2}&\boxtimes&10&9&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes&\boxtimes\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}2}&\boxtimes&10&9&\boxtimes\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{5}}&\bm{6}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{2}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}2}&\boxtimes&10&9&\boxtimes\\
0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}5}&6&\boxtimes\\
0&0&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{3}}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}},

where the numbers highlighted in
blue
are
pivots
, as defined previously (Definition
. In the context of Gaussian elimination, a pivot element is the first nonzero element in a row when performing row operations to transform a matrix into its row echelon form or reduced row echelon form.). The resulting matrix is referred to as the
row echelon form
of
\bm{A}
.
In this example, the fourth row becomes a zero row. To continue, we perform additional row operations to ensure that all entries above the pivots are zero:

\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}2}&\boxtimes&10&9&\boxtimes\\
0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}5}&6&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}3}&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{3}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}2}&\boxtimes&\bm{0}&\bm{-3}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}5}&6&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}3}&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{3}\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{4}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}2}&\boxtimes&0&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}5}&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}3}&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{4}\bm{E}_{3}\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}},

where
\bm{E}_{3}
subtracts twice the second row from the first row, while
\bm{E}_{4}
adds the third row to the first row and subtracts twice the third row from the second row. To achieve the full
reduced row echelon form (RREF)
, we need to ensure that all pivots are set to 1:

\footnotesize\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}2}&\boxtimes&0&0&\boxtimes\\
0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}5}&0&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}3}&\boxtimes\\
0&0&0&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{4}\bm{E}_{3}\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{E}_{5}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{{}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{1}}&\bm{\boxtimes}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{1}}&\bm{0}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{1}}&\bm{\boxtimes}\\
0&0&0&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\textstyle\mathstrut\bm{E}_{5}\bm{E}_{4}\bm{E}_{3}\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}},

(5.1)

where
\bm{E}_{5}
adjusts the pivots to equal 1. Unlike the matrices used in LU decomposition, which are generally lower triangular, the transformation matrices
\bm{E}_{1},\bm{E}_{2},\ldots,\bm{E}_{5}
may also include permutation matrices or other forms. The resulting matrix is the
reduced row echelon form (RREF)
of
\bm{A}
, characterized by having pivots of 1 and zeros above the pivots.

For a general matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, let its row echelon form (with zeros above the pivots) be denoted as
\bm{F}
. To transform
\bm{F}
into the RREF of
\bm{A}
, we apply a sequence of transformations
\bm{E}_{1},\bm{E}_{2},\ldots,\bm{E}_{r}
, defined as:

\bm{E}_{i}=\bm{E}_{i;\eta}=\begin{bmatrix}1&&&&&&\\
&\ddots&&&&&\\
&&1&&&&\\
&&&\eta&&&\\
&&&&1&&\\
&&&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&&&1\\
\end{bmatrix}=\bm{I}+(\eta-1)\bm{e}_{i}\bm{e}_{i}^{\top},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{with $1\leq i\leq r$},

where
\bm{e}_{i}
is the
i
-th unit basis vector,
r
is the rank of
\bm{A}
(the number of nonzero rows in
\bm{F}
), and
\eta
is the inverse of the
i
-th pivot in
\bm{F}
. Each
\bm{E}_{i}
is invertible with
\bm{E}_{i;\eta}^{-1}=\bm{E}_{i;\eta^{-1}}=\bm{I}+(\frac{1}{\eta}-1)\bm{e}_{i}\bm{e}_{i}^{\top}
.
The reduced row echelon form of
\bm{A}
can then be obtained by
(\bm{E}_{r}\bm{E}_{r-1}\ldots\bm{E}_{1}\bm{F})
.

We formally define the RREF as follows:

Definition 52
(Reduced Row Echelon Form, RREF).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
. The row echelon form of
\bm{A}
is defined by the following conditions:

1.

The leading nonzero entry (pivot, Definition
) of the
(i+1)
-th row appears to the right of the leading nonzero entry of the
i
-th row.

2.

All entries below a pivot in a given column are zeros.

3.

Any row that contains only zeros is positioned at the bottom of the matrix.

Although Property 2 follows directly from Property 1, we include it here for emphasis.
If a matrix in row echelon form satisfies the following additional conditions, it is said to be
in reduced row echelon form:

4.

The leading nonzero entry (pivot) in each row is equal to 1.

5.

All entries above each pivot are zeros.

Exercise
\thetheo

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any matrix. Show that there exists a sequence of row transformations
\bm{E}_{1},\bm{E}_{2},\ldots,\bm{E}_{k}
such that
\bm{B}=\bm{E}_{k}\bm{E}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}
is in reduced row echelon form.
Hint: Use induction
.

Lemma 53
(Rank and Pivots).

The rank of a matrix
\bm{A}
is equal to the number of pivots (in its reduced row echelown form).
Consequently, the rank of a matrix is the same as the rank of its RREF.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
According to Proposition
, the row space of
\bm{A}
is identical to the row space of its RREF. Since the rank of the RREF is defined as the number of its pivots, it follows that the rank of
\bm{A}
is also equal to the number of pivots.

Lemma 54
(RREF in CR).

The reduced row echelon form of the matrix
\bm{A}
, excluding zero rows, corresponds to the matrix
\bm{R}
in the CR decomposition.

Proof
[Informal Proof of Lemma
]
Informally, using the example provided earlier in (
), we express the matrix
\bm{A}
as:

\bm{E}_{5}\bm{E}_{4}\bm{E}_{3}\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{A}=\bm{R}_{0}\quad\longrightarrow\quad\bm{A}=(\bm{E}_{5}\bm{E}_{4}\bm{E}_{3}\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1})^{-1}\bm{R}_{0}.

We observe that columns 1, 3, and 4 of
\bm{R}_{0}
each contain a single nonzero entry, equal to 1. This observation allows us to construct a matrix
\bm{C}
(identical to the “column matrix” in the CR decomposition) whose first three columns are equal to columns 1, 3, and 4 of
\bm{A}
, i.e.,
\bm{C}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{3},\bm{a}_{4}]
. Additionally, because the last row of
\bm{R}_{0}
consists entirely of zeros, the last row of
\bm{R}_{0}
can be safely disregarded in computations.
Notably, this matrix
\bm{C}
is unique in its ability to reconstruct columns 1, 3, and 4 of
\bm{A}
, as the pivots of
\bm{R}_{0}
are all 1.
Thus, we obtain the CR decomposition:

\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{R}
.

We present a rigorous proof of the uniqueness of the RREF of a matrix.

Theorem 55
(Uniqueness of RREF).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any matrix of rank
r
.
Suppose
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
are two reduced row echelon forms of
\bm{A}
, obtained by applying two sequences of elementary row operations
\bm{E}_{1},\bm{E}_{2},\ldots,\bm{E}_{p}
and
\bm{F}_{1},\bm{F}_{2},\ldots,\bm{F}_{q}
, respectively, where

\bm{X}=\underbrace{\bm{E}_{p}\ldots\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}}_{\bm{E}}\bm{A}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{Y}=\underbrace{\bm{F}_{q}\ldots\bm{F}_{2}\bm{F}_{1}}_{\bm{F}}\bm{A}.

Then, the two reduced row echelon forms are the same, i.e.,
\bm{X}=\bm{Y}
and
\bm{E}=\bm{F}
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Let
\bm{B}=\bm{E}\bm{F}^{-1}=\bm{E}_{p}\ldots\bm{E}_{2}\bm{E}_{1}\bm{F}_{1}^{-1}\bm{F}_{2}^{-1}\ldots\bm{F}_{q}^{-1}
. Then, we have
\bm{X}=\bm{B}\bm{Y}
and
\bm{Y}=\bm{B}^{-1}\bm{X}
.
The
i
-th column of
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
can be expressed as
\bm{x}_{i}=\bm{X}\bm{e}_{i}
and
\bm{y}_{i}=\bm{Y}\bm{e}_{i}
, respectively, where
\bm{e}_{i}
represents the
i
-th standard basis vector in
n
. .

Zero columns match.

If
\bm{x}_{i}=\mathbf{0}
, then
\bm{y}_{i}=\bm{B}^{-1}\bm{x}_{i}=\mathbf{0}
. Similarly, if
\bm{y}_{i}=\mathbf{0}
, then
\bm{x}_{i}=\bm{B}\bm{y}_{i}=\mathbf{0}
.
Thus, the zero columns in
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
are aligned.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
contain no zero columns for the subsequent analysis.

First column.

Since we assume
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
do not contain zero columns, the first columns must be
\bm{x}_{1}=\bm{y}_{1}=\bm{e}_{1}
. This also implies the first column of
\bm{B}
is
\bm{e}_{1}
.
We refer to columns in
\bm{X}
or
\bm{Y}
that contain pivots as
pivot columns
, and those do not as
non-pivot columns
.

Non-pivot columns between the first and second pivot columns.

Suppose the indices of the pivot columns in
\bm{X}
are
\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r}\}
, and the indices of the pivot columns in
\bm{Y}
are
\{j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{r}\}
. According to Lemma
, there are
r
pivot columns.
And we have already shown that
i_{1}=j_{1}=1
. Then for
k\in\{2,3,\ldots,j_{2}-1\}
, we suppose
\bm{y}_{k}=\lambda\bm{e}_{1}
for some nonzero
\lambda
. We have

\bm{x}_{k}=\bm{B}\bm{y}_{k}=\bm{B}\lambda\bm{e}_{1}=\lambda\bm{b}_{1}=\lambda\bm{e}_{1}\quad\implies\quad\bm{x}_{k}=\bm{y}_{k},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,k\in\{2,3,\ldots,j_{2}-1\}.

Conversely, suppose
\bm{x}_{k}=\lambda\bm{e}_{1}
for some nonzero
\lambda
with
k\in\{2,3,\ldots,i_{2}-1\}
. In this case, we also find that
\bm{x}_{k}=\bm{y}_{k}
. This implies the non-pivot columns
k=2,3,\ldots,j_{2}-1
of
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
are the same, and also
i_{2}=j_{2}
(the second index for the pivot column of
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
are the same as well, and
\bm{x}_{j_{2}}=\bm{y}_{j_{2}}=\bm{e}_{2}
).

To conclude, we have demonstrated that the first
j_{2}
columns of
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
are identical.

Non-pivot columns between the second and third pivot columns.

Since
\bm{x}_{j_{2}}=\bm{y}_{j_{2}}=\bm{e}_{2}
, we have
\bm{x}_{j_{2}}=\bm{B}\bm{y}_{j_{2}}=\bm{B}\bm{e}_{2}=\bm{b}_{2}=\bm{e}_{2}
, i.e., the second column of
\bm{B}
is
\bm{e}_{2}
. On the other hand, considering the non-pivot columns of
k\in\{j_{2}+1,j_{2}+2,\ldots,j_{3}-1\}
in
\bm{Y}
, we assume
\bm{y}_{k}=\lambda_{1}\bm{e}_{1}+\lambda_{2}\bm{e}_{2}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{k}=\bm{B}\bm{y}_{k}=\bm{B}(\lambda_{1}\bm{e}_{1}+

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}\bm{e}_{2})=\lambda_{1}\bm{b}_{1}+\lambda_{2}\bm{b}_{2}=\lambda_{1}\bm{e}_{1}+\lambda_{2}\bm{e}_{2}

\displaystyle\implies\quad\bm{x}_{k}=\bm{y}_{k},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,k\in\{j_{2}+1,j_{2}+2,\ldots,j_{3}-1\}.

Conversely, suppose
\bm{x}_{k}=\lambda_{1}\bm{e}_{1}+\lambda_{2}\bm{e}_{2}
with
k\in\{i_{2}+1,i_{2}+2,\ldots,i_{3}-1\}
, we also have
\bm{x}_{k}=\bm{y}_{k}
.
Consequently, the non-pivot columns
k=j_{2}+1,j_{2}+2,\ldots,j_{3}-1
of
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
are the same, and also
j_{3}=i_{3}
(the third index for the pivot column of
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
are the same, and
\bm{x}_{j_{3}}=\bm{y}_{j_{3}}=\bm{e}_{3}
).

By iterating this process, we show that all columns of
\bm{X}
and
\bm{X}
are identical, completing the proof.

Readers interested in an inductive proof of the above theorem can also refer to
Gallier and Quaintance 2019
.

Exercise
\thetheo
(Determinant of RREF)

Show that the determinant of a matrix
\bm{A}
is nonzero if and only if its RREF is
\bm{I}
.
Consequently, from the multiplicative property of determinants (
\det(\bm{A}\bm{B})=\det(\bm{A})\det(\bm{B})
), the determinant of
\bm{A}
can be determined by tracking the elementary row operations performed during the process of transforming
\bm{A}
into its RREF.

In summary, we begin by calculating the reduced row echelon form of matrix
\bm{A}
, denoted as
rref(\bm{A})
.
Then, in the CR decomposition, the matrix
\bm{C}
is formed by selecting from
\bm{A}
only those columns that correspond to pivot columns in
rref(\bm{A})
.
Simultaneously, the factor
\bm{R}
is obtained by removing all zero rows from
rref(\bm{A})
.
This process represents a specialized case of the
rank decomposition
(Theorem
), but it stands out because it explicitly involves the RREF. Thus, we introduce it here for its specific relevance.

An important characteristic of
\bm{R}
is that a subset of its
r
columns, each containing a pivot, together form an
r\times r
identity matrix. It’s worth reiterating that we can obtain this matrix
\bm{R}
simply by eliminating the zero rows from the RREF. As noted in
Strang 2021
, a notation for the RREF that retains the zero rows is denoted by
\bm{R}_{0}
:

\bm{R}_{0}=rref(\bm{A})=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{I}_{r}&\bm{F}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{P},\penalty\

where the
n\times n
permutation matrix
\bm{P}
arranges the columns of the
r\times r
identity matrix
\bm{I}_{r}
into their correct positions, aligning them with the first
r
linearly independent columns of the original matrix
\bm{A}
.

We previously established a fundamental theorem in linear algebra using the UTV framework, which demonstrated the equality of row rank and column rank for any matrix (Theorem
).
The CR decomposition offers an alternative elucidation of this theorem.

Proof
[of Theorem
, the Third Way]
Consider the CR decomposition of matrix
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{R}
can be expressed as
\bm{R}=[\bm{I}_{r},\bm{F}]\bm{P}
, and
\bm{P}
is an
n\times n
permutation matrix used to arrange the columns of the
r\times r
identity matrix
\bm{I}_{r}
in their appropriate positions.
It is straightforward to verify that the
r
rows of
\bm{R}
are linearly independent due to the nonsingular submatrix
\bm{I}_{r}
. Therefore, the row rank of
\bm{R}
is
r
.

First, according to the definition of the CR decomposition, the
r
columns of
\bm{C}
are derived from
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
, and the column rank of
\bm{A}
is
r
. Furthermore,

•

Since
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{R}
, every row of
\bm{A}
can be represented as a linear combinations of the rows of
\bm{R}
. Hence, the row space of
\bm{A}
is contained within the row space of
\bm{R}
:
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\bm{R}^{\top})
.

•

From
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{R}
, we also have
(\bm{C}^{\top}\bm{C})^{-1}\bm{C}^{\top}\bm{C}\bm{R}=(\bm{C}^{\top}\bm{C})^{-1}\bm{C}^{\top}\bm{A}
, simplifying to
\bm{R}=(\bm{C}^{\top}\bm{C})^{-1}\bm{C}^{\top}\bm{A}
.
Because
\bm{C}
has full column rank
r
,
\bm{C}^{\top}\bm{C}
is nonsingular.
Consequently, the rows of
\bm{R}
are linear combinations of the rows of
\bm{A}
, meaning the row space of
\bm{R}
is contained within the row space of
\bm{A}
:
\mathcal{C}(\bm{R}^{\top})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
.

By this “sandwich” argument, the row spaces of
\bm{A}
and
\bm{R}
are equal, and thus their row ranks are equal:
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})=\mathcal{C}(\bm{R}^{\top})
.

Since the column rank of
\bm{A}
is also
r
by the definition of the CR decomposition, it follows that both the row rank and column rank of
\bm{A}
are equal to
r
.

5.4
Rank Decomposition

We previously noted that the CR decomposition is a special case of rank decomposition. Here, we formally prove the existence of the rank decomposition in the following theorem.

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any matrix of rank
r
. Then
\bm{A}
can be factored into what is known as the
rank decomposition
(or
full-rank decomposition
) as follows:

\underset{m\times n}{\bm{A}}=\underset{m\times r}{\bm{D}}\,\,\,\underset{r\times n}{\bm{F}},

where
\bm{D}\in\real^{m\times r}
and
\bm{F}\in\real^{r\times n}
both have (full) rank
r
.
The storage for the decomposition is then reduced or potentially increased from
mn
floating-point numbers to
r(m+n)
floating-point numbers.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Based on the ULV decomposition in Theorem
, we can express
\bm{A}
as:

\bm{A}=\bm{U}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{V}.

Let
\bm{U}_{0}=\bm{U}_{:,1:r}
and
\bm{V}_{0}=\bm{V}_{1:r,:}
, i.e.,
\bm{U}_{0}
comprises the first
r
columns of
\bm{U}
, and
\bm{V}_{0}
includes the first
r
rows of
\bm{V}
.
Thus,
\bm{A}
can also be written as:
\bm{A}=\bm{U}_{0}\bm{L}\bm{V}_{0}
, where
\bm{U}_{0}\in\real^{m\times r}
and
\bm{V}_{0}\in\real^{r\times n}
. This is also referred to as the reduced ULV decomposition. Let {
\bm{D}=\bm{U}_{0}\bm{L}
and
\bm{F}=\bm{V}_{0}
} or {
\bm{D}=\bm{U}_{0}
and
\bm{F}=\bm{L}\bm{V}_{0}
}, we achieve a valid rank decomposition.

The rank decomposition is not unique. Even through elementary transformations, we can write:

\bm{A}=\bm{E}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{Z}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{E}_{2},

where
\bm{E}_{1}\in\real^{m\times m}
and
\bm{E}_{2}\in\real^{n\times n}
are products of nonsingular elementary row and column operations, and
\bm{Z}\in\real^{r\times r}
. There exist many such
\bm{E}_{1},\bm{E}_{2},
and
\bm{Z}
matrices.
When
\bm{Z}=\bm{I}_{r}
, where
r
is the rank of matrix
\bm{A}
, this decomposition is known as the
Smith decomposition or Smith form
of
\bm{A}

(
Bernstein 2009
)
.
Using constructions similar to those demonstrated in the proof above, alternative rank decompositions can be derived from SVD, URV, CR, CUR, and other decompositional algorithms.
However, we may establish a connection between these various rank decompositions through the following lemma.

Corollary 56
(Connection Between Rank Decompositions).

Let
\bm{A}=\bm{D}_{1}\bm{F}_{1}=\bm{D}_{2}\bm{F}_{2}\in\real^{m\times n}
be two rank decompositions of
\bm{A}
. Then there exists a nonsingular matrix
\bm{P}
such that

\bm{D}_{1}=\bm{D}_{2}\bm{P}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{F}_{1}=\bm{P}^{-1}\bm{F}_{2}.

More generally, given
\bm{A},\bm{B}\in\real^{m\times n}
,
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
are biequivalent if and only if
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
share the same Smith form.

Proof
[of Corollary
]
Given
\bm{D}_{1}\bm{F}_{1}=\bm{D}_{2}\bm{F}_{2}
, postmultiplying by
\bm{F}_{1}^{\top}
yields
\bm{D}_{1}\bm{F}_{1}\bm{F}_{1}^{\top}=\bm{D}_{2}\bm{F}_{2}\bm{F}_{1}^{\top}
. Since
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{F}_{1}\bm{F}_{1}^{\top})=\mathrm{rank}(\bm{F}_{1})=r
,
\bm{F}_{1}\bm{F}_{1}^{\top}
is a square matrix with full rank and thus nonsingular. Therefore, we have
\bm{D}_{1}=\bm{D}_{2}\bm{F}_{2}\bm{F}_{1}^{\top}(\bm{F}_{1}\bm{F}_{1}^{\top})^{-1}
. Let
\bm{P}=\bm{F}_{2}\bm{F}_{1}^{\top}(\bm{F}_{1}\bm{F}_{1}^{\top})^{-1}
, we have
\bm{D}_{1}=\bm{D}_{2}\bm{P}
and
\bm{F}_{1}=\bm{P}^{-1}\bm{F}_{2}
. The second part of the corollary can be proven similarly.

5.5
Application: Rank and Trace of an Idempotent Matrix

The CR decomposition or rank decomposition plays a pivotal role in proving several essential theorems in linear algebra.
For instance, it is instrumental in establishing the existence of the pseudo-inverse; it aids in determining the basis for the four fundamental subspaces in linear algebra
(
Lu 2021c
)
.

Moreover, the CR factorization finds practical applications in data analysis and computational problem-solving. For instance, it proves valuable in solving least squares problems by reducing the system to a minimal set of variables, thereby eliminating redundancy. Throughout this book, readers will encounter various applications of the CR decomposition across different contexts.

The CR decomposition is a powerful tool for analyzing the rank characteristics of idempotent matrices. Its utility in orthogonal projections is further explored in the Appendix of
Lu 2021c
.

Proposition 57
(Rank and Trace of an Idempotent Matrix).

Let
\bm{A}
be an idempotent matrix (i.e.,
\bm{A}^{2}=\bm{A}
). Then the rank of
\bm{A}
is equal to its trace.

Proof
[of Proposition
]
Consider an
n\times n
idempotent matrix
\bm{A}
with rank
r
. By the CR decomposition, we can express
\bm{A}
as
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{C}\in\real^{n\times r}
and
\bm{R}\in\real^{r\times n}
are both of full rank
r
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{2}=\bm{A}\quad\implies\quad\bm{C}\bm{R}\bm{C}\bm{R}=\bm{C}\bm{R}\quad\implies\quad\bm{R}\bm{C}\bm{R}=\bm{R}\quad\implies\quad\bm{R}\bm{C}=\bm{I}_{r},

where
\bm{I}_{r}
denotes the
r\times r
identity matrix. Consequently,

\mathrm{tr}(\bm{A})=\mathrm{tr}(\bm{C}\bm{R})=\mathrm{tr}(\bm{R}\bm{C})=\mathrm{tr}(\bm{I}_{r})=r,

which corresponds to the rank of
\bm{A}
. This equality holds due to the invariance of the trace under cyclic permutations.

æ

.

Chapter 6
Skeleton/CUR Decomposition

6.1
Skeleton/CUR Decomposition

The CR decomposition utilizes the actual columns of a matrix, whereas the skeleton decomposition extends this idea by incorporating both the actual columns and rows.

Any rank-
r
matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
can be decomposed as

\underset{m\times n}{\bm{A}}=\underset{m\times r}{\bm{C}}\,\,\,\underset{r\times r}{\bm{U}^{-1}}\,\,\,\underset{r\times n}{\bm{R}},

where
\bm{C}
contains some
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
,
\bm{R}
contains some
r
linearly independent rows of
\bm{A}
, and
\bm{U}
is the nonsingular submatrix formed by the intersection of these rows and columns.

•

The storage requirement for this decomposition may be reduced (or potentially increased) compared to storing the full matrix, from
mn
floating-point numbers to
r(m+n)+r^{2}
floating-point numbers.

•

Alternatively, if we only record the indices of the selected rows and columns, it requires
mr
and
nr
floating-point numbers for storing
\bm{C}
and
\bm{R}
, respectively. Additionally,
2r
integers are needed to store the positions of the columns in
\bm{C}
and rows in
\bm{R}
within
\bm{A}
, which allows reconstruction of
\bm{U}
from
\bm{C}
and
\bm{R}
.

The skeleton decomposition is also referred to as the
CUR decomposition
, named after its notation.
Compared to the singular value decomposition (SVD), CUR offers significant advantages in terms of
reification
and
interpretability
. While SVD relies on artificial singular vectors that may not accurately reflect physical realities, CUR uses the actual columns and rows of the matrix, which makes it more interpretable and aligned with the structure of the original data
(
Mahoney and Drineas 2009
)
. Furthermore, CUR preserves sparsity in the underlying data, making it particularly suitable for applications involving sparse matrices.

Conversely, similar to SVD, CUR is a versatile tool widely used across various domains for tasks like data compression, feature extraction, and data analysis. It provides a computationally efficient way to approximate matrices, making it well-suited for handling large-scale datasets
(
Mahoney and Drineas 2009
;
An et al. 2012
;
Lee and Choi 2008
)
.
For example, CUR reduces the storage and computational requirements by selecting only a subset of the original matrix’s rows and columns. This results in a low-rank approximation that retains the essential information. It is particularly useful in numerical linear algebra for tasks like solving linear systems, eigenvalue problems, and matrix inversion.
CUR is also employed for image compression and analysis. By approximating the original image matrix with a lower-dimensional representation, CUR reduces storage costs while preserving important visual features.
In machine learning, CUR is effective for dimensionality reduction, feature extraction, and data representation, which enhance the efficiency of machine learning algorithms and reduce computational overhead.
In collaborative filtering, CUR approximates large user-item interaction matrices in recommendation systems, improving scalability and efficiency.
CUR decomposition can also be extended to higher-dimensional arrays (tensors) for applications in multi-linear algebra and data analysis, enabling the processing of complex datasets
(
Kishore Kumar and Schneider 2017
)
.

An illustration of skeleton decomposition is shown in Figure
, where
yellow
vectors denote the linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
, and
green
vectors denote the linearly independent rows of
\bm{A}
. Specifically, given index vectors
I
and
J
, both of size
r
, containing the indices of rows and columns selected from
\bm{A}
to form
\bm{R}
and
\bm{C}
, respectively, the submatrix
\bm{U}
can be expressed as
\bm{U}=\bm{A}[I,J]
using the Matlab-style notation.

Figure 6.1:
Demonstration of the skeleton decomposition of a matrix.

6.2
Existence of the Skeleton Decomposition

In Theorem
, we established that the row rank and the column rank of a matrix are equal. In other words, the dimension of the column space is equal to the dimension of the row space. This property is essential for the existence of the skeleton decomposition. We are now prepared to prove the existence of the skeleton decomposition. The proof is straightforward and relies on fundamental linear algebra concepts.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
The proof is based on demonstrating the existence of a nonsingular matrix
\bm{U}
, which is central to the skeleton decomposition.

Existence of such a nonsingular matrix
\bm{U}
.

Since the matrix
\bm{A}
is of rank
r
, we can select
r
linearly independent columns from
\bm{A}
.
Let these columns be
\bm{a}_{i1},\bm{a}_{i2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{ir}
, and form the
m\times r
matrix
\bm{N}=[\bm{a}_{i1},\bm{a}_{i2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{ir}]\in\real^{m\times r}
.
The column space of
\bm{N}
has a dimension of
r
, which implies, by Theorem
, that its row space also has dimension
r
.
From the rows of
\bm{N}
, we select
r
linearly independent rows
\bm{n}_{j1}^{\top},\bm{n}_{j2}^{\top},\ldots,\bm{n}_{jr}^{\top}
to construct an
r\times r
matrix
\bm{U}=[\bm{n}_{j1}^{\top};\bm{n}_{j2}^{\top};\ldots;\bm{n}_{jr}^{\top}]\in\real^{r\times r}
.
By Theorem
again, the column space of
\bm{U}
also has dimension
r
, meaning
\bm{U}
has
r
linearly independent columns. Thus,
\bm{U}
is such a nonsingular matrix with size
r\times r
.

Main proof.

Upon identifying a nonsingular
r\times r
matrix
\bm{U}
within
\bm{A}
, we proceed to establish the skeleton decomposition.
Let
\bm{U}=\bm{A}[I,J]
, where
I
and
J
are index vectors of size
r
representing the selected rows and columns. Since
\bm{U}
is a nonsingular matrix, its columns are linearly independent. Thus, the columns of matrix
\bm{C}
, formed by selecting the same
r
columns from
\bm{A}
are also linearly independent:
\bm{C}=\bm{A}[:,J]
. Here, the matrix
\bm{C}
is equivalent to the previously constructed
\bm{N}
.

Because the rank of
\bm{A}
is
r
, any column
\bm{a}_{i}
of
\bm{A}
can be expressed as a linear combination of the columns of
\bm{C}
. Specifically, there exists a vector
\bm{x}
such that
\bm{a}_{i}=\bm{C}\bm{x}
, for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. Let
r
rows (entries) of
\bm{a}_{i}\in\real^{n}
corresponding to the row entries of
\bm{U}
be
\bm{r}_{i}\in\real^{r}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
(i.e.,
\bm{r}_{i}
contains
r
entries of
\bm{a}_{i}
). That is, select the
r
entries of
\bm{a}_{i}
’s corresponding to the entries of
\bm{U}
as follows:

\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]\in\real^{m\times n}\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad\bm{A}[I,:]=[\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{n}]\in\real^{r\times n}.

Since
\bm{a}_{i}=\bm{C}\bm{x}
,
\bm{U}
is a submatrix inside
\bm{C}
, and
\bm{r}_{i}
is a subvector inside
\bm{a}_{i}
, we have
\bm{r}_{i}=\bm{U}\bm{x}
, which states that
\bm{x}=\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{r}_{i}
. Thus, for every
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, we have
\bm{a}_{i}=\bm{C}\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{r}_{i}
. Combining the
n
columns of such
\bm{r}_{i}
into
\bm{R}=[\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{n}]
, we obtain

\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]=\bm{C}\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{R},

from which the result follows.

In summary, the skeleton decomposition is constructed by identifying
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
and placing them into
\bm{C}\in\real^{m\times r}
. Subsequently, we extract an
r\times r
nonsingular submatrix
\bm{U}
from
\bm{C}
. The
r
rows of
\bm{A}
, corresponding to the entries of
\bm{U}
, contribute to reconstruct the columns of
\bm{A}
. This process is visually illustrated in Figure
.

In the case where
\bm{A}
is square and invertible, the skeleton decomposition simplifies to
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{R}
, with
\bm{C}=\bm{R}=\bm{U}=\bm{A}
. Thus, the decomposition essentially reduces to
\bm{A}=\bm{A}\bm{A}^{-1}\bm{A}
.

CR decomposition vs skeleton decomposition.

The CR decomposition and skeleton decompositions share a similar structure and even comparable notation, with
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{R}
for CR decomposition and
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{R}
for skeleton decomposition.

In both decompositions, we have the flexibility to select the
first

r
linearly independent columns to form the matrix
\bm{C}
(denoted the same way in both decompositions).
Consequently, the
\bm{C}
matrices in the CR and skeleton decompositions are identical when the same columns are selected. However, the distinction lies in the interpretation of
\bm{R}
: in the CR decomposition, it represents the reduced row echelon form without zero rows, while in skeleton decomposition, it corresponds to
r
linearly independent rows selected directly from
\bm{A}
.
This difference reflects a fundamental variation in how the two methods conceptualize
\bm{R}
.

Uniqueness of CR and skeleton decompositions.

As mentioned earlier, in both decompositions, we can select the
first

r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
to construct
\bm{C}
. In this sense, the CR and skeleton decompositions have a unique form.
However, if we instead choose the
last

r
linearly independent columns, the resulting CR or skeleton decomposition will differ. For simplicity, we will not address this variation here, as it lies beyond the primary focus of this discussion.

To summarize, the construction of the skeleton decomposition involves selecting
r
linearly independent columns from
\bm{A}
to form the matrix
\bm{C}\in\real^{m\times r}
. Subsequently, we extract an
r\times r
nonsingular submatrix
\bm{U}
from
\bm{C}
. Finally, we identify the
r
rows of
\bm{A}
that correspond to the entries of
\bm{U}
to form the row matrix
\bm{R}\in\real^{r\times n}
.
A further question can be posed that if matrix
\bm{A}
has rank
r
, matrix
\bm{C}
contains
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
, and matrix
\bm{R}
contains
r
linearly independent rows of
\bm{A}
, is the
r\times r
“intersection” of
\bm{C}
and
\bm{R}
necessarily invertible?

Corollary 58
(Nonsingular Intersection).

If matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
has rank
r
, matrix
\bm{C}
contains
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
, and matrix
\bm{R}
contains
r
linearly independent rows of
\bm{A}
, then the
r\times r
“intersection” matrix
\bm{U}
of
\bm{C}
and
\bm{R}
is invertible.

Proof
[of Corollary
]
Let
I
and
J
be the indices of the rows and columns selected from
\bm{A}
to form
\bm{R}
and
\bm{C}
, respectively. Then,
\bm{R}
can be denoted as
\bm{R}=\bm{A}[I,:]
,
\bm{C}
can be represented as
\bm{C}=\bm{A}[:,J]
, and
\bm{U}
can be denoted as
\bm{U}=\bm{A}[I,J]
.

Since
\bm{C}
contains
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
, any column
\bm{a}_{i}
of
\bm{A}
can be represented as
\bm{a}_{i}=\bm{C}\bm{x}_{i}=\bm{A}[:,J]\bm{x}_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. This implies that the
r
entries of
\bm{a}_{i}
corresponding to the indices in
I
can be represented by the columns of
\bm{U}
such that
\bm{a}_{i}[I]=\bm{U}\bm{x}_{i}\in\real^{r}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, i.e.,

\bm{a}_{i}=\bm{C}\bm{x}_{i}=\bm{A}[:,J]\bm{x}_{i}\in\real^{m}\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad\bm{a}_{i}[I]=\bm{A}[I,J]\bm{x}_{i}=\bm{U}\bm{x}_{i}\in\real^{r}.

Since
\bm{R}
contains
r
linearly independent rows of
\bm{A}
, the row rank and column rank of
\bm{R}
are equal to
r
. Combining the facts above, the
r
columns of
\bm{R}
corresponding to the indices in
J
(i.e., the
r
columns of
\bm{U}
) are linearly independent.

Finally, by Theorem
, the row space of
\bm{U}
also has dimension
r
. This implies that
\bm{U}
has
r
linearly independent rows, making it invertible.

6.3
Pseudoskeleton Decomposition via the SVD

We will delve deeply into singular value decomposition (SVD) in Chapter
. For now, let us assume a basic understanding of SVD and demonstrate how it can be utilized to approximate the skeleton decomposition. Feel free to skip this section during a first reading.

For a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, we aim to construct an approximation of
\bm{A}
with a rank
\gamma\leq\min(m,n)
in the form of the skeleton decomposition. That is,
\bm{A}
is approximated as
\bm{A}\approx\bm{C}\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{C}
and
\bm{R}
are matrices containing
\gamma
selected columns and rows, respectively, and
\bm{U}
is the intersection of
\bm{C}
and
\bm{R}
.
Specifically, if
I
and
J
are the indices of the selected rows and columns, then
\bm{U}=\bm{A}[I,J]
. Note that
\gamma
is not necessarily equal to the rank
r
of
\bm{A}
.

Unlike standard skeleton decomposition, which selects
r
linearly independent columns from
\bm{A}
, we choose
k
random columns (where
k>r
or even
k=\min\{m,n\}
) to form
\bm{C}
. The column indices
J
determine
\bm{C}=\bm{A}[:,J]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k}
. Concurrently,
k
rows of
\bm{A}
are selected using the indices
I
, forming
\bm{R}=\bm{A}[I,:]
. The rows are chosen such that the intersection matrix
\bm{U}=\bm{A}[I,J]
has maximal volume, i.e.,
\det(\bm{U})
is maximized. While
\bm{C}
is selected randomly, the choice of
\bm{R}
is deterministic. This leads to the decomposition:

\bm{A}=\bm{C}_{m\times k}\bm{U}_{k\times k}^{-1}\bm{R}_{k\times n}.

However, the inverse of
\bm{U}_{k\times k}
can be unstable due to the random selection of
\bm{C}
.
To address this issue, we perform a full SVD on
\bm{U}_{k\times k}
(see Chapter
for the distinction between reduced and full SVD):

\bm{U}_{k\times k}=\bm{U}_{k}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\bm{V}_{k}^{\top},

where
\bm{U}_{k},\bm{V}_{k}\in\real^{k\times k}
are orthogonal matrices,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}
is a diagonal matrix containing
k
singular values
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\sigma_{k}
, some of which may be zero.
We then select
\gamma
singular values greater than a threshold
\epsilon
and truncate
\bm{U}_{k}
,
\bm{V}_{k}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}
accordingly, approximating
\bm{U}_{k\times k}
by a rank-
\gamma
matrix:
\bm{U}_{k\times k}\approx\bm{U}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\gamma}\bm{V}_{\gamma}^{\top}
, where
\bm{U}_{\gamma},\bm{V}_{\gamma}\in\real^{k\times\gamma}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\gamma}\in\real^{\gamma\times\gamma}
. Therefore, the pseudo-inverse of
\bm{U}_{k\times k}
is

\bm{U}^{+}=(\bm{U}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\gamma}\bm{V}_{\gamma}^{\top})^{+}=\bm{V}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\gamma}^{-1}\bm{U}_{\gamma}^{\top}.

This allows us to approximate
\bm{A}
as a rank-
\gamma
matrix:

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle\approx\bm{C}\bm{V}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\gamma}^{-1}\bm{U}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{R}

(6.1)

\displaystyle=\bm{C}_{2}\bm{R}_{2},\qquad(\text{let $\bm{C}_{2}=\bm{C}\bm{V}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\gamma}^{-1/2}$ and $\bm{R}_{2}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\gamma}^{-1/2}\bm{U}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{R}$})

where
\bm{C}_{2}
and
\bm{R}_{2}
are rank-
\gamma
matrices.
For guidance on selecting
\epsilon
, see
Goreinov et al. 1997
and
Kishore Kumar and Schneider 2017
.
In the above approach,
\bm{C}
is chosen randomly, while
\bm{R}
is determined. Algorithms like those in
Zhu and Lin 2011
, which select both
\bm{C}
and
\bm{R}
randomly, yield more stable results.

Data interpretation methods like CR and skeleton decomposition help identify subsets of columns or rows that retain essential information from the matrix.
While Equation (
) may not immediately appear to reflect this capability, note that
\bm{C}_{2}=\bm{C}(\bm{V}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\gamma}^{-1/2})
, and the columns of
\bm{V}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\gamma}^{-1/2}
are orthogonal. Thus, the columns of
\bm{C}_{2}
represent those of
\bm{C}
in a different coordinate system. The same applies to the rows of
\bm{R}_{2}
. For further insights into these transformations, refer to Section
.

, where

Chapter 7
Interpolative Decomposition (ID)

7.1
Interpolative Decomposition (ID)

The column interpolative decomposition (ID) factors a matrix into the product of two matrices: one containing selected columns from the original matrix, and the other composed of a subset of columns that includes an identity matrix and values no greater than 1 in magnitude. Formally, the details of the column ID are presented in the following theorem.

Any rank-
r
matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
can be decomposed as

\underset{m\times n}{\bm{A}}=\underset{m\times r}{\bm{C}}\,\,\,\underset{r\times n}{\bm{W}},

where
\bm{C}\in\real^{m\times r}
contains
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
,
\bm{W}\in\real^{r\times n}
is the matrix used to reconstruct
\bm{A}
. The factor
\bm{W}
contains an
r\times r
identity submatrix (under a mild column permutation) and satisfies:

\max|w_{ij}|\leq 1,\,\,\forall\,\,\,i\in[1,r],j\in[1,n].

The storage requirements for this decomposition are reduced (or potentially increased) from
mn
floating-point numbers to
mr
and
(n-r)r
floating-point numbers for storing
\bm{C}
and
\bm{W}
, respectively.
Additionally,
r
integers are needed to track the position of each column of
\bm{C}
within
\bm{A}
.

Figure 7.1:
Demonstration of the column ID of a matrix, where the
yellow
vectors denote the linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
, white entries denote zero, and
purple
entries denote one.

Figure
demonstrates the column ID of a matrix, where the
yellow
vectors represent the linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
, and the
purple
vectors in
\bm{W}
form an
r\times r
identity submatrix. The positions of the
purple
vectors inside
\bm{W}
correspond to the positions of the
yellow
vectors in
\bm{A}
.
Column ID closely resembles the CR decomposition (Theorem
): both methods select
r
linearly independent columns into the first factor, with the second factor containing an
r\times r
identity submatrix.
However, CR decomposition specifically selects the first
r
linearly independent columns, and the identity submatrix corresponds to the pivot columns (Definition
). The second factor in CR decomposition is derived from the reduced row echelon form (Lemma
).
Consequently, column ID finds applications in CR decomposition scenarios, such as proving that the rank of a symmetric idempotent matrix equals its trace (Proposition
), and establishing the fundamental linear algebra theorem that the column rank equals the row rank of a matrix (Theorem
).
Moreover, column ID also serves as a special case of the rank decomposition (Theorem
) and is apparently not unique. Connections between different column IDs are given in Corollary
.

Notations for subsequent sections.

Using Matlab-style notation, let
J_{s}
be an index vector of size
r
, containing the indices of columns selected from
\bm{A}
to form
\bm{C}
. Then,
\bm{C}
can be denoted by
\bm{C}=\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]
.
The matrix
\bm{C}
contains “skeleton” columns of
\bm{A}
, hence the subscript
s
in
J_{s}
. From the “skeleton” index vector
J_{s}
, the
r\times r
identity submatrix inside
\bm{W}
can be recovered by

\bm{W}[:,J_{s}]=\bm{I}_{r}\in\real^{r\times r}.

Let
J_{r}
denote the indices of the remaining columns of
\bm{A}
, where

J_{s}\cap J_{r}=\varnothing\qquad\text{and}\qquad J_{s}\cup J_{r}=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}.

The remaining
n-r
columns of
\bm{W}
consist of an
r\times(n-r)

expansion matrix
:

\bm{E}=\bm{W}[:,J_{r}]\in\real^{r\times(n-r)},

where the entries of
\bm{E}
are called
expansion coefficients
.
Finally, let
\bm{P}\in\real^{n\times n}
be a (column) permutation matrix (Definition
) defined as
\bm{P}=\bm{I}_{n}[:,(J_{s},J_{r})]
, such that

\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{A}[:,(J_{s},J_{r})]=\left[\bm{C},\bm{A}[:,J_{r}]\right],

and

\bm{W}\bm{P}=\bm{W}[:,(J_{s},J_{r})]=\left[\bm{I}_{r},\bm{E}\right]\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\bm{W}=\left[\bm{I}_{r},\bm{E}\right]\bm{P}^{\top}.

(7.1)

7.2
Existence of the Column Interpolative Decomposition

Cramer’s rule.

The proof of the existence of the column ID relies on Cramer’s rule, which we will briefly discuss; see Problem
\sim
for more details. Consider a system of
n
linear equations with
n
unknowns, represented in matrix form as:

\bm{M}\bm{x}=\bm{l},

where
\bm{M}\in\real^{n\times n}
is nonsingular, and
\bm{x},\bm{l}\in\real^{n}
. Cramer’s rule states that this system has a unique solution, where each unknown is given by:

x_{i}=\frac{\det(\bm{M}_{i})}{\det(\bm{M})},\qquad\text{for all}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\},

where
\bm{M}_{i}
is the matrix formed by replacing the
i
-th column of
\bm{M}
with the column vector
\bm{l}
.
In a more general setting, consider the matrix equation:

\bm{M}\bm{X}=\bm{L},

where
\bm{M}\in\real^{n\times n}
is nonsingular, and
\bm{X},\bm{L}\in\real^{n\times m}
. Let
I=[i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}]
and
J=[j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{k}]
be two index vectors, where
1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\ldots\leq i_{k}\leq n
and
1\leq j_{1}\leq j_{2}\leq\ldots\leq j_{k}\leq n
. Then,
\bm{X}[I,J]
denotes a
k\times k
submatrix of
\bm{X}
. Let further
\bm{M}_{\bm{L}}(I,J)
be the
n\times n
matrix formed by replacing the
(i_{s})
-th column of
\bm{M}
with
(j_{s})
-th column of
\bm{L}
for all
s\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
. Then, we have:

\det(\bm{X}[I,J])=\frac{\det\left(\bm{M}_{\bm{L}}(I,J)\right)}{\det(\bm{M})}.

When
I
and
J
are of size 1, this simplifies to:

x_{ij}=\frac{\det\left(\bm{M}_{\bm{L}}(i,j)\right)}{\det(\bm{M})}.

(7.2)

With this understanding, we are now ready to prove the existence of the column ID.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
We have mentioned that the proof relies on Cramer’s rule. To complete the proof, we will show that the entries of
\bm{W}
can be expressed using the formula in Equation (
), where the numerator is smaller than the denominator. Note that the denominator in Equation (
) corresponds to the determinant of a square matrix. Here is the key idea.

Step 1: column ID for full row rank matrix.

First, consider a full row rank matrix
\bm{A}
(which implies
r=m
,
m\leq n
, and
\bm{A}\in\real^{r\times n}
such that the matrix
\bm{C}\in\real^{r\times r}
is a square matrix in the column ID
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{W}
that we want to obtain). Determine the “skeleton” index vector
J_{s}
by

\boxed{J_{s}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{J}\left\{|\det(\bm{A}[:,J])|:\text{$J$ is a subset of $\{1,2,\ldots,n\}$ with size $r=m$}\right\},}

(7.3)

i.e.,
J_{s}
is the index vector that is determined by maximizing the magnitude of the determinant of
\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]
. AFrom the previous discussion, there exists a (column) permutation matrix
\bm{P}
such that:

\bm{A}\bm{P}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]&\bm{A}[:,J_{r}]\end{bmatrix}.

Since
\bm{C}=\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]
has full column rank
r=m
, it is then nonsingular. Rewriting
\bm{A}
:

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]&\bm{A}[:,J_{r}]\end{bmatrix}\bm{P}^{\top}=\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]\bigg[\bm{I}_{r}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]^{-1}\bm{A}[:,J_{r}]\bigg]\bm{P}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\bm{C}\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\bm{I}_{r}&\bm{C}^{-1}\bm{A}[:,J_{r}]\end{bmatrix}\bm{P}^{\top}}_{\bm{W}},

where the matrix
\bm{W}
is given by

\begin{bmatrix}\bm{I}_{r}&\bm{C}^{-1}\bm{A}[:,J_{r}]\end{bmatrix}\bm{P}^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{I}_{r}&\bm{E}\end{bmatrix}\bm{P}^{\top}
, from Equation (
). To prove the claim that the magnitude of
\bm{W}
is at most 1, we need to show that the entries in
\bm{E}=\bm{C}^{-1}\bm{A}[:,J_{r}]\in\real^{r\times(n-r)}
are not greater than 1 in absolute value.

Define the index vector
[j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{n}]
as a permutation of
[1,2,\ldots,n]
such that

[j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{n}]=[1,2,\ldots,n]\bm{P}=[J_{s},J_{r}].\penalty\

Thus, it follows from
\bm{C}\bm{E}=\bm{A}[:,J_{r}]
that

\displaystyle\underbrace{[\bm{a}_{j_{1}},\bm{a}_{j_{2}},\ldots,\bm{a}_{j_{r}}]}_{=\bm{C}=\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]}\bm{E}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{[\bm{a}_{j_{r+1}},\bm{a}_{j_{r+2}},\ldots,\bm{a}_{j_{n}}]}_{=\bm{A}[:,J_{r}]:=\bm{B}},

where
\bm{a}_{i}
denotes the
i
-th column of
\bm{A}
, and we let
\bm{B}=\bm{A}[:,J_{r}]
.
Therefore, by Cramer’s rule in Equation (
), we have

e_{kl}=\frac{\det\left(\bm{C}_{\bm{B}}(k,l)\right)}{\det\left(\bm{C}\right)},

(7.4)

where
e_{kl}
is the entry (
k,l
) of
\bm{E}
, and
\bm{C}_{\bm{B}}(k,l)
is the
r\times r
matrix formed by replacing the
k
-th column of
\bm{C}
with the
l
-th column of
\bm{B}
. For example,

\displaystyle e_{11}

\displaystyle=\frac{\det\left([{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{a}_{j_{r+1}}},\bm{a}_{j_{2}},\ldots,\bm{a}_{j_{r}}]\right)}{\det\left([\bm{a}_{j_{1}},\bm{a}_{j_{2}},\ldots,\bm{a}_{j_{r}}]\right)},\qquad

\displaystyle e_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{\det\left([{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{a}_{j_{r+2}}},\bm{a}_{j_{2}},\ldots,\bm{a}_{j_{r}}]\right)}{\det\left([\bm{a}_{j_{1}},\bm{a}_{j_{2}},\ldots,\bm{a}_{j_{r}}]\right)},

\displaystyle e_{21}

\displaystyle=\frac{\det\left([\bm{a}_{j_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{a}_{j_{r+1}}},\ldots,\bm{a}_{j_{r}}]\right)}{\det\left([\bm{a}_{j_{1}},\bm{a}_{j_{2}},\ldots,\bm{a}_{j_{r}}]\right)},\qquad

\displaystyle e_{22}

\displaystyle=\frac{\det\left([\bm{a}_{j_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{a}_{j_{r+2}}},\ldots,\bm{a}_{j_{r}}]\right)}{\det\left([\bm{a}_{j_{1}},\bm{a}_{j_{2}},\ldots,\bm{a}_{j_{r}}]\right)}.

Since
J_{s}
maximizes the magnitude of
\det(\bm{C})
in Equation (
), it follows that

|e_{kl}|\leq 1,\qquad\text{for all}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,k\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\},l\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-r\}.

Step 2: apply to general matrices.

To summarize, for any matrix
\bm{F}\in\real^{r\times n}
with
full
rank
r\leq n
, the column ID exists such that
\bm{F}=\bm{C}_{0}\bm{W}
, where the entries of
\bm{W}
are bounded by 1 in absolute value.

For a general matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
with rank
r\leq\{m,n\}
, the matrix admits a rank decomposition (Theorem
):

\underset{m\times n}{\bm{A}}=\underset{m\times r}{\bm{D}}\,\,\,\underset{r\times n}{\bm{F}},

where
\bm{D}
and
\bm{F}
have full column rank
r
and full row rank
r
, respectively.
Applying the column ID to
\bm{F}=\bm{C}_{0}\bm{W}
, where
\bm{C}_{0}=\bm{F}[:,J_{s}]
contains
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{F}
. We notice from
\bm{A}=\bm{D}\bm{F}
such that

\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]=\bm{D}\bm{F}[:,J_{s}],

i.e., the columns indexed by
J_{s}
of
(\bm{D}\bm{F})
can be obtained by
\bm{D}\bm{F}[:,J_{s}]
, which in turn are the columns of
\bm{A}
indexed by
J_{s}
. This makes

\underbrace{\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]}_{\bm{C}}=\underbrace{\bm{D}\bm{F}[:,J_{s}]}_{\bm{D}\bm{C}_{0}},

and

\bm{A}=\bm{D}\bm{F}=\bm{D}\bm{C}_{0}\bm{W}=\underbrace{\bm{D}\bm{F}[:,J_{s}]}_{\bm{C}}\bm{W}=\bm{C}\bm{W}.

This completes the proof.

The above proof provides an intuitive way to compute the “optimal” column ID of a matrix
\bm{A}
. However, any algorithm guaranteed to achieve such an optimally conditioned factorization necessarily involves combinatorial complexity
(
Martinsson 2019
;
Lu 2022a
;
Lu 2022b
)
. To address this, randomized algorithms, along with approximations via column-pivoted QR (Section
) and rank-revealing QR (Section
), are commonly employed to obtain a relatively well-conditioned column ID decomposition.
In these approaches, the matrix
\bm{W}
is designed to have a small norm rather than strictly ensuring that all its entries are within the range
[-1,1]
. On the other hand, the Bayesian approach can strictly constrain the entries of
\bm{W}
to lie within
[-1,1]

(
Lu 2022a
;
Lu 2022b
)
. However, the details of this method are beyond the scope of this discussion.

Example 59
(Compute the Column ID).

Let

\bm{A}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}56&41&30\\
32&23&18\\
80&59&42\end{bmatrix}

be a rank-2 matrix. The process for computing the column ID of
\bm{A}
is demonstrated as follows.
First, we find a rank decomposition:

\bm{A}=\bm{D}\bm{F}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
2&-1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}56&41&30\\
32&23&18\end{bmatrix}.

Since
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=2
, the index vector
J_{s}
can take one of the following values:
[1,2],[0,2],[0,1]
, where the absolute determinants of
\bm{F}[:,J_{s}]
are
48,48
, and
24
, respectively. We proceed with
J_{s}=[0,2]
, which yields:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{C}}

\displaystyle=\bm{F}[:,J_{s}]=\begin{bmatrix}56&30\\
32&18\end{bmatrix},\qquad\bm{M}=\bm{F}[:,J_{r}]=\begin{bmatrix}41\\
23\end{bmatrix}.

Thus,

\bm{F}\bm{P}=\bm{F}[:(J_{s},J_{r})]=\bm{F}[:,(0,2,1)]\quad\implies\quad\bm{P}=\begin{bmatrix}1&&\\
&&1\\
&1&\end{bmatrix}.

In this example,
\bm{E}\in\real^{2\times 1}
:

\displaystyle e_{11}

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}41&30\\
23&18\end{bmatrix}\right)\bigg/\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}56&30\\
32&18\end{bmatrix}\right)=1;

\displaystyle e_{21}

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}56&41\\
32&23\end{bmatrix}\right)\bigg/\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}56&30\\
32&18\end{bmatrix}\right)=-\frac{1}{2}.

This makes

\bm{E}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
-\frac{1}{2}\end{bmatrix}\quad\implies\quad\bm{W}=[\bm{I}_{2},\bm{E}]\bm{P}^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}1&1&0\\
0&-\frac{1}{2}&1\end{bmatrix}.

The final selected columns are

\bm{C}=\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]=\begin{bmatrix}56&30\\
32&18\\
80&42\end{bmatrix}.

The resulting decomposition is:

\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{W}=\begin{bmatrix}56&30\\
32&18\\
80&42\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&1&0\\
0&-\frac{1}{2}&1\end{bmatrix}.

As expected, the entries of
\bm{W}
have magnitudes no greater than 1.

To conclude this section, we discuss the non-uniqueness of the column ID.

Remark 60
(Non-uniqueness of the Column ID).

The column ID is not unique, as illustrated in Example
. Specifically, both
\bm{F}[:,(1,2)]
and
\bm{F}[:,(0,2)]
yield the maximum absolute determinant.
Either choice results in a valid column ID for
\bm{A}
. Whilst, we only select one
J_{s}
from
[1,2],[0,2]
, and
[0,1]
.
Additionally, when selecting the index set
J_{s}
, any permutation of it is also valid. For example,
J_{s}=[0,2]
and
J_{s}=[2,0]
are both acceptable.
This flexibility in selecting the column indices introduces non-uniqueness into the column ID.

7.3
Row ID and Two-Sided ID

The decomposition described above is called the column ID, a name that is not arbitrary. It is related to other decompositions, as outlined below:

Any rank-
r
matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\text{Column ID: }

\displaystyle\underset{m\times n}{\bm{A}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boxed{\underset{m\times r}{\bm{C}}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\underset{r\times n}{\bm{W}};

\displaystyle\text{Row ID: }

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{m\times r}{\bm{Z}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\boxed{\underset{r\times n}{\bm{R}}};

\displaystyle\text{Two-Sided ID: }

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{m\times r}{\bm{Z}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\boxed{\underset{r\times r}{\bm{U}}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\underset{r\times n}{\bm{W}},

where

•

\bm{C}=\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]\in\real^{m\times r}
contains
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
,
\bm{W}\in\real^{r\times n}
is the matrix used to reconstruct
\bm{A}
, which contains an
r\times r
identity submatrix (under a mild column permutation):
\bm{W}[:,J_{s}]=\bm{I}_{r}
;

•

\bm{R}=\bm{A}[I_{s},:]\in\real^{r\times n}
contains
r
linearly independent rows of
\bm{R}
,
\bm{Z}\in\real^{m\times r}
is the matrix used to reconstruct
\bm{A}
, which contains an
r\times r
identity submatrix (under a mild row permutation):
\bm{Z}[I_{s},:]=\bm{I}_{r}
;

•

The entries in
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
have values no larger than 1 in magnitude:
\max|w_{ij}|\leq 1
and
\max|z_{ij}|\leq 1
;

•

\bm{U}=\bm{A}[I_{s},J_{s}]\in\real^{r\times r}
is the nonsingular submatrix at the intersection of
\bm{C}
and
\bm{R}
;

•

The three matrices
\bm{C},\bm{R},
and
\bm{U}
in the
\boxed{\text{boxed}}
representations share the same notation and interpretation as in the skeleton decomposition (Theorem
). Specifically,
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{R}
represents the skeleton decomposition.

The proof of the row ID follows similarly from the column ID. Assume the column ID of
\bm{A}^{\top}
is given as
\bm{A}^{\top}=\bm{C}_{0}\bm{W}_{0}
, where
\bm{C}_{0}
contains
r
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}^{\top}
(i.e.,
r
linearly independent rows of
\bm{A}
). Let
\bm{R}=\bm{C}_{0}^{\top}
and
\bm{Z}=\bm{W}_{0}^{\top}
. Then, the row ID is obtained by
\bm{A}=\bm{Z}\bm{R}
.

From the skeleton decomposition, where
\bm{U}
is the intersection of
\bm{C}
and
\bm{R}
, it follows that
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{R}
. Using the row ID, we get
\bm{C}\bm{U}^{-1}=\bm{Z}
, which implies
\bm{C}=\bm{Z}\bm{U}
. Substituting into the column ID yields
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{W}=\bm{Z}\bm{U}\bm{W}
, proving the existence of the two-sided ID.

Data storage.

For each ID, the storage requirements are summarized as follows:

•

Column ID.
It requires
mr
and
(n-r)r
floating-point numbers to store
\bm{C}
and
\bm{W}
, respectively, and
r
integers to store the indices of the selected columns in
\bm{A}
;

•

Row ID.
It requires
nr
and
(m-r)r
floating-point numbers to store
\bm{R}
and
\bm{Z}
, respectively, and
r
integers to store the indices of the selected rows in
\bm{A}
;

•

Two-Sided ID.
It requires
(m-r)r
,
(n-r)r
, and
r^{2}
floating-point numbers to store
\bm{Z},\bm{W}
, and
\bm{U}
, respectively. And an extra
2r
integers are required to store the indices of the selected rows and columns in
\bm{A}
.

Storage reduction for sparse matrices.

For sparse matrices, further storage reduction can be achieved. Consider the column ID:
\bm{A}=\bm{C}\bm{W}
, where
\bm{C}=\bm{A}[:,J_{s}]
, and a good spanning rows index set
I_{s}
of
\bm{C}
exists such that:

\bm{A}[I_{s},:]=\bm{C}[I_{s},:]\bm{W}.

Since
\bm{C}[I_{s},:]=\bm{A}[I_{s},J_{s}]\in\real^{r\times r}
is nonsingular, we can compute:

\bm{W}=(\bm{A}[I_{s},J_{s}])^{-1}\bm{A}[I_{s},:].

Thus,
\bm{W}
does not need to be explicitly stored; only
\bm{A}[I_{s},:]
and
(\bm{A}[I_{s},J_{s}])^{-1}
are required.
Alternatively, If the inverse of
\bm{A}[I_{s},J_{s}]
is computed dynamically, only
r
integers for
J_{s}
are necessary, as
\bm{A}[I_{s},J_{s}]
can be reconstructed from
\bm{A}[I_{s},:]
. This approach is particularly efficient for sparse matrices, where the storage of
\bm{A}[I_{s},:]
is economical.

æ

Consider the linear system

\bm{l}

\bm{M}

\big(\mathrm{adj}(\bm{M})\bm{l}\big)_{i}=\det(\bm{M}_{\bm{l}}(i)),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}.

Cramer’s rule.
In the same setting as Problem

In the same setting as Problem

\bm{M}\bm{I}_{\bm{l}}(i)=\bm{M}_{\bm{l}}(i),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\},

where

i

\bm{l}
.

7.7.

Determinant of inverses for subsets, Jacobi’s equality.
Let
\bm{M}\in\real^{n\times n}
, and let

be two index sets (their complementary sets are

and

\det\big(\bm{M}^{-1}[I^{C},J^{C}]\big)=(-1)^{\gamma}\frac{\det(\bm{M}[J,I])}{\det(\bm{M})},

(7.12)

where

is the sum of indices.
When

, this also indicates

\det\big(\bm{M}^{-1}[I^{C},I^{C}]\big)=\frac{\det(\bm{M}[I,I])}{\det(\bm{M})},

(7.13)

which is known as
Jacobi’s equality
.

.

\det(\bm{A}[I,K])\det(\bm{A}[J,L])=\det(\bm{A}[I,L])\det(\bm{A}[J,K])
.
