Part V
Eigenvalue Problem

\nopagecolor

Chapter 10
Eigenvalue and Jordan Decomposition

10.1
Eigenvalue Decomposition

Any square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
with linearly independent eigenvectors can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{X}^{-1},

where
\bm{X}
contains the eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
as its columns, and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is a diagonal matrix
\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},

\ldots,\lambda_{n})
, with
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
representing the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
.

Eigenvalue decomposition (EVD) is also referred to as diagonalizing the matrix
\bm{A}
.
If none of the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
are repeated, the eigenvectors are guaranteed to be linearly independent, which allows
\bm{A}
to be diagonalized.
Note that without
n
linearly independent eigenvectors, diagonalization is not possible. In Section
, we will explore further conditions under which a matrix has linearly independent eigenvectors.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Let
\bm{X}=[\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{n}]
be the linearly independent eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
. Clearly, we have

\bm{A}\bm{x}_{1}=\lambda_{1}\bm{x}_{1},\qquad\bm{A}\bm{x}_{2}=\lambda_{2}\bm{x}_{2},\qquad\ldots,\qquad\bm{A}\bm{x}_{n}=\lambda_{n}\bm{x}_{n}.

In matrix form, we can write this as:

\bm{A}\bm{X}=[\bm{A}\bm{x}_{1},\bm{A}\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{A}\bm{x}_{n}]=[\lambda_{1}\bm{x}_{1},\lambda_{2}\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\bm{x}_{n}]=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}.

Since we assume the eigenvectors are linearly independent,
\bm{X}
has full rank and is invertible. Therefore, we obtain

\bm{A}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{X}^{-1}.

This completes the proof.

In the spectral decomposition chapter (Chapter
), we will discuss similar forms of eigenvalue decomposition, where the matrix
\bm{A}
is required to be symmetric, and
\bm{X}
is not only nonsingular but also orthogonal. Alternatively,
\bm{A}
may be a
simple matrix
, meaning the algebraic and geometric multiplicities of its eigenvalues are equal. In this case,
\bm{X}
will be a trivial nonsingular matrix. The decomposition also has a geometric interpretation, which we will explore in Section
.

A matrix decomposition of the form
\bm{A}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{X}^{-1}
has a notable property, allowing for efficient computation of the
m
-th power of
\bm{A}
.

Remark 74
(
m
-th Power).

The
m
-th power of
\bm{A}
is given by
\bm{A}^{m}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{m}\bm{X}^{-1}
if
\bm{A}
can be factored as
\bm{A}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{X}^{-1}
.
Computing
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{m}
is easy because we can apply this operation individually to each diagonal element.
Moreover, if
\bm{A}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{X}^{-1}
, then the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}^{m}
are the
m
-th powers of the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
.

We observe that for the eigenvalue decomposition to exist,
\bm{A}
must have linearly independent eigenvectors. This condition is naturally satisfied under specific circumstances.

Lemma 75
(Different Eigenvalues).

If the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
of
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
are all distinct, the corresponding eigenvectors are linearly independent. In other words, any square matrix with distinct eigenvalues can be diagonalized.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Assume that the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
are distinct, but the eigenvectors
\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{n}
are linearly dependent. Without loss of generality, assume there exists a nonzero vector
\bm{c}=[c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{n-1}]^{\top}
such that:

\bm{x}_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}c_{i}\bm{x}_{i}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\bm{A}\bm{x}_{n}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}c_{i}\bm{x}_{i}\right)=c_{1}\lambda_{1}\bm{x}_{1}+c_{2}\lambda_{2}\bm{x}_{2}+\ldots+c_{n-1}\lambda_{n-1}\bm{x}_{n-1}.

and

\displaystyle\bm{A}\bm{x}_{n}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{n}\bm{x}_{n}=\lambda_{n}(c_{1}\bm{x}_{1}+c_{2}\bm{x}_{2}+\ldots+c_{n-1}\bm{x}_{n-1}).

Equating these two expressions gives:

\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(\lambda_{n}-\lambda_{i})c_{i}\bm{x}_{i}=\mathbf{0}.

This leads to a contradiction since
\lambda_{n}\neq\lambda_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
, thus proving that the eigenvectors are linearly independent.

Remark 76
(Limitation of Eigenvalue Decomposition).

There are certain limitations to eigenvalue decomposition, which will be addressed in the following chapters:

•

The eigenvectors in
\bm{X}
are generally not orthogonal, and there may not be enough eigenvectors (i.e., some eigenvalues are repeated).

•

To compute the eigenvalues and eigenvectors,
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\lambda\bm{x}
,
\bm{A}
must be square. Rectangular matrices cannot be diagonalized using eigenvalue decomposition.

10.2
Jordan Decomposition

In eigenvalue decomposition, we assume that the matrix
\bm{A}
has
n
linearly independent eigenvectors. However, this assumption does not hold for all square matrices. To address this limitation, we introduce a generalized form of eigenvalue decomposition called the
Jordan decomposition
, named after
Camille Jordan

(
Jordan 1870
)
.

To describe Jordan decomposition, we first define
Jordan blocks
and the
Jordan form
.

Definition 77
(Jordan Block).

An
m\times m
upper triangular matrix
B(\lambda,m)
is called a Jordan block if all
m
diagonal elements are equal to
\lambda
, and all superdigonal elements are 1. Mathematically,

B(\lambda,m)=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\lambda&1&0&\ldots&0&0&0\\
0&\lambda&1&\ldots&0&0&0\\
0&0&\lambda&\ldots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\ldots&\lambda&1&0\\
0&0&0&\ldots&0&\lambda&1\\
0&0&0&\ldots&0&0&\lambda\end{bmatrix}_{m\times m}.

Definition 78
(Jordan Form).

Given an
n\times n
matrix
\bm{A}
, a Jordan form
\bm{J}
for
\bm{A}
is a block diagonal matrix of the form:

\bm{J}=\mathrm{diag}(B(\lambda_{1},m_{1}),B(\lambda_{2},m_{2}),\ldots B(\lambda_{k},m_{k})),

where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{k}
are eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
(with possible repetitions), and
m_{1}+m_{2}+\ldots+m_{k}=n
.

Although not all matrices can be decomposed using eigenvalue decomposition, they can be factored using Jordan decomposition. A non-diagonalizable matrix
\bm{A}
with multiple eigenvalues can be reduced to its Jordan canonical form through a similarity transformation.

Any square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{X}\bm{J}\bm{X}^{-1},

where
\bm{X}
is a nonsingular matrix containing the
generalized eigenvectors
of
\bm{A}
as its columns, and
\bm{J}
is a Jordan form matrix represented as
\mathrm{diag}(\bm{J}_{1},\bm{J}_{2},\ldots,\bm{J}_{k})
. Each block
\bm{J}_{i}\in\real^{m_{i}\times m_{i}}
is defined as:

\bm{J}_{i}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}&1&0&\ldots&0&0&0\\
0&\lambda_{i}&1&\ldots&0&0&0\\
0&0&\lambda_{i}&\ldots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\ldots&\lambda_{i}&1&0\\
0&0&0&\ldots&0&\lambda_{i}&1\\
0&0&0&\ldots&0&0&\lambda_{i}\end{bmatrix}_{m_{i}\times m_{i}}

where
\lambda_{i}
is an eigenvalue of
\bm{A}
, and
m_{1}+m_{2}+\ldots+m_{k}=n
. These blocks
\bm{J}_{i}
are referred to as Jordan blocks.
Furthermore, the nonsingular matrix
\bm{X}
is called the
matrix of generalized eigenvectors
of
\bm{A}
.

For example, a Jordan form
\bm{J}
can take the following structure:

\displaystyle\bm{J}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{diag}(B(\lambda_{1},m_{1}),\ldots,B(\lambda_{k},m_{k}))=\footnotesize\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&1&0\\
0&\lambda_{1}&1\\
0&0&\lambda_{1}\end{bmatrix}&&&&\\
&\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{2}\end{bmatrix}&&&\\
&&\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{3}&1\\
0&\lambda_{3}\end{bmatrix}&&\\
&&&\ddots&&\\
&&&&&\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{k}&1\\
0&\lambda_{k}\end{bmatrix}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Note that zeros can appear on the superdiagonal of
\bm{J}
, and the first column is always a vector containing only eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
in each block.
Although Jordan decomposition is theoretically significant, it is rarely used in practice due to its extreme sensitivity to perturbations. Even small random changes to a matrix can render it diagonalizable
(
van de Geijn and Myers 2020
)
. Consequently, no major mathematical software libraries or tools provide direct computation of Jordan decomposition. Moreover, its proof spans dozens of pages, making it beyond the scope of this discussion. Interested readers are encouraged to explore the references for further details

(
Gohberg and Goldberg 1996
;
Hales and Passi 1999
;
Lu 2021c
)
.

10.3
Application: Computing Fibonacci Numbers

Eigenvalue decomposition provides a powerful method for computing Fibonacci numbers
(
Strang 2009
)
. The Fibonacci sequence is defined recursively, with each number
F_{k+2}
being the sum of the two preceding numbers,
F_{k+1}+F_{k}
. The sequence begins as
0,1,1,2,3,5,8,\ldots
.
A natural question arises: what is the value of
F_{100}
?
Eigenvalue decomposition allows us to derive a general formula for the Fibonacci sequence.

Let,
\bm{u}_{k}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}F_{k+1}\\
F_{k}\end{bmatrix}
.
By definition of the Fibonacci sequence, we have
\bm{u}_{k+1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}F_{k+2}\\
F_{k+1}\end{bmatrix}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\bm{u}_{k}
.
Define
\bm{A}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
.
It follows that
\bm{u}_{100}=\bm{A}^{100}\bm{u}_{0}
, where
\bm{u}_{0}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}
.

The eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
are solutions to
\det(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})=0
when
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
\bm{A}
(Remark
). Solving the characteristic equation
\det(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})=\lambda^{2}-\lambda+1=0
, we find the eigenvalues and the corresponding eigenvectors:

(\lambda_{1},\bm{x}_{1})=\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2},\,\,\,\,\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}\\
1\end{bmatrix}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad(\lambda_{2},\bm{x}_{2})=\left(\frac{1-\sqrt{5}}{2},\,\,\,\,\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{2}\\
1\end{bmatrix}\right).

As per Remark
,
we can express
\bm{A}^{100}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{100}\bm{X}^{-1}=\bm{X}\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}^{100}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}^{100}\end{bmatrix}\bm{X}^{-1}
, where
\bm{X}^{-1}
can be easily calculated as
\bm{X}^{-1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}&\frac{-\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\\
-\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}&\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\end{bmatrix}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\sqrt{5}}{5}&\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{10}\\
-\frac{\sqrt{5}}{5}&\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{10}\end{bmatrix}
. We notice that
\bm{u}_{100}=\bm{A}^{100}\bm{u}_{0}
corresponds to the first column of
\bm{A}^{100}
, which can be represented as:

\bm{u}_{100}=\begin{bmatrix}F_{101}\\
F_{100}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\lambda_{1}^{101}-\lambda_{2}^{101}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\\
\frac{\lambda_{1}^{100}-\lambda_{2}^{100}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\end{bmatrix}.

Upon a simple check of the calculation, we have
F_{100}=3.542248481792631e+20
. Or more generally, we can express
\bm{u}_{K}
as follows:

\bm{u}_{K}=\begin{bmatrix}F_{K+1}\\
F_{K}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\lambda_{1}^{K+1}-\lambda_{2}^{K+1}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\\
\frac{\lambda_{1}^{K}-\lambda_{2}^{K}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\end{bmatrix},

where the general form of
F_{K}
is given by
F_{K}=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{K}-\lambda_{2}^{K}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}
.

10.4
Application: Matrix Polynomials

We previously demonstrated in Problems
and
that
f(\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{-1})=\bm{P}f(\bm{A})\bm{P}^{-1}
if
f(\bm{C})=\gamma_{m}\bm{C}^{m}+\gamma_{m-1}\bm{C}^{m-1}+\ldots+\gamma_{0}
is a polynomial.
Let

\bm{A}=\bm{X}\bm{J}\bm{X}^{-1}

be the Jordan decomposition of
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
,
where
\bm{X}\in\real^{n\times n}
is a nonsingular matrix containing the
generalized eigenvectors
of
\bm{A}
as its columns, and
\bm{J}\in\real^{n\times n}
is a Jordan form matrix
\mathrm{diag}(\bm{J}_{m_{1}}(\lambda_{1}),\bm{J}_{m_{2}}(\lambda_{2}),\ldots,\bm{J}_{m_{k}}(\lambda_{k}))=\mathrm{diag}(\bm{J}_{1},\bm{J}_{2},\ldots,\bm{J}_{k})
, where
\bm{J}_{m_{i}}(\lambda_{i})\in\real^{m_{i}\times m_{i}}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}=n
.
Using this decomposition, we have:

f(\bm{A})=\bm{X}f(\bm{J})\bm{X}^{-1}=\bm{X}\mathrm{diag}(f(\bm{J}_{1}),f(\bm{J}_{2}),\ldots,f(\bm{J}_{k}))\bm{X}^{-1},

(10.1)

where

{\normalsize f(\bm{J}_{1})}=\footnotesize\begin{bmatrix}f(\lambda_{i})&f^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})&\frac{1}{2!}f^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{i})&\ldots&\frac{1}{(m_{i}-1)!}f^{(m_{i}-1)}(\lambda_{i})\\
0&f(\lambda_{i})&f^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})&\ldots&\frac{1}{(m_{i}-2)!}f^{(m_{i}-2)}(\lambda_{i})\\
0&0&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&f(\lambda_{i})&f^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})\\
0&0&0&\ldots&f(\lambda_{i})\\
\end{bmatrix},

(10.2)

and
f^{(k)}(x)
denotes the
k
-th derivative of
f(x)
.
Therefore, it is easy to extend this to common matrix functions that can be expressed as polynomials as well
(
Zhang 2017
)
:

Powers of a matrix.

\bm{A}^{m}=\bm{X}\bm{J}^{m}\bm{X}^{-1}=\bm{X}f(\bm{J})\bm{X}^{-1}
, where
f(x)=x^{m}
for all
m=1,2,\ldots
.

Matrix logarithm.

Let
f(x)=\ln(1+x)
. Then,

\ln(\bm{I}+\bm{A})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{i-1}}{i}\bm{A}^{i}=\bm{X}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{i-1}}{i}\bm{A}^{i}\right)\bm{X}^{-1}=\bm{X}f(\bm{J})\bm{X}^{-1}.

(10.3)

Sine and cosine functions.

Let
f_{1}(x)=\sin(x)
and
f_{2}(x)=\cos(x)
. Then,

\displaystyle\sin(\bm{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{i}}{(2i+1)!}\bm{A}^{2i+1}=\bm{X}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{i}}{(2i+1)!}\bm{J}^{2i+1}\right)\bm{X}^{-1}=\bm{X}f_{1}(\bm{J})\bm{X}^{-1};

(10.4)

\displaystyle\cos(\bm{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{i}}{(2i)!}\bm{A}^{2i}=\bm{X}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{i}}{(2i)!}\bm{J}^{2i}\right)\bm{X}^{-1}=\bm{X}f_{2}(\bm{J})\bm{X}^{-1}.

(10.5)

Matrix exponentials.

Let
f_{1}(x)=e^{x}
and
f_{2}(x)=e^{-x}
. Then,

\displaystyle e^{\bm{A}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}\bm{A}^{i}=\bm{X}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}\bm{J}^{i}\right)\bm{X}^{-1}=\bm{X}f_{1}(\bm{J})\bm{X}^{-1};

(10.6)

\displaystyle e^{-\bm{A}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}(-1)^{i}\bm{A}^{i}=\bm{X}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}(-1)^{i}\bm{J}^{i}\right)\bm{X}^{-1}=\bm{X}f_{2}(\bm{J})\bm{X}^{-1}.

(10.7)

Matrix exponential functions.

Let
f_{1}(x)=e^{xt}
and
f_{2}(x)=e^{-xt}
. Then,

\displaystyle e^{\bm{A}t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}\bm{A}^{i}t^{i}=\bm{X}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}\bm{J}^{i}t^{i}\right)\bm{X}^{-1}=\bm{X}f_{1}(\bm{J})\bm{X}^{-1};

(10.8)

\displaystyle e^{-\bm{A}t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}(-1)^{i}\bm{A}^{i}t^{i}=\bm{X}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}(-1)^{i}\bm{J}^{i}t^{i}\right)\bm{X}^{-1}=\bm{X}f_{2}(\bm{J})\bm{X}^{-1}.

(10.9)

æ
Chapter 10 Problems
6

10.9.

Given a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
where all entries are equal to 1, find the
n
eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
.

10.9.

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
be an idempotent matrix (i.e.,
\bm{A}^{2}=\bm{A}
). Show that the matrices
\bm{B}\bm{A}
and
\bm{A}\bm{B}\bm{A}
share the same eigenvalues.

10.9.

Consider a Householder transformation matrix
\bm{H}=\bm{I}-2\bm{u}\bm{u}^{\top}\in\real^{n\times n}
, where
\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert=1
. Show that
\bm{u}
is an eigenvector
\bm{H}
and determine its corresponding eigenvalue. Provide a geometric interpretation of the eigenvalues of
\bm{H}
.
Suppose further that
\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{u}=0
, where
\bm{v}
is a nonzero vector. Show that
\bm{v}
is also an eigenvector of
\bm{H}
and find its corresponding eigenvalue.

10.9.

Let
\lambda
be an eigenvalue of
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
. Show that
\lambda-\mu
is an eigenvalue of
\bm{A}-\mu\bm{I}
.

10.9.

Let
\lambda
be an eigenvalue of a nonsingular matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
. Show that
\lambda^{-1}
is an eigenvalue of
\bm{A}^{-1}
.

10.9.

Derive the general formula for
\bm{u}_{K}=\bm{A}^{K}\bm{u}_{0}
, where
\bm{A}
is a general
2\times 2
matrix.

Consider the matrix
\bm{A}=\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\in\real^{n\times n}

n
linearly independent eigenvectors of

\bm{x}_{i}=\mathbf{1}-n\bm{e}_{i}
and
\mathbf{1}
.

\bm{A}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}5&-1&-1\\
-1&5&-1\\
-1&-1&5\end{bmatrix}
?

Many scientific packages address the generalized
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\lambda\bm{B}\bm{x}
, where
\bm{B}
is nonsingular. If

\bm{B}

.

Matrix exponentials.

Chapter 11
Schur Decomposition

11.1
Schur Decomposition

Any
real
square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
with
real
eigenvalues can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{U}\bm{Q}^{\top},

where
\bm{Q}
is a (real) orthogonal matrix, and
\bm{U}
is a (real) upper triangular matrix. In other words, any real square matrix
\bm{A}
with real eigenvalues can be triangularized.

The first columns of
\bm{A}\bm{Q}
and
\bm{Q}\bm{U}
are given by
\bm{A}\bm{q}_{1}
and
u_{11}\bm{q}_{1}
, respectively.
Consequently,
u_{11}
corresponds to an eigenvalue of
\bm{A}
, while
\bm{q}_{1}
serves as its associated eigenvector.
However, the other columns of
\bm{Q}
are not necessarily eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
.

Schur decomposition for symmetric matrices.

For a symmetric matrix
\bm{A}=\bm{A}^{\top}
, the relation
\bm{Q}\bm{U}\bm{Q}^{\top}=\bm{Q}\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{Q}^{\top}
holds. In this case,
\bm{U}
is a diagonal matrix, and this diagonal matrix contains the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
. Furthermore, all columns of
\bm{Q}
are eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
.
Thus, we conclude that all symmetric matrices are diagonalizable, even in the presence of repeated eigenvalues.

11.2
Existence of the Schur Decomposition

To validate Theorem
, we rely on the following lemmas.

Lemma 79
(Determinant Intermezzo).

The determinant of a matrix (Definition
) satisfies the following properties:

•

The determinant of the product of two matrices is given by
\det(\bm{A}\bm{B})=\det(\bm{A})\det(\bm{B})
;

•

The determinant of the transpose of a matrix is the same as the determinant of the original matrix:
\det(\bm{A}^{\top})=\det(\bm{A})
;

•

If matrix
\bm{A}
has an eigenvalue
\lambda
, then
\det(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})=0
;

•

The determinant of the identity matrix is
1
;

•

For an orthogonal matrix
\bm{Q}
, the determinant satisfies:

\det(\bm{Q})=\det(\bm{Q}^{\top})=\pm 1,\qquad\text{since }\det(\bm{Q}^{\top})\det(\bm{Q})=\det(\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{Q})=\det(\bm{I})=1;

•

For any square matrix
\bm{A}
and an orthogonal matrix
\bm{Q}
, the determinant relation holds:

\det(\bm{A})=\det(\bm{Q}^{\top})\det(\bm{A})\det(\bm{Q})=\det(\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Q});

•

For a square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, the determinant of
-\bm{A}
is given by
\det(-\bm{A})=(-1)^{n}\det(\bm{A})
.

Lemma 80
(Submatrix with Same Eigenvalue).

Let
\bm{A}_{k+1}\in\real^{(k+1)\times(k+1)}
be a square matrix with real eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{k+1}
. Then, we can construct a
k\times k
matrix
\bm{A}_{k}
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\ldots,\lambda_{k+1}
as follows:

\bm{A}_{k}=\begin{bmatrix}-\bm{p}_{2}^{\top}-\\
-\bm{p}_{3}^{\top}-\\
\vdots\\
-\bm{p}_{k+1}^{\top}-\end{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{k+1}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{p}_{2}&\bm{p}_{3}&\ldots&\bm{p}_{k+1}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{p}_{1}
is a unit-norm eigenvector of
\bm{A}_{k+1}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
, and
\bm{p}_{2},\bm{p}_{3},\ldots,\bm{p}_{k+1}
denote any mutually orthonormal vectors orthogonal to
\bm{p}_{1}
, i.e.,
\bm{p}_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\perp}([\bm{p}_{2},\bm{p}_{3},\ldots,\bm{p}_{k+1}])
.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Let
\bm{P}_{k+1}=[\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2},\ldots,\bm{p}_{k+1}]
.
It follows that
\bm{P}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{P}_{k+1}=\bm{I}
, and

\bm{P}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k+1}\bm{P}_{k+1}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{A}_{k}\end{bmatrix}.

For any eigenvalue
\lambda\in\{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\ldots,\lambda_{k+1}\}
, by Lemma
, we have

\displaystyle\det(\bm{A}_{k+1}-\lambda\bm{I})

\displaystyle=\det(\bm{P}_{k+1}^{\top}(\bm{A}_{k+1}-\lambda\bm{I})\bm{P}_{k+1})=\det(\bm{P}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k+1}\bm{P}_{k+1}-\lambda\bm{P}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{P}_{k+1})

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}-\lambda&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{A}_{k}-\lambda\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}\right)=(\lambda_{1}-\lambda)\det(\bm{A}_{k}-\lambda\bm{I}).

Since
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
\bm{A}
and
\lambda\neq\lambda_{1}
, then
\det(\bm{A}_{k+1}-\lambda\bm{I})=(\lambda_{1}-\lambda)\det(\bm{A}_{k}-\lambda\bm{I})=0
means
\lambda
is also an eigenvalue of
\bm{A}_{k}
.

We establish the existence of the Schur decomposition using an inductive proof.

Proof
[of Theorem
: Existence of Schur Decomposition]
We begin by noting that the theorem is trivial when
n=1
, as we can simply set
Q=1
and
U=A
. Now, suppose the theorem holds true for
n=k
for some
k>1
. To complete the proof, we must show that the theorem also holds for
n=k+1
.
Assume for
n=k
, the theorem is valid, so that any matrix
\bm{A}_{k}\in\real^{k\times k}
can be expressed as
\bm{A}_{k}=\bm{Q}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{Q}_{k}^{\top}
, where
\bm{Q}_{k}
is orthogonal, and
\bm{U}_{k}
is upper triangular.

For
n=k+1
, let
\bm{A}_{k+1}
be a matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{k+1}
. Using Lemma
, construct an orthogonal matrix
\bm{P}_{k+1}=[\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2},\ldots,\bm{p}_{k+1}]
, where
\bm{p}_{1}
is a unit-norm eigenvector of
\bm{A}_{k+1}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
, and
\bm{p}_{2},\ldots,\bm{p}_{k+1}
are mutually orthonormal vectors orthogonal to
\bm{p}_{1}
.
Since we assume the theorem is true for
n=k
, we can find a matrix
\bm{A}_{k}\in\real^{k\times k}
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\ldots,\lambda_{k+1}
, satisfying
\bm{A}_{k}=\bm{Q}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{Q}_{k}^{\top}
.
By Lemma
, the following properties hold:

\bm{P}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k+1}\bm{P}_{k+1}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{A}_{k}\end{bmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{A}_{k+1}\bm{P}_{k+1}=\bm{P}_{k+1}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{A}_{k}\end{bmatrix}.

Let

\bm{Q}_{k+1}=\bm{P}_{k+1}\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}_{k}\end{bmatrix}.

Then, it follows that

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{k+1}\bm{Q}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{k+1}\bm{P}_{k+1}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}_{k}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{P}_{k+1}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{A}_{k}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}_{k}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{P}_{k+1}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{A}_{k}\bm{Q}_{k}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\bm{P}_{k+1}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{P}_{k+1}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}_{k}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{U}_{k}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{Q}_{k+1}\bm{U}_{k+1},

where we let
\bm{U}_{k+1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{U}_{k}\end{bmatrix}
.
Therefore,
\bm{A}_{k+1}=\bm{Q}_{k+1}\bm{U}_{k+1}\bm{Q}_{k+1}^{\top}
, where
\bm{U}_{k+1}
is an upper triangular matrix, and
\bm{Q}_{k+1}
is an orthogonal matrix since
\bm{P}_{k+1}
and

\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Q}_{k}\end{bmatrix}
are both orthogonal matrices.

11.3
Other Forms of the Schur Decomposition

In the proof of the Schur decomposition, the upper triangular matrix
\bm{U}_{k+1}
is constructed by appending the eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
to
\bm{U}_{k}
. This ensures that the diagonal elements consistently represent the eigenvalues of the matrix. Consequently, we can decompose the upper triangular matrix into two distinct components.

Any real matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
with real eigenvalues can be decomposed as

\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Q}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}+\bm{T}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\bm{A}=\bm{Q}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}+\bm{T})\bm{Q}^{\top},

where
\bm{Q}
is an orthogonal matrix,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
is a diagonal matrix containing the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
, and
\bm{T}
is a
strictly upper triangular
matrix (with zeros on the diagonal).

A strictly upper triangular matrix is an upper triangular matrix in which all diagonal and lower-triangular entries are zero.
Another proof of this decomposition is that
\bm{A}
and
\bm{U}
(where
\bm{U}=\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Q}
) are similar matrices so that they have the same eigenvalues (Proposition
).
Moreover, the eigenvalues of any upper triangular matrices are on the diagonal.
To see this, for any upper triangular matrix
\bm{R}\in\real^{n\times n}
, where the diagonal values are
r_{ii}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. We have

\bm{R}\bm{e}_{i}=r_{ii}\bm{e}_{i},

where
\bm{e}_{i}
is the
i
-th basis vector in
n
.
Thus, we can decompose
\bm{U}
into the sum of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
and
\bm{T}
.

Remark 81
(
m
-th Power).

The above observation shows that the eigenvalues of the
m
-th power
\bm{A}^{m}
are the
m
-th powers of the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
.

A final observation about the second form of the Schur decomposition is as follows. From the equation
\bm{A}\bm{Q}=\bm{Q}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}+\bm{T})
, it follows that

\bm{A}\bm{q}_{k}=\lambda_{k}\bm{q}_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}t_{ik}\bm{q}_{i},

where
t_{ik}
is the (
i,k
)-th entry of
\bm{T}
. The form is quite similar to the eigenvalue decomposition. However, instead of being eigenvectors, the columns of
\bm{Q}
are orthonormal bases that are correlated with each other.

In the main result of Theorem
, we focus on real matrices with real eigenvalues. However, this restriction is not always practical in various scenarios. A more general case is addressed in the following theorem.
This decomposition is attributed to
Issai Schur
(1875–1941), a Russian mathematician who spent most of his life studying and working in Germany.

Let
\bm{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
be any
complex
square matrix. Then, it can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{U}\bm{T}\bm{U}^{\ast},

where
\bm{U}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
is a unitary matrix, and
\bm{T}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
is an upper triangular matrix (not necessarily real).

æ

; (
