Chapter 12
Spectral Decomposition (Theorem)

12.1
Spectral Decomposition (Theorem)

The spectral theorem, also known as the spectral decomposition for symmetric matrices, states that symmetric matrices have real eigenvalues and can be diagonalized using a (real) orthonormal basis . In the following sections, we will present the main result and defer detailed discussions.

A real matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is symmetric if and only if there exists an orthogonal matrix
\bm{Q}
and a diagonal matrix
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
such that

\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top},

where the columns of
\bm{Q}=[\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{n}]
are eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
and are mutually orthonormal, and the entries of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
are the corresponding eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
, which are real. This is known as the
spectral decomposition
or
spectral theorem
for real symmetric matrices. Specifically, we have the following properties:

1.

A symmetric matrix has only
real eigenvalues
.

2.

The eigenvectors are orthogonal and can be chosen to be
orthonormal
by normalization.

3.

The rank of
\bm{A}
is equal to the number of nonzero eigenvalues.

4.

If the eigenvalues are distinct, the eigenvectors are linearly independent.

In the eigenvalue decomposition (Theorem
), we require the matrix
\bm{A}
to be square and the eigenvectors to be linearly independent. In contrast, the spectral theorem applies to any symmetric matrix, and the eigenvectors are chosen to be orthonormal.

In Proposition
, we proved that similar matrices have the same eigenvalues. From the spectral decomposition, we observe that
\bm{A}
and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
are similar matrices and thus have the same eigenvalues. For any diagonal matrix, the eigenvalues are the diagonal entries. To see this, note that

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{e}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\bm{e}_{i},

where
\bm{e}_{i}
is the
i
-th basis vector. Therefore, the matrix
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
contains the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
.

12.2
Existence of the Spectral Decomposition

We will prove the theorem in several steps.

Symmetric Matrix Property 1 of 4

Lemma 82
(Real Eigenvalues).

The eigenvalues of any symmetric matrix are all real.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Suppose an eigenvalue
\lambda
is a complex number, expressed as
\lambda=a+ib
, where
a
and
b
are real numbers. Its complex conjugate is
\bar{\lambda}=a-ib
. Similarly, for the corresponding complex eigenvector
\bm{x}=\bm{c}+i\bm{d}
, its complex conjugate is
\bar{\bm{x}}=\bm{c}-i\bm{d}
, where
\bm{c}
and
\bm{d}
are real vectors. The following properties hold:

\bm{A}\bm{x}=\lambda\bm{x}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{ leads to }}\qquad\bm{A}\bar{\bm{x}}=\bar{\lambda}\bar{\bm{x}}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{ transpose to }}\qquad\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{A}=\bar{\lambda}\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}.

WTaking the dot product of the first equation with
\bar{\bm{x}}
and the last equation with
\bm{x}
, we get:

\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\lambda\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{x}\qquad\text{and }\qquad\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bar{\lambda}\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{x}.

Equating these, we find
\lambda\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{x}=\bar{\lambda}\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{x}
. Since
\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{x}=(\bm{c}-i\bm{d})^{\top}(\bm{c}+i\bm{d})=\bm{c}^{\top}\bm{c}+\bm{d}^{\top}\bm{d}
, this is a real number.
Therefore, the imaginary part of
\lambda
must be zero, implying
\lambda
is real.

Symmetric Matrix Property 2 of 4

Lemma 83
(Orthogonal Eigenvectors).

The eigenvectors corresponding to distinct eigenvalues of any symmetric matrix are orthogonal. Therefore, these eigenvectors can be normalized to form an orthonormal set because
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\lambda\bm{x}\underrightarrow{\text{ leads to }}\bm{A}\frac{\bm{x}}{\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert}=\lambda\frac{\bm{x}}{\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert}
, which preserves the eigenvalue
\lambda
.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Let eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
correspond to eigenvectors
\bm{x}_{1}
and
\bm{x}_{2}
, respectively, such that
\bm{A}\bm{x}_{1}=\lambda\bm{x}_{1}
and
\bm{A}\bm{x}_{2}=\lambda_{2}\bm{x}_{2}
. The following equalities hold:

\bm{A}\bm{x}_{1}=\lambda_{1}\bm{x}_{1}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\bm{x}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}=\lambda_{1}\bm{x}_{1}^{\top}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\bm{x}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{2}=\lambda_{1}\bm{x}_{1}^{\top}\bm{x}_{2},

and

\bm{A}\bm{x}_{2}=\lambda_{2}\bm{x}_{2}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\bm{x}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{2}=\lambda_{2}\bm{x}_{1}^{\top}\bm{x}_{2}.

Equating these, we get
\lambda_{1}\bm{x}_{1}^{\top}\bm{x}_{2}=\lambda_{2}\bm{x}_{1}^{\top}\bm{x}_{2}
. Since eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}
, the eigenvectors are orthogonal.

In Lemma
, we proved that the eigenvectors corresponding to distinct eigenvalues of symmetric matrices are orthogonal. More generally, we now prove a significant result: eigenvectors corresponding to distinct eigenvalues of any matrix are linearly independent.

Theorem 84
(Independent Eigenvector Theorem).

If a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
has
k
distinct eigenvalues, then any set of
k
corresponding (nonzero) eigenvectors are linearly independent.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
We prove the theorem by induction. First, we show that any two eigenvectors corresponding to distinct eigenvalues are linearly independent. Let eigenvectors
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
correspond to distinct eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
, respectively.
Assume, for contradiction, that
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
are linearly dependent. Then there exist a nonzero vector
\bm{x}=[x_{1},x_{2}]\neq\mathbf{0}
such that

x_{1}\bm{v}_{1}+x_{2}\bm{v}_{2}=\mathbf{0}.

(12.1)

Multiplying Equation (
) on the left by
\bm{A}
gives:

x_{1}\lambda_{1}\bm{v}_{1}+x_{2}\lambda_{2}\bm{v}_{2}=\mathbf{0}.

(12.2)

Similarly, multiplying Equation (
) by
\lambda_{2}
yields:

x_{1}\lambda_{2}\bm{v}_{1}+x_{2}\lambda_{2}\bm{v}_{2}=\mathbf{0}.

(12.3)

Subtracting the first equation from the second gives:

x_{1}(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})\bm{v}_{1}=\mathbf{0}.

Since
\lambda_{2}\neq\lambda_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{1}\neq\mathbf{0}
, it must be that
x_{1}=0
. Since
\bm{v}_{2}\neq\mathbf{0}
, substituting
x_{1}=0
back into Equation (
) shows that
x_{2}=0
, leading to a contradiction. Thus,
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
are linearly independent.

Suppose that any
j<k
eigenvectors corresponding to distinct eigenvalues are linearly independent. We aim to show that any
j+1
eigenvectors are also linearly independent.
Let
\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{j}
be linearly independent eigenvectors corresponding to distinct eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{j}
.
Assume, for contradiction, that an additional eigenvector
\bm{v}_{j+1}
, corresponding to a different eigenvalue
\lambda_{j+1}
, is linearly dependent on
\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{j}
. Then there exist scalars
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{j}
, not all zero, such that:

\bm{v}_{j+1}=x_{1}\bm{v}_{1}+x_{2}\bm{v}_{2}+\ldots+x_{j}\bm{v}_{j}.

(12.4)

Multiplying Equation (
) on the left by
\bm{A}
gives:

\lambda_{j+1}\bm{v}_{j+1}=x_{1}\lambda_{1}\bm{v}_{1}+x_{2}\lambda_{2}\bm{v}_{2}+\ldots+x_{j}\lambda_{j}\bm{v}_{j}.

(12.5)

Similarly, multiplying Equation (
) by
\lambda_{j+1}
yields:

\lambda_{j+1}\bm{v}_{j+1}=x_{1}\lambda_{j+1}\bm{v}_{1}+x_{2}\lambda_{j+1}\bm{v}_{2}+\ldots+x_{j}\lambda_{j+1}\bm{v}_{j}.

(12.6)

Subtracting the two equations gives:

x_{1}(\lambda_{j+1}-\lambda_{1})\bm{v}_{1}+x_{2}(\lambda_{j+1}-\lambda_{2})\bm{v}_{2}+\ldots+x_{j}(\lambda_{j+1}-\lambda_{j})\bm{v}_{j}=\mathbf{0}.

From the assumption,
\lambda_{j+1}\neq\lambda_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,j\}
, and
\bm{v}_{i}\neq\mathbf{0}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,j\}
. We must have
x_{1}=x_{2}=\ldots=x_{j}=0
, which leads to a contradiction. Thus, the eigenvectors
\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{j},\bm{v}_{j+1}
are linearly independent. By induction, any
k
eigenvectors corresponding to
k
distinct eigenvalues are linearly independent.

An immediate consequence of the above theorem is as follows:

Corollary 85
(Independent Eigenvector Theorem, CNT.).

If a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
has
n
distinct eigenvalues, then any set of
n
corresponding eigenvectors form a basis for
n
.

Symmetric Matrix Property 3 of 4

Lemma 86
(Orthonormal Eigenvectors for Duplicate Eigenvalue).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
be symmetric.
If
\bm{A}
has a repeated eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
with multiplicity
k\geq 2
, then there exist
k
orthonormal eigenvectors corresponding to
\lambda_{i}
.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
First, note that there exists at least one eigenvector
\bm{x}_{i1}
corresponding to
\lambda_{i}
.
For this eigenvector
\bm{x}_{i1}
, we can always find
n-1
additional orthonormal vectors
\bm{y}_{2},\bm{y}_{3},\ldots,\bm{y}_{n}
, such that the set
\{\bm{x}_{i1},\bm{y}_{2},\bm{y}_{3},\ldots,\bm{y}_{n}\}
forms an orthonormal basis of
n
.
Define the matrices
\bm{Y}_{1}
and
\bm{P}_{1}
as follows:

\bm{Y}_{1}=[\bm{y}_{2},\bm{y}_{3},\ldots,\bm{y}_{n}]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{P}_{1}=[\bm{x}_{i1},\bm{Y}_{1}].

Since
\bm{A}
is symmetric, we then have

\bm{P}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}_{1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{Y}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{1}\end{bmatrix}.

Since
\bm{P}_{1}
is nonsingular and orthogonal,
\bm{A}
and
\bm{P}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}_{1}
are similar matrices such that they have the same eigenvalues (see Proposition
). Consequently,

\det(\bm{P}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}_{1}-\lambda\bm{I}_{n})=\penalty\ (\lambda_{i}-\lambda)\det(\bm{Y}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{1}-\lambda\bm{I}_{n-1}).

If
\lambda_{i}
has multiplicity
k\geq 2
, the term
(\lambda_{i}-\lambda)
appears
k
times in the polynomial from the determinant
\det(\bm{P}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}_{1}-\lambda\bm{I}_{n})
, i.e., the term appears
k-1
times in the polynomial from
\det(\bm{Y}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{1}-\lambda\bm{I}_{n-1})
.
Thus,
\det(\bm{Y}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{1}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I}_{n-1})=0
, and
\lambda_{i}
is an eigenvalue of
\bm{Y}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{1}
with multiplicity
k-1
.

Define
\bm{B}=\bm{Y}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{1}
. Since
\det(\bm{B}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I}_{n-1})=0
, the null space of
\bm{B}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I}_{n-1}
is nontrivial. Suppose
(\bm{B}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I}_{n-1})\bm{n}=\mathbf{0}
, i.e.,
\bm{B}\bm{n}=\lambda_{i}\bm{n}
, where
\bm{n}
is an eigenvector of
\bm{B}
.

From
\bm{P}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}_{1}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{B}\end{bmatrix},

we have
\bm{A}\bm{P}_{1}\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}z\\
\bm{n}\end{bmatrix}\normalsize=\bm{P}_{1}\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{B}\end{bmatrix}\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}z\\
\bm{n}\end{bmatrix}
, where
z
is any scalar. From the left side of this equation, we have

\displaystyle\bm{A}\bm{P}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}z\\
\bm{n}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}\bm{x}_{i1},\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}z\\
\bm{n}\end{bmatrix}=\lambda_{i}z\bm{x}_{i1}+\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{1}\bm{n}.

(12.7)

From the right side of the equation, we have

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{B}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}z\\
\bm{n}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{x}_{i1}&\bm{Y}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{B}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}z\\
\bm{n}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}\bm{x}_{i1}&\bm{Y}_{1}\bm{B}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}z\\
\bm{n}\end{bmatrix}

(12.8)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i}z\bm{x}_{i1}+\bm{Y}_{1}\bm{B}\bm{n}=\lambda_{i}z\bm{x}_{i1}+\lambda_{i}\bm{Y}_{1}\bm{n},

where the last equality follows from
\bm{B}\bm{n}=\lambda_{i}\bm{n}
.
Combining Equations (
) and (
), we obtain

\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{1}\bm{n}=\lambda_{i}\bm{Y}_{1}\bm{n},

which means
\bm{Y}_{1}\bm{n}
is an eigenvector of
\bm{A}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
(the same eigenvalue corresponding to
\bm{x}_{i1}
). Since
\bm{Y}_{1}\bm{n}
is a linear combination of
\bm{y}_{2},\bm{y}_{3},\ldots,\bm{y}_{n}
, which are orthonormal to
\bm{x}_{i1}
, it can be chosen to be orthonormal to
\bm{x}_{i1}
.

To conclude, if there exists an eigenvector,
\bm{x}_{i1}
, corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
, with a multiplicity
k\geq 2
, we can construct a second eigenvector by selecting a vector from the null space of
(\bm{B}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I}_{n-1})
, as outlined above. Assume that we have constructed this second eigenvector,
\bm{x}_{i2}
, which is orthonormal to
\bm{x}_{i1}
.
With these eigenvectors,
\bm{x}_{i1}
and
\bm{x}_{i2}
, we can always find
n-2
additional orthonormal vectors
\bm{y}_{3},\bm{y}_{4},\ldots,\bm{y}_{n}
, such that the set
\{\bm{x}_{i1},\bm{x}_{i2},\bm{y}_{3},\bm{y}_{4},\ldots,\bm{y}_{n}\}
forms an orthonormal basis for
n
.
Arrange these vectors
\bm{y}_{3},\bm{y}_{4},\ldots,\bm{y}_{n}
into matrix
\bm{Y}_{2}
and
\{\bm{x}_{i1},\bm{x}_{i2},\bm{y}_{3},\bm{y}_{4},\ldots,\bm{y}_{n}\}
into matrix
\bm{P}_{2}
:

\bm{Y}_{2}=[\bm{y}_{3},\bm{y}_{4},\ldots,\bm{y}_{n}]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{P}_{2}=[\bm{x}_{i1},\bm{x}_{i2},\bm{Y}_{1}].

Since
\bm{A}
is symmetric, we then have

\bm{P}_{2}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}&0&\mathbf{0}\\
0&\lambda_{i}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\bm{Y}_{2}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}&0&\mathbf{0}\\
0&\lambda_{i}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\bm{C}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{C}=\bm{Y}_{2}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{2}
such that
\det(\bm{P}_{2}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}_{2}-\lambda\bm{I}_{n})=(\lambda_{i}-\lambda)^{2}\det(\bm{C}-\lambda\bm{I}_{n-2})
. If the multiplicity of
\lambda_{i}
is
k\geq 3
, then
\det(\bm{C}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I}_{n-2})=0
, implying the null space of
\bm{C}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I}_{n-2}
is non-empty. From the null space of
\bm{C}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I}_{n-2}
, we can select a vector
\bm{n}
such that
\bm{C}\bm{n}=\lambda_{i}\bm{n}
. Using this vector, we construct
\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}z_{1}\\
z_{2}\\
\bm{n}\end{bmatrix}\in\real^{n}
, where
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are any scalar values, such that

\bm{A}\bm{P}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}z_{1}\\
z_{2}\\
\bm{n}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{P}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}&0&\mathbf{0}\\
0&\lambda_{i}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\bm{C}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}z_{1}\\
z_{2}\\
\bm{n}\end{bmatrix}.

Similarly, from the left side of the above equation, we will get
\lambda_{i}z_{1}\bm{x}_{i1}+\lambda_{i}z_{2}\bm{x}_{i2}+\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{2}\bm{n}
. From the right side of the above equation, we will get
\lambda_{i}z_{1}\bm{x}_{i1}+\lambda_{i}z_{2}\bm{x}_{i2}+\lambda_{i}\bm{Y}_{2}\bm{n}
. As a result,

\bm{A}\bm{Y}_{2}\bm{n}=\lambda_{i}\bm{Y}_{2}\bm{n},

where
\bm{Y}_{2}\bm{n}
is an eigenvector of
\bm{A}
, orthogonal to
\bm{x}_{i1}
and
\bm{x}_{i2}
. This eigenvector can also be normalized to ensure orthonormality with the first two eigenvectors.

By iterating this process, we can construct
k
orthonormal eigenvectors corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
.

Finally, the dimension of the null space of
\bm{P}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}_{1}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I}_{n}
equals the multiplicity
k
. This confirms that if
\lambda_{i}
has a multiplicity of
k
, there cannot be more than
k
orthonormal eigenvectors corresponding to
\lambda_{i}
. Otherwise, we would obtain more than
n
orthogonal eigenvectors in
n
, which is a contradiction.

The existence of the spectral decomposition can be straightforwardly established using the lemmas previously discussed. Another method to demonstrate its existence is through the application of the Schur decomposition.

Proof
[of Theorem
: Existence of Spectral Decomposition, Alternative Proof]
According to Theorem
, a symmetric matrix
\bm{A}=\bm{A}^{\top}
can be decomposed as
\bm{Q}\bm{U}\bm{Q}^{\top}=\bm{Q}\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{Q}^{\top}
, where
\bm{Q}
is orthogonal and
\bm{U}
is upper triangular. This implies
\bm{U}
is a diagonal matrix, and this diagonal matrix contains eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
. Consequently, the columns of
\bm{Q}
serve as the eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
. We thus conclude that every symmetric matrix can be orthogonally diagonalized, even in cases of repeated eigenvalues.

For a more concise proof,
Lu 2021c
offers an approach utilizing the Gram-Schmidt process combined with mathematical induction.

For a symmetric matrix
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
, the rank remains unchanged from that of
\bm{A}
, a property we will utilize in proving the singular value decomposition in the following chapter.
However, in general, the rank of a product of two matrices does not exceed the rank of either matrix.

Lemma 87
(Rank of
\bm{A}\bm{B}
).

Given matrices
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
and
\bm{B}\in\real^{n\times k}
, the rank of their product
\bm{A}\bm{B}\in\real^{m\times k}
satisfies
\mathrm{rank}
(
\bm{A}\bm{B}
)
\leq\min\{\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}),\mathrm{rank}(\bm{B})\}
.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Considering the matrix multiplication
\bm{A}\bm{B}
:

•

Each row of
\bm{A}\bm{B}
is a linear combinations of the rows of
\bm{B}
, implying that the row space of
\bm{A}\bm{B}
is contained within that of
\bm{B}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{rank}
(
\bm{A}\bm{B}
)
\leq
\mathrm{rank}
(
\bm{B}
).

•

Similarly, each column of
\bm{A}\bm{B}
is a linear combinations of the columns of
\bm{A}
, so the column space of
\bm{A}\bm{B}
is contained within that of
\bm{A}
. Hence,
\mathrm{rank}
(
\bm{A}\bm{B}
)
\leq
\mathrm{rank}
(
\bm{A}
).

Combining these observations, it follows that
\mathrm{rank}
(
\bm{A}\bm{B}
)
\leq\min\{\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}),\mathrm{rank}(\bm{B})\}
.

Symmetric Matrix Property 4 of 4

Lemma 88
(Rank of Symmetric Matrices).

If
\bm{A}
is an
n\times n
real symmetric matrix, then
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})
=
the total number of nonzero eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
.
In particular,
\bm{A}
has full rank if and only if
\bm{A}
is nonsingular. Furthermore,
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
is the linear space spanned by the eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
corresponding to nonzero eigenvalues.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
For any symmetric matrix
\bm{A}
, it can be expressed in spectral form as
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}
, where
\bm{Q}
is an orthogonal matrix and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is a diagonal matrix containing the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
.
Using Lemma
, we have:

•

From
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}
, we have
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})\leq\mathrm{rank}(\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda})\leq\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})
.

•

From
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Q}
, we have
\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})\leq\mathrm{rank}(\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A})\leq\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})
.

This implies
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})
, which is the total number of nonzero eigenvalues.

Since
\bm{A}
is nonsingular if and only if all its eigenvalues are nonzero, we can affirm that
\bm{A}
has full rank if and only if
\bm{A}
is nonsingular.

Analogous to eigenvalue decomposition, the spectral decomposition enables efficient computation of the
m
-th power of a matrix
\bm{A}
.

Remark 89
(
m
-th Power).

If
\bm{A}
admits a spectral decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}
, then the
m
-th power of
\bm{A}
can be computed as
\bm{A}^{m}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{m}\bm{Q}^{\top}
.

12.3
Uniqueness of Spectral Decomposition

It’s important to note that spectral decomposition is not unique, largely because of the multiplicity of eigenvalues. When two or more eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
and
\lambda_{j}
(for
1\leq i,j\leq n
) are identical, swapping their corresponding eigenvectors in the orthogonal matrix
\bm{Q}
results in an equivalent spectral decomposition, even though the specific form of the decomposition changes.

However, the
eigenspaces
associated with each eigenvalue—specifically, the null spaces
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I})
for each eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
—remain fixed. This means that while the choice of eigenvectors within each eigenspace can vary, leading to different decompositions, the decomposition in terms of eigenspaces is unique. Any orthonormal basis for these eigenspaces can be selected without altering the overall spectral decomposition.

12.4
Other Forms, Connecting Eigenvalue Decomposition*

In this section, we explore different forms of spectral decomposition under various conditions. To facilitate this discussion, we first rigorously define the characteristic polynomial.

Definition 90
(Characteristic Polynomial).

For any square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, its characteristic polynomial is defined as:

\displaystyle\det(\lambda\bm{I}-\bm{A})

\displaystyle=\lambda^{n}+\gamma_{n-1}\lambda^{n-1}+\ldots+\gamma_{1}\lambda+\gamma_{0}=(\lambda-\lambda_{1})^{k_{1}}(\lambda-\lambda_{2})^{k_{2}}\ldots(\lambda-\lambda_{m})^{k_{m}},

where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{m}
are the distinct roots of
\det(\lambda\bm{I}-\bm{A})=0
, which are also the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
. The sum of the multiplicities
k_{1}+k_{2}+\ldots+k_{m}=n
, indicating,
\det(\lambda\bm{I}-\bm{A})
is a polynomial of degree
n
for any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
(see proof of Lemma
).
The equation
\det(\lambda\bm{I}-\bm{A})=0
is referred to as the characteristic equation of
\bm{A}
.

The characteristic polynomial plays a crucial role in defining two important concepts: algebraic multiplicity and geometric multiplicity.

Definition 91
(Algebraic Multiplicity and Geometric Multiplicity).

Given the characteristic polynomial of a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
:

\displaystyle\det(\lambda\bm{I}-\bm{A})=(\lambda-\lambda_{1})^{k_{1}}(\lambda-\lambda_{2})^{k_{2}}\ldots(\lambda-\lambda_{m})^{k_{m}},

the integer
k_{i}
is called the algebraic multiplicity of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
, i.e., it equals the multiplicity of the corresponding root of the characteristic polynomial.

The eigenspace associated to eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
is the null space of
(\bm{A}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I})
, denoted by
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I})
.
And the dimension of the eigenspace associated with
\lambda_{i}
,
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I})
, is known as the geometric multiplicity of
\lambda_{i}
.

For brevity, we denote the algebraic multiplicity of
\lambda_{i}
by
alg(\lambda_{i})
and its geometric multiplicity by
geo(\lambda_{i})
.

Remark 92
(Geometric Multiplicity).

For a matrix
\bm{A}
and its eigenspace
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I})
corresponding to an eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
, the dimension of the eigenspace reflects the number of linearly independent eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
associated with
\lambda_{i}
. This means that while there are infinitely many eigenvectors associated with each eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
, they form a subspace that can be described using a finite set of basis vectors. In other words, the geometric multiplicity indicates the maximum number of linearly independent eigenvectors available for
\lambda_{i}
.

By definition, the sum of the algebraic multiplicities of all eigenvalues equals
n
, whereas the sum of the geometric multiplicities can be strictly smaller.

Corollary 93
(Multiplicity in Similar Matrices).

Similar matrices share the same algebraic and geometric multiplicities for their eigenvalues.

Proof
[of Corollary
]
From Proposition
, we know that similar matrices have identical eigenvalues, which implies they also share the same algebraic multiplicities.

Consider two similar matrices
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}=\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{-1}
, where
\bm{P}
is nonsingular. Suppose the geometric multiplicity of an eigenvalue
\lambda
of
\bm{A}
is
k
. This means there exist
k
linearly independent eigenvectors
\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{k}
forming a basis for the eigenspace
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})
such that
\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}=\lambda\bm{v}_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
. Then,
\bm{w}_{i}=\bm{P}\bm{v}_{i}
’s are the eigenvectors of
\bm{B}
associated with
\lambda
.
Since
\bm{P}
is nonsingular, these
\bm{w}_{i}
’s are also linearly independent. Thus, the dimension of the eigenspace
\mathcal{N}(\bm{B}-\lambda\bm{I})
is at least
k
, implying
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I}))\leq\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{B}-\lambda\bm{I}))
.

Conversely, if we start with a set of
k
linearly independent eigenvectors
\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2},\ldots,\bm{w}_{k}
for
\bm{B}
corresponding to
\lambda
, then the vectors
\bm{v}_{i}=\bm{P}^{-1}\bm{w}_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
are eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
associated with
\lambda
. This gives us
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{B}-\lambda\bm{I}))\leq\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I}))
.

By combining both inequalities, we conclude that
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I}))=\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{B}-\lambda\bm{I}))
, establishing the equality of geometric multiplicities for similar matrices.

Lemma 94
(Bounded Geometric Multiplicity).

For any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
and its eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
, the geometric multiplicity is bounded by the algebraic multiplicity:

geo(\lambda_{i})\leq alg(\lambda_{i}).

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Suppose
\bm{P}_{1}=[\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{k}]
contains a set of linearly independent eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
associated with
\lambda_{i}
. That is, the
k
vectors form a basis for the eigenspace
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I})
, and the geometric multiplicity associated with
\lambda_{i}
is
k
.
Extend
\bm{P}_{1}
to a full basis

\bm{P}=[\bm{P}_{1},\bm{P}_{2}]=[\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{k},\bm{v}_{k+1},\ldots,\bm{v}_{n}],

where
\bm{P}
is nonsingular. Then we have
\bm{A}\bm{P}=[\lambda_{i}\bm{P}_{1},\bm{A}\bm{P}_{2}]
.

Construct a matrix
\bm{B}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}\bm{I}_{k}&\bm{C}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{D}\end{bmatrix}
, where
\bm{A}\bm{P}_{2}=\bm{P}_{1}\bm{C}+\bm{P}_{2}\bm{D}
, then
\bm{P}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{B}
such that
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
are similar matrices.
We can always find such matrices
\bm{C}
and
\bm{D}
satisfying above condition, since
\bm{v}_{i}
’s are linearly independent vectors spanning the entire space
n
, and any column of
\bm{A}\bm{P}_{2}
belongs to the column space of
\bm{P}=[\bm{P}_{1},\bm{P}_{2}]
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\det(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})

\displaystyle=\det(\bm{P}^{-1})\det(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})\det(\bm{P})=\det(\bm{P}^{-1}(\bm{A}-\lambda\bm{I})\bm{P})=\det(\bm{B}-\lambda\bm{I})

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda)\bm{I}_{k}&\bm{C}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{D}-\lambda\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}\right)=(\lambda_{i}-\lambda)^{k}\det(\bm{D}-\lambda\bm{I}).

This implies

geo(\lambda_{i})\leq alg(\lambda_{i}).

And we complete the proof.

Building on the proof of Lemma
, it becomes clear that symmetric matrices exhibit equal algebraic and geometric multiplicities for all eigenvalues. Such matrices are termed simple matrices.

Definition 95
(Simple Matrix).

A matrix is termed simple if its algebraic multiplicity equals its geometric multiplicity for each eigenvalue.

Definition 96
(Diagonalizable).

A square matrix
\bm{A}
is considered diagonalizable if there exists a nonsingular matrix
\bm{P}
and a diagonal matrix
\bm{D}
such that
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{D}\bm{P}^{-1}
.

Diagonal matrices have a straightforward structure, which simplifies computations involving determinants and inverses. The eigenvalue decomposition (Theorem
) and spectral decomposition (Theorem
) are examples of diagonalization techniques for specific classes of matrices.

Lemma 97
(Simple Matrices are Diagonalizable).

A matrix is simple if and only if it is diagonalizable.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Suppose that
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is a simple matrix, meaning that the algebraic and geometric multiplicities for each eigenvalue are equal.
For a specific eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
, let
\{\bm{v}_{1}^{i},\bm{v}_{2}^{i},\ldots,\bm{v}_{k_{i}}^{i}\}
be a basis for the eigenspace
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}-\lambda_{i}\bm{I})
.
In other words,
\{\bm{v}_{1}^{i},\bm{v}_{2}^{i},\ldots,\bm{v}_{k_{i}}^{i}\}
is a set of linearly independent eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
associated with
\lambda_{i}
, where
{k_{i}}
is the algebraic or geometric multiplicity of
\lambda_{i}
:
alg(\lambda_{i})=geo(\lambda_{i})=k_{i}
. Suppose there are
m
distinct eigenvalues.
Since
k_{1}+k_{2}+\ldots+k_{m}=n
, the set of eigenvectors consists of the union of
n
vectors.
Consider a linear combination of these eigenvectors:

\bm{z}=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{1}}x_{j}^{1}\bm{v}_{j}^{1}+\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}}x_{j}^{2}\bm{v}_{j}^{2}+\ldots+\sum_{j=1}^{k_{m}}x_{j}^{m}\bm{v}_{j}^{m}=\mathbf{0}.

(12.9)

Let
\bm{w}^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}x_{j}^{i}\bm{v}_{j}^{i}
. Then
\bm{w}^{i}
is either an eigenvector associated with
\lambda_{i}
or the zero vector. Therefore,
\bm{z}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bm{w}^{i}
is a sum of either zero vectors or eigenvectors associated with different eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
. Since eigenvectors associated with different eigenvalues are linearly independent. We must have
\bm{w}^{i}=\mathbf{0}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,m\}
. That is,

\bm{w}^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}x_{j}^{i}\bm{v}_{j}^{i}=\mathbf{0},\qquad\text{for all $i\in\{1,2,\ldots,m\}$}.

Since we assume the eigenvectors
\bm{v}_{j}^{i}
’s associated with
\lambda_{i}
are linearly independent, we must have
x_{j}^{i}=0
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,m\},j\in\{1,2,\ldots,k_{i}\}
. Thus, the
n
vectors are linearly independent:

\{\bm{v}_{1}^{1},\bm{v}_{2}^{1},\ldots,\bm{v}_{k_{i}}^{1}\},\{\bm{v}_{1}^{2},\bm{v}_{2}^{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{k_{i}}^{2}\},\ldots,\{\bm{v}_{1}^{m},\bm{v}_{2}^{m},\ldots,\bm{v}_{k_{i}}^{m}\}.

According to the eigenvalue decomposition presented in Theorem
, matrix
\bm{A}
can be diagonalizable.

Conversely, suppose
\bm{A}
is diagonalizable. That is, there exists a nonsingular matrix
\bm{P}
and a diagonal matrix
\bm{D}
such that
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\bm{D}\bm{P}^{-1}
.
\bm{A}
and
\bm{D}
are similar matrices such that they have the same eigenvalues (Proposition
), same algebraic multiplicities, and geometric multiplicities (Corollary
). It can be readily verified that a diagonal matrix has equal algebraic and geometric multiplicities. Therefore,
\bm{A}
is a simple matrix.

Remark 98
(Equivalence on Diagonalization).

From Theorem
that any eigenvectors corresponding to different eigenvalues are linearly independent, and Remark
that the geometric multiplicity is the dimension of the eigenspace. We realize, if the geometric multiplicity is equal to the algebraic multiplicity (for all eigenvalues), the eigenspaces can span the entire space
n
for a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
. Hence, the above lemma equivalently claims that if the eigenspaces span the entire space
n
, then
\bm{A}
can be diagonalized.

Corollary 99
.

A square matrix
\bm{A}
is considered simple if it has a complete set of linearly independent eigenvectors. Alternatively, any symmetric matrix
\bm{A}
also qualifies as a simple matrix by definition.

The proof of this corollary follows directly from the eigenvalue decomposition outlined in Theorem
and the spectral theorem detailed in Theorem
.

We now present an alternative expression for the spectral decomposition:

For a
simple matrix

\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, we can express it as a weighted sum of idempotent matrices:

\bm{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\bm{A}_{i},

where each
\lambda_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, represents an eigenvalue of
\bm{A}
(potentially repeated).
The idempotent matrices
\bm{A}_{i}
satisfy the following properties:

1. Idempotent:
\bm{A}_{i}^{2}=\bm{A}_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
;

2. Orthogonal:
\bm{A}_{i}\bm{A}_{j}=\mathbf{0}
for all
i\neq j
;

3. Additivity:
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{A}_{i}=\bm{I}_{n}
;

4. Rank-Additivity:
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{1})+\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{2})+\ldots+\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{n})=n
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Given that
\bm{A}
is a simple matrix, according to Lemma
, there exists a nonsingular matrix
\bm{P}
and a diagonal matrix
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
such that
\bm{A}=\bm{P}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{P}^{-1}
, where
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
,
\lambda_{i}
’s are eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
, and the columns of
\bm{P}
consist of the corresponding eigenvectors. Suppose

\bm{P}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}_{1}&\bm{v}_{2}&\ldots&\bm{v}_{n}\end{bmatrix}\qquad\text{and }\qquad\bm{P}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{w}_{1}^{\top};\bm{w}_{2}^{\top};\ldots;\bm{w}_{n}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}

are the column and row partitions of
\bm{P}
and
\bm{P}^{-1}
, respectively. Then, we can rewrite
\bm{A}
as

\bm{A}=\bm{P}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{P}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}_{1}&\bm{v}_{2}&\ldots&\bm{v}_{n}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{w}_{1}^{\top}\\
\bm{w}_{2}^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{w}_{n}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\bm{v}_{i}\bm{w}_{i}^{\top}.

By defining
\bm{A}_{i}=\bm{v}_{i}\bm{w}_{i}^{\top}
, we obtain
\bm{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\bm{A}_{i}
.
It follows from
\bm{P}^{-1}\bm{P}=\bm{I}
that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\bm{w}_{i}^{\top}\bm{v}_{j}=1,&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}i=j;\\
&\bm{w}_{i}^{\top}\bm{v}_{j}=0,&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}i\neq j.\end{aligned}\right.

Thus,

\bm{A}_{i}\bm{A}_{j}=\bm{v}_{i}\bm{w}_{i}^{\top}\bm{v}_{j}\bm{w}_{j}^{\top}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\bm{v}_{i}\bm{w}_{i}^{\top}=\bm{A}_{i},&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}i=j;\\
&\mathbf{0},&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}i\neq j.\end{aligned}\right.

This confirms both the idempotency and orthogonality of the
\bm{A}_{i}
matrices. Furthermore,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{A}_{i}=\bm{P}\bm{P}^{-1}=\bm{I}
, which verifies their additivity. The rank-additivity property holds trivially because
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{i})=1
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.

This form of decomposition bears a close relationship to
Cochran’s theorem
and finds application in the distribution theory of linear models
(
Lu 2021c
;
Lu 2021e
)
.

Going further, suppose we have
k
distinct eigenvalues, we have the following result.

For a
simple matrix

\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}

with
k
distinct eigenvalues
, we can express it as a weighted sum of a set of idempotent matrices

\bm{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}k}}\lambda_{i}\bm{A}_{i},

where each
\lambda_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}k}\}
, represents one of the distinct eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
.
The idempotent matrices
\bm{A}_{i}
satisfy the following properties:

1. Idempotent:
\bm{A}_{i}^{2}=\bm{A}_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}k}\}
;

2. Orthogonal:
\bm{A}_{i}\bm{A}_{j}=\mathbf{0}
for all
i\neq j
;

3. Additivity:
\sum_{i=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}k}}\bm{A}_{i}=\bm{I}_{n}
;

4. Rank-Additivity:
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{1})+\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{2})+\ldots+\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}k}})=n
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Building on Theorem
, we can express
\bm{A}
as
\bm{A}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\beta_{j}\bm{B}_{j}
, where
\beta_{j}
are the eigenvalues and
\bm{B}_{j}
are the corresponding idempotent matrices from the second form of the spectral decomposition.
Assume without loss of generality that the eigenvalues are ordered such that
\beta_{1}\leq\beta_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\beta_{n}
, allowing duplicates.
Let
\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{k}\}
denote the set of
k
distinct eigenvalues, and let
\bm{A}_{i}
represent the sum of the
\bm{B}_{j}
matrices associated with
\lambda_{i}
.
Suppose the multiplicity of
\lambda_{i}
is
m_{i}
, and the set of
\bm{B}_{j}
matrices associated with
\lambda_{i}
can be denoted by
\{\bm{B}_{1}^{i},\bm{B}_{2}^{i},\ldots,\bm{B}_{m_{i}}^{i}\}
. Thus,
\bm{A}_{i}
can be defined as
\bm{A}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\bm{B}_{j}^{i}
. Consequently, we have
\bm{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\bm{A}_{i}
.

Idempotency.

\bm{A}_{i}^{2}=(\bm{B}_{1}^{i}+\bm{B}_{2}^{i}+\ldots\bm{B}_{m_{i}}^{i})(\bm{B}_{1}^{i}+\bm{B}_{2}^{i}+\ldots\bm{B}_{m_{i}}^{i})=\bm{B}_{1}^{i}+\bm{B}_{2}^{i}+\ldots\bm{B}_{m_{i}}^{i}=\bm{A}_{i}
due to the idempotency and orthogonality of the
\bm{B}_{j}^{i}
matrices.

Ortogonality.

\bm{A}_{i}\bm{A}_{j}=(\bm{B}_{1}^{i}+\bm{B}_{2}^{i}+\ldots\bm{B}_{m_{i}}^{i})(\bm{B}_{1}^{j}+\bm{B}_{2}^{j}+\ldots\bm{B}_{m_{j}}^{j})=\mathbf{0}
due to the orthogonality of the
\bm{B}_{j}^{i}
matrices.

Additivity.

It is evident that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\bm{A}_{i}=\bm{I}_{n}
.

Rank-Additivity.

\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{i})=\mathrm{rank}(\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\bm{B}_{j}^{i})=m_{i}
such that
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{1})+\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{2})+\ldots+\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{k})=m_{1}+m_{2}+\ldots+m_{k}=n
.

If a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
with
k
distinct eigenvalues can be decomposed as a sum of a set of idempotent matrices

\bm{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\bm{A}_{i},

where each
\lambda_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
, represents one of the distinct eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
, and
the
\bm{A}_{i}
matrices satisfy the four conditions outlined in Theorem
, then the matrix
\bm{A}
is a simple matrix.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Assume that
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{i})=r_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
. By ULV decomposition in Theorem
,
\bm{A}_{i}
can be decomposed as

\bm{A}_{i}=\bm{U}_{i}\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{i}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{V}_{i},

where
\bm{L}_{i}\in\real^{r_{i}\times r_{i}}
is lower triangular, and
\bm{U}_{i}\in\real^{n\times n}
and
\bm{V}_{i}\in\real^{n\times n}
are orthogonal. Define

\bm{X}_{i}=\bm{U}_{i}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{L}_{i}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{V}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{Y}_{i}\\
\bm{Z}_{i}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{X}_{i}
is of size
\real{}^{n\times r_{i}}
, and
\bm{Y}_{i}\in\real^{r_{i}\times n}
consists of the first
r_{i}
rows of
\bm{V}_{i}
. Consequently, we have

\bm{A}_{i}=\bm{X}_{i}\bm{Y}_{i}.

This can be seen as a
reduced
ULV decomposition of
\bm{A}_{i}
. Appending the
\bm{X}_{i}
’s and
\bm{Y}_{i}
’s into matrices
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
:

\bm{X}=[\bm{X}_{1},\bm{X}_{2},\ldots,\bm{X}_{k}],\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{Y}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{Y}_{1};\bm{Y}_{2};\ldots;\bm{Y}_{k}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{X}\in\real^{n\times n}
and
\bm{Y}\in\real^{n\times n}
(by rank-additivity). Using block matrix multiplication and leveraging the additivity property of the
\bm{A}_{i}
’s, we have

\bm{X}\bm{Y}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\bm{X}_{i}\bm{Y}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\bm{A}_{i}=\bm{I}.

Therefore,
\bm{Y}
is the inverse of
\bm{X}
, and

\bm{Y}\bm{X}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{Y}_{1}\\
\bm{Y}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{Y}_{k}\end{bmatrix}[\bm{X}_{1},\bm{X}_{2},\ldots,\bm{X}_{k}]=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{Y}_{1}\bm{X}_{1}&\bm{Y}_{1}\bm{X}_{2}&\ldots&\bm{Y}_{1}\bm{X}_{k}\\
\bm{Y}_{2}\bm{X}_{1}&\bm{Y}_{2}\bm{X}_{2}&\ldots&\bm{Y}_{2}\bm{X}_{k}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\bm{Y}_{k}\bm{X}_{1}&\bm{Y}_{k}\bm{X}_{2}&\ldots&\bm{Y}_{k}\bm{X}_{k}\\
\end{bmatrix}=\bm{I},

such that

\bm{Y}_{i}\bm{X}_{j}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\bm{I}_{r_{i}},&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}i=j;\\
&\mathbf{0},&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}i\neq j.\end{aligned}\right.

This implies

\bm{A}_{i}\bm{X}_{j}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\bm{X}_{i},&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}i=j;\\
&\mathbf{0},&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}i\neq j,\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{A}\bm{X}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\bm{X}_{i}.

Finally, we conclude that

\displaystyle\bm{A}\bm{X}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}[\bm{X}_{1},\bm{X}_{2},\ldots,\bm{X}_{k}]=[\lambda_{1}\bm{X}_{1},\lambda_{2}\bm{X}_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{k}\bm{X}_{k}]=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda},

where

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1}\bm{I}_{r_{1}},\lambda_{2}\bm{I}_{r_{2}},\ldots,\lambda_{k}\bm{I}_{r_{k}})

is a diagonal matrix. This implies
\bm{A}
can be diagonalized, and by Lemma
,
\bm{A}
is indeed a simple matrix.

Corollary 100
(Forward and Backward Spectral).

Combining Theorem
and Theorem
, we can claim that a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is a simple matrix with
k
distinct eigenvalues if and only if it can be decomposed as a sum of a set of idempotent matrices

\bm{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\bm{A}_{i},

where each
\lambda_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
, represents one of the distinct eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
, and the
\bm{A}_{i}
matrices satisfy the four conditions outlined in Theorem
.

12.5
Skew-Symmetric Matrix and its Properties*

We presented the spectral decomposition of symmetric matrices. Another significant class of matrices related to symmetry is called
skew-symmetric matrices
.

Definition 101
(Skew-Symmetric Matrix).

A matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is termed a skew-symmetric matrix if it satisfies the condition

\bm{A}^{\top}=-\bm{A}
.
Under this definition, the diagonal entries
a_{ii}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
must satisfy the equation
a_{ii}=-a_{ii}
. This implies that all diagonal entries are equal to zero.

Previously, in Lemma
, we established that the eigenvalues of symmetric matrices are real. Similarly, it can be shown that all eigenvalues of skew-symmetric matrices are purely imaginary or zero.

Lemma 102
(Imaginary Eigenvalues).

The eigenvalues of any skew-symmetric matrix are either purely imaginary or zero.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Suppose the eigenvalue
\lambda
of the skew-symmetric matrix
\bm{A}
is a complex number
\lambda=a+ib
, where
a
and
b
are real. Its complex conjugate is
\bar{\lambda}=a-ib
.
Similarly, for the corresponding complex eigenvector
\bm{x}=\bm{c}+i\bm{d}
, its complex conjugate is
\bar{\bm{x}}=\bm{c}-i\bm{d}
, where
\bm{c}
and
\bm{d}
are real vectors. The eigenvalue equation and its conjugate can be written as:

\bm{A}\bm{x}=\lambda\bm{x}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{ leads to }}\qquad\bm{A}\bar{\bm{x}}=\bar{\lambda}\bar{\bm{x}}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{ transpose to }}\qquad\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\top}=\bar{\lambda}\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}.

Taking the dot product of the first equation with
\bar{\bm{x}}
and the last equation with
\bm{x}
:

\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\lambda\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{x}\qquad\text{and }\qquad\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{x}=\bar{\lambda}\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{x}.

Then we have the equality
-\lambda\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{x}=\bar{\lambda}\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{x}
(since
\bm{A}^{\top}=-\bm{A}
).
Since
\bar{\bm{x}}^{\top}\bm{x}=(\bm{c}-i\bm{d})^{\top}(\bm{c}+i\bm{d})=\bm{c}^{\top}\bm{c}+\bm{d}^{\top}\bm{d}
is a real number, the real part of
\lambda
must be zero, indicating
\lambda
is either imaginary or zero.

Lemma 103
(Odd Skew-Symmetric Determinant).

For any skew-symmetric matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, if
n
is odd, then
\det(\bm{A})=0
.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
When
n
is odd, we have

\det(\bm{A})=\det(\bm{A}^{\top})=\det(-\bm{A})=(-1)^{n}\det(\bm{A})=-\det(\bm{A}).

This implies
\det(\bm{A})=0
.

A real skew-symmetric matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{Z}\bm{D}\bm{Z}^{\top},

where
\bm{Z}
is an
n\times n
nonsingular matrix, and
\bm{D}
is a block-diagonal matrix of the following form

\bm{D}=\mathrm{diag}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{bmatrix},\ldots,\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{bmatrix},0,\ldots,0\right).

Proof
[of Theorem
]
We will prove this theorem by recursive calculation.
As usual, we denote the entry (
i,j
) of a matrix
\bm{A}
by
a_{ij}
.

Case 1).

Suppose the first row of
\bm{A}
is nonzero, note that
\bm{E}\bm{A}\bm{E}^{\top}
is skew-symmetric for any matrix
\bm{E}
if
\bm{A}
is skew-symmetric.
Therefore, both the diagonals of
\bm{A}
and
\bm{E}\bm{A}\bm{E}^{\top}
zero. The upper-left
2\times 2
submatrix of
\bm{E}\bm{A}\bm{E}^{\top}
takes the following form

(\bm{E}\bm{A}\bm{E}^{\top})_{1:2,1:2}=\begin{bmatrix}0&x\\
-x&0\end{bmatrix}.

Since we suppose the first row of
\bm{A}
is nonzero, there exists a permutation matrix
\bm{P}
(Definition
), such that we will exchange the nonzero value, say
a
, in the first row to the second column of
\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}
. The upper-left
2\times 2
submatrix of
\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}
becomes

(\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top})_{1:2,1:2}=\begin{bmatrix}0&a\\
-a&0\end{bmatrix}.

Construct a nonsingular matrix
\bm{M}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}1/a&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{I}_{n-1}\end{bmatrix}
such that the upper left
2\times 2
submatrix of
\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top}
has the following form

(\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top})_{1:2,1:2}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{bmatrix}.

This finishes block-diagonalizing the upper-left
2\times 2
block.
Next, if there exists a nonzero value, say
b
, in the first row of
(\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top})
with entry
(1,j)
for some
j>2
, we can construct a nonsingular matrix
\bm{L}=\bm{I}-b\cdot\bm{E}_{j2}
, where
\bm{E}_{j2}
is an all-zero matrix except the entry (
j,2
) is 1, such that
\bm{L}(\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top})\bm{L}^{\top}
will set the entry with value
b
to 0.

For example, suppose
\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top}
is a
3\times 3
matrix with the following value

\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&b\\
-1&0&\times\\
\times&\times&0\end{bmatrix},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{L}=\bm{I}-b\cdot\bm{E}_{j2}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&-b&1\end{bmatrix},

where
j=3
for this specific example. This results in

\bm{L}\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{L}^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&-b&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&1&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}b}\\
-1&0&\times\\
\times&\times&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&-b\\
0&0&1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}0}\\
-1&0&\times\\
\times&\times&0\end{bmatrix}.

Similarly, if the second row of
\bm{L}\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{L}^{\top}
contains a nonzero value, say
c
, we can construct a nonsingular matrix
\bm{K}=\bm{I}+c\cdot\bm{E}_{j1}
such that
\bm{K}(\bm{L}\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{L}^{\top})\bm{K}^{\top}
will introduce a zero for the entry with value
c
.

For example, suppose
\bm{L}\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{L}^{\top}
is a
3\times 3
matrix with the following value

\bm{L}\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{L}^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0\\
-1&0&c\\
\times&\times&0\end{bmatrix},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{K}=\bm{I}+c\cdot\bm{E}_{j1}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
c&0&1\end{bmatrix},

where
j=3
for this specific example. This results in

\bm{K}\bm{L}\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{L}^{\top}\bm{K}^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
c&0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0\\
-1&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}c}\\
\times&\times&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&0&c\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0\\
-1&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}0}\\
\times&\times&0\end{bmatrix}.

Since we have shown that
\bm{K}\bm{L}\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{L}^{\top}\bm{K}^{\top}
is also skew-symmetric, then it simplifies to

\bm{K}\bm{L}\bm{M}\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{L}^{\top}\bm{K}^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0\\
-1&0&{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}0}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}0}&0\end{bmatrix},

so we do not need to address the first two columns further.

Apply this process iteratively to the bottom-right
(n-2)\times(n-2)
submatrix can complete the block-diagonalization.

Case 2).

If the first row of
\bm{A}
is zero, we can use a permutation matrix to move the first row to the last row and then apply the process described in case 1 to complete the proof.

The block-diagonalization of skew-symmetric matrices, as discussed earlier, demonstrates that the rank of a skew-symmetric matrix is always even. Moreover, we can prove that the determinant of a skew-symmetric matrix of even order is nonnegative, as stated in the following lemma:

Lemma 104
(Even Skew-Symmetric Determinant).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
be x a skew-symmetric matrix. If
n
is even, then
\det(\bm{A})\geq 0
.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Applying Theorem
,
\bm{A}
can be block-diagonalized as
\bm{A}=\bm{Z}\bm{D}\bm{Z}^{\top}
, resulting in

\det(\bm{A})=\det(\bm{Z}\bm{D}\bm{Z}^{\top})=\det(\bm{Z})^{2}\det(\bm{D})\geq 0.

This completes the proof.

12.6
Applications

12.6.1
Application: Eigenvalue of Projection Matrix

In Section
, we demonstrate how the QR, UTV, SVD decompositions can be applied to solve the least squares problem. Specifically, we consider the overdetermined system
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{b}
, where
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
is the data matrix, and
\bm{b}\in\real^{m}
is the observation vector, with
m\geq n
. Typically,
\bm{A}
is assumed to have full column rank, as real-world data is often sufficiently diverse to ensure linear independence, or the data can be made linearly dependent after prepossing.

Since
\bm{A}
has full column rank,
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is invertible, and
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})=\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})
, the least squares solution is given by
\bm{x}_{LS}=(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
, minimizing
\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}
. The recovered observation vector is
\hat{\bm{b}}=\bm{A}\bm{x}_{LS}=\bm{A}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
. While the observed vector
\bm{b}
may not belong to the column space of
\bm{A}
, the recovered vector
\hat{\bm{b}}
lies within it.

We define the matrix
\bm{H}=\bm{A}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}
as the projection matrix, which projects
\bm{b}
onto the column space of
\bm{A}
. This matrix is also known as the hat matrix because it “puts a hat” on
\bm{b}
. The projection matrix can be easily verified to be both symmetric (
\bm{H}=\bm{H}^{\top}
) and idempotent (
\bm{H}^{2}=\bm{H}
).

Remark 105
(Column Space of Projection Matrix).

The hat matrix
\bm{H}=\bm{A}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}
projects any vector in
m
into the column space of
\bm{A}
, i.e.,
\bm{H}\bm{y}\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
. Notably,
\bm{H}\bm{y}
is a linear combination of the columns of
\bm{H}
, which implies
\mathcal{C}(\bm{H})=\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
.

More generally, for any projection matrix
\bm{H}
that projects vectors onto a subspace
\mathcal{V}
, it holds that
\mathcal{C}(\bm{H})=\mathcal{V}
. This property can be formally established using singular value decomposition (Chapter
).

We now show that any projection matrix has specific eigenvalues.

Proposition 106
(Eigenvalue of Projection Matrix).

The eigenvalues of a projection matrix are restricted to 0 and 1.

Proof
[of Proposition
]
Since
\bm{H}
is symmetric, it has a spectral decomposition
\bm{H}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}
. Using the idempotent property of
\bm{H}
, we have:

\displaystyle(\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top})^{2}

\displaystyle=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}\,\,\implies\,\,\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{2}\bm{Q}^{\top}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}\,\,\implies\,\,\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{2}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\,\,\implies\,\,\lambda_{i}^{2}=\lambda_{i},

Thus,
\lambda_{i}\in\{0,1\}
.

This property is significant in the analysis of distribution theory for linear models. For further details, see
Lu 2021e
.
Building on the eigenvalues of the projection matrix, we can also define the perpendicular projection
\bm{I}-\bm{H}
.

Proposition 107
(Project onto
\mathcal{V}^{\perp}
).

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a subspace, and
\bm{H}
be the projection matrix onto
\mathcal{V}
. Then,
\bm{I}-\bm{H}
serves as the projection matrix onto
\mathcal{V}^{\perp}
.

Proof
[of Proposition
]
First,
(\bm{I}-\bm{H})
is symmetric,
(\bm{I}-\bm{H})^{\top}=\bm{I}-\bm{H}^{\top}=\bm{I}-\bm{H}
since
\bm{H}
is symmetric. Furthermore, it follows that

(\bm{I}-\bm{H})^{2}=\bm{I}^{2}-\bm{I}\bm{H}-\bm{H}\bm{I}+\bm{H}^{2}=\bm{I}-\bm{H},

which shows that
\bm{I}-\bm{H}
is idempotent.
Thus,
\bm{I}-\bm{H}
qualifies as a projection matrix. Using the spectral theorem, write
\bm{H}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}
. Then,
\bm{I}-\bm{H}=\bm{Q}\bm{Q}^{\top}-\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}=\bm{Q}(\bm{I}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda})\bm{Q}^{\top}
.
Consequently, the column space of
\bm{I}-\bm{H}
is spanned by the eigenvectors of
\bm{H}
corresponding to the zero eigenvalues of
\bm{H}
(by Proposition
), which aligns with
\mathcal{V}^{\perp}
.

For a more detailed analysis of the projection matrix and its applications, refer to
Lu 2021c
, although these results extend beyond the primary focus of matrix decomposition.

12.6.2
Application: An Alternative Definition of PD and PSD of Matrices

In Definition
, positive definite (PD) and positive semidefinite (PSD) matrices are defined based on their quadratic forms. Here, we establish that a symmetric matrix is positive definite (resp. positive semidefinite) if and only if all its eigenvalues are positive (resp. nonnegative).

Lemma 108
(Eigenvalues of PD and PSD Matrices i.e., the Eigenvalue Characterization Theorem).

A symmetric matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is positive definite (PD) if and only if all eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
are positive.
And a symmetric matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is positive semidefinite (PSD) if and only if all eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
are nonnegative.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Suppose
\bm{A}
is PD. Then, for any eigenvalue
\lambda
and its corresponding eigenvector
\bm{v}
of
\bm{A}
, we have
\bm{A}\bm{v}=\lambda\bm{v}
. Thus,

\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{v}=\lambda\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert^{2}>0.

This implies
\lambda>0
.

Conversely, suppose all eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
are positive, and consider the spectral decomposition of
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}
, where
\bm{Q}
is orthogonal and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is diagonal. Let
\bm{x}
be any nonzero vector, and let
\bm{y}=\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{x}
. We have:

\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{x}^{\top}(\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top})\bm{x}=(\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{Q})\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{x})=\bm{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{y}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}y_{i}^{2}>0.

Thus,
\bm{A}
is PD.
The proof for the PSD case follows similarly

A real symmetric matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is PSD if and only if
\bm{A}
can be factored as
\bm{A}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{P}
, where
\bm{P}\in\real^{n\times n}
; and it is PD if and only if
\bm{P}
is nonsingular.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Suppose
\bm{A}
is PSD. From its spectral decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}
, we can decompose
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}
(since eigenvalues of PSD matrices are nonnegative). Let
\bm{P}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}\bm{Q}^{\top}
. Then,
\bm{A}
can be decomposed as
\bm{A}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{P}
.

Conversely, suppose
\bm{A}
can be factored as
\bm{A}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{P}
. Then, all eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
are nonnegative since for any eigenvalues
\lambda
and its corresponding eigenvector
\bm{v}
of
\bm{A}
, we have

\lambda=\frac{\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{v}}{\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{v}}=\frac{\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{P}\bm{v}}{\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{v}}=\frac{\left\lVert\bm{P}\bm{v}\right\rVert^{2}}{\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert^{2}}\geq 0.

Therefore,
\bm{A}
is PSD by Lemma
.

Similarly, we can prove the second part for PD matrices, where the positive definiteness will result in the nonsingular
\bm{P}
; and the nonsingularity of
\bm{P}
implies the positivity of the eigenvalues.

12.6.3
Proof for Semidefinite Rank-Revealing Decomposition

In this section, we provide a proof for Theorem
, which establishes the existence of a rank-revealing decomposition for positive semidefinite matrices.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
The proof leverages the nonsingular factorization of PSD matrices (Theorem
) and the column-pivoted QR decomposition (Theorem
).

By Theorem
, a PSD matrix
\bm{A}
can be factored as
\bm{A}=\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}
, where
\bm{Z}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}\bm{Q}^{\top}
and
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}
is the spectral decomposition of
\bm{A}
.

From Lemma
, the rank of
\bm{A}
equals the number of its nonzero eigenvalues, which for a PSD matrix corresponds to its positive eigenvalues. Consequently, only
r
diagonal elements of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}
are nonzero, making
\bm{Z}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}\bm{Q}^{\top}
a rank-
r
matrix with
r
linearly independent columns.
Applying the column-pivoted QR decomposition to
\bm{Z}
, we obtain

\bm{Z}\bm{P}=\bm{Q}\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{P}
is a permutation matrix,
\bm{R}_{11}\in\real^{r\times r}
is upper triangular with positive diagonals, and
\bm{R}_{12}\in\real^{r\times(n-r)}
. Therefore,

\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}\bm{P}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}^{\top}&\mathbf{0}\\
\bm{R}_{12}^{\top}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}.

Let

\bm{R}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{R}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}.

Thus, the rank-revealing decomposition of the PSD matrix
\bm{A}
is:
\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{P}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
.

This decomposition is produced by using complete pivoting, where at each step, the algorithm selects the largest diagonal element in the active submatrix as the pivot. This process closely resembles the partial pivoting technique discussed in Section
.

12.6.4
Application: Cholesky via QR and Spectral Decompositions

In this section, we present an alternative proof for the existence of the Cholesky decomposition.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
From Theorem
, the PD matrix
\bm{A}
can be factored as
\bm{A}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{P}
, where
\bm{P}
is a nonsingular matrix. Applying the QR decomposition to
\bm{P}
, we write
\bm{P}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
, which implies:

\bm{A}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{P}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{Q}\bm{R}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}.

This result closely resembles the Cholesky decomposition, with the exception that
\bm{R}
is not explicitly required to have positive diagonal entries. However, by considering the CGS algorithm for QR decomposition (discussed in Section
), it can be observed that the diagonal entries of
\bm{R}
are nonnegative. Moreover, if
\bm{P}
is nonsingular, these diagonal entries are strictly positive.

The proof above relies on the existence of both the QR decomposition and the spectral decomposition. Thus, in this context, the existence of the Cholesky decomposition can be demonstrated using these two fundamental decomposition methods.

12.6.5
Application: Unique Power Decomposition of PD Matrices

Any
n\times n
positive definite matrix
\bm{A}
can be
uniquely
decomposed as a product of a positive definite matrix
\bm{B}
such that
\bm{A}=\bm{B}^{2}
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
We first prove the existence of a positive definite matrix
\bm{B}
satisfying
\bm{A}=\bm{B}^{2}
and then demonstrate its uniqueness.

Existence.

Since
\bm{A}
symmetric and positive definite, its spectral decomposition is given by
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}
.
By Lemma
, the eigenvalues of a PD matrix are strictly positive. Hence, the square root of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
exists, allowing us to define
\bm{B}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}\bm{Q}^{\top}
. It follows that
\bm{A}=\bm{B}^{2}
, and since
\bm{B}
is symmetric with positive eigenvalues, it is also positive definite.

Uniqueness.

Suppose the factorization is not unique. Then, there exist two positive definite matrices
\bm{B}_{1}
and
\bm{B}_{2}
such that

\bm{A}=\bm{B}_{1}^{2}=\bm{B}_{2}^{2},

where
\bm{B}_{1}
and
\bm{B}_{2}
are both PD. Their spectral decompositions are given by

\bm{B}_{1}=\bm{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{1}\bm{Q}_{1}^{\top}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{B}_{2}=\bm{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{2}\bm{Q}_{2}^{\top}.

We notice that
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{1}^{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{2}^{2}
contain the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
, and both eigenvalues of
\bm{B}_{1}
and
\bm{B}_{2}
contained in
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{2}
are positive (since
\bm{B}_{1}
and
\bm{B}_{2}
are both PD). Without loss of generality, we suppose
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{2}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
such that
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{n}
. By
\bm{B}_{1}^{2}=\bm{B}_{2}^{2}
, we have

\bm{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}_{1}^{\top}=\bm{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}_{2}^{\top}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\bm{Q}_{2}^{\top}\bm{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}_{2}^{\top}\bm{Q}_{1}.

Let
\bm{Z}=\bm{Q}_{2}^{\top}\bm{Q}_{1}
. This implies
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
and
\bm{Z}
commute, and
\bm{Z}
must be a block diagonal matrix whose partitioning conforms to the block structure of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
. This results in
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}=\bm{Z}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}\bm{Z}^{\top}
and

\bm{B}_{2}=\bm{Q}_{2}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}\bm{Q}_{2}^{\top}=\bm{Q}_{2}\bm{Q}_{2}^{\top}\bm{Q}_{1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{1/2}\bm{Q}_{1}^{\top}\bm{Q}_{2}\bm{Q}_{2}^{\top}=\bm{B}_{1}.

This completes the proof.

Similarly, we can prove the unique decomposition of a PSD matrix
\bm{A}
such that
\bm{A}=\bm{B}^{2}
, where
\bm{B}
is PSD. For a more detailed discussion on this topic, refer to
Koeber and Schäfer 2006
.

Decompositions for PD matrices.

To summarize, a PD matrix
\bm{A}
can be decomposed in several ways: we can factor it into
\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{R}
is an upper triangular matrix with positive diagonals as shown in Theorem
by Cholesky decomposition;
\bm{A}=\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{P}
, where
\bm{P}
is nonsingular in Theorem
; and
\bm{A}=\bm{B}^{2}
, where
\bm{B}
is PD in Theorem
.

12.4.

Fan’s inequality
(
Fan 1949
;
Borwein and Lewis 2006
)
.

Let
\bm{A},\bm{B}
be real symmetric, and let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\downarrow}(\bm{A})
be the vector containing the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}
in nonincreasing order. Show that
\mathrm{tr}(\bm{A}\bm{B})\leq\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\downarrow}(\bm{A})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\downarrow}(\bm{B})

The equality holds if and only if

and

Subspace in symmetric.
