12.4.

Show that
\bm{A}
is symmetric
\iff

\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\bm{A}\\
\bm{A}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}
is symmetric

\iff

\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\bm{A}\\
-\bm{A}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}

is skew-symmetric.

Chapter 13
Singular Value Decomposition (SVD)

13.1
Singular Value Decomposition (SVD)

In eigenvalue decomposition, a matrix is typically factored into a diagonal matrix. However, this is not always possible. If
\bm{A}
lacks linearly independent eigenvectors, diagonalization cannot be performed. The singular value decomposition (SVD) overcomes this limitation. Instead of decomposing a matrix into an eigenvector matrix, SVD splits the matrix into two orthogonal matrices.
We present the result of the SVD in the following theorem and will discuss its existence in later sections.

Given any real
m\times n
matrix
\bm{A}
with rank
r
, the matrix
\bm{A}
can be decomposed as

\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top},

where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\in\real^{r\times r}
is a diagonal matrix,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\ldots,\sigma_{r})
with
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\sigma_{r}>0
and

•

The
\sigma_{i}
’s are the nonzero
singular values
of
\bm{A}
, in the meantime, they are also the (positive) square roots of the nonzero
eigenvalues
of
{\bm{A}}^{\top}\bm{A}
and
\bm{A}{\bm{A}}^{\top}
.

•

The columns of
\bm{U}\in\real^{m\times r}
contain the
r
eigenvectors of
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
corresponding to the
r
nonzero eigenvalues of
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
.

•

The columns of
\bm{V}\in\real^{n\times r}
contain the
r
eigenvectors of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
corresponding to the
r
nonzero eigenvalues of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
.

•

Moreover, the columns of
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
are called the
left and right singular vectors
of
\bm{A}
, respectively.

•

Furthermore, the columns of both
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
are mutually orthonormal (by spectral theorem
).

In particular, we can express the matrix decomposition as the sum of outer products of vectors
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}
, which represents a sum of
r
rank-one matrices.

If we append
m-r
additional orthonormal columns to
\bm{U}
, orthogonal to the
r
eigenvectors of
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
(similar to the silent columns in the QR decomposition, Section
), we obtain an orthogonal matrix
\bm{U}\in\real^{m\times m}
.
The same principle applies to the columns of
\bm{V}
, yielding the full SVD.
A comparison between the reduced and full SVD is shown in Figure
, where white entries represent zero, and
blue
entries are not necessarily zero.

(a)

Reduced SVD decomposition.

(b)

Full SVD decomposition.

Figure 13.1:
Comparison between the reduced and full SVD.

13.2
Existence of the SVD

To prove the existence of the SVD, we need to use the following lemmas.
As mentioned earlier, the singular values are the square roots of the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
. Since negative values do not have real square roots, the eigenvalues must be nonnegative.

Lemma 109
(Nonnegative Eigenvalues of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
).

Given any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, the matrix
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
has nonnegative eigenvalues.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Let
\lambda
be an eigenvalue of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
with the corresponding eigenvector
\bm{x}
. We have

\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\lambda\bm{x}\implies\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\lambda\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x}.

Since
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert^{2}\geq 0
and
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x}>0
, we have
\lambda\geq 0
.

Since
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
has nonnegative eigenvalues, we can define the
singular value

\sigma\geq 0
of
\bm{A}
, such that
\sigma^{2}
is the eigenvalue of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
. In other words,
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{v}=\sigma^{2}\bm{v}
. This is crucial for the existence of the SVD.

We also showed in Lemma
that
\mathrm{rank}
(
\bm{A}\bm{B}
)
\leq
min
\{\mathrm{rank}
(
\bm{A}
),
\mathrm{rank}
(
\bm{B}
)}.
However, the symmetric matrix
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is special because its rank is equal to the rank of
\bm{A}
. We now prove this.

Lemma 110
(Rank of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
).

The matrices
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
and
\bm{A}
have same rank.
Extending this observation to
\bm{A}^{\top}
, we can also prove that
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
and
\bm{A}
share the same rank.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Let
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
, we have

\bm{A}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}\implies\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0},

i.e.,
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})\implies\bm{x}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})
. This shows that
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})\subseteq\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})
.

Next, let
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})
. We obtain

\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}\implies\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=0\implies\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert^{2}=0\implies\bm{A}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}.

Thus,
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})
implies
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
, and therefore,
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})\subseteq\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
.

By the ”sandwiching” argument, we conclude that

\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})=\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}))=\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})).

By the fundamental theorem of linear algebra (Theorem
), it follows that
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
and
\bm{A}
have the same rank.

Applying the observation to
\bm{A}^{\top}
, we can also conclude that
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
and
\bm{A}
have the same rank:

\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})=\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}).

In the form of the SVD, we claim that the matrix
\bm{A}
is a sum of
r
rank-one matrices, where
r
denotes the number of nonzero singular values. This count of nonzero singular values is, in fact, equal to the rank of the matrix.

Lemma 111
(The Number of Nonzero Singular Values vs the Rank).

The number of nonzero singular values of a matrix
\bm{A}
is equal to its rank.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
By Lemma
, the rank of any symmetric matrix (such as
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
) is equal to the number of nonzero eigenvalues (counting multiplicities). Thus, the number of nonzero singular values of
\bm{A}
equals the rank of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
. By Lemma
, the number of nonzero singular values is therefore also equal to the rank of
\bm{A}
.

We are now ready to prove the existence of the SVD.

Proof
[of Theorem
: Existence of the Reduced SVD]
Since
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is a symmetric matrix, by spectral theorem
and Lemma
, there exists a semi-orthogonal matrix
\bm{V}\in\real^{n\times r}
such that

{\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}=\bm{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\bm{V}^{\top}},

where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is a diagonal matrix containing the
r
nonzero singular values of
\bm{A}
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}
contains the nonzero eigenvalues of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
.
Specifically,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r})
, and the set
\{\sigma_{1}^{2},\sigma_{2}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r}^{2}\}
represents the nonzero eigenvalues of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
, with
r
being the rank of
\bm{A}
.
Now we proceed with the core of the proof. Starting from the equation
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{2}\bm{v}_{i}
,
\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
, i.e., the eigenvector
\bm{v}_{i}
of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is corresponding to the eigenvalue
\sigma_{i}^{2}
.

1. Multiplying both sides by
\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}
:

\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{2}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{v}_{i}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}\right\rVert^{2}=\sigma_{i}^{2}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}\right\rVert=\sigma_{i}.

2. Multiplying both sides by
\bm{A}
:

\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{2}\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}\frac{\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}}{\sigma_{i}}=\sigma_{i}^{2}\frac{\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}}{\sigma_{i}}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{u}_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{2}\bm{u}_{i},

where we notice that this form can find the eigenvector of
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
corresponding to
\sigma_{i}^{2}
, which is
\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}
. Since the length of
\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}
is
\sigma_{i}
, we then define
\bm{u}_{i}=\frac{\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}}{\sigma_{i}}
with a norm of 1.

These
\bm{u}_{i}
’s are orthogonal because
(\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i})^{\top}(\bm{A}\bm{v}_{j})=\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{v}_{j}=\sigma_{j}^{2}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{v}_{j}=0
if
i\neq j
.
Thus, we conclude that

{\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\bm{U}^{\top}},

where
\bm{U}=[\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{r}]
.
Since
\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}=\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}
, we have

[\bm{A}\bm{v}_{1},\bm{A}\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{A}\bm{v}_{r}]=[\sigma_{1}\bm{u}_{1},\sigma_{2}\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r}\bm{u}_{r}]\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\bm{A}\bm{V}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}.

(13.1)

At this point, since
\bm{V}\bm{V}^{\top}\neq\bm{I}
, we cannot directly obtain the reduced SVD.
However, by appending
\bm{V}
with additional orthogonal columns, we can construct an orthogonal matrix
\widetilde{\bm{V}}=[\bm{V},\bm{V}_{2}]
, and similarly append
\bm{U}
with orthogonal columns to form
\widetilde{\bm{U}}=[\bm{U},\bm{U}_{2}]
. This leads to the full SVD (since
\widetilde{\bm{V}}\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{\top}=\bm{I}
):

\bm{A}\widetilde{\bm{V}}=\widetilde{\bm{U}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{where}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\bm{A}=\widetilde{\bm{U}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{\top}.

Finally, simplifying the product, we have

\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}+\bm{U}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{0}\cdot\bm{V}_{2}^{\top}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top},

which is the reduced SVD. This completes the proof.

The proof also shows that if
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
is the reduced SVD of
\bm{A}
, it follows from (
) that
\bm{A}\bm{V}\bm{V}^{\top}=\bm{A}
. This implies that
\bm{V}\bm{V}^{\top}
(where
\bm{V}\in\real^{n\times r}
is semi-orthogonal) is an (orthogonal) projection matrix that maps each row of
\bm{A}
onto itself (a projection matrix onto the row space of
\bm{A}
).

SVD-related orthogonal projections.

In the context of SVD, several important orthogonal projections arise from the four fundamental subspaces. In simple terms, orthogonal projection matrices are symmetric and idempotent. Such a projection matrix projects any vector onto its column space.
Idempotency means that applying the projection twice is the same as applying it once. Symmetry has a geometric interpretation: the projection minimizes the distance between the original vector and its projection, where the projection lies within the column space of the projection matrix.
Suppose
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
is the full SVD of
\bm{A}
with rank
r
. Consider the following column partitions:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\bm{U}=&\bm{U}_{r}&\bm{U}_{m}\\
&m\times r&m\times(m-r)\\
\end{array},\qquad\begin{array}[]{ccc}\bm{V}=&\bm{V}_{r}&\bm{V}_{n}\\
&n\times r&n\times(n-r)\\
\end{array},

where
\bm{U}_{r}
and
\bm{V}_{r}
consist of the first
r
columns of
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
, respectively.
The four orthogonal projections can then be written as:

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{r}\bm{V}_{r}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\text{projection onto $\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})$},\quad

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{n}\bm{V}_{n}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\text{projection onto $\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})$},

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{r}\bm{U}_{r}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\text{projection onto $\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})$},\quad

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{m}\bm{U}_{m}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\text{projection onto $\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top})$}.

Spectral decomposition of
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
.

An additional result from the above proof is that the spectral decomposition of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}=\bm{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\bm{V}^{\top}
leads to the spectral decomposition of
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\bm{U}^{\top}
, with the same eigenvalues.

Corollary 112
(Eigenvalues of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
and
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
).

The nonzero eigenvalues of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
and
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
are identical.

We have shown in Lemma
that the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
are nonnegative. Therefore, the eigenvalues of
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
must also be nonnegative.

Corollary 113
(Nonnegative Eigenvalues of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
and
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
).

The eigenvalues of both
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
and
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
are nonnegative.

The existence of the SVD is crucial for defining the effective rank of a matrix.

Definition 114
(Effective Rank vs Exact Rank).

The effective rank, also known as the numerical rank, is defined as follows:
From Lemma
, we know that the number of nonzero singular values is equal to the rank of a matrix. Let the
i
-th largest singular value of
\bm{A}
be denoted as
\sigma_{i}(\bm{A})
. If
\sigma_{r}(\bm{A})\gg\sigma_{r+1}(\bm{A})\approx 0
, then
r
is called the numerical rank of
\bm{A}
. In contrast, if
\sigma_{i}(\bm{A})>\sigma_{r+1}(\bm{A})=0
, we say that
\bm{A}
has exact rank
r
, as discussed in most of our previous examples.

13.3
Properties of the SVD

13.3.1
Four Subspaces in SVD

For any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, we have the following properties:

•

The null space
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
is the orthogonal complement of the row space
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
in
n
:
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}))+\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top}))=n
.

•

The left null space
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top})
is the orthogonal complement of the column space
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
in
m
:
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}))+\dim(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}))=m
.

This is known as the fundamental theorem of linear algebra, also referred to as the rank-nullity theorem (Theorem
).
Using the SVD, we can identify an orthonormal basis for each subspace.

Figure 13.2:
Orthonormal bases that diagonalize
\bm{A}
using the SVD.

Proposition 115
(Four Orthonormal Bases).

Given the full SVD of a matrix
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
, where
\bm{U}=[\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{m}]
and
\bm{V}=[\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{n}]
are the column partitions of
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
, respectively, we have the following properties:

•

\{\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{r}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
;

•

\{\bm{v}_{r+1},\bm{v}_{r+2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
;

•

\{\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{r}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
;

•

\{\bm{u}_{r+1},\bm{u}_{r+2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{m}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top})
.

The relationship among these four subspaces is shown in Figure
, where
\bm{A}
maps the row basis
\bm{v}_{i}
to column basis
\bm{u}_{i}
by the equation
\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}=\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
.

Proof
[of Proposition
]
From Lemma
, for the symmetric matrix
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
, the subspace
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})
is spanned by the eigenvectors, thus
\{\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{r}\}
forms an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})
.
We proceed as follows:

1.

Since
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is symmetric, the row space of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is equal to its column space.

2.

All rows of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
are linear combinations of the rows of
\bm{A}
, meaning the row space of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}

\subseteq
the row space of
\bm{A}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
.

3.

Since
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})=\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})
by Lemma
, we then have:

The row space of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
= the column space of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
= the row space of
\bm{A}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})=\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
. Thus,
\{\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{r}\}
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})
.

Moreover, the space spanned by
\{\bm{v}_{r+1},\bm{v}_{r+2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{n}\}
is an orthogonal complement to the space spanned by
\{\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{r}\}
, so
\{\bm{v}_{r+1},\bm{v}_{r+2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
.

Applying a similar argument to
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
proves the remaining claims. Alternatively, we can observe that
\{\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{r}\}
forms a basis for the column space of
\bm{A}
by Lemma
, since
\bm{u}_{i}=\frac{\bm{A}\bm{v}_{i}}{\sigma_{i}}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
.

13.3.2
Relationship between Singular Values and Determinant

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
be a square matrix, and let its singular value decomposition be given by
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
. It follows that

|\det(\bm{A})|=|\det(\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top})|=|\det(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})|=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\ldots\sigma_{n}.

If all the singular values are nonzero, then
\det(\bm{A})\neq 0
. That is,
\bm{A}
is
nonsingular
.
If at least one singular value is zero, i.e.,
\sigma_{i}=0
, then
\det(\bm{A})=0
, implying that
\bm{A}
does not have full rank and is not invertible.
In this case,
\bm{A}
is called
singular
. This explains why the
\sigma_{i}
’s are referred to as the singular values of
\bm{A}
.

13.3.3
Orthogonal Equivalence

We have defined in Definition
that
\bm{A}
and
\bm{P}\bm{A}\bm{P}^{-1}
are similar matrices for any nonsingular matrix
\bm{P}
. The concept of
orthogonal equivalence
is defined in a similar way.

Definition 116
(Orthogonal Equivalent Matrices).

Given two orthogonal matrices
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
, the matrices
\bm{A}
and
\bm{U}\bm{A}\bm{V}
are called orthogonally equivalent matrices.
In the complex domain, when
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
are unitary matrices, the matrices are called unitarily equivalent.

T
We now state the following property for orthogonal equivalent matrices:

Lemma 117
(Orthogonal Equivalent Matrices).

If
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
are orthogonal equivalent matrices, then their singular values are the same.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Since
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
are orthogonally equivalent, there exist orthogonal matrices that
\bm{B}=\bm{U}\bm{A}\bm{V}
. We then have

\bm{B}\bm{B}^{\top}=(\bm{U}\bm{A}\bm{V})(\bm{V}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{U}^{\top})=\bm{U}\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{U}^{\top}.

This implies
\bm{B}\bm{B}^{\top}
and
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
are similar matrices. By Proposition
, the eigenvalues of similar matrices are the same, which implies that the singular values of
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
are the same.

13.3.4
SVD for QR

Lemma 118
(SVD for QR).

Suppose the full QR decomposition of a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, with
m\geq n
, is given by
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{Q}\in\real^{m\times m}
is orthogonal and
\bm{R}\in\real^{m\times n}
is upper triangular. Then,
\bm{A}
and
\bm{R}
have the same singular values and right singular vectors.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
We observe that
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
such that
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
and
\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
have the same eigenvalues and eigenvectors. Consequently,
\bm{A}
and
\bm{R}
have the same singular values and right singular vectors (i.e., the eigenvectors of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
or
\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
).

The above lemma implies that the SVD of a matrix can be derived from its QR decomposition. Suppose the QR decomposition of
\bm{A}
is given by
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
, and the SVD of
\bm{R}
is given by
\bm{R}=\bm{U}_{0}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
. Then, the SVD of
\bm{A}
can be expressed as:

\bm{A}=\underbrace{\bm{Q}\bm{U}_{0}}_{\bm{U}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}.

13.4
Polar Decomposition

A decomposition closely related to the SVD is the
polar form
of a matrix.
In the context of continuum mechanics, it is imperative to distinguish between stretching and rotation.
The polar decomposition factors any matrix into an orthogonal matrix (which corresponds to rotation) and a symmetric matrix (which corresponds to stretching or compression, see Section
).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
. Then
\bm{A}
can be factored as

•

Case
m>n
: left polar decomposition.

\bm{A}=\bm{Q}_{l}\bm{S}_{l}
, where
\bm{S}_{l}^{2}=\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is PSD and is
uniquely
determined.
The factor
\bm{Q}_{l}
has orthonormal columns, and it is
uniquely
determined if
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=n
.

•

Case
m<n
: right polar decomposition.

\bm{A}=\bm{S}_{r}\bm{Q}_{r}
, where
\bm{S}_{r}^{2}=\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
is PSD and is
uniquely
determined.
The factor
\bm{Q}_{r}
has orthonormal rows, and it is
uniquely
determined if
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=m
.

•

Case
m=n
: left/right polar decomposition.

\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{S}_{l}=\bm{S}_{r}\bm{Q}
, where
\bm{S}_{l}^{2}=\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
and
\bm{S}_{r}^{2}=\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
are PSD and are
uniquely
determined. The factor
\bm{Q}
is orthonoal, and it is the same for both the left and right polar decompositions.
\bm{Q}
is
uniquely
determined if
\bm{A}
is nonsingular (
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=n
).

Note in all cases, the PSD factors are uniquely determined, and become PD if
\bm{A}
has full rank (full row rank or column rank). The semi-orthogonal factors
\bm{Q}_{l},\bm{Q}_{r}
, and
\bm{Q}
are uniquely determined only when
\bm{A}
has full rank.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Let the SVD of
\bm{A}
be
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}={(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\top})}{(\bm{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top})}=\bm{Q}_{l}\bm{S}_{l}
such that
\bm{S}_{l}^{2}=\bm{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}\bm{V}^{\top}=\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
. Since
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is PSD,
\bm{S}_{l}
is uniquely determined (Theorem
).
If further
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=n
, i.e.,
\bm{A}
has full (column) rank,
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is PD and
\bm{S}_{l}
has full rank (Theorem
) such that
\bm{Q}_{l}=\bm{A}\bm{S}_{l}^{-1}
, implying
\bm{Q}_{l}
is uniquely determined.

The second case can be similarly proved such that
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}=(\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{U}^{\top})(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\top})=\bm{S}_{r}\bm{Q}_{r}
.
Since
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
is PSD,
\bm{S}_{r}
is uniquely determined. If further
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=m
,
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
is PD and
\bm{S}_{r}
has full rank such that
\bm{Q}_{r}=\bm{S}_{r}^{-1}\bm{A}
is uniquely determined.

The third case is a combination of the previous two. This completes the proof.

Exercise
\thetheo
(Trace of PSD in Polar Decomposition)

Show that the PSD matrices in the polar decomposition satisfy
\mathrm{tr}(\bm{S}_{r})
or
\mathrm{tr}(\bm{S}_{l})
is equal to the sum of the singular values of
\bm{A}
.

Exercise
\thetheo
(Normal from Polar)

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
be nonsingular, and suppose it admits the polar decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{S}_{r}\bm{Q}
, where
\bm{S}_{r}
is PD and
\bm{Q}
is orthogonal. Show that
\bm{A}
is normal if and only if
\bm{S}_{r}\bm{Q}=\bm{Q}\bm{S}_{r}
.

Exercise
\thetheo

Let
\bm{A},\bm{B}\in\real^{n\times n}
be orthogonal, and let
\bm{A}+\bm{B}
be nonsingular. Show that the orthogonal factor in the polar decomposition of
\bm{A}+\bm{B}
is
\bm{A}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{B})^{1/2}
.

13.5
Coordinate Transformation in Matrix Decomposition

Consider a vector
\bm{v}\in\real^{3}
with elements
\bm{v}=[3,7,2]^{\top}
.
It is essential to clarify the significance of these values: In the Cartesian coordinate system, they represent a component of 3 along the
x
-axis, a component of 7 along the
y
-axis, and a component of 2 along the
z
-axis.
These scalar values are the
coordinates
of
\bm{v}
with respect to the basis of the Cartesian system.
Matrix multiplication, on the other hand, gains significance when applied in high-dimensional spaces.

Coordinate defined by a nonsingular matrix.

Suppose we have a
3\times 3
nonsingular matrix
\bm{B}
, which is invertible and possesses linearly independent columns.
Consequently, the three columns of
\bm{B}
collectively form a basis for the
3
space.
Taking a step further, the three columns of
\bm{B}
can serve as the basis for a
new coordinate system
, referred to as the
B
coordinate system
.

Returning to the Cartesian coordinate system, we also have a set of three vectors forming a basis, denoted by
\{\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2},\bm{e}_{3}\}
.
If we arrange the three vectors as columns in a matrix, this matrix will be the identity matrix.
Therefore, when we multiply a vector
\bm{v}
by the identity matrix, denoted by
\bm{I}\bm{v}
, we are essentially performing a coordinate transformation that leaves
\bm{v}
in the same coordinate system.
In other words,
\bm{I}\bm{v}=\bm{v}
means
transferring
\bm{v}
from the Cartesian coordinate system into the Cartesian coordinate system
, the same coordinate.

Similarly, when we multiply a vector
\bm{v}
by the matrix
\bm{B}
, denoted by
\bm{B}\bm{v}
,
we are transforming
\bm{v}
from the Cartesian coordinate system into the
B
coordinate system
.
To illustrate this with a specific example, consider
\bm{v}=[3,7,2]^{\top}
and
\bm{B}=[\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\bm{b}_{3}]
. In this case, we have
\bm{u}=\bm{B}\bm{v}=3\bm{b}_{1}+7\bm{b}_{2}+2\bm{b}_{3}
, i.e., vector
\bm{u}
contains 3 units of the first basis
\bm{b}_{1}
of
\bm{B}
, 7 units of the second basis
\bm{b}_{2}
of
\bm{B}
, and 2 units of the third basis
\bm{b}_{3}
of
\bm{B}
.
Now, if we wish to transform the vector
\bm{u}
from the
B
coordinate system back to the Cartesian coordinate system, we can achieve this by multiplying
\bm{u}
by the inverse of
\bm{B}
, denoted by
\bm{B}^{-1}
. This operation results in
\bm{B}^{-1}\bm{u}=\bm{v}
.

Coordinate defined by an orthogonal matrix.

A
3\times 3
orthogonal matrix
\bm{Q}
defines a “better” coordinate system since its three columns, forming the basis, are mutually orthonormal (same as those in the Cartesian coordinate system).
The operation
\bm{Q}\bm{v}
facilitates the transition of
\bm{v}
from the Cartesian coordinate system to the one defined by the orthogonal matrix.
Since the basis vectors from the orthogonal matrix exhibit orthonormality, just like the three vectors
\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2},\bm{e}_{3}
in the Cartesian coordinate system, the transformation induced by the orthogonal matrix involves rotating or reflecting the Cartesian system.
To revert to the Cartesian coordinate system, one can utilize
\bm{Q}^{-1}=\bm{Q}^{\top}
.

Figure 13.3:
Eigenvalue decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{X}^{-1}
:
\bm{X}^{-1}
undergoes a transformation into a different coordinate system, followed by stretching with
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
, and then transforming back with
\bm{X}
.

\bm{X}^{-1}
and
\bm{X}
are nonsingular, which will change the basis of the system, and the angle between the vectors
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
will
not
be preserved. In other words, the angle between
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
is
different
from the angle between
\bm{v}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\bm{v}_{2}^{\prime}
. The lengths of
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
are also
not
preserved; that is,
\left\lVert\bm{v}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}\neq\left\lVert\bm{v}_{1}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}
and
\left\lVert\bm{v}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}\neq\left\lVert\bm{v}_{2}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}
.

Eigenvalue Decomposition

A square matrix
\bm{A}
with linearly independent eigenvectors can be factored as
\bm{A}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{X}^{-1}
, where
\bm{X}
and
\bm{X}^{-1}
are nonsingular so that they define a system transformation inherently.
The operation
\bm{A}\bm{u}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{X}^{-1}\bm{u}
firstly transfers
\bm{u}
into the coordinate system defined by
\bm{X}^{-1}
, which we shall refer to as the
eigen coordinate system
.
Subsequently, the operation
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\cdot)
stretches each component of the vector in the eigen system by the length of the corresponding eigenvalue.
Finally,
\bm{X}
facilitates the transformation of the resultant vector back to the Cartesian coordinate system.
A visual representation of the coordinate system transformation via eigenvalue decomposition is presented in Figure
, where
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
are two linearly independent eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
such that they form a basis for
2
.

Figure 13.4:
Spectral decomposition
\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}
:
\bm{Q}^{\top}
rotates or reflects,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
stretches the cycle to an ellipse, and
\bm{Q}
rotates or reflects back. Orthogonal matrices
\bm{Q}^{\top}
and
\bm{Q}
only change the basis of the system. However, they preserve both the angle between the vectors
\bm{q}_{1}
and
\bm{q}_{2}
, and their lengths.

Spectral Decomposition

A symmetric matrix
\bm{A}
can be decomposed as
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}
, where
\bm{Q}
and
\bm{Q}^{\top}
are orthogonal matrices so that they define a coordinate system transformation inherently as well. The operation
\bm{A}\bm{u}=\bm{Q}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{u}
firstly rotates or reflects
\bm{u}
into the coordinate system defined by
\bm{Q}^{\top}
, which we shall refer to as the
spectral coordinate system
.
The operation
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\cdot)
stretches each component of the vector in the spectral system by the length of the corresponding eigenvalue.
Subsequently,
\bm{Q}
facilitates the rotation or reflection of the resultant vector back to the original coordinate system.
A demonstration of how the spectral decomposition transforms between coordinate systems in
2
space is shown in Figure
, where
\bm{q}_{1}
and
\bm{q}_{2}
represent two linearly independent eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
such that they form a basis for
2
. The coordinate transformation in the spectral decomposition is similar to that in the eigenvalue decomposition, with the distinction that in the spectral decomposition, orthogonal vectors transformed by
\bm{Q}^{\top}
remain orthogonal.
This is also a property of orthogonal matrices. That is, orthogonal matrices can be viewed as matrices, which change the basis of other matrices while preserving the angle (inner product) between vectors:

\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{v}=(\bm{Q}\bm{u})^{\top}(\bm{Q}\bm{v}).

The invariance of the angle between vectors also relies on the invariance of their lengths:

\left\lVert\bm{Q}\bm{u}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\bm{u}\right\rVert.

SVD

Figure 13.5:
SVD
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
:
\bm{V}^{\top}
and
\bm{U}
rotate or reflect,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
stretches the circle to an ellipse. Orthogonal matrices
\bm{V}^{\top}
and
\bm{U}
only change the basis of the system. However, they preserve both the angle between the vectors
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
, and their lengths.

Figure 13.6:
\bm{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
from SVD or polar decomposition:
\bm{V}^{\top}
rotates or reflects,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
stretches the cycle to an ellipse, and
\bm{V}
rotates or reflects back. Orthogonal matrices
\bm{V}^{\top}
and
\bm{V}
only change the basis of the system. However, they preserve both the angle between the vectors
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
, and their lengths.

Any
m\times n
matrix with rank
r
can be factored as
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
, which represents the SVD. The operation
\bm{A}\bm{u}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}\bm{u}
then firstly rotates or reflects vector
\bm{u}
into the system defined by
\bm{V}^{\top}
, which we refer to as the
V
coordinate system
.
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
stretches the first
r
components of the resulting vector in the
V
system by the lengths of the singular values. If
n\geq m
, then
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
only keeps
m-r
additional components, which are scaled to zero, while removing the final
n-m
components. If
m>n
, then
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
scales
n-r
components to zero and also adds
m-n
additional zero components. Finally,
\bm{U}
rotates or reflects the resulting vector into the
U
coordinate system
defined by
\bm{U}
.
A visual demonstration of how the SVD transforms in a
2\times 2
example is shown in Figure
. Further, Figure
demonstrates the transformation of
\bm{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
by a
2\times 2
example.
Similar to the spectral decomposition, orthogonal matrices
\bm{V}^{\top}
and
\bm{U}
only change the basis of the system but preserve the angle between vectors
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
.

Figure 13.7:
Polar decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}_{l}\bm{S}
:
\bm{V}^{\top}
rotates or reflects,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
stretches the cycle to an ellipse, and
\bm{V}
rotates or reflects back. Orthogonal matrices
\bm{V}^{\top}
,
\bm{V}
, and
\bm{Q}_{l}
only change the basis of the system. However, they preserve both the angle between the vectors
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
, and their lengths.

Polar Decomposition

Any square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
can be factored as the left polar decomposition
\bm{A}=(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\top})(\bm{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top})=\bm{Q}_{l}\bm{S}
. Similarly, the operation
\bm{A}\bm{u}=\bm{Q}_{l}(\bm{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top})\bm{u}
transforms
\bm{u}
into the system defined by
\bm{V}^{\top}
, and stretch each component by the lengths of the corresponding singular values.
Subsequently, the resulting vector is transferred back into the Cartesian coordinate system by
\bm{V}
. Finally,
\bm{Q}_{l}
will rotate or reflect the resulting vector from the Cartesian coordinate system into the
Q
system defined by
\bm{Q}_{l}
.
The right polar decomposition carries a similar interpretation.
Similar to the spectral decomposition, orthogonal matrices
\bm{V}^{\top}
and
\bm{V}
only change the basis of the system but preserve the angle between the vectors
\bm{v}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{2}
.

13.6
Application: Least Squares via QR, UTV, and SVD

Least Squares via the Full QR Decomposition

Let us consider the overdetermined system
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{b}
, where
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
is the data matrix, and
\bm{b}\in\real^{m}
is the observation vector, with
m\geq n
. Typically,
\bm{A}
will have full column rank because real-world data is often uncorrelated or becomes uncorrelated after preprocessing. The least squares (LS) solution is given by
\bm{x}_{LS}=(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
to minimize
\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}
, where
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is invertible since
\bm{A}
has full column rank, and
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})=\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})
.

However, computing the inverse of a matrix can be computationally expensive. Instead, we can use the QR decomposition to find the least squares solution, as described in the following theorem:

Theorem 119
(LS via QR for Full Column Rank Matrix).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, and let
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
be its full QR decomposition, where
\bm{Q}\in\real^{m\times m}
is an orthogonal matrix, and
\bm{R}\in\real^{m\times n}
is an upper triangular matrix appended by
m-n
additional zero rows.
Assume
\bm{A}
has full column rank with
m\geq n
. Suppose further
\bm{R}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{1}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}
, where
\bm{R}_{1}\in\real^{n\times n}
is the square upper triangular part of
\bm{R}
, and
\bm{b}\in\real^{m}
. Then, the LS solution to
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{b}
is given by

\bm{x}_{LS}=\bm{R}_{1}^{-1}\bm{c},

where
\bm{c}
is the first
n
components of
\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{b}
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Since
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
is the full QR decomposition of
\bm{A}
and
m\geq n
, the last
m-n
rows of
\bm{R}
are zero as shown in Figure
. Hence,
\bm{R}_{1}\in\real^{n\times n}
is the square upper triangular part of
\bm{R}
, and

\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}=\bm{R}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{1}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})^{\top}(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})=(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})^{\top}\bm{Q}\bm{Q}^{\top}(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}}{{=}}\left\lVert\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\begin{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{1}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{x}-\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{b}\right\|^{2}=\left\lVert\bm{R}_{1}\bm{x}-\bm{c}\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\bm{d}\right\rVert^{2},

where equality (
{\dagger}
) follows from the invariance of norms under orthogonal transformations. Here,
\bm{c}
represents the first
n
components of
\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{b}
, and
\bm{d}
represents the last
m-n
components of
\bm{Q}^{\top}\bm{b}
. The least squares solution is obtained by solving the upper triangular system
\bm{R}_{1}\bm{x}=\bm{c}
, which can be expressed as
\bm{x}_{LS}=\bm{R}_{1}^{-1}\bm{c}
.

Least Squares via ULV/URV for Rank-Deficient Matrices

In the previous section, we introduced the least squares method using the full QR decomposition for full-rank matrices. However, matrices are often rank-deficient in practice. If
\bm{A}
does not have full column rank,
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is not invertible. To address this, we can use the ULV/URV decomposition to find the least squares solution, as stated in the following theorem.

Theorem 120
(LS via ULV/URV for Rank-Deficient Matrix).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be a matrix with rank
r
and
m\geq n
. Suppose
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\bm{T}\bm{V}
is its full ULV/URV decomposition, where
\bm{U}\in\real^{m\times m}
and
\bm{V}\in\real^{n\times n}
are orthogonal matrices, and

\bm{T}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{T}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{T}_{11}\in\real^{r\times r}
is a lower or upper triangular matrix.
Given
\bm{b}\in\real^{m}
, the LS solution with the minimal
\ell_{2}
norm to
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{b}
is given by

\bm{x}_{LS}=\bm{V}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{T}_{11}^{-1}\bm{c}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{c}
represents the first
r
components of
\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b}
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Since
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\bm{T}\bm{V}
is the full UTV decomposition of
\bm{A}
and
m\geq n
, thus we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})^{\top}(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})=(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})^{\top}\bm{U}\bm{U}^{\top}(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})=\left\lVert\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{U}\bm{T}\bm{V}\bm{x}-\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert\bm{T}\bm{V}\bm{x}-\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert\bm{T}_{11}\bm{e}-\bm{c}\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\bm{d}\right\rVert^{2},

where
\bm{c}
is the first
r
components of
\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b}
,
\bm{d}
is the last
m-r
components of
\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b}
,
\bm{e}
is the first
r
components of
\bm{V}\bm{x}
, and
\bm{f}
is the last
n-r
components of
\bm{V}\bm{x}
:

\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{c}\\
\bm{d}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\bm{V}\bm{x}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{e}\\
\bm{f}\end{bmatrix}.

The LS solution can be calculated by performing backward/forward substitution of the upper/lower triangular system
\bm{T}_{11}\bm{e}=\bm{c}
, i.e.,
\bm{e}=\bm{T}_{11}^{-1}\bm{c}
. To ensure
\bm{x}
has the minimal
\ell_{2}
norm,
\bm{f}
must be zero. Thus,

\bm{x}_{LS}=\bm{V}^{\top}\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{T}_{11}^{-1}\bm{c}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}.

This completes the proof.

A note on the minimal
\ell_{2}
norm LS solution.

For the least squares problem, the set of all minimizers

\mathcal{X}=\{\bm{x}\in\real^{n}:\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b}\right\rVert=\min\}

is convex.
If
\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{X}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
, then

\left\lVert\bm{A}(\lambda\bm{x}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\bm{x}_{2})-\bm{b}\right\rVert\leq\lambda\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{b}\right\rVert+(1-\lambda)\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{b}\right\rVert=\mathop{\min}_{\bm{x}\in\real^{n}}\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b}\right\rVert.

Thus,
\lambda\bm{x}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\bm{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{X}
. In the proof above, if we do not set
\bm{f}=\mathbf{0}
, we will find more least squares solutions. However, the minimal
\ell_{2}
norm least squares solution is unique. For the full-rank case, as discussed in the previous section, the least squares solution is always unique and must have the minimal
\ell_{2}
norm. See also
Foster 2003
;
Golub and Van Loan 2013
for further discussion on this topic.

Least Squares via SVD for Rank-Deficient Matrices

Apart from the UTV decomposition for rank-deficient least squares solution, SVD serves as an alternative.

Theorem 121
(LS via SVD for Rank-Deficient Matrix).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, and let
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
represent its full SVD decomposition, where
\bm{U}\in\real^{m\times m}
and
\bm{V}\in\real^{n\times n}
are orthogonal matrices, and
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=r
. Suppose
\bm{U}=[\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{m}]
,
\bm{V}=[\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{n}]
, and
\bm{b}\in\real^{m}
. Then the LS solution with the minimal
\ell_{2}
norm to
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{b}
is given by

\bm{x}_{LS}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\bm{b}}{\sigma_{i}}\bm{v}_{i}=\bm{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{+}\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b},

(13.2)

where the upper-left side of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{+}\in\real^{n\times m}
is a diagonal matrix, structured as:
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{+}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{+}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}
with
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{+}=\mathrm{diag}(\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}},\frac{1}{\sigma_{2}},\ldots,\frac{1}{\sigma_{r}})
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
To minimize the loss function, we start by writing:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}=(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})^{\top}(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})=(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})^{\top}\bm{U}\bm{U}^{\top}(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}}{{=}}\left\lVert\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{V}\bm{V}^{\top}\bm{x}-\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b}\right\rVert^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\sigma_{i}\alpha_{i}-\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\bm{b})^{2}+\sum_{i=r+1}^{m}(\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\bm{b})^{2},

where equality (
{\dagger}
) follows from the invariance under orthogonal transformations, equality (+) follows by setting
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\bm{V}^{\top}\bm{x}
, and equality (
*
) follows because
\sigma_{r+1}=\sigma_{r+2}=\ldots=\sigma_{m}=0
.
Since
\bm{x}
only appears in
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, we minimize the loss by setting
\alpha_{i}=\frac{\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\bm{b}}{\sigma_{i}}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
. Any value assigned to
\alpha_{r+1},\alpha_{r+2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
will not affect the result. From the regularization point of view (or here, we want the minimal
\ell_{2}
norm), we can set them to 0. This yields the LS solution via SVD:

\bm{x}_{LS}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\bm{b}}{\sigma_{i}}\bm{v}_{i}=\bm{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{+}\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b}=\bm{A}^{+}\bm{b},

where
\bm{A}^{+}=\bm{V}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{+}\bm{U}^{\top}\in\real^{n\times m}
is known as the
pseudo-inverse
of
\bm{A}
.

13.7
Application: PCA via the Spectral Decomposition and the SVD

An important application of SVD is its use in
principal component analysis (PCA)
.
PCA is widely employed to identify patterns in data and to analyze the variance-covariance structure of the data. This enables two primary goals:

1.

Data reduction
. Reducing the dimensionality of the data by selecting a smaller number of
principal components
.

2.

Interpretation
. Uncovering relationships within the data that were previously unobserved.

Given a data set of
n
observations
\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{n}\}
, where each
\bm{x}_{i}\in\real^{p}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, the goal is to project the data onto a lower-dimensional space with dimensionality
m
(
m<p
).
To facilitate this, the sample mean vector and the sample covariance matrix are defined as follows:

\overline{\bm{x}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{x}_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{S}=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{x}_{i}-\overline{\bm{x}})(\bm{x}_{i}-\overline{\bm{x}})^{\top},

where the
n-1
term in
\bm{S}
ensures it is an unbiased and consistent estimator of the covariance matrix
(
Lu 2021e
)
.
Alternatively, the covariance matrix can also be defined as
\bm{S}=\frac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{x}_{i}-\overline{\bm{x}})(\bm{x}_{i}-\overline{\bm{x}})^{\top}
, which is also a consistent estimator of the covariance matrix .

Each data point
\bm{x}_{i}
is then projected onto a scalar value using a vector
\bm{u}_{1}
(see discussion below), such that the projection is given by
\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{x}_{i}
. The mean of the projected data is obtained by
\mathbb{E}[\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{x}_{i}]=\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\overline{\bm{x}}
, and the variance of the projected data is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}[\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{x}_{i}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\overline{\bm{x}})^{2}=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}(\bm{x}_{i}-\overline{\bm{x}})(\bm{x}_{i}-\overline{\bm{x}})^{\top}\bm{u}_{1}=\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{u}_{1}.

To retain the maximum information from the projected data, the projected variance
\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{u}_{1}
is maximized with respect to
\bm{u}_{1}
.
To prevent
\bm{u}_{1}
from scaling indefinitely, a constraint is imposed:
\left\lVert\bm{u}_{1}\right\rVert^{2}=\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{u}_{1}=1
.
Using the method of Lagrange multipliers (see, for example,
Bishop 2006
;
Boyd et al. 2004
), the optimization problem becomes:

\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{u}_{1}+\lambda_{1}(1-\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{u}_{1}).

Solving this yields the equation:

\bm{S}\bm{u}_{1}=\lambda_{1}\bm{u}_{1}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{u}_{1}=\lambda_{1}.

This shows that
\bm{u}_{1}
is an eigenvector of
\bm{S}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
. The direction of maximum variance,
\bm{u}_{1}
, corresponds to the largest eigenvalue of
\bm{S}
. The eigenvector
\bm{u}_{1}
is referred to as the
first principal axis
.

The subsequent principal axes are defined by the remaining eigenvectors of
\bm{S}
, arranged in descending order of their eigenvalues. By selecting the top
m
principal components, the dimensionality of the data can be effectively reduced. This process is known as the
maximum-variance formulation
of PCA
(
Hotelling 1933
;
Bishop 2006
;
Shlens 2014
)
.
Alternative perspectives on the maximum-variance formulation, such as from data reconstruction, data projection, and autoencoders, are discussed in
(
Lu 2021c
)
.
Another approach, known as the
minimum-error formulation
of PCA, is discussed in
(
Pearson 1901
;
Bishop 2006
)
.

PCA via the spectral decomposition.

Now, let’s assume that the data are centered, meaning the sample mean vector
\overline{\bm{x}}
is zero.
Alternatively, we can centralize the data by setting
\bm{x}_{i}:=\bm{x}_{i}-\overline{\bm{x}}
, which involves subtracting the mean from each data point.
Let the data matrix
\bm{X}\in\real^{n\times p}
contain the data observations as rows. The covariance matrix is given by

\bm{S}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}}{n-1},

which is a symmetric matrix. Its spectral decomposition is:

\bm{S}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\bm{U}^{\top},

(13.3)

where
\bm{U}
is an orthogonal matrix of eigenvectors (the columns of
\bm{U}
are eigenvectors of
\bm{S}
), and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{p})
is a diagonal matrix containing the eigenvalues (ordered such that
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{p}
). The eigenvectors are called the
principal axes
of the data, and they
decorrelate
the covariance matrix. Projections of the data onto the principal axes are called the
principal components
. The
i
-th principal component is given by the
i
-th column of
\bm{X}\bm{U}
.
If our objective is to reduce the dimension from
p
to
m
, we simply select the first
m
columns of
\bm{X}\bm{U}
.

PCA via SVD.

If the SVD of
\bm{X}
is given by
\bm{X}=\bm{P}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{Q}^{\top}
, then the covariance matrix can be expressed as

\bm{S}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}}{n-1}=\bm{Q}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{2}}{n-1}\bm{Q}^{\top},

(13.4)

where
\bm{Q}\in\real^{p\times p}
is an orthogonal matrix containing the right singular vectors of
\bm{X}
, and the upper-left part of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is a diagonal matrix containing the singular values
\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots)
, ordered such that
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\ldots
. The number of singular values is equal to
\min\{n,p\}
, which will not be larger than
p
, and some of these values may be zero.

By comparing Equation (
) with Equation (
), we can see that Equation (
) also represents a spectral decomposition of
\bm{S}
.
This is because both the eigenvalues in
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
and the singular values in
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
are ordered in descending order, and the spectral decomposition in terms of the eigenspaces is unique (as discussed in Section
).

In other words, the right singular vectors
\bm{Q}
can also serve as the principal axes, which decorrelate the covariance matrix.
The singular values are related to the eigenvalues of the covariance matrix through the relationship:
\lambda_{i}=\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{n-1}
. To reduce the dimensionality of the data from
p
to
m
, we select the largest
m
singular values and their corresponding right singular vectors.
This process is related to the truncated SVD (TSVD), where:
\bm{X}_{m}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}\bm{p}_{i}\bm{q}_{i}^{\top}
, where
\bm{p}_{i}
’s and
\bm{q}_{i}
’s are the columns of
\bm{P}
and
\bm{Q}
, respectively.

A byproduct of PCA via SVD for high-dimensional data.

For a principal axis
\bm{u}_{i}
of
\bm{S}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}}{n-1}
, we have

\frac{\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}}{n-1}\bm{u}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\bm{u}_{i}.

Multiplying both sides by
\bm{X}
on the left, we obtain:

\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}}{n-1}(\bm{X}\bm{u}_{i})=\lambda_{i}(\bm{X}\bm{u}_{i}),

which implies that
\lambda_{i}
is also an eigenvalue of
\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}}{n-1}\in\real^{n\times n}
, and the corresponding eigenvector is
\bm{X}\bm{u}_{i}
. This relationship is also discussed in the proof of Theorem
, which establishes the existence of the SVD. If
p\gg n
, instead of finding the eigenvectors of
\bm{S}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}}{n-1}
, i.e., the principal axes of
\bm{S}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}}{n-1}
, we can find the eigenvectors of
\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}}{n-1}
. This reduces the computational complexity from
\mathcal{O}(p^{3})
to
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
, which is more efficient when
p\gg n
.

Now, returning to the principal axes of
\bm{S}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}}{n-1}
, suppose the eigenvector of
\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}}{n-1}
is
\bm{v}_{i}
, corresponding to a nonzero eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
:

\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}}{n-1}\bm{v}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\bm{v}_{i}.

Multiplying both sides by
\bm{X}^{\top}
, we obtain

\frac{\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}}{n-1}(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{v}_{i})=\bm{S}(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{v}_{i})=\lambda_{i}(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{v}_{i}),

which shows that the eigenvector
\bm{u}_{i}
of
\bm{S}
is proportional to
\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{v}_{i}
, where
\bm{v}_{i}
is the eigenvector of
\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}}{n-1}
corresponding to the same eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
. Note that a further normalization step is required to ensure that
\left\lVert\bm{u}_{i}\right\rVert=1
.
Thus, when
p\gg n
, we can also find the principal axes using the SVD of
\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}}{n-1}
.

13.8
Application: Low-Rank Approximation

In the context of low-rank approximation, two types of problems arise due to the interplay between rank and error:
fixed-precision approximation problem
and
fixed-rank
approximation problem
. In the fixed-precision approximation problem, given a matrix
\bm{A}
and a tolerance
\epsilon
, the goal is to find a matrix
\bm{B}

with rank
r=r(\epsilon)
such that
\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{B}\right\rVert\leq\epsilon
in an appropriate matrix norm. On the contrary, in the fixed-rank approximation problem, one seeks a matrix
\bm{B}
with a fixed rank
k
that minimizes the error
\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{B}\right\rVert
. This section focuses on the latter. For further details and examples, see
Kishore Kumar and Schneider 2017
;
Martinsson 2019
.

To approximate a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
of rank
r
with a rank-
k
matrix
\bm{B}
(
k<r
), the approximation can be evaluated using the spectral norm:

\bm{B}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{B}}\ \ \left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{B}\right\rVert_{2},

where the spectral norm is defined as follows:

Definition 122
(Spectral Norm).

The spectral norm of a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
is defined as

\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{2}=\mathop{\max}_{\bm{x}\neq\mathbf{0}}\frac{\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}}=\mathop{\max}_{\bm{x}\in\real^{n}:\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}=1}\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2},

which is equivalent to the largest singular value of
\bm{A}
, i.e.,
\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{2}=\sigma_{1}(\bm{A})
.

Then, we can recover the optimal rank-
k
approximation by the following theorem.

Theorem 123
(Eckart-Young-Misky Theorem w.r.t. Spectral Norm).

Given a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
,
1\leq k\leq\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=r
, and let
\bm{A}_{k}
be the truncated SVD (TSVD) of
\bm{A}
with the largest
k
singular terms, i.e.,
\bm{A}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}
from the SVD of
\bm{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}
by zeroing out the
r-k
trailing singular values of
\bm{A}
. Then,
\bm{A}_{k}
is the optimal rank-
k
approximation to
\bm{A}
in terms of the spectral norm.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
For any matrix
\bm{B}
with
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{B})=k
, we aim to show that
\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{B}\right\rVert_{2}\geq\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{A}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}
.

Since
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{B})=k
,
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\bm{B}))=n-k
. Thus, any set of
k+1
basis vectors in
n
intersects
\mathcal{N}(\bm{B})
.
From Proposition
, the singular vectors
\{\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{r}\}
form an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}^{\top})\subset\real^{n}
; so we can choose the first
k+1

\bm{v}_{i}
’s as a
k+1
basis for
n
. Let
\bm{V}_{k+1}=[\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{k+1}]
. Then there exists a unit vector
\bm{x}
such that

\bm{x}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{B})\cap\mathcal{C}(\bm{V}_{k+1}),\qquad\text{s.t.}\,\,\,\,\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}=1.

That is,
\bm{x}
can be expressed as
\bm{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}a_{i}\bm{v}_{i}
, and
\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}a_{i}\bm{v}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}a_{i}^{2}=1
.
Additionally,
\bm{B}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
.
Using these properties, the following inequality chain holds:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{B}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\lVert(\bm{A}-\bm{B})\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\big/\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}}{{=}}\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\sigma_{i}^{2}(\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{x})^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\geq}}\sigma_{k+1}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}(\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{x})^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\ddagger}}}{{\geq}}\sigma_{k+1}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}a_{i}^{2}=\sigma_{k+1}^{2},

where the first inequality follows from the defintion of the spectral norm, equality (
{\dagger}
) follows from the fact that
\bm{x}
is in null space of
\bm{B}
,
equality (
+
) follows from the fact that
\bm{x}
is orthogonal to
\bm{v}_{k+2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{r}
, inequality (
*
) follows from
\sigma_{k+1}\leq\sigma_{k}\leq\ldots\leq\sigma_{1}
, and inequality (
{\ddagger}
) follows from
\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{x}=a_{i}
.
On the other hand, it is evident that
\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{A}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left\lVert\sum_{i=k+1}^{r}\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\sigma_{k+1}^{2}
. Thus,
\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{A}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{B}\right\rVert_{2}
, which completes the proof.

Moreover, it can also be shown that
\bm{A}_{k}
is the optimal rank-
k
approximation to
\bm{A}
in terms of the Frobenius norm. The minimal error is given by the Euclidean norm of the singular values that have been zeroed out in the process:
\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{A}_{k}\right\rVert_{F}=\sqrt{\sigma_{k+1}^{2}+\sigma_{k+2}^{2}+\ldots+\sigma_{r}^{2}}
(see, for example,
Lu 2021c
).

Definition 124
(Frobenius Norm).

The
Frobenius norm
of a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
is defined as

\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1,j=1}^{m,n}(a_{ij})^{2}}=\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}(\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top})}=\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})}=\sqrt{\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}+\ldots+\sigma_{r}^{2}},

where
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r}
are the nonzero singular values of
\bm{A}
.

Exercise
\thetheo

Given the definition of the Frobenius norm, show that the truncated SVD
\bm{A}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}
is also the optimal rank-
k
approximation to
\bm{A}
in terms of the Frobenius norm.

13.2.

Given a square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, show that
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
and
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
are similar (Definition
).
Hint: Proceeding with the SVD of
\bm{A}
.

13.2.

Show that all eigenvalues of a square matrix are less than or equal to its largest singular value
\sigma_{1}
.

13.2.

Suppose
\bm{x}
is an eigenvector of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
corresponding to a nonzero eigenvalue. Discuss the corresponding eigenvector of
\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}
.
Hint: Premultiply by
\bm{A}
.

13.2.

Given the SVD of a nonsingular square matrix
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}\in\real^{n\times n}
, determine the singular values of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
.

13.2.

Find the optimal rank-1 approximation (in terms of the spectral norm) for the following matrix:

\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}.

Skew-symmetric.
Given a skew-symmetric and tridiagonal matrix
\bm{S}\in\real^{n\times n}
, show that it can be decomposed as:

\bm{P}^{\top}\bm{S}\bm{P}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\bm{B}^{\top}\\
\bm{B}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{B}\in\real^{m\times m}
,
n=2m
, and
\bm{P}
is a permutation matrix.
Given further the SVD of
\bm{B}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}
, find the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of

.
