Part VI
Special Topics

\nopagecolor

Chapter 14
Alternating Least Squares (ALS)

14.1
Preliminary: Least Squares Approximations

The linear model is a fundamental technique in regression analysis, relying on the least squares approximation to minimize the sum of squared errors (refer to Section
). This method naturally emerges when aiming to identify the regression function that minimizes the corresponding expected squared error.
Over the past several decades, linear models have found extensive applications across diverse domains, including decision-making
(
Dawes and Corrigan 1974
)
, time series analysis
(
Christensen 1991
;
Lu 2017
)
, quantitative finance
(
Menchero et al. 2011
)
, and various other fields such as production science, social science, and soil science
(
Fox 1997
;
Lane 2002
;
Schaeffer 2004
;
Mrode 2014
)
.

To be more concrete, consider an overdetermined system represented by
\bm{b}=\bm{A}\bm{x}
, where
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
represents the
input data matrix
(also known as the
predictor variables
),
\bm{b}\in\real^{m}
is the
observation vector
(or
target/response vector
), and the number of samples
m
exceeds the number of predictors
n
.
The vector
\bm{x}
represents the weights of the linear model.
Typically,
\bm{A}
is assumed to have full column rank, as real-world data is often uncorrelated or can be preprocessed to meet this condition.
In practical scenarios, a
bias term
(a.k.a., an
intercept
) is added to the first column of
\bm{A}
. This adjustment enables the least squares method to solve equations of the form:

\widetilde{\bm{A}}\widetilde{\bm{x}}=[\bm{1},\bm{A}]\begin{bmatrix}x_{0}\\
\bm{x}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{b}.

(14.1)

However, it is common for the equation
\bm{b}=\bm{A}\bm{x}
to have no exact solution (the system is
inconsistent
) due to the being overdetermined–that is, there are more equations than unknowns.
Define the column space of
\bm{A}
as
\{\bm{A}\boldsymbol{\gamma}:\,\,\forall\,\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\real^{n}\}
, denoted by
\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})
.
In essence, when we say
\bm{b}=\bm{A}\bm{x}
has no solution, it implies that
\bm{b}
lies outside the column space of
\bm{A}
.
In other words, the error
\bm{e}=\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}
cannot be reduced to zero.
The objective then becomes minimizing the error, which is typically measured using the mean squared error.
The resulting solution
\bm{x}_{LS}
, which minimizes
\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}_{LS}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, is referred to as the
least squares solution
. The least squares method is a cornerstone of mathematical sciences, and a wealth of resources are dedicated to its study and application, including works by
Trefethen and Bau III 1997
;
Strang 2019
;
Strang 2021
;
Lu 2021e
.

Least squares by calculus.

When
\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
is differentiable and the parameter space of
\bm{x}
spans the entire space
n
(i.e., unconstrained optimization)
,
the least squares estimate corresponds to the root of the gradient of
\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
.
This leads us to the following lemma.

Lemma 125
(Least Squares by Calculus).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be a fixed data matrix with full rank and
m\geq n
(i.e., its columns are linearly independent) .
For the overdetermined system
\bm{b}=\bm{A}\bm{x}
, the least squares solution, obtained by setting the partial derivatives in every direction of
\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
to zero (i.e., the gradient vanishes), is given by
\bm{x}_{LS}=(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
. The value,
\bm{x}_{LS}=(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
, is commonly referred to as the ordinary least squares (OLS) estimate or simply the least squares (LS) estimate of
\bm{x}
.

To prove the lemma above, we must show that
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is invertible. Given that
\bm{A}
has full rank and
m\geq n
, the matrix
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is invertible if it has rank
n
, which matches the rank of
\bm{A}
. This claim is verified in Lemma
.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Using calculus, a function
f(\bm{x})
attains a minimum at
\bm{x}_{LS}
when its gradient
\nabla f(\bm{x})=\mathbf{0}
. The gradient of
\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
is given by
2\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}-2\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
.
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is invertible since we assume
\bm{A}
is fixed and has full rank with
m\geq n
(Lemma
).
Consequently, the OLS solution for
\bm{x}
is
\bm{x}_{LS}=(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
, which completes the proof.

Definition 126
(Normal Equation).

The condition for the gradient of
\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
to be zero can be expressed as
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{LS}=\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
. This is called the normal equation.
Under the assumption that
\bm{A}
has full rank with
m\geq n
, the matrix
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is invertible, leading to the solution
\bm{x}_{LS}=(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
.

(a)

A convex function.

(b)

A concave function.

(c)

A random function.

Figure 14.1:
Three functions.

However, it is not immediately evident whether the least squares estimate derived in Lemma
corresponds to a minimum, maximum, or neither. An example illustrating this ambiguity is shown in Figure
.
What we can assert with confidence is the existence of at least one root for the gradient of the function
f(\bm{x})=\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
.
This root represents a necessary condition for a minimum point, but not a sufficient one.
The following remark provides further clarification on this matter.

Remark 127
(Verification of Least Squares Solution).

Why does a zero gradient imply the least mean squared error?
The typical explanation relies on convex analysis, which we will explore shortly. However, here we directly confirm that the OLS solution minimizes the mean squared error.
For any
\bm{x}\neq\bm{x}_{LS}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}_{LS}+\bm{A}\bm{x}_{LS}-\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}_{LS}+\bm{A}(\bm{x}_{LS}-\bm{x})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}_{LS}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert\bm{A}(\bm{x}_{LS}-\bm{x})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+2\left(\bm{A}(\bm{x}_{LS}-\bm{x})\right)^{\top}(\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}_{LS})

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}_{LS}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert\bm{A}(\bm{x}_{LS}-\bm{x})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+2(\bm{x}_{LS}-\bm{x})^{\top}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}-\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{LS}),

where the third term is zero because of the normal equation, and
\left\lVert\bm{A}(\bm{x}_{LS}-\bm{x})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq 0
. Therefore,

\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}_{LS}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Thus, we have demonstrated that the OLS estimate corresponds to a minimum, not a maximum or a saddle point. .
As a matter of fact, this condition from the least squares estimate is also known as the sufficiency of stationarity under convexity. When
\bm{x}
is defined over the entire space
n
, this condition is also known as the necessity of stationarity under convexity.

Another question that arises is: Why does the normal equation appear to “magically” provide solutions for
\bm{x}
?
A simple analogy can help illustrate this concept. The equation
x^{2}=-1
has no real solution.
However,
x\cdot x^{2}=x\cdot(-1)
does have a real solution
\hat{x}=0
, in which case,
\hat{x}
minimizes the difference between
x^{2}
and
-1
, making them as close as possible.

Example 128
(Altering the Solution Set by Left Multiplication).

Consider the data matrix and target vector:

\tiny\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}-3&-4\\
4&6\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}

and

\bm{b}=\tiny\begin{bmatrix}1\\
-1\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

It can be easily verified that the system
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{b}
has no solution for
\bm{x}
.
However, if we multiply both sides on the left by

\bm{B}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}0&-1&6\\
0&1&-4\end{bmatrix},

then the solution
\bm{x}_{LS}=[1/2,-1/2]^{\top}
satisfies
\bm{B}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{B}\bm{b}
.
This example illustrates why the normal equation can lead to the least squares solution. Multiplying a linear system on the left alters the solution set, effectively projecting the problem into a different space where the least squares solution can be computed.

Rank-deficiency.

In this discussion, we assume
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
has full rank with
m\geq n
, ensuring that
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
is invertible.
However, if two or more columns of
\bm{A}
are perfectly correlated, the matrix
\bm{A}
becomes deficient, and
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}
becomes singular.
To address this issue, one can choose
\bm{x}
that minimizes
\left\lVert\bm{x}_{LS}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
while satisfying the normal equation. That is, we select the least squares solution with the smallest magnitude.
In Section
, we briefly discuss how to use UTV decomposition and SVD to address this rank-deficient least squares problems.
See Problems
\sim
or the following paragraph for more insights.

Regularizations and stability.

A common problem that arise in the ordinary least square solution is the near-singularity of
\bm{A}
.
Let the SVD of
\bm{A}
be
\bm{A}=\bm{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\bm{V}^{\top}\in\real^{m\times n}
, where
\bm{U}\in\real^{m\times m}
and
\bm{V}\in\real^{n\times n}
are orthogonal, and the main diagonal of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\in\real^{m\times n}
contains the singular values. Consequently,
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}=\bm{V}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\bm{V}^{\top}=\bm{V}\bm{S}\bm{V}^{\top}
, where
\bm{S}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1}^{2},\sigma_{2}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{n}^{2})\in\real^{n\times n}
contains the squared singular values of
\bm{A}
. When
\bm{A}
is nearly singular,
\sigma_{n}^{2}\approx 0
, making the inverse operation
(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}=\bm{V}\bm{S}^{-1}\bm{V}^{\top}
numerically unstable.
As a result, the solution
\bm{x}_{LS}=(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
may diverge.
To mitigate this issue, an
\ell_{2}
regularization term is typically added, leading to the solution of the following optimization problem:

\bm{x}_{Tik}=\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{x}}\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{A}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lambda\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(14.2)

This approach is known as the
Tikhonov regularization method
(or simply the
\ell_{2}
regularized method)
(
Tikhonov 1963
)
.
The gradient of the problem is
2(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}+\lambda\bm{I})\bm{x}-2\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
. Thus, the least squares solution is given by

\bm{x}_{Tik}=(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}+\lambda\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}.

The inverse operation becomes
(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}+\lambda\bm{I})^{-1}=\bm{V}(\bm{S}+\lambda\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{V}^{\top}
, where
\widetilde{\bm{S}}=(\bm{S}+\lambda\bm{I})=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\lambda,\sigma_{2}^{2}+\lambda,\ldots,\sigma_{n}^{2}+\lambda)
.
The solutions for OLS and Tikhonov regularized LS are given, respectively, by

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{LS}

\displaystyle=(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}=\bm{V}\left(\bm{S}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\right)\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b};

(14.3)

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{Tik}

\displaystyle=(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}+\lambda\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}=\bm{V}\left((\bm{S}+\lambda\bm{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\right)\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{b},

where the main diagonals of
\left(\bm{S}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\right)
are
\mathrm{diag}(\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}},\frac{1}{\sigma_{2}},\ldots,\frac{1}{\sigma_{n}})
; and the main diagonals of
\left((\bm{S}+\lambda\bm{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\right)
are
\mathrm{diag}(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{1}^{2}+\lambda},\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\sigma_{2}^{2}+\lambda},\ldots,\frac{\sigma_{n}}{\sigma_{n}^{2}+\lambda})
. The latter solution is more stable if
\lambda
is greater than the smallest nonzero squared singular value.
The condition number, which measures the sensitivity of the problem to perturbations, becomes smaller if the smallest singular value
\sigma_{n}
is close to zero:

\kappa(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})=\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}\qquad\rightarrow\qquad\kappa(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}+\lambda\bm{I})=\frac{\lambda+\sigma_{1}^{2}}{\lambda+\sigma_{n}^{2}}.

Tikhonov regularization effectively prevents divergence in the least squares solution

\bm{x}_{LS}=(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A})^{-1}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{b}
when the matrix
\bm{A}
is nearly singular or even rank-deficient. This improvement enhances the convergence properties of both the LS algorithm and its variants, such as alternating least squares, while addressing identifiability issues in various settings (see Section
). As a result, Tikhonov regularization has become a widely applied technique.

Data least squares.

While the OLS method accounts for errors in the response variable
\bm{b}
, the
data least sqaures (DLS)
method considers errors in the predictor variables:

\bm{x}_{DLS}=\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{x},\widetilde{\bm{A}}}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{A}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2},\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\bm{b}\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}+\widetilde{\bm{A}}),

(14.4)

where
\widetilde{\bm{A}}
represents a perturbation of
\bm{A}
(i.e., a noise in the predictor variables).
That is,
(\bm{A}+\widetilde{\bm{A}})\bm{x}_{DLS}=\bm{b}
, assuming the measured response
\bm{b}
is noise-free.
The Lagrangian function and its gradient w.r.t.
\bm{x}
are, respectively, given by

\displaystyle L(\bm{x},\widetilde{\bm{A}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\bm{A}}\widetilde{\bm{A}}^{\top})+\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}(\bm{A}\bm{x}+\widetilde{\bm{A}}\bm{x}-\bm{b});

\displaystyle\nabla_{\widetilde{\bm{A}}}L(\bm{x},\widetilde{\bm{A}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\bm{A}}+\boldsymbol{\lambda}\bm{x}^{\top}=\mathbf{0}\quad\implies\quad\widetilde{\bm{A}}=-\boldsymbol{\lambda}\bm{x}^{\top},

where
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\real^{m}
is a vector of Lagrange multipliers.
Substituting the value of the vanishing gradient into
(\bm{A}+\widetilde{\bm{A}})\bm{x}=\bm{b}
yields
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\frac{\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b}}{\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x}}
and
\widetilde{\bm{A}}=-\frac{(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})\bm{x}^{\top}}{\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x}}
.
Therefore, using the invariance of cyclic permutation of factors in trace, the objective function becomes

\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{x}}\frac{(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})^{\top}(\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b})}{\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x}}.

Total least squares.

Similar to data least squares, the
total least squares (TLS)
method accounts for errors in both the predictor variables and the response variables. The TLS problem can be formulated as:

\bm{x}_{TLS}=\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{x},\widetilde{\bm{A}},\widetilde{\bm{b}}}\left\lVert[\widetilde{\bm{A}},\widetilde{\bm{b}}]\right\rVert_{F}^{2},\quad\text{s.t.}\quad(\bm{b}+\widetilde{\bm{b}})\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}+\widetilde{\bm{A}}),

(14.5)

where
\widetilde{\bm{A}}
and
\widetilde{\bm{b}}
are perturbations in the predictor variables and the response variable, respectively.
To simplify, define
\bm{C}=[\bm{A},\bm{b}]\in\real^{m\times(n+1)}
,
\bm{D}=[\widetilde{\bm{A}},\widetilde{\bm{b}}]\in\real^{m\times(n+1)}
, and
\bm{y}\in\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{x}\\
-1\end{bmatrix}
, the problem can be equivalently stated as

\bm{x}_{TLS}=\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{y},\bm{D}}\left\lVert\bm{D}\right\rVert_{F}^{2},\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\bm{D}\bm{y}=-\bm{C}\bm{y},

(14.6)

14.2
Netflix Recommender and Matrix Factorization

The rapid advancements in sensor technology and computer hardware have led to an explosion of data, presenting new challenges for data analysis. This data is often vast, noisy, and distorted, necessitating pre-processing to enable effective deductive science. For instance, signals captured by antenna arrays are frequently contaminated by noise and other degradations. To analyze such data effectively, it is essential to reconstruct or represent it in a manner that reduces inaccuracies while adhering to feasibility conditions.

In many cases, data collected from complex systems arises from multiple interrelated variables acting in unison. When these variables are not well-defined, the original data can exhibit overlapping and unclear information. By constructing a reduced system model, it is possible to achieve a level of accuracy comparable to that of the original system. A common approach to noise reduction, model simplification, data compression, and reconstruction is to replace the original data with a lower-dimensional representation derived via subspace approximation.
As a result,
low-rank matrix approximations (LRMA) or low-rank matrix decompositions
are integral to numerous applications, such as data compression, feature selection, and noise filtering.

Low-rank matrix decomposition is a powerful tool in machine learning and data mining for expressing a given matrix as the product of two or more matrices with lower dimensions. It captures the essential structure of a matrix while filtering out noise and redundancies. Common methods for low-rank matrix decomposition include singular value decomposition (SVD), principal component analysis (PCA), multiplicative update nonnegative matrix factorization (NMF), and the alternating least squares (ALS) approach, which will be introduced in this section.

For example, in the Netflix Prize competition
(
Bennett et al. 2007
)
, the goal is to predict the ratings of users for different movies, given the existing ratings (resp. interaction) of those users for other movies (resp. items).
We index
M
movies with
m=1,2,\ldots,M
and
N
users
with
n=1,2,\ldots,N
. (In the matrix approximation context, lowercase letters e.g.,
m,n,k
, are used for the subscripts in running indices, while uppercase letters
M,N,K
denote the upper bound of an index.) We denote the rating of the
n
-th user for the
m
-th movie by
a_{mn}
.
Define
\bm{A}
as an
M\times N
rating matrix (a
movie-by-user matrix
) with columns
\{\bm{a}_{n}\}\in\real^{M}
containing ratings provided by the
n
-th user (also known as the
preference matrix
). Note that many ratings
\{a_{mn}\}
are missing, and our goal is to predict these missing ratings accurately, i.e., to complete the matrix.

It is clear that without some inherent structure in the matrix, and consequently in the way users rate items, there would be no relationship between the observed and unobserved entries. This would mean there is no unique method to complete the matrix. Therefore, it is crucial to impose some structure on the matrix. A common structural assumption is that of low rank: we aim to fill in the missing entries of matrix
\bm{A}
, assuming
\bm{A}
is a low-rank matrix. This assumption makes the problem well-posed and allows for a unique solution, as the low-rank structure connects the entries of the matrix (i.e., a
matrix completion
problem). Consequently, the unobserved entries can no longer be independent of the observed values.

It is important to note that, except for very special data structures, a matrix cannot be compressed/decomposed without incurring some compression error, since a low-rank matrix representation is only an approximation of the original matrix.
This procedure, often known as
collaborative filtering
, seeks to exploit co-occurring patterns in the observed behaviors across users in order to predict future behaviors of users.

Consider the mask matrix
\bm{M}\in\{0,1\}^{M\times N}
, where
m_{mn}\in\{0,1\}
indicates whether user
n
has rated movie
m
or not.
Then the low-rank matrix completion problem can be formulated as

\widetilde{\bm{A}}=\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{X}\in\real^{M\times N}}\sum_{m,n=1}^{M,N}(x_{mn}-a_{mn})^{2}\cdot m_{mn}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{s.t.}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathrm{rank}(\bm{X})\leq K.

(14.7)

However, this problem is NP-hard (non-deterministic polynomial)
(
Hardt et al. 2014
)
. While it can be equivalently written (proof from singular value decomposition) in the following unconstrained form:

\widetilde{\bm{A}}=\widetilde{\bm{W}}\widetilde{\bm{Z}}=\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}\\
\bm{Z}\in\real^{K\times N}\end{subarray}}\sum_{m,n=1}^{M,N}((\bm{W}\bm{Z})_{mn}-a_{mn})^{2}\cdot m_{mn},

(14.8)

which allows for indirect solution or approximation using alternate algorithms.

We then formally consider algorithms for solving the following problem: The matrix
\bm{A}
is approximately factorized into an
M\times K
matrix
\bm{W}
and a
K\times N
matrix
\bm{Z}
.
Typically,
K
is selected to be smaller than both
M
and
N
, ensuring that
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
have reduced dimensions compared to the original matrix
\bm{A}
.
This reduction in dimensionality results in a compressed version of the original data matrix.
An appropriate decision on the value of
K
is critical in practice; but the choice of
K
is very
often problem-dependent.
The factorization is significant in the sense that if
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{N}]
and
\bm{Z}=[\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}]
are the column partitions of
\bm{A}
and
\bm{Z}
, respectively, then we have
\bm{a}_{n}=\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n}
. This means each column
\bm{a}_{n}
is approximated by a linear combination of the columns of
\bm{W}
, weighted by the components in
\bm{z}_{n}
.
Therefore, the columns of
\bm{W}
can be thought of as containing the column basis (
template columns
, or the approximation of the column basis) of
\bm{A}
; and
\bm{z}_{n}
indicates the coordinates (or
activations
) of
\bm{a}_{n}
in the basis
\bm{W}
.
This concept is similar to the factorization methods discussed in the data interpretation part (Part
).
The key difference is that we do not restrict
\bm{W}
to consist of exact columns from
\bm{A}
.

Algorithm 6

2-Block Coordinate Descent: Framework of Most ALS and NMF Algorithms

1:

A loss function for a variable with two blocks
\bm{X}=(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
:
f(\bm{X})=f(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
, and data matrix
\bm{A}
;

2:

Constraint on
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
;

3:

Generate some initial matrices
\bm{W}^{(0)}
and
\bm{Z}^{(0)}
;

4:

for

t=1,2,\ldots

do

5:

\bm{W}^{(t)}\leftarrow\text{update}\big(\bm{A},\bm{Z}^{(t-1)},\bm{W}^{(t-1)}\big)
;

6:

\bm{Z}^{(t)}\leftarrow\text{update}\big(\bm{A},\bm{W}^{(t)T},\bm{Z}^{(t-1)T}\big)
;

7:

end

for

However, in most cases, the resulting factorization problem has no exact solution, thus requiring optimization procedures to find suitable numerical approximations. The problem is usually solved using a
two-block coordinate descent (2-BCD)
approach (see Algorithm
for a general illustration).
In order to obtain the approximation
\bm{A}\approx\bm{W}\bm{Z}
, we must establish a loss function such that the distance between
\bm{A}
and
\bm{W}\bm{Z}
can be measured.
In our discussion, the chosen loss function is the Frobenius norm (a.k.a., the Euclidean distance, Definition
) between two matrices, which vanishes to zero if
\bm{A}=\bm{W}\bm{Z}
, and its advantages will become evident shortly.

To simplify the problem, let’s first assume that there are no missing ratings.
We project data vectors
\bm{a}_{n}\in\real^{M}
into a lower dimension
\bm{z}_{n}\in\real^{K}
with
K<\min\{M,N\}

in a way that the
reconstruction error
(a.k.a.,
criterion function, objective function, cost function, or loss function
) as measured by the Frobenius norm (a.k.a., sum of squared loss) is minimized (assume
K
is known):

L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})=D(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\left(a_{mn}-\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2},\penalty\

(14.9)

where
\bm{W}=[\bm{w}_{1}^{\top};\bm{w}_{2}^{\top};\ldots;\bm{w}_{M}^{\top}]\in\real^{M\times K}
and
\bm{Z}=[\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}]\in\real^{K\times N}
contain
\bm{w}_{m}
’s and
\bm{z}_{n}
’s as
rows and columns
, respectively.
In (
),
L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
indicates it is a loss function w.r.t.
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
, and
D(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})
implies it is a distance/divergence between
\bm{A}
and
\bm{W}\bm{Z}
(we will use the two terms interchangeably when necessary).

Moreover, the loss function
L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
demonstrates convexity concerning
\bm{Z}
when
\bm{W}
is held constant, and analogously, with respect to
\bm{W}
when
\bm{Z}
is fixed.
This characteristic motivates the alternate algorithm that alternately fixes one of the variables and
optimizes over the other.
Therefore, we can first minimize the loss with respect to
\bm{Z}

while keeping
\bm{W}
fixed, and subsequently minimize it with respect to
\bm{W}
with
\bm{Z}
fixed.
This leads to two optimization problems, denoted by ALS1 and ALS2, respectively:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \bm{Z}&\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z});\qquad\text{(ALS1)}\\
\bm{W}&\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z}).\qquad\text{(ALS2)}\end{aligned}\right.

This is known as the
two-block coordinate descent (2-BCD) algorithm
as mentioned previously, where we alternate between optimizing the least squares with respect to
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
.
Hence, it is also called the
alternating least squares (ALS)
algorithm
(
Comon et al. 2009
;
Takács and Tikk 2012
;
Giampouras et al. 2018
)
. Convergence is guaranteed if the loss function
L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
decreases at each iteration, and we shall discuss this further in the sequel.

Remark 129
(Convexity and Global Minimum).

Although the loss function defined by the Frobenius norm
\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
is convex either with respect to
\bm{W}
when
\bm{Z}
is fixed or vice versa (called marginally convex), it is not jointly convex in both variables simultaneously. Therefore, locating the global minimum is infeasible.
However, the convergence is assured to find local minima.

More generally, let
D(\bm{A},\bm{B})
be convex in the second argument
\bm{B}
. Then,
D(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})
is convex in
\bm{W}
for
\bm{Z}
fixed and vice versa; see Problem
.

Given
\bm{W}
, Optimizing
\bm{Z}

Now, let’s examine the problem of
\bm{Z}\leftarrow\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
. When there exists a unique minimum of the loss function
L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
with respect to
\bm{Z}
, we refer to it as the
least squares
minimizer of
\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
.
With
\bm{W}
fixed,
L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
can be represented as
L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})
(or more compactly, as
L(\bm{Z})
) to emphasize the dependence on
\bm{Z}
:

\displaystyle 2L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\left\|\bm{W}[\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}]-[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{N}]\right\|^{2}=\left\lVert\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{W}\bm{z}_{1}-\bm{a}_{1}\\
\bm{W}\bm{z}_{2}-\bm{a}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{W}\bm{z}_{N}-\bm{a}_{N}\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}.

Now, if we define

\footnotesize\widetilde{\bm{W}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{W}&\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{W}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{W}\end{bmatrix}\in\real^{MN\times KN},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\widetilde{\bm{z}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{z}_{1}\\
\bm{z}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{z}_{N}\end{bmatrix}\in\real^{KN},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\widetilde{\bm{a}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{a}_{1}\\
\bm{a}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{a}_{N}\end{bmatrix}\in\real^{MN},

then the (ALS1) problem can be reduced to the ordinary least squares problem for minimizing
\big\|{\widetilde{\bm{W}}\widetilde{\bm{z}}-\widetilde{\bm{a}}}\big\|_{2}^{2}
with respect to
\widetilde{\bm{z}}
. And the solution is given by

\widetilde{\bm{z}}=(\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{W}})^{-1}\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{a}}.

However, it is not advisable to obtain the result using this approach, as computing the inverse of
\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{W}}
requires
2(KN)^{3}
flops
(
Lu 2021c
)
.
Alternatively, a more direct way to solve the (ALS1) problem is to find the gradient of
L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})
with respect to
\bm{Z}
(assuming all partial derivatives of this function exist):

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\,\,\mathrm{tr}\left((\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})(\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})^{\top}\right)}{\partial\bm{Z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{=}}\bm{W}^{\top}(\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})\in\real^{K\times N},

(14.10)

where the first equality arises from the definition of the Frobenius norm (Definition
) such that
\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}=\sqrt{\sum_{m=1,n=1}^{M,N}(a_{mn})^{2}}=\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}(\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top})}
, and equality (
\star
) is a consequence of the fact that
\frac{\partial\mathrm{tr}(\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top})}{\partial\bm{A}}=2\bm{A}
. When the loss function is a differentiable function of
\bm{Z}
, we can determine the least squares solution using differential calculus.
Since we optimize over an open set
K×N
, any minimum of the function

L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})
must satisfy the condition:

\nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})=\mathbf{0}.

By finding the root of the equation above, we obtain the “candidate” update for
\bm{Z}
that finds the minimizer of
L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})
:

\textbf{(``Candidate" update for $\bm{Z}$)}:\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\bm{Z}=(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W}).}

(14.11)

This computation requires
2K^{3}
flops to compute the inverse of
\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}
, compared to
2(KN)^{3}
flops to get the inverse of
\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{W}}
.
Prior to confirming that a root of the equation above is indeed a minimizer (as opposed to a maximizer, hence the term “candidate” update), it is imperative to establish the convexity of the function.
For a twice continuously differentiable function, this verification can be equivalently achieved by confirming (see Problem
for more details):

\nabla^{2}_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})\succ 0.\penalty\

That is, the Hessian matrix is positive definite (Definition
; see, for example,
Beck 2014
). To demonstrate this, we explicitly express the Hessian matrix as

\nabla^{2}_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})=\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{W}}\in\real^{KN\times KN},\penalty\

(14.12)

which maintains full rank if
\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}
has full rank and
K<M
(Lemma
).

Remark 130
(Positive Definite Hessian if
\bm{W}
Has Full Rank).

We claim that if
\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}
has full rank
K
with
K<M
, then
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{2}L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})
is positive definite. This can be demonstrated by confirming that when
\bm{W}
has full rank, the equation
\bm{W}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
holds true only when
\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
, since the null space of
\bm{W}
has a dimension of 0. Therefore,

\bm{x}^{\top}(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})\bm{x}>0,\qquad\text{for any nonzero vector $\bm{x}\in\real^{K}$}.

And this in turn implies
\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{W}}\succ\mathbf{0}
.

Now, the problem becomes
whether
\bm{W}
has full rank so that the Hessian of
L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})
is positive definite
; otherwise, we cannot claim the update of
\bm{Z}
in Equation (
) reduces the loss (due to convexity) so that the matrix decomposition progressively improves the approximation of the original matrix
\bm{A}
by
\bm{W}\bm{Z}
in each iteration.
We will address the positive definiteness of the Hessian matrix shortly, relying on the following lemma.

Lemma 131
(Rank of
\bm{Z}
after Updating).

Suppose
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}
has full rank with
M\leq N
and
\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}
has full rank with
K<M
(i.e.,
K<M\leq N
). Then the update of
\bm{Z}=(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}\in\real^{K\times N}
in Equation (
) has full rank.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Since
\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\in\real^{K\times K}
has full rank if
\bm{W}
has full rank (Lemma
), it follows that
(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}
has full rank.

Suppose
\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
. This implies that
(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
. Thus, the following two null spaces satisfy:

\mathcal{N}(\bm{W}^{\top})\subseteq\mathcal{N}\left((\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\right).

Moreover, suppose
\bm{x}
lies in the null space of
(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}
such that
(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
. And since
(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}
is invertible, it implies
\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{x}=(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})\mathbf{0}=\mathbf{0}
, leading to

\mathcal{N}\left((\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{N}(\bm{W}^{\top}).

Consequently, through “sandwiching,” it follows that

\mathcal{N}(\bm{W}^{\top})=\mathcal{N}\left((\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\right).

(14.13)

Therefore,
(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}
has full rank
K
. Let
\bm{T}=(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\in\real^{K\times M}
, and suppose
\bm{T}^{\top}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
. This implies
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{T}^{\top}\bm{x}=\mathbf{0}
, yielding

\mathcal{N}(\bm{T}^{\top})\subseteq\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{T}^{\top}).

Similarly, suppose
\bm{A}^{\top}(\bm{T}^{\top}\bm{x})=\mathbf{0}
. Since
\bm{A}
has full rank with the dimension of the null space being 0:
\dim\left(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top})\right)=0
,
(\bm{T}^{\top}\bm{x})
must be zero. The claim follows since
\bm{A}
has full rank
M
with the row space of
\bm{A}^{\top}
being equal to the column space of
\bm{A}
, where
\dim\left(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})\right)=M
and
\dim\left(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top})\right)=M-\dim\left(\mathcal{C}(\bm{A})\right)=0
.
Consequently,
\bm{x}
is in the null space of
\bm{T}^{\top}
if
\bm{x}
is in the null space of
\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{T}^{\top}
:

\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{T}^{\top})\subseteq\mathcal{N}(\bm{T}^{\top}).

By “sandwiching” again, we obtain

\mathcal{N}(\bm{T}^{\top})=\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{T}^{\top}).

(14.14)

Since
\bm{T}^{\top}
has full rank
K<M\leq N
, it follows that
\dim\left(\mathcal{N}(\bm{T}^{\top})\right)=\dim\left(\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{T}^{\top})\right)=0
.
Therefore,

\bm{Z}^{\top}=\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{T}^{\top}
has full rank
K
.
We complete the proof.

Given
\bm{Z}
, Optimizing
\bm{W}

The matrix factorization problem exhibits symmetry:
\bm{A}=\bm{W}\bm{Z}
if and only if
\bm{A}^{\top}=\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\top}
such that
D(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})=D(\bm{A}^{\top},\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\top})
. The analysis of optimizing
\bm{W}
given
\bm{Z}
directly follows from the previously discussed methodology.
Below, we provide a brief outline of the results.
With
\bm{Z}
fixed,
L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
can be expressed as
L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})
(or more compactly, as
L(\bm{W})
) to emphasize the dependence on
\bm{W}
:

\begin{aligned} L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})&=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}.\end{aligned}

To solve the optimization problem (ALS2) directly, we need to compute the gradient of
L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})
with respect to
\bm{W}
:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\mathrm{tr}\left((\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})(\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})^{\top}\right)}{\partial\bm{W}}=(\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})\bm{Z}^{\top}\in\real^{M\times K}.

Similarly, the “candidate” update for
\bm{W}
can be obtained by identifying the root of the gradient
\nabla_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})
:

\textbf{(``Candidate" update for $\bm{W}$)}:\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\bm{W}^{\top}=(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top})^{-1}\bm{Z}\bm{A}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z}).}

(14.15)

Once more, we emphasize that the update is merely a “candidate” update.
Further validation is necessary to ascertain the positive definiteness of the Hessian matrix.
The Hessian matrix is obtained as follows:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{W}}^{2}L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})=\widetilde{\bm{Z}}\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\in\real^{KM\times KM},

(14.16)

where
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}=\mathrm{diag}(\bm{Z},\bm{Z},\ldots,\bm{Z})\in\real^{KM\times NM}
is defined analogously to
\widetilde{\bm{W}}
in (
).
Therefore, by similar analysis, if
\bm{Z}
has full rank with
K<N
, the Hessian matrix is positive definite.

In Lemma
, we proved that
\bm{Z}
has full rank under certain conditions, ensuring that the Hessian matrix in Equation (
) is positive definite, and the update in Equation (
) exists.
We now prove that
\bm{W}
also has full rank under certain conditions, such that the Hessian in Equation (
) is positive definite, and the update in Equation (
) exists.

Lemma 132
(Rank of
\bm{W}
after Updating).

Suppose
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}
has full rank with
M\geq N
and
\bm{Z}\in\real^{K\times N}
has full rank with
K<N
(i.e.,
K<N\leq M
). Then the update of
\bm{W}^{\top}=(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top})^{-1}\bm{Z}\bm{A}^{\top}
in Equation (
) has full rank.

The proof of Lemma
is similar to that of Lemma
, and we shall not repeat the details.

Key observation.

Combining the observations in Lemma
and Lemma
, as long as we
initialize
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{W}
to have full rank
, the updates in Equation (
) and Equation (
) are reasonable
since the Hessians in Equation (
) and (
) are positive definite
.

Note that we need an additional condition to satisfy
both Lemma

and Lemma
:
M=N
, meaning there must be an equal number of movies and users.

We will relax this condition in the next section through regularization.
(Alternatively, Problems
\sim
relax this condition using pseudo-inverse.)
We summarize the process in Algorithm
.
Since the loss
\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{W}\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
in each iteration is monotonically nonincreasing and bounded below, it converges
(
Lu 2021c
;
Gillis 2020
)
.
In particular,
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})=\mathbf{0}
and
\nabla_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})=\mathbf{0}
when
iter\rightarrow\infty
.

Algorithm 7

Alternating Least Squares

1:

Matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}

with
M=N
;

2:

Initialize
\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}
,
\bm{Z}\in\real^{K\times N}

with full rank and
K<M=N
;

3:

Choose a stop criterion on the approximation error
\delta
;

4:

Choose the maximal number of iterations
C
;

5:

iter=0
;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

6:

while

\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{W}\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}>\delta
and
iter<C

do

7:

iter=iter+1
;

8:

\bm{Z}\leftarrow(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})
;

9:

\bm{W}^{\top}\leftarrow(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top})^{-1}\bm{Z}\bm{A}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})
;

10:

end

while

11:

Output
\bm{W},\bm{Z}
;

14.3
Regularization and Identifiability: Extension to General Matrices

Regularization
is a machine learning technique employed to prevent overfitting and improve model generalization. Overfitting occurs when a model is overly complex and fits the training data too closely, resulting in poor performance on unseen data.
To mitigate this issue, regularization introduces a constraint or a penalty term into the loss function used for model optimization, discouraging the development of overly complex models.
This creates a trade-off between having a simple, generalizable model and fitting the training data well.
Common types of regularization include
\ell_{1}
regularization,
\ell_{2}
regularization (Tikhonov regularization), and elastic net regularization (a combination of
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
regularizations).
Regularization finds extensive applications in machine learning algorithms such as linear regression, logistic regression, and neural networks.

In the context of the alternating least squares problem, we can add an
\ell_{2}
regularization term to minimize the following loss:

L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\bm{W}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2},\qquad\lambda_{w}>0,\lambda_{z}>0,

(14.17)

where the gradient with respect to
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{W}
are given, respectively, by

\left\{\begin{aligned} \nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})&=\bm{W}^{\top}(\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})+\lambda_{z}\bm{Z}\in\real^{K\times N};\\
\nabla_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})&=(\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})\bm{Z}^{\top}+\lambda_{w}\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}.\end{aligned}\right.

(14.18)

The Hessian matrices are given, respectively, by

\left\{\begin{aligned} \nabla^{2}_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})&=\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{W}}+\lambda_{z}\bm{I}\in\real^{KN\times KN};\\
\nabla^{2}_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})&=\widetilde{\bm{Z}}\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\top}+\lambda_{w}\bm{I}\in\real^{KM\times KM},\\
\end{aligned}\right.

which are positive definite due to the perturbation by the regularization:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \bm{x}^{\top}(\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{W}}+\lambda_{z}\bm{I})\bm{x}&=\underbrace{\bm{x}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{W}}\bm{x}}_{\geq 0}+\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}>0,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{for nonzero $\bm{x}$};\\
\bm{x}^{\top}(\widetilde{\bm{Z}}\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\top}+\lambda_{w}\bm{I})\bm{x}&=\underbrace{\bm{x}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{Z}}\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\bm{x}}_{\geq 0}+\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}>0,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{for nonzero $\bm{x}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

The regularization ensures that the Hessian matrices remain positive definite, even if
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
are rank-deficient
.
Consequently, matrix decomposition can be extended to any matrix, regardless of whether
M>N
or
M<N
. In rare cases,
K
even can be chosen as
K>\max\{M,N\}
to obtain a
high-rank approximation
of
\bm{A}
. However, in most scenarios, we aim to find a
low-rank approximation
of
\bm{A}
with
K<\min\{M,N\}
. For example, ALS can be employed to discover low-rank neural networks, reducing memory usage of neural networks while improving performance
(
Lu 2021c
)
.
Therefore, the minimizers can be determined by identifying the roots of the gradients:

\left.\begin{aligned} \bm{Z}&=(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}+\lambda_{z}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{W}^{\top}=(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}+\lambda_{w}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{Z}\bm{A}^{\top}.\end{aligned}\right.

(14.19)

The regularization parameters
\lambda_{z},\lambda_{w}\in\real_{++}
are used to balance the trade-off
between the accuracy of the approximation and the smoothness of the computed solution. The selection of these parameters is typically problem-dependent and can be determined through
cross-validation
(CS). Again, we summarize the process in Algorithm
.
We will also introduce the
alternating direction methods of multipliers (ADMM)
for solving matrix factorization problems with
\ell_{2}
or
\ell_{1}
regularization in Section
, where the method can be extended to other regularizations and constraints, such as nonnegative constraints.

The
\ell_{2}
(or
\ell_{1}
) regularizations can be applied to generalize the ALS problem to general matrices.
However, we will consider the missing entries in the following sections, thus the problem becomes a matrix completion formulation.
In this sense, the
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
regularizations are not the only applicable regularizations; for example, the
nuclear norm
of
\bm{W}\bm{Z}
(the sum of singular values of the matrix) can be applied, for which the
Soft-Impute for matrix completion
algorithm guarantees the recovery of the matrix when the number of observed entries
z
satisfies

z\geq Crn\log n,

where the underlying matrix
\bm{A}
is of size
n×n
and
C>0
is a fixed universal constant
(
Gross 2011
;
Hastie et al. 2015
)
.
However, the
\ell_{2}
regularization on
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
can somehow be reformulated into the nuclear norm form (see Problem
).

Algorithm 8

Alternating Least Squares with Regularization

1:

Matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}
;

2:

Initialize
\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}
,
\bm{Z}\in\real^{K\times N}

randomly without condition on the rank and the relationship between
M,N,K
;

3:

Choose a stop criterion on the approximation error
\delta
;

4:

Choose regularization parameters
\lambda_{w},\lambda_{z}
;

5:

Choose the maximal number of iterations
C
;

6:

iter=0
;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

7:

while

\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{W}\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}>\delta
and
iter<C

do

8:

iter=iter+1
;

9:

\bm{Z}\leftarrow(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}+\lambda_{z}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})
;

10:

\bm{W}^{\top}\leftarrow(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}+\lambda_{w}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{Z}\bm{A}^{\top}\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})
;

11:

end

while

12:

Output
\bm{W},\bm{Z}
;

Regularization as constraints and identifiability.

Regularization terms, such as
\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\bm{W}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
in (
), can be interpreted as constraints
\left\lVert\bm{W}\right\rVert_{F}\leq C
, where
C
is a constant, via Lagrangian multipliers (see, for example,
Boyd et al. 2004
).
Different constraints can be placed on the factors
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
. For example, the nonnegative constraint discussed in Chapter
and the sparsity constraint discussed in Section
.
Moreover, the two matrices
\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}
and
\bm{Z}\in\real^{K\times N}
have
(M+N)K
degrees of freedom.
However, due to the scaling degree of freedom of the columns of
\bm{W}
and rows of
\bm{Z}
in
\bm{A}=\bm{W}\bm{Z}
, the factorization
\bm{W}\bm{Z}
has
(M+N-1)K
degrees of freedom:
\bm{W}[:,k]\bm{Z}[k,:]=(\gamma\bm{W}[:,k])(\frac{1}{\gamma}\bm{Z}[k,:])
for any scalar
\gamma\neq 0
and
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
.
Therefore, the factorization is not identifiable. Regularization helps reduce overfitting and addresses the issue of identifiability by incorporating prior information through regularizations/constraints.

14.4
Missing Entries and Rank-One Update

Since matrix decomposition via ALS is extensively used in the context of Netflix recommender data, where a substantial number of entries are missing due to users not having watched certain movies or choosing not to rate them for various reasons.
In this scenario, the low-rank matrix decomposition problem is also known as
matrix completion
that can help recover unobserved entries
(
Jain et al. 2017
)
.
To address this, we can introduce an additional mask matrix
\bm{M}\in\{0,1\}^{M\times N}
, where
m_{mn}\in\{0,1\}
indicates whether user
n
has rated movie
m
or not. Therefore, the loss function can be defined as

L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{M}\circledast\bm{A}-\bm{M}\circledast(\bm{W}\bm{Z})\right\rVert_{F}^{2},

where
\circledast
represents the
Hadamard product
between matrices.
The above formulation concisely expresses our goal of finding a completion of the ratings matrix that is both of low rank and consistent with observed user ratings.
To find the solution to this problem, we decompose the updates in Equation (
) into:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \bm{z}_{n}&=(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}+\lambda_{z}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{a}_{n},&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,&\text{for $n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}$};\\
\bm{w}_{m}&=(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}+\lambda_{w}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{Z}\bm{b}_{m},&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,&\text{for $m\in\{1,2,\ldots,M\}$},\end{aligned}\right.

(14.20)

where
\bm{Z}=[\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}]
and
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{N}]
represent the column partitions of
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{A}
, respectively. Similarly,
\bm{W}^{\top}=[\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2},\ldots,\bm{w}_{M}]
and
\bm{A}^{\top}=[\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{M}]
are the column partitions of
\bm{W}^{\top}
and
\bm{A}^{\top}
, respectively. This decomposition of the updates indicates the updates can be performed in a column-by-column fashion (the rank-one updates).

Given
\bm{W}
.

Let
\bm{o}_{n}\in\{0,1\}^{M}
represent the movies rated by user
n
, where
o_{nm}=1
if user
n
has rated movie
m
, and
o_{nm}=0
otherwise. Then the
n
-th column of
\bm{A}
without missing entries can be denoted using the Matlab-style notation as
\bm{a}_{n}[\bm{o}_{n}]
.
And we want to approximate the existing entries of the
n
-th column by
\bm{a}_{n}[\bm{o}_{n}]\approx\bm{W}[\bm{o}_{n},:]\bm{z}_{n}
, which is indeed a rank-one least squares problem:

\displaystyle\bm{z}_{n}

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{W}[\bm{o}_{n},:]^{\top}\bm{W}[\bm{o}_{n},:]+\lambda_{z}\bm{I}\right)^{-1}\bm{W}[\bm{o}_{n},:]^{\top}\bm{a}_{n}[\bm{o}_{n}],\quad\text{for $n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}$}.

(14.21)

Moreover, the loss function with respect to
\bm{z}_{n}
and
\bm{Z}
can be described, respectively, by

\displaystyle L(\bm{z}_{n}|\bm{W})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\bm{o}_{n}}\left(a_{mn}-\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}\right)^{2}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\ \sum_{m\in\bm{o}_{n}}\left(a_{mn}-\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}\right)^{2}.

Given
\bm{Z}
.

Similarly, if
\bm{p}_{m}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
denotes the users who have rated movie
m
, with
p_{mn}=1
if movie
m
has been rated by user
n
, and
p_{mn}=0
otherwise. Then the
m
-th row of
\bm{A}
without missing entries can be denoted by the Matlab-style notation as
\bm{b}_{m}[\bm{p}_{m}]
. We want to approximate the existing entries of the
m
-th row by
\bm{b}_{m}[\bm{p}_{m}]\approx\bm{Z}[:,\bm{p}_{m}]^{\top}\bm{w}_{m}
,

which is again a rank-one least squares problem:

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{m}

\displaystyle=(\bm{Z}[:,\bm{p}_{m}]\bm{Z}[:,\bm{p}_{m}]^{\top}+\lambda_{w}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{Z}[:,\bm{p}_{m}]\bm{b}_{m}[\bm{p}_{m}],\quad\text{for $m\in\{1,2,\ldots,M\}$}.

(14.22)

Similarly, the loss function with respect to
\bm{w}_{m}
and
\bm{W}
can be described, respectively, by

\displaystyle L(\bm{w}_{m}|\bm{Z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\bm{p}_{m}}\left(a_{mn}-\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}\right)^{2}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,L(\bm{W}|\bm{Z})=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{n\in\bm{p}_{m}}\left(a_{mn}-\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}\right)^{2}.

The procedure is once again presented in Algorithm
.
Other approaches, such as
singular value projection (SVP)
, also exist to address the matrix completion problem. At a high level, SVP is a type of projected gradient descent (PGD) method that updates iteratively via gradient descent, projecting the updated matrix into a low-rank form through singular value decomposition at each step. However, the alternating least squares approach generally outperforms SVP in the context of matrix completion, so we will not delve into SVP here. For more details, refer to
Jain et al. 2017
.

Algorithm 9

Alternating Least Squares with Missing Entries and Regularization

1:

Matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}
;

2:

Initialize
\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}
,
\bm{Z}\in\real^{K\times N}

randomly without condition on the rank and the relationship between
M,N,K
;

3:

Choose a stop criterion on the approximation error
\delta
;

4:

Choose regularization parameters
\lambda_{w},\lambda_{z}
;

5:

Compute the mask matrix
\bm{M}
from
\bm{A}
;

6:

Choose the maximal number of iterations
C
;

7:

iter=0
;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

8:

while

\left\lVert\bm{M}\circledast\bm{A}-\bm{M}\circledast(\bm{W}\bm{Z})\right\rVert_{F}^{2}>\delta
and
iter<C

do

9:

iter=iter+1
;

10:

for

n=1,2,\ldots,N

do

11:

\bm{z}_{n}\leftarrow\left(\bm{W}[\bm{o}_{n},:]^{\top}\bm{W}[\bm{o}_{n},:]+\lambda_{z}\bm{I}\right)^{-1}\bm{W}[\bm{o}_{n},:]^{\top}\bm{a}_{n}[\bm{o}_{n}]
;
\triangleright

n
-th column of
\bm{Z}

12:

end

for

13:

for

m=1,2,\ldots,M

do

14:

\bm{w}_{m}\leftarrow(\bm{Z}[:,\bm{p}_{m}]\bm{Z}[:,\bm{p}_{m}]^{\top}+\lambda_{w}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{Z}[:,\bm{p}_{m}]\bm{b}_{m}[\bm{p}_{m}]
;
\triangleright

m
-th column of
\bm{W}^{\top}

15:

end

for

16:

end

while

17:

Output
\bm{W}^{\top}=[\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2},\ldots,\bm{w}_{M}],\bm{Z}=[\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}]
;

14.5
Vector Inner Product and Hidden Vectors

We have observed that the ALS algorithm seeks to find lower-dimensional matrices
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
such that their product
\bm{W}\bm{Z}
can approximate
\bm{A}\approx\bm{W}\bm{Z}
in terms of the squared loss:

\mathop{\min}_{\bm{W},\bm{Z}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\left(a_{mn}-\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}\right)^{2},

that is, each entry
a_{mn}
in
\bm{A}
can be approximated as the inner product of two vectors
\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}
. The geometric definition of the vector inner product is given by

\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}=\left\lVert\bm{w}_{m}\right\rVert_{2}\cdot\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}\cos\theta,

where
\theta
represents the angle between vectors
\bm{w}_{m}
and
\bm{z}_{n}
. Thus, if the vector norms of
\bm{w}_{m}
and
\bm{z}_{n}
are determined, the smaller the angle, the larger the inner product.

In the context of Netflix data, movie ratings range from 0 to 5,
with higher ratings indicating a stronger user preference for the movie.
If
\bm{w}_{m}
and
\bm{z}_{n}
fall sufficiently “close,” the value
\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}
becomes larger.
This concept elucidates the essence of ALS, where
\bm{w}_{m}
represents the features or attributes of movie
m
, while
\bm{z}_{n}
encapsulates the features or preferences of user
n
.
In other words, ALS associates each user with a
latent vector of preference
and each movie with a
latent vector of attributes
.
Furthermore, each element in
\bm{w}_{m}
and
\bm{z}_{n}
signifies a specific feature. For example, it could be that the second feature
w_{m2}
(
w_{m2}
denotes the second element of vector
\bm{w}_{m}
) represents whether the movie is an action movie or not, and
z_{n2}
might denote whether user
n
has a preference for action movies. When this holds true, then
\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}
becomes large and provides a good approximation of
a_{mn}
.

In the decomposition
\bm{A}\approx\bm{W}\bm{Z}
, it is established that the rows of
\bm{W}
contain the hidden features of the movies, and the columns of
\bm{Z}
contain the hidden features of the users.
Nevertheless, the explicit meanings of the rows in
\bm{W}
or the columns in
\bm{Z}
remain undisclosed.
Although they might correspond to categories or genres of the movies, fostering underlying connections between users and movies, their precise nature remains uncertain.
It is precisely this ambiguity that gives rise to the terminology “latent” or “hidden.”

14.6
Gradient Descent

In Algorithm
,
, and
, we reduce the loss through the inversion of matrices (e.g., using LU decomposition).
The reality, however, is frequently far from straightforward, particularly in the big data era of today. As data volumes explode, the size of the inversion matrix will grow at a pace proportional to the cube of the number of samples, which poses a great challenge to the storage and computational resources.
On the other hand, this leads to the creation of an ongoing development of the gradient-based optimization technique.
The
gradient descent (GD)
method and its variant, the
stochastic gradient descent (SGD)
method, are among them the simplest, fastest, and most efficient methods
(
Lu 2022c
)
. Convex loss function optimization problems are frequently solved using this type of approach. We now go into more details about its principle.

In Equation (
), we derive the column-by-column update rules directly from the full matrix approach outlined in Equation (
) (with regularization taken into account).
To understand the underlying concept, consider the loss function with regularization, as given by Equation (
).
When minimizing the loss in (
) with respect to
\bm{z}_{n}
, we can break down the loss as follows:

\displaystyle L(\bm{z}_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\bm{W}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n}-\bm{a}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C_{z_{n}},

(14.23)

where
C_{z_{n}}
is a constant with respect to
\bm{z}_{n}
, and
\bm{Z}=[\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}]
and
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{N}]
represent the column partitions of
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{A}
, respectively.
The gradient and the root are given, respectively, by

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{z}_{n}}L(\bm{z}_{n})=\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n}-\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{a}_{n}+\lambda_{z}\bm{z}_{n}\,\,\implies\,\,\bm{z}_{n}=(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}+\lambda_{z}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{a}_{n},\,\,\forall\,\,n.

This solution corresponds to the first update rule in the column-wise update in Equation (
).
Similarly, when minimizing the loss with respect to
\bm{w}_{m}
, we have:

\displaystyle L(\bm{w}_{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{W}-\bm{A}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\bm{W}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{w}_{m}-\bm{b}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\bm{w}_{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C_{w_{m}},

(14.24)

where
C_{w_{m}}
is a constant with respect to
\bm{w}_{m}
, and
\bm{W}^{\top}=[\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2},\ldots,\bm{w}_{M}]
and
\bm{A}^{\top}=[\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,

\bm{b}_{M}]
represent the column partitions of
\bm{W}^{\top}
and
\bm{A}^{\top}
, respectively.
Analogously, taking the gradient with respect to
\bm{w}_{m}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{w}_{m}}L(\bm{w}_{m})=\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{w}_{m}-\bm{Z}\bm{b}_{n}+\lambda_{w}\bm{w}_{m}\,\,\implies\,\,\bm{w}_{m}=(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}+\lambda_{w}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{Z}\bm{b}_{m},\,\,\forall\,\,m.

This solution corresponds to the second update rule in the column-wise update in Equation (
):

Now suppose we express the iteration number (
t=1,2,\ldots
) as the superscript, and we want to find the updates
\{\bm{z}^{(t+1)}_{n},\bm{w}^{(t+1)}_{m}\}
in the
(t+1)
-th iteration base on
\{\bm{Z}^{(t)},\bm{W}^{(t)}\}
in the
t
-th iteration:

\left.\begin{aligned} \bm{z}^{(t+1)}_{n}&\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{z}_{n}^{(t)}}L(\bm{z}_{n}^{(t)})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{w}_{m}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{w}_{m}^{(t)}}L(\bm{w}_{m}^{(t)}).\end{aligned}\right.

For simplicity, we will only derive for
\bm{z}^{(t+1)}_{n}\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{z}_{n}^{(t)}}L(\bm{z}_{n}^{(t)})
, and the derivation for the update on
\bm{w}_{m}^{(t+1)}
will follow a similar process.

Approximation by linear update.

Suppose we want to approximate
\bm{z}^{(t+1)}_{n}
using a
linear update
on
\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n}
:

\textbf{(Linear Update)}:\qquad{\bm{z}^{(t+1)}_{n}=\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n}+\eta\bm{v}.}

The problem now reduces to finding the solution of
\bm{v}
such that

\bm{v}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{v}}L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n}+\eta\bm{v}).

By Taylor’s formula,
L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n}+\eta\bm{v})
can be approximated by

L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n}+\eta\bm{v})\approx L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n})+\eta\bm{v}^{\top}\nabla L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n}),

where
\eta
is a small value, and
\nabla L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n})
represents the gradient of
L(\bm{z})
at
\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n}
.
To find
\bm{v}
under the constraint
\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{2}=1
for a positive
\eta
, we perform the following minimization:

\bm{v}=\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{2}=1}L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n}+\eta\bm{v})\approx\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{2}=1}\left\{L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n})+\eta\bm{v}^{\top}\nabla L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n})\right\}.

This approach is known as the
greedy search
. The optimal
\bm{v}
can be obtained by

\bm{v}=-\nabla L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n})\big/{\big\|{\nabla L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n})}\big\|_{2}},

which means
\bm{v}
points in the opposite direction of
\nabla L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n})
. Therefore, it is reasonable to update
\bm{z}_{n}^{(t+1)}
as follows:

\bm{z}^{(t+1)}_{n}=\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n}+\eta\bm{v}=\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n}-\eta{\nabla L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n})}\big/{\big\|{\nabla L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n})}\big\|_{2}},

which is commonly referred to as the
gradient descent
(GD). Similarly, the gradient descent for
\bm{w}_{m}^{(t+1)}
is given by

\bm{w}^{(t+1)}_{m}=\bm{w}^{(t)}_{m}+\eta\bm{v}=\bm{w}^{(t)}_{m}-\eta{\nabla L(\bm{w}^{(t)}_{m})}\big/{\big\|{\nabla L(\bm{w}^{(t)}_{m})}\big\|_{2}}.

The revised procedure for Algorithm
employing a gradient descent approach is presented in Algorithm
.

It’s noteworthy that the ALS without GD (Algorithm
) lacks explicit parameters like step size.
This characteristic can be both advantageous and disadvantageous.
On one hand, it absolves the user from the time-consuming task of fine-tuning parameters, making the method more accessible and less demanding.
On the other hand, this absence of adjustable parameters also restricts the user’s control to directly influence the progression of the algorithm, leaving the convergence of ALS entirely contingent upon the inherent structure of the optimization problem at hand.

In practical applications, it is customary to alternate between the pure ALS iterations outlined in Algorithm
and the modified, gradient-descent variants mentioned in this section. These descent adaptations offer the user a degree of control through a tunable step length parameter, allowing for a more customized approach to the optimization process.

Algorithm 10

Alternating Least Squares with Full Entries and Gradient Descent

1:

Matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}
;

2:

Initialize
\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}
,
\bm{Z}\in\real^{K\times N}

randomly without condition on the rank and the relationship between
M,N,K
;

3:

Choose a stop criterion on the approximation error
\delta
;

4:

\lambda_{w},\lambda_{z}
, and step sizes
\eta_{w},\eta_{z}
;

5:

;

6:

;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

7:

\left\lVert\bm{A}-(\bm{W}\bm{Z})\right\rVert_{F}^{2}>\delta
and
iter<C

do

8:

iter=iter+1
;

9:

for

n=1,2,\ldots,N

10:

\bm{z}^{(t+1)}_{n}\leftarrow\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n}-\eta_{z}{\nabla L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n})}\big/{\big\|{\nabla L(\bm{z}^{(t)}_{n})}\big\|_{2}}
;
\triangleright

n
-th column of
\bm{Z}

11:

end

for

12:

for

m=1,2,\ldots,M

do

13:

\bm{w}^{(t+1)}_{m}\leftarrow\bm{w}^{(t)}_{m}-\eta_{w}{\nabla L(\bm{w}^{(t)}_{m})}\big/{\big\|{\nabla L(\bm{w}^{(t)}_{m})}\big\|_{2}}
;
\triangleright

m
-th column of
\bm{W}^{\top}

14:

end

for

15:

end

while

16:

Output
\bm{W}^{\top}=[\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2},\ldots,\bm{w}_{M}],\bm{Z}=[\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}]
;

Geometrical interpretation of gradient descent.

Lemma 133
(Direction of Gradients).

Gradients of variables given a loss function are perpendicular to the level curves (a.k.a., level surfaces).

Proof
[of Lemma
, the informal proof]
This proof involves showing that the gradient is orthogonal to the tangent of the level curve. For simplicity, let’s start with the two-dimensional case.
Suppose the level curve takes the form
f(x,y)=c
.
This implicitly establishes a relationship between
x
and
y
such that
y=y(x)
, where
y
can be regarded as a function of
x
. Therefore, the level curve can be expressed as

f(x,y(x))=c.

Applying the chain rule, we get:

\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\underbrace{\frac{dx}{dx}}_{=1}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}\frac{dy}{dx}=0\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\implies\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\left\langle\frac{\partial f}{\partial x},\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}\right\rangle\cdot\left\langle\frac{dx}{dx},\frac{dy}{dx}\right\rangle=0.

That is, the gradient is perpendicular to the tangent.

In full generality, consider the level curve of a vector
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
:
f(\bm{x})=f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})=c
. Each variable
x_{i}
can be regarded as a function of a parameter
t
on the level curve
f(\bm{x})=c
:
f(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t),\ldots,x_{n}(t))=c
. Differentiating the equation with respect to
t
using the chain rule:

\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{1}}\frac{dx_{1}}{dt}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{2}}\frac{dx_{2}}{dt}+\ldots+\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{n}}\frac{dx_{n}}{dt}=0.

Thus, the gradient is perpendicular to the tangent in the
n
-dimensional case:

\left\langle\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{1}},\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{2}},\ldots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{n}}\right\rangle\cdot\left\langle\frac{dx_{1}}{dt},\frac{dx_{2}}{dt},\ldots\frac{dx_{n}}{dt}\right\rangle=0.

This completes the proof.

The lemma reveals the geometrical interpretation of gradient descent. When seeking a solution to minimize a convex function
L(\bm{z})
, gradient descent proceeds in the direction opposite to the gradient, which leads to a reduction in the loss. Figure
depicts a two-dimensional case, where
-\nabla L(\bm{z})
pushes the loss to decrease for the convex function
L(\bm{z})
.

(a)

A two-dimensional convex function
L(\bm{z})
.

(b)

L(\bm{z})=c
is a constant.

Figure 14.2:
Figure
shows surface and contour plots for a specific function (
blue
=low,
yellow
=high), where the upper graph is the surface plot, and the lower one is its projection (i.e., contour). Figure
:
-\nabla L(\bm{z})
pushes the loss to decrease for the convex function
L(\bm{z})
.

14.7
Regularization: A Geometrical Interpretation

Figure 14.3:
Constrained gradient descent with
\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}\leq C
. The
green
vector
\bm{w}
represents the projection of
\bm{v}_{1}
onto the set
\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}\leq C
, where
\bm{v}_{1}
is the component of
-\nabla l(\bm{z})
that is perpendicular to
\bm{z}_{1}
.
The image on the right illustrates the next step after the update in the left picture.
\bm{z}^{\star}
denotes the optimal solution of {
\min l(\bm{z})
}.

In Section
, we discussed how regularization can extend the ALS algorithm to general matrices.
Gradient descent can provide a geometric interpretation of regularization.
To avoid confusion, we denote the loss function without regularization as
l(\bm{z})
and the loss function with regularization as
L(\bm{z})=\l(\bm{z})+\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, where
l(\bm{z}):\real^{n}\rightarrow\real
. When minimizing
l(\bm{z})
, a descent method searches for a solution in
n
. However, in machine learning, searching across the entire space
n
can lead to overfitting.
A partial solution is to search within a subset of the vector space, e.g., searching in
\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}<C
for some constant
C
.
This can be formulated as:

\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{z}}\,\,l(\bm{z}),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{s.t.,}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}\leq C.

As demonstrated above, a basic gradient descent method will go further in the direction of
-\nabla l(\bm{z})
, i.e., update
\bm{z}
as
\bm{z}\leftarrow\bm{z}-\eta\nabla l(\bm{z})
for a small step size
\eta
. When the level curve is
l(\bm{z})=c_{1}
and the current position of parameter
\bm{z}
is
\bm{z}=\bm{z}_{1}
, where
\bm{z}_{1}
is the intersection of
\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}=C
and
l(\bm{z})=c_{1}
, the descent direction
-\nabla l(\bm{z}_{1})
will be perpendicular to the level curve of
l(\bm{z}_{1})=c_{1}
, as shown in the left picture of Figure
(by Lemma
). However, if we further restrict that the optimal value must lie within
\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}\leq C
, the trivial descent direction
-\nabla l(\bm{z}_{1})
will lead the update
\bm{z}_{2}=\bm{z}_{1}-\eta\nabla l(\bm{z}_{1})
beyond the boundary of
\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}\leq C
.
One solution is to decompose the step
-\nabla l(\bm{z}_{1})
into

-\nabla l(\bm{z}_{1})=a\bm{z}_{1}+\bm{v}_{1},

where
a\bm{z}_{1}
represents the component perpendicular to the curve of
\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}=C
, and
\bm{v}_{1}
is the component parallel to the curve of
\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}=C
. By keeping only the step
\bm{v}_{1}
, the update becomes

\bm{z}_{2}=\text{project}(\bm{z}_{1}+\eta\bm{v}_{1})=\text{project}\bigg(\bm{z}_{1}+\eta\underbrace{(-\nabla l(\bm{z}_{1})-a\bm{z}_{1})}_{\bm{v}_{1}}\bigg),\penalty\

which will lead to a smaller loss from
l(\bm{z}_{1})
to
l(\bm{z}_{2})
while maintaining the constraint
\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}\leq C
. This approach is known as
projection gradient descent (PGD)
.
It is not hard to see that the update
\bm{z}_{2}=\text{project}(\bm{z}_{1}+\eta\bm{v}_{1})
can be understood as finding a vector
\bm{w}
(represented by the
green
vector in the left picture of Figure
) such that
\bm{z}_{2}=\bm{z}_{1}+\bm{w}
lies inside the curve of
\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}\leq C
. Mathematically, the vector
\bm{w}
can be determined as
-\nabla l(\bm{z}_{1})-2\lambda\bm{z}_{1}
for some
\lambda
, as shown in the middle picture of Figure
. This is precisely the negative gradient of
L(\bm{z})=l(\bm{z})+\lambda\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
such that

\displaystyle\bm{w}=-\nabla L(\bm{z})

\displaystyle=-\nabla l(\bm{z})-2\lambda\bm{z}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\implies\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{z}_{2}=\bm{z}_{1}+\bm{w}=\bm{z}_{1}-\nabla L(\bm{z}).

And in practice, a small step size
\eta
prevents the trajectory from moving outside the curve of
\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{z}\leq C
:

\bm{z}_{2}=\bm{z}_{1}-\eta\nabla L(\bm{z}),

which aligns with the regularization term discussed in Section
.

Figure 14.4:
Constrained gradient descent with
\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{1}\leq C
, where the
red
dot denotes the breakpoint in the
\ell_{1}
norm. The right picture illustrates the next step after the update in the left picture.
\bm{z}^{\star}
denotes the optimal solution of {
\min l(\bm{z})
}.

Sparsity.

In certain scenarios, we seek to identify a sparse solution
\bm{z}
such that
l(\bm{z})
is minimized.
For example, in facial feature extraction, sparsity leads to more localized features, meaning that fewer features are used to reconstruct each input image.
Regularization to be constrained in
\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{1}\leq C
exists to this purpose, where
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1}
is the
\ell_{1}
norm of a vector or a matrix.
Similar to the previous case, the
\ell_{1}
constrained optimization pushes the gradient descent towards the border of the level set
\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{1}=C
. The situation in the two-dimensional case is shown in Figure
.
In high-dimensional cases, many elements in
\bm{z}
will be pushed towards the breakpoint of
\left\lVert\bm{z}\right\rVert_{1}=C
so that the solution favors sparsity, as shown in the right picture of Figure
.

14.8
Stochastic Gradient Descent

The gradient descent method is a valuable optimization algorithm; however, it exhibits certain limitations in practical applications.
To comprehend the issues associated with the gradient descent method, we consider the mean squared error (MSE) derived from Equation (
):

\frac{1}{MN}\mathop{\min}_{\bm{W},\bm{Z}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\left(a_{mn}-\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}\right)^{2}.

(14.25)

The MSE requires calculating the residual
e_{mn}=(a_{mn}-\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n})^{2}
for each observed entry
a_{mn}
, representing the squared difference between predicted and actual values. The total sum of residual squares is denoted by
e=\sum_{m,n=1}^{MN}e_{mn}
.
In cases with a substantial number of training entries (i.e., large
MN
), the entire computation process becomes notably slow.
Additionally, the gradients from different input samples may cancel out, resulting in small changes in the final update.
To address these challenges, researchers have enhanced the gradient descent method with the
stochastic gradient descent (SGD)
method (see, for example,
Lu 2022c
).
In the SGD algorithm, instead of calculating the full gradient of the objective function with respect to the parameters across all samples in the data set, which can be computationally expensive, the algorithm takes a more efficient approach. It randomly chooses one sample and calculates the gradient of the objective function with respect to the parameters using only this single sample.
This gradient estimate is then used to update the parameters in the direction that minimizes the objective function.
By using a single sample at each iteration, the SGD algorithm provides a fast and often sufficient approximation of the full gradient, making it particularly useful for large data sets.

In particular, we consider again the per-example loss:

L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\left(a_{mn}-\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{w}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\left\lVert\bm{w}_{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{z}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

As we iteratively reduce the loss term
l(\bm{w}_{m},\bm{z}_{n})=\frac{1}{2}\left(a_{mn}-\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\bm{w}_{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
for all
m\in\{1,2,\ldots,M\},n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
(referred to as the per-example loss term), the overall loss
L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
decreases accordingly.
This process is also known as
stochastic coordinate descent
. The gradients with respect to
\bm{w}_{m}
and
\bm{z}_{n}
, and their roots are given, respectively, by

\left\{\begin{aligned} \nabla_{\bm{z}_{n}}l(\bm{z}_{n})&=\bm{w}_{m}\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}\bm{z}_{n}+\lambda_{z}\bm{z}_{n}-a_{mn}\bm{w}_{m}&\implies&\,\,\bm{z}_{n}=a_{mn}(\bm{w}_{m}\bm{w}_{m}^{\top}+\lambda_{z}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{w}_{m};\\
\nabla_{\bm{w}_{m}}l(\bm{w}_{m})&=\bm{z}_{n}\bm{z}_{n}^{\top}\bm{w}_{m}+\lambda_{w}\bm{w}_{m}-a_{mn}\bm{z}_{n}&\implies&\,\,\bm{w}_{m}=a_{mn}(\bm{z}_{n}\bm{z}_{n}^{\top}+\lambda_{w}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{z}_{n}.\end{aligned}\right.

Alternatively, the update can be performed using gradient descent. Since we update based on the per-example loss, this approach is also known as the
stochastic gradient descent (SGD)
:

\left.\begin{aligned} \bm{z}_{n}&\leftarrow\bm{z}_{n}-\eta_{z}\frac{\nabla_{\bm{z}_{n}}l(\bm{z}_{n})}{\left\lVert\nabla_{\bm{z}_{n}}l(\bm{z}_{n})\right\rVert_{2}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{w}_{m}\leftarrow\bm{w}_{m}-\eta_{w}\frac{\nabla_{\bm{w}_{m}}l(\bm{w}_{m})}{\left\lVert\nabla_{\bm{w}_{m}}l(\bm{w}_{m})\right\rVert_{2}}.\end{aligned}\right.

The stochastic gradient descent update for ALS is formulated in Algorithm
.
It is possible that the gradient descent or stochastic gradient descent algorithm may fail to converge. In such cases, it is appropriate to re-run the algorithm using a smaller learning rate.
And in practice, the values of
m
and
n
in the algorithm can be randomly generated, which is why the method is termed “stochastic.”

Algorithm 11

Alternating Least Squares with Full Entries and SGD

1:

Matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}
;

2:

Initialize
\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}
,
\bm{Z}\in\real^{K\times N}

randomly without condition on the rank and the relationship between
M,N,K
;

3:

Choose a stop criterion on the approximation error
\delta
;

4:

Choose regularization parameters
\lambda_{w},\lambda_{z}
, and step size
\eta_{w},\eta_{z}
;

5:

Choose the maximal number of iterations
C
;

6:

iter=0
;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

7:

while

\left\lVert\bm{A}-(\bm{W}\bm{Z})\right\rVert_{F}^{2}>\delta
and
iter<C

do

8:

iter=iter+1
;

9:

for

n=1,2,\ldots,N

do

10:

for

m=1,2,\ldots,M

do

\triangleright
in practice,
m,n
can be randomly produced

11:

\bm{z}_{n}\leftarrow\bm{z}_{n}-\eta_{z}{\nabla l(\bm{z}_{n})}/{\left\lVert\nabla l(\bm{z}_{n})\right\rVert_{2}}
;
\triangleright

n
-th column of
\bm{Z}

12:

\bm{w}_{m}\leftarrow\bm{w}_{m}-\eta_{w}{\nabla l(\bm{w}_{m})}/{\left\lVert\nabla l(\bm{w}_{m})\right\rVert_{2}}

\triangleright

m
-th column of
\bm{W}^{\top}

end

end

for
