16:

Output
\bm{W}^{\top}=[\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2},\ldots,\bm{w}_{M}],\bm{Z}=[\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}]
;

14.9
Bias Term

Figure 14.5:
Bias terms in alternating least squares, where the
yellow
entries denote ones (which are fixed), and the
cyan
entries denote the added features to fit the bias terms. The dotted boxes provide an example of how the bias terms work.

In the context of ordinary least squares, a bias term is usually incorporated into the raw matrix to improve model performance, as illustrated in Equation (
).
A similar approach can be applied to the ALS problem.
Specifically, a fixed column filled with all ones can be appended to the
last column
of matrix
\bm{W}
, thus an extra row should be added to the last row of matrix
\bm{Z}
to fit the features introduced by the bias term in
\bm{W}
.
Analogously, a fixed row with all ones can be added to the
first row
of
\bm{Z}
, and an extra column in the first column of
\bm{W}
can be added to fit the features. The setup is depicted in Figure
.

Given the loss function with respect to the columns of
\bm{Z}
in Equation (
), let

\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\bm{z}_{n}\end{bmatrix}\in\real^{K+2}
be the
n
-th column of
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}
, we have

\displaystyle 2L(\bm{z}_{n})

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{W}}\widetilde{\bm{Z}}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{W}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{Z}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\left\|\widetilde{\bm{W}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\bm{z}_{n}\end{bmatrix}-\bm{a}_{n}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\underbrace{\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}_{=\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{z}}+C_{z_{n}}

(14.26)

\displaystyle=\left\|\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\bm{w}}_{0}&\overline{\bm{W}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\bm{z}_{n}\end{bmatrix}-\bm{a}_{n}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C_{z_{n}}=\bigg\|\overline{\bm{W}}\bm{z}_{n}-\underbrace{(\bm{a}_{n}-\overline{\bm{w}}_{0})}_{\overline{\bm{a}}_{n}}\bigg\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C_{z_{n}},

where
\overline{\bm{w}}_{0}
represents the first column of
\widetilde{\bm{W}}
,
\overline{\bm{W}}
denotes the remaining
K+1
columns of
\widetilde{\bm{W}}
(i.e.,
\widetilde{\bm{W}}=[\overline{\bm{w}}_{0},\overline{\bm{W}}]
), and
C_{z_{n}}
is a constant with respect to
\bm{z}_{n}
. Let
\overline{\bm{a}}_{n}=\bm{a}_{n}-\overline{\bm{w}}_{0}
, the update for
\bm{z}_{n}
is just similar to the one in Equation (
), with the gradient given by

\nabla_{\bm{z}_{n}}L(\bm{z}_{n})=\overline{\bm{W}}^{\top}\overline{\bm{W}}\bm{z}_{n}-\overline{\bm{W}}^{\top}\overline{\bm{a}}_{n}+\lambda_{z}\bm{z}_{n}.

Therefore, the update for
\bm{z}_{n}
is given by determining the root of the gradient above:

\textbf{(update for $\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n}$)}:\quad\bm{z}_{n}=(\overline{\bm{W}}^{\top}\overline{\bm{W}}+\lambda_{z}\bm{I})^{-1}\overline{\bm{W}}^{\top}\overline{\bm{a}}_{n}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\implies\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\bm{z}_{n}\end{bmatrix},\,\forall\,n.

Similarly, following the loss with respect to each row of
\bm{W}
in Equation (
), let
\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{m}=\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{w}_{m}\\
1\end{bmatrix}\in\real^{K+2}
be the
m
-th row of
\widetilde{\bm{W}}
(or
m
-th column of
\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}
), we have

\displaystyle 2L(\bm{w}_{m})=\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}-\bm{A}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{W}}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{Z}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{m}-\bm{b}_{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\underbrace{\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}_{=\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\bm{w}_{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{w}}+C_{w_{m}}

(14.27)

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\bm{Z}}^{\top}&\overline{\bm{z}}_{0}\end{bmatrix}\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}\bm{w}_{m}\\
1\end{bmatrix}\footnotesize-\bm{b}_{m}\bigg\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\bm{w}_{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C_{w_{m}}=\left\|\overline{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\bm{w}_{m}-(\bm{b}_{m}-\overline{\bm{z}}_{0})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\bm{w}_{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+C_{w_{m}},

where
\overline{\bm{z}}_{0}
represents the last column of
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\top}
,
\overline{\bm{Z}}^{\top}
contains the remaining
K+1
columns of
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\top}
(i.e.,
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\top}=[\overline{\bm{Z}}^{\top},\overline{\bm{z}}_{0}]
),

C_{w_{m}}
is a constant with respect to
\bm{w}_{m}
.

\bm{W}^{\top}=[\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2},\ldots,\bm{w}_{M}]
and
\bm{A}^{\top}=[\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{M}]
are the column partitions of
\bm{W}^{\top}
and
\bm{A}^{\top}
, respectively. Let
\overline{\bm{b}}_{m}=\bm{b}_{m}-\overline{\bm{z}}_{0}
, the update for
\bm{w}_{m}
is again just similar to the one in Equation (
), with the gradient given by

\nabla_{\bm{w}_{m}}L(\bm{w}_{m})=\overline{\bm{Z}}\cdot\overline{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\bm{w}_{m}-\overline{\bm{Z}}\cdot\overline{\bm{b}}_{m}+\lambda_{w}\bm{w}_{m}.

Therefore, the update for
\bm{w}_{m}
is given by the root of the gradient above:

\textbf{(update for $\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{m}$)}:\quad\bm{w}_{m}=(\overline{\bm{Z}}\cdot\overline{\bm{Z}}^{\top}+\lambda_{w}\bm{I})^{-1}\overline{\bm{Z}}\cdot\overline{\bm{b}}_{m}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\implies\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\widetilde{\bm{w}}_{m}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{w}_{m}\\
1\end{bmatrix},\forall\,m.

Similar updates can be derived using gradient descent, taking into account the bias terms and handling missing entries (see Section
and
for a reference).

14.10
Low-Rank Hadamard Decomposition

In the field of linear algebra and data analysis, matrix decomposition techniques are essential for extracting meaningful information from complex datasets. One common objective is to approximate a given matrix using a lower-rank representation, which simplifies the data while preserving its key characteristics. The Hadamard product, also known as the element-wise product, provides an alternative approach to matrix decomposition compared to traditional matrix multiplication.

As discussed previously, the alternating least squares (ALS) algorithm is an iterative method used to find the best low-rank approximation of a matrix by decomposing it into two or more matrices. ALS is particularly advantageous for large-scale problems, such as those found in recommender systems, where the goal is to predict missing entries in a user-item interaction matrix. During each iteration, the ALS algorithm alternates between updating one matrix while keeping the other fixed, thereby minimizing the reconstruction error at every step.
Nonnegative matrix factorization (NMF), introduced in Chapter
, is a variant of matrix factorization where both the original matrix and the resulting factorized matrices have nonnegative entries. This constraint makes NMF especially suitable for applications where the data represents quantities that cannot be negative, such as images, audio signals, or document-term matrices in text mining.

Ws further explore the Hadamard decomposition of a matrix
\bm{A}
, where
\bm{A}
can be expressed as the Hadamard product of two low-rank matrices:
\bm{A}=\bm{A}_{1}\circledast\bm{A}_{2}
. This type of decomposition is advantageous when the data exhibits multiplicative relationships, and a low-rank approximation is desired to reduce complexity or enhance interpretability.

Non-Factorizability Issue.

Although when
\bm{A}_{1}\in\real^{n^{2}\times n^{2}}
and
\bm{A}_{2}\in\real^{n^{2}\times n^{2}}
share the same rank
n
, the Hadamard product
\bm{A}_{1}\circledast\bm{A}_{2}
can achieve a maximum rank of
n^{2}
(Problem
), not all matrices
\bm{A}\in\real^{n^{2}\times n^{2}}
of rank
n^{2}
can be represented as the Hadamard product of two lower-rank matrices:

•

The Hadamard decomposition
\bm{A}=\bm{A}_{1}\circledast\bm{A}_{2}
, where
\bm{A}_{1}
and
\bm{A}_{2}
are rank-
n
factors, encodes a system of nonlinear equations.

•

This system comprises
n^{2}\times n^{2}=n^{4}
equations (one per entry of
\bm{A}
) and, due to the low-rank constraint on the two Hadamard factors
\bm{A}_{1}
and
\bm{A}_{2}
, only
(n^{2}n+nn^{2})=2n^{3}
variables exist.

•

For
n>2
, there are more equations than variables, suggesting that all the equations will be simultaneously satisfied only in special cases.
For example, if the matrix
\bm{A}
includes a row or a column with all but a single entry being zero, then not all the equations in the system can be satisfied
(
Ciaperoni et al. 2024
)
.

Therefore, we focus on solving the low-rank reconstruction problem for the Hadamard decomposition.
Assuming that
\bm{A}_{1}
and
\bm{A}_{2}
share the same rank
K
, our aim is to reconstruct the design matrix
\bm{A}
through the Hadamard product
\bm{A}_{1}\circledast\bm{A}_{2}
.
Building upon the matrix factorization method used in alternating least squares (Section
),
we now concentrate on algorithms for solving the
low-rank Hadamard decomposition
problem (we may use the term “Hadamard decomposition” to refer to “low-rank Hadamard decomposition” for brevity, when it is clear from the context):

•

Given a real matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}
, find matrix factors
\bm{A}_{1}\in\real^{M\times N}
and
\bm{A}_{2}\in\real^{M\times N}
such that:

\min\,\,L(\bm{C}_{1},\bm{D}_{1},\bm{C}_{2},\bm{D}_{2})=\left\lVert\bm{A}_{1}\circledast\bm{A}_{2}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\left\lVert(\bm{C}_{1}\bm{D}_{1})\circledast(\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2})-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2},

(14.28)

where
\bm{C}_{1},\bm{C}_{2}\in\real^{M\times K}
, and
\bm{D}_{1},\bm{D}_{2}\in\real^{K\times N}
:
\bm{A}_{1}=\bm{C}_{1}\bm{D}_{1}
and
\bm{A}_{2}=\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2}
such that
\bm{A}_{1}
and
\bm{A}_{2}
are rank-
K
matrices.

Low-rank (Hadamard) decomposition is often necessary because many natural phenomena exhibit multiplicative or conjunctive relationships
(
Ciaperoni et al. 2024
)
.
For instance, consider a study on risk factors for a disease with two predictors: smoking status (yes/no) and alcohol consumption (yes/no). The multiplicative model would account for not only the main effects of smoking and alcohol consumption but also their interaction.
The (low-rank) Hadamard decomposition offers an alternative approach.

Following the alternating descent framework (Section
), in each iteration, the matrices
\bm{C}_{1},\bm{D}_{1},\bm{C}_{2}
, and
\bm{D}_{2}
are updated sequentially by taking a step in the direction opposite to the gradient of the objective function (a.k.a., the reconstruction error in this context).
It then can be shown that

\nabla L(\bm{C}_{1})=\nabla L(\bm{C}_{1}|\bm{D}_{1},\bm{C}_{2},\bm{D}_{2})=2\big(\left((\bm{C}_{1}\bm{D}_{1})\circledast(\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2})-\bm{A}\right)\circledast(\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2})\big)\bm{D}_{1}^{\top}.

Proof

For simplicity, we derive the gradient of
\bm{E}
for
f(\bm{E})=\left\lVert\bm{E}\bm{F}\circledast\bm{C}-\bm{D}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
.
We have

\displaystyle f(\bm{E})

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\bm{E}\bm{F}\circledast\bm{C}-\bm{D}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\mathrm{tr}\left((\bm{E}\bm{F}\circledast\bm{C}-\bm{D})^{\top}(\bm{E}\bm{F}\circledast\bm{C}-\bm{D})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\left((\bm{E}\bm{F}\circledast\bm{C})^{\top}(\bm{E}\bm{F}\circledast\bm{C})\right)-2\mathrm{tr}\left((\bm{E}\bm{F}\circledast\bm{C})^{\top}\bm{D}\right)+\mathrm{tr}(\bm{D}^{\top}\bm{D}).

Considering the first term, we get

\frac{\partial\mathrm{tr}\left((\bm{E}\bm{F}\circledast\bm{C})^{\top}(\bm{E}\bm{F}\circledast\bm{C})\right)}{\partial\bm{E}}=2(\bm{E}\bm{F})\circledast\bm{C}\circledast\bm{C}\cdot\bm{F}^{\top}.\penalty\

For the second term, it follows that

-2\frac{\partial\mathrm{tr}\left((\bm{E}\bm{F}\circledast\bm{C})^{\top}\bm{D}\right)}{\partial\bm{E}}=-2\bm{D}\circledast\bm{C}\cdot\frac{\partial\bm{E}\bm{F}}{\partial\bm{E}}=-2\bm{D}\circledast\bm{C}\cdot\bm{F}^{\top}.\penalty\

The third term is a constant w.r.t. to
\bm{E}
.
Therefore,
\frac{\partial f(\bm{E})}{\partial\bm{E}}=2(\bm{E}\bm{F})\circledast\bm{C}\circledast\bm{C}\cdot\bm{F}^{\top}-2\bm{D}\circledast\bm{C}\cdot\bm{F}^{\top}=2\big((\bm{E}\bm{F})\circledast\bm{C}-\bm{D}\big)\circledast\bm{C}\cdot\bm{F}^{\top}.

Substituting
\bm{E}=\bm{C}_{1}
,
\bm{F}=\bm{D}_{1}
,
\bm{C}=\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2}
, and
\bm{D}=\bm{A}
completes the proof.

The gradients with respect to
\bm{D}_{1},\bm{C}_{2}
, and
\bm{D}_{2}
can be derived analogously.
Thus, the alternating descent method for obtaining the low-rank approximation of Hadamard decomposition can be described by Algorithm
.

Algorithm 12

Alternating Descent with Gradient Descent for Low-Rank Hadamard Decomposition: A regularization can also be added into the gradient descent update (see Section
).

1:

Matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}
;

2:

Initialize
\bm{C}_{1},\bm{C}_{2}\in\real^{M\times K}
, and
\bm{D}_{1},\bm{D}_{2}\in\real^{K\times N}
;

3:

Choose a stoping criterion on the approximation error
\delta
;

4:

Choose step size
\eta
;

5:

Choose the maximum number of iterations
C
;

6:

iter=0
;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

7:

while

\left\lVert(\bm{C}_{1}\bm{D}_{1})\circledast(\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2})-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}>\delta
and
iter<C

do

8:

iter=iter+1
;

9:

\Delta\leftarrow\left((\bm{C}_{1}\bm{D}_{1})\circledast(\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2})-\bm{A}\right)
;

10:

\bm{C}_{1}\leftarrow\bm{C}_{1}-\eta\nabla L(\bm{C}_{1})=\bm{C}_{1}-\eta\cdot 2\left(\Delta\circledast(\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2})\right)\bm{D}_{1}^{\top}
;

11:

\bm{D}_{1}\leftarrow\bm{D}_{1}-\eta\nabla L(\bm{D}_{1})=\bm{D}_{1}-\eta\cdot 2\left\{\left(\Delta^{\top}\circledast(\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2})^{\top}\right)\bm{C}_{1}\right\}^{\top}
;

12:

\bm{C}_{2}\leftarrow\bm{C}_{2}-\eta\nabla L(\bm{C}_{2})=\bm{C}_{2}-\eta\cdot 2\left(\Delta\circledast(\bm{C}_{1}\bm{D}_{1})\right)\bm{D}_{2}^{\top}
;

13:

\bm{D}_{2}\leftarrow\bm{D}_{2}-\eta\nabla L(\bm{D}_{2})=\bm{D}_{2}-\eta\cdot 2\left\{\left(\Delta^{\top}\circledast(\bm{C}_{1}\bm{D}_{1})^{\top}\right)\bm{C}_{2}\right\}^{\top}
;

14:

end

while

15:

Output
\bm{C}_{1},\bm{D}_{1},\bm{C}_{2},\bm{D}_{2}
;

14.10.1
Rank-One Update

Following the rank-one update approach in ALS (Section
), we consider updating the
n
-th column
\bm{d}_{1,n}
of
\bm{D}_{1}
,
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
.
Analogously, we can obtain the gradient of
\bm{d}_{1,n}
:

\displaystyle\nabla L(\bm{d}_{1,n})=\frac{\partial L(\bm{d}_{1,n})}{\partial\bm{d}_{1,n}}

\displaystyle=2\bm{C}_{1}^{\top}\left((\bm{C}_{1}\bm{d}_{1,n})\circledast\bm{a}_{2,n}\circledast\bm{a}_{2,n}\right)-2\bm{C}_{1}^{\top}(\bm{a}_{n}\circledast\bm{a}_{2,n})

(14.29)

\displaystyle=2\bm{C}_{1}^{\top}\left(\left[(\bm{C}_{1}\bm{d}_{1,n})\circledast\bm{a}_{2,n}-\bm{a}_{n}\right]\circledast\bm{a}_{2,n}\right),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\},

where
\bm{a}_{2,n}
is the
n
-th column of
\bm{A}_{2}=\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2}
,
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
. The gradient of the columns of
\bm{D}_{2}
can be calculated similarly.

Suppose further that
\bm{C}_{1}^{\top}=[\bm{c}_{1,1},\bm{c}_{1,2},\ldots,\bm{c}_{1,M}]\in\real^{K\times M}
,
\bm{B}=\bm{A}^{\top}=[\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{M}]\in\real^{N\times M}
, and
\bm{B}_{2}=\bm{A}_{2}^{\top}=(\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2})^{\top}=[\bm{b}_{2,1},\bm{b}_{2,2},\ldots,\bm{b}_{2,M}]\in\real^{N\times M}
, i.e., the row partitions of
\bm{C}_{1}
,
\bm{A}
, and
\bm{A}_{2}=(\bm{C}_{2}\bm{D}_{2})
, respectively.
Then, the gradient of
\bm{c}_{1,m}
is

\nabla L(\bm{c}_{1,m})=\frac{\partial L(\bm{c}_{1,m})}{\partial\bm{c}_{1,m}}=2\bm{D}_{1}\left([(\bm{D}_{1}^{\top}\bm{c}_{1,m})\circledast\bm{b}_{2,m}-\bm{b}_{m}]\circledast\bm{b}_{2,m}\right),\,m\in\{1,2,\ldots,M\}.

(14.30)

The gradient of the rows of
\bm{C}_{2}
can be obtained analogously.
Therefore, Algorithm
can be modified to update the columns of
\bm{D}_{1},\bm{D}_{2}
and the rows of
\bm{C}_{1},\bm{C}_{2}
iteratively (referred to as rank-one updates).

14.10.2
Missing Entries

The rank-one update can be extended to the Netflix context, in which case many entries of
\bm{A}
are missing.
Assuming
\bm{A}
is a low-rank matrix, we aim to fill in the missing entries of matrix
\bm{A}
(where
M
represents the number of movies, and
N
represents the number of users).

Let
\bm{o}_{n}\in\{0,1\}^{M},n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
, represent the movies rated by user
n
, where
o_{nm}=1
if user
n
has rated movie
m
, and
o_{nm}=0
otherwise.
Similarly, let
\bm{p}_{m}\in\{0,1\}^{N},m\in\{1,2,\ldots,M\}
denote the users who have rated movie
m
, with
p_{mn}=1
if the movie
m
has been rated by user
n
, and
p_{mn}=0
otherwise.
Then, Equations (
) and (
) become

\displaystyle\nabla L(\bm{d}_{1,n})

\displaystyle=2\bm{C}_{1}[\bm{o}_{n},:]^{\top}\left(\left[(\bm{C}_{1}[\bm{o}_{n},:]\bm{d}_{1,n})\circledast\bm{a}_{2,n}[\bm{o}_{n}]-\bm{a}_{n}[\bm{o}_{n}]\right]\circledast\bm{a}_{2,n}[\bm{o}_{n}]\right),

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\};

(14.31)

\displaystyle\nabla L(\bm{c}_{1,m})

\displaystyle=2\bm{D}_{1}[:,\bm{p}_{m}]\left(\left[(\bm{D}_{1}[:,\bm{p}_{m}]^{\top}\bm{c}_{1,m})\circledast\bm{b}_{2,m}[\bm{p}_{m}]-\bm{b}_{m}[\bm{p}_{m}]\right]\circledast\bm{b}_{2,m}[\bm{p}_{m}]\right),

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,m\in\{1,2,\ldots,M\}.

(14.32)

Since the Hadamard product commutes, the gradient of
L(\bm{d}_{2,n}),n\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
and
\bm{c}_{2,m},m\in\{1,2,\ldots,M\}
can be obtained similarly due to symmetry.
The process for predicting the missing entries in
\bm{A}
is then formulated in Algorithm
.

;

14.11
Movie Recommender

The ALS algorithm has been extensively developed for movie recommendation systems.
To illustrate this, we use the “MovieLens 100K” data set from MovieLens
(
Harper and Konstan 2015
)
.
This data set is widely recognized and used in the field of recommender systems research due to its comprehensive set of user ratings for movies.
It consists of 100,000 ratings from 943 users for 1,682 movies, with rating values ranging from 0 to 5. The data was collected through the MovieLens website over a seven-month period from September 19th,
1997 to April 22nd, 1998. This data has been cleaned up—users
who had less than 20 ratings or did not have complete demographic
information were removed from this data set such that simple demographic info for the users (age, gender, occupation, zip) can be obtained. However, our focus will solely be on the raw rating matrix to evaluate how well the low-rank ALS approach can capture the underlying structure of the data, leading to accurate and meaningful recommendations.

The data set is split into training and validation set, comprising approximately 95,015 and 4,985 ratings, respectively, to fit by the ALS algorithm.
The error is quantified using the
root mean squared error (RMSE)
. The RMSE is a common measure of the difference between actual and predicted values. For a set of values
\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
and their predictions
\{\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},\ldots,\hat{x}_{n}\}
, the RMSE can be described as

\text{RMSE}(\bm{x},\hat{\bm{x}})=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\hat{x}_{i})^{2}}.

For evaluating the ALS algorithm, the minimum RMSE for the validation set is achieved with
K=62
and
\lambda_{w}=\lambda_{z}=0.15
, resulting in an RMSE of
0.806
(less than 1), as shown in Figure
.
Given that ratings range from 0 to 5, the ALS algorithm can predict whether a user is likely to enjoy a movie (e.g., ratings of 4 to 5) or not (e.g., ratings of 0 to 2).

(a)

Training set.

(b)

Validation set.

Figure 14.6:
Comparison of training and validation error for the “MovieLens 100K” data set with different reduction dimensions and regularization parameters.

Recommender 1.

A simple recommender system suggests movie
m
to user
n
if
a_{mn}\geq 4
and user
n
has not yet rated movie
m
.

Recommender 2.

Alternatively, we can recommend similar movies to those highly rated by the user.
Suppose user
n
has rated movie
m
with a 5 (
a_{mn}=5
). Under the ALS approximation
\bm{A}=\bm{W}\bm{Z}
, where each row of
\bm{W}
represents the hidden features of each movie (see Section
on vector inner products), the solution involves identifying the most similar movies that user
n
has not rated (or watched), to movie
m
. Mathematically, this is expressed as:

\mathop{\text{arg max}}_{\bm{w}_{i}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{similarity}(\bm{w}_{i},\bm{w}_{m}),\qquad\text{for all}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,i\notin\bm{o}_{n},

where
\bm{w}_{i}
’s are the rows of
\bm{W}
, each representing the hidden features of movie
i
, and
\bm{o}_{n}
represents a mask vector, indicating the movies that user
n
has rated.

The method described above relies on a similarity function applied to two vectors. The
cosine similarity
is the most commonly used measure. It is defined as the cosine of the angle between the two vectors:

\cos(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\frac{\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{y}}{\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}\cdot\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}},

where the value ranges from
-1
to 1, with
-1
representing perfectly dissimilar and 1 being perfectly similar. Based on this definition, it follows that the cosine similarity depends only on the angle between the two nonzero vectors, but not on their magnitudes since it can be regarded as the inner product between the normalized versions of these vectors.
Another measure for calculating similarity is the
Pearson similarity
:

\text{Pearson}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\frac{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}(\bm{x},\bm{y})}{\sigma_{x}\cdot\sigma_{y}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})(y_{i}-\bar{y})}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}}.

It is calculated as the ratio between the covariance of two variables and the product of their standard deviations, whose range varies between
-1
and 1, where
-1
is perfectly dissimilar, 1 is perfectly similar, and 0 indicates no linear relationship. Pearson similarity is commonly used to measure the linear correlation between two sets of data.

Both Pearson correlation and cosine similarity are widely used in various fields, including machine learning and data analysis. Pearson correlation is often used in regression analysis, while cosine similarity is commonly used in recommendation systems and information retrieval.
In our context, cosine similarity performs better in
precision-recall (PR) curve
analysis.

(a)

Cosine Bin Plot.

(b)

Pearson Bin Plot.

(c)

PR Curve.

Figure 14.7:
Distribution of the insample and outsample using cosine and Pearson similarities, and the Precision-Recall curves for both.

Building upon the previous example using the MovieLens 100K data set, we set
\lambda_{w}=\lambda_{z}=0.15
for regularization and a rank of
62
to minimize RMSE.
We aim to analyze the similarity between different movie hidden vectors, and the goal of Recommender 2 is to see whether the matrix factorization can help differentiate high-rated from low-rated movies, thereby recommending movies correlated with the user’s high-rated ones.
Define further the term “insample” as the similarity between the movies having rates
5
for each user, and “outsample” as the similarity between the movies having rates
5
and
1
for each user. Figure
and
depict the bin plots of the distributions of insample and outsample under cosine and Pearson similarities, respectively.
In both scenarios, a clear distinction is observed between the distributions of the “insample” and “outsample” data, indicating that ALS decomposition can actually find the hidden features of different movies for each user.
Figure
displays the
precision-recall (PR) curve
for these scenarios, where we find cosine similarity outperforms Pearson similarity, achieving over
73\%
recall with
90\%
precision. However, Pearson similarity can identify only about
64\%
of the high-rated movies with the same precision.
In practice, other measures, such as
negative Euclidean distance
, can also be explored. The Euclidean distance measures the “dissimilarity” between two vectors; and a negative value thus represents their similarity.

The ALS method for recommendation discussed here is designed for
explicit data
, where the ratings provided by each user have a clear hierarchical meaning. In contrast, there are also recommendation systems for
implicit data
, where the system automatically infers users’ preferences by tracking their actions, such as which items they viewed, where they clicked, which products they purchased, or how long they spent on a web page.
In such cases, ALS can be extended to more complex models, such as using a dictionary matrix to transform the explicit data into user and item latent vectors
(
He et al. 2017
)
, incorporating multinomial prior into a variational auto-encoder, and enhancing the model’s ability to handle implicit feedback by leveraging probabilistic modeling techniques
(
Liang et al. 2018
)
.

14.21.

Least squares for rank-deficiency.
Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
and
\bm{b}\in\real^{m}
. Show that the least squares problem
L(\bm{x})=\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
has a minimizer
\bm{x}^{*}\in\real^{n}
if and only if there exists a vector
\bm{y}\in\real^{n}
such that
\bm{x}^{*}=\bm{A}^{+}\bm{b}+(\bm{I}-\bm{A}^{+}\bm{A})\bm{y}
, where
\bm{A}^{+}
is the pseudo-inverse of
\bm{A}
.

•

This shows that the least squares has a
unique
minimizer of
\bm{x}^{*}=\bm{A}^{+}\bm{b}
only when
\bm{A}^{+}
is a left inverse of
\bm{A}
(i.e.,
\bm{A}^{+}\bm{A}=\bm{I}
). The solution in Lemma
is a special case.

•

The optimal value is
L(\bm{x}^{*})=\bm{b}^{\top}(\bm{I}-\bm{A}\bm{A}^{+})\bm{b}
.

•

If
\bm{y}\neq\mathbf{0}
:
\left\lVert\bm{A}^{+}\bm{b}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\bm{A}^{+}\bm{b}+(\bm{I}-\bm{A}^{+}\bm{A})\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}
.

Hint: See LS via SVD in Section
.

14.21.

Orthogonal and projective matrix factorization.
Consider the optimization
\mathop{\min}_{\bm{W}}\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{W}\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
such that
\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}=\bm{I}_{K}
, where
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N},\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K},\bm{Z}\in\real^{K\times N}
, and
K\leq\min\{M,N\}
. Show that the optimal value
\bm{W}^{*}
given
\bm{Z}
is
\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top}
.
This indicates that the matrix factorization optimization can be equivalently stated as
\mathop{\min}_{\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}=\bm{I}_{K}}\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
. And the relaxed problem is called the
projective matrix factorization

(
Yuan and Oja 2005
;
Yang and Oja 2010
)
:

\mathop{\min}_{\bm{Z}}\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2},

where each row of
\bm{A}
is projected onto a
K
-dimensional subspace, hence the name.
The interpretations of orthogonal and projective matrix factorizations are discussed in Problem
.

14.21.

Regularized least squares (RLS).

Given
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n},\bm{b}\in\real^{m},\bm{B}\in\real^{p\times n}
, and
\lambda\in\real_{++}
, we consider the constrained least squares problem:

\mathop{\min}_{\bm{x}\in\real^{n}}\left\lVert\bm{A}\bm{x}-\bm{b}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lambda\left\lVert\bm{B}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Show that the constrained least squares (RLS) problem has a unique solution if and only if
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})\cap\mathcal{N}(\bm{B})=\{\mathbf{0}\}
.

14.21.

Denoising via RLS.
Consider a noisy measurement of a signal
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
:

\bm{y}=\bm{x}+\bm{e},

where
\bm{y}
is the observed measurement, and
\bm{e}
is the noise vector. We want to find an estimate
\bm{x}
of the observed measurement
\bm{y}
such that
\bm{x}\approx\bm{y}
:

\min\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Apparently, the optimal solution of this optimization is given by
\bm{x}=\bm{y}
; however, it is meaningless.
To improve the estimate, we can add a penalty term for the differences between consecutive observations:

R(\bm{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(x_{i}-x_{i+1})^{2}.

Then,

•

Find the constrained least squares representation for this problem and derive the constrained least squares solution.

•

Find some applications of this denoising problem. For example, when we model the profit and loss signal of a financial asset, the two observations over consecutive days of the underlying asset should exhibit smooth transitions rather than abrupt changes.
