14.21.

First-order optimality condition for local optima points.

Consider
Fermat’s theorem
: for a one-dimensional function
g(\cdot)
defined and differentiable over an interval (
a,b
), if a point
x^{*}\in(a,b)
is a local maximum or minimum, then
g^{\prime}(x^{*})=0
.
Prove the first-order optimality conditions for multivariate functions based on Fermat’s theorem for one-dimensional functions.
That is, let
f:{\mathbb{S}}\rightarrow\real
be a function defined on a set
{\mathbb{S}}\subseteq\real^{n}
. Suppose that
\bm{x}^{*}\in\text{int}({\mathbb{S}})
, i.e., in the interior point of the set, is a local optimum point and that all the partial derivatives of
f
exist at
\bm{x}^{*}
. Then
\nabla f(\bm{x}^{*})=\mathbf{0}
, i.e., the gradient vanishes at all local optimum points. (Note that, this optimality condition is a necessary condition but not sufficient; however, there could be vanished points which are not local maximum or minimum point.)

Hint: Consider the one-dimensional function
g(t)=f(\bm{x}^{*}+t\bm{e}_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.

14.21.

Global minimum point of convex functions.
Let function
f
be a twice continuously differentiable function defined over
n
. Suppose that the Hessian
\nabla^{2}f(\bm{x})\succeq 0
for any
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
(i.e., the Hessian is always positive semidefinite ).
This property is also referred to as the
convexity
.
Show that
\bm{x}^{*}
is a global minimum point of
f
if
\nabla f(\bm{x}^{*})=\mathbf{0}
.
Hint: Use the linear approximation theorem.

14.21.

Two-sided matrix least squares
(
Friedland and Torokhti 2007
;
Aggarwal et al. 2020
)
.
Let
\bm{B}
be an
M\times K
matrix and
\bm{C}
be a
P\times N
matrix. Find the
K\times P
matrix
\bm{X}
such that
L(\bm{X})=\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{B}\bm{X}\bm{C}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
is minimized, where
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}
is known.

•

Derive the derivative of
L
with respect to
\bm{X}
and the optimality conditions.

•

Show that one possible solution to the optimality conditions is
\bm{X}^{*}=\bm{B}^{+}\bm{A}\bm{C}^{+}
, where
\bm{B}^{+}
and
\bm{C}^{+}
are the pseudo-inverses of
\bm{B}
and
\bm{C}
, respectively.

Similarly, consider the optimization with
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{X})\leq p
:

L(\bm{X})=\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{B}\bm{X}\bm{C}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
, s.t.

\mathrm{rank}(\bm{X})\leq p
.
Show that

•

One possible solution to this is
\bm{X}^{*}=\bm{B}^{+}\bm{A}_{p}\bm{C}^{+}
, where
\bm{A}_{p}
a truncated SVD of
\bm{B}\bm{B}^{+}\bm{A}\bm{C}^{+}\bm{C}
by replacing all but the
p
largest singular values by zero.

•

\bm{X}^{*}
also minimizes
\left\lVert\bm{X}\right\rVert_{F}
, i.e., has the smallest magnitude among all solutions.

•

\bm{X}^{*}
is the
unique
solution if and only if either
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{B}\bm{B}^{+}\bm{A}\bm{C}^{+}\bm{C})\leq p
or both
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{B}\bm{B}^{+}\bm{A}\bm{C}^{+}\bm{C})\geq p
and
\sigma_{p+1}(\bm{B}\bm{B}^{+}\bm{A}\bm{C}^{+}\bm{C})<\sigma_{p}(\bm{B}\bm{B}^{+}\bm{A}\bm{C}^{+}\bm{C})
.

14.21.

Monotonic progress of gradient descent.
Consider the gradient descent for a differentiable function
f(\bm{x}):\real^{n}\rightarrow\real
that is
L
-strongly smooth . Suppose the iterate
\bm{x}^{(t+1)}
is obtained from iterate
\bm{x}^{(t)}
by

\bm{x}^{(t+1)}=\bm{x}^{(t)}-\eta\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)}).

Show that

•

If the step size
\eta\leq\frac{2}{L}
, the function value
f
is nonincreasing:
f(\bm{x}^{(t+1)})\leq f(\bm{x}^{(t)})
.

•

If the step size
\eta\in[\frac{1}{2L},\frac{1}{L}]
, the gradient satisfies
\left\lVert\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\epsilon
after
T=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}})
steps.

14.21.

(
Rennie and Srebro 2005
;
Mazumder et al. 2010
)
Consider the nuclear norm
\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{n}
of any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
with rank
r
. Show that

\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{n}=\mathop{\min}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{W}\in\real^{m\times r}\\
\bm{Z}\in\real^{r\times n}\\
\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{2}(\left\lVert\bm{W}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\left\lVert\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{s.t.}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{A}=\bm{W}\bm{Z}

14.21.

Let
\bm{A}_{1},\bm{A}_{2}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any
m\times n
matrices with rank
r_{1}
and rank
r_{2}
, respectively.
Show that their Hadamard product
\bm{A}_{1}\circledast\bm{A}_{2}
has rank at most
r_{1}\cdot r_{2}
:
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{1}\circledast\bm{A}_{2})\leq\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{1})\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}_{2})
.

Chapter 15
Nonnegative Matrix Factorization (NMF)

15.1
Nonnegative Matrix Factorization

In the era of big data, extracting meaningful patterns and latent structures from high-dimensional data sets has become a central challenge in various scientific and technological fields.
Singular value decomposition (SVD) is supported by robust theoretical foundations and is applicable in a wide range of contexts. However, it does have limitations; for example, when applied to a nonnegative matrix , SVD can produce negative values, which can be challenging to interpret.
To overcome this limitation,
nonnegative matrix factorization (NMF)
has emerged as a powerful and interpretable tool for dimensionality reduction, feature extraction, and discovering latent structures within complex data.
Early consideration of the NMF problem was due to
Paatero and Tapper 1994
;
Cohen and Rothblum 1993
, who referred it as
positive matrix factorization
.
Later,
Lee and Seung 2001
popularized the problem with the introduction of the
multiplicative update
rule.

Following the matrix factorization using the alternating least squares (ALS) method, we now consider algorithms for solving the NMF problem:

•

Given a nonnegative matrix
\bm{A}\in\real_{+}^{M\times N}
with rank
r
, find nonnegative matrix factors
\bm{W}\in\real_{+}^{M\times K}
and
\bm{Z}\in\real_{+}^{K\times N}
such that:

\bm{A}\approx\bm{W}\bm{Z}.

As mentioned in the ALS section, the fundamental challenge in linear data analysis involves transforming or decomposing a high-dimensional data vector into a linear combination of lower-dimensional vectors. This transformation captures the essential characteristics of the original data, making it suitable for pattern recognition tasks. Consequently, these lower-dimensional vectors are often referred to as
“hidden vectors,” “pattern vectors,” or “feature vectors.”

When conducting data analysis, building models, and processing information, it is crucial to meet two primary requirements for a pattern vector:

•

Interpretability
. Each component of a pattern vector should possess clear physical or physiological significance, allowing for a meaningful understanding of the data.

•

Statistical fidelity
. In cases where the data are reliable and contain minimal error or noise, the components of a pattern vector should effectively capture the variability within the data, reflecting its primary distribution of information.

The NMF approach addresses these issues in various applications. For example:

•

In document collections, documents are represented as vectors, with each vector element indicating the frequency (often weighted) of a specific term within the document. Arranging these document vectors sequentially forms a nonnegative term-by-document matrix, which provides a numerical representation of the entire document collection.

•

In image collections, each image is depicted by a vector, where each vector element represents a pixel. The value of each element, a nonnegative number, reflects the intensity and color of the corresponding pixel, leading to a nonnegative pixel-by-image matrix.

•

In gene expression analysis, observations from gene sequences under different experimental conditions are compiled into gene-by-experiment matrices. These matrices encapsulate the variations in gene expression across experiments.

•

For item sets or recommendation systems, customer purchase histories or ratings for a selection of items are recorded in a nonnegative sparse matrix. This matrix efficiently captures the sparse nature of user interactions with a large number of potential items.

Unlike arbitrary linear combinations, the linear combinations in the NMF context involve only nonnegative weights of nonnegative template vectors (basis vectors, i.e., the columns of
\bm{W}
). As a result, there are no effects such as destructive interference, where a positive component could be canceled out by adding a negative component. Instead, the data vectors must be explained using purely constructive methods, involving only positive components.
The nonnegativity constraint inherently imposes sparsity, enabling the factorization to capture additive features, which is especially advantageous in applications where parts-based representations are meaningful.
This constraint make it find applications in diverse fields such as text mining, image processing, document analysis, and bioinformatics, where the identified components often correspond to distinct parts or features.
For example, in image processing, NMF has proven valuable for tasks such as object detection, image segmentation, and facial recognition
(
Lee and Seung 2001
;
Gillis 2014
;
Gillis 2020
)
. The decomposition into nonnegative components aligns with the intuitive notion that images are composed of identifiable parts.
In the topic recovery problem, each column of
\bm{A}
denotes a document; the NMF aligns with a soft clustering approach where each column of
\bm{W}
represents a topic, and the positive entries of each column of
\bm{Z}
represent the positive weights of each document for those topics
(
Shahnaz et al. 2006
)
.
On the other hand, a nonnegative matrix factorization
\bm{A}\approx\bm{W}\bm{Z}
can be applied for clustering algorithm.
Specifically, the data vector
\bm{a}_{j}
is assigned to cluster
i
if
z_{ij}
is the largest element in column
j
of
\bm{Z}

(
Brunet et al. 2004
;
Gao and Church 2005
)
.
See also the applications of the NMF in the survey paper
Berry et al. 2007
.
In conclusion, the popularity of NMF stems from its ability to automatically extract sparse and easily interpretable factors.

To measure the quality of the approximation, we evaluate the loss by computing the Frobenius norm of the difference between the original matrix and the approximation:

L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})=D(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2},\penalty\

(15.1)

where
L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
indicates it is a loss function w.r.t.
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
, and
D(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})
implies it is a distance/divergence between
\bm{A}
and
\bm{W}\bm{Z}
(we will use the two notations interchangeably when necessary).
The Frobenius norm is arguably the most widely used norm for NMF because it corresponds to Gaussian additive noise, which is reasonable in many situations and allows for the design of particularly efficient algorithms.
For nonnegative data, Gaussian noise can be interpreted as a truncated version of Gaussian noise
(
Lu 2023
)
.
In later sections, we will extend this approach to include more general
\beta
-divergences (Section
).

When we want to find two nonnegative matrices
\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times r}_{+}
and
\bm{Z}\in\real_{+}^{r\times N}
such that
\bm{A}=\bm{W}\bm{Z}
, the problem is known as the
Exact NMF
of
\bm{A}
of size
r
. Exact NMF is NP-hard
(
Vavasis 2010
;
Gillis 2020
)
.
Thus, we only consider the approximation of NMF here.

In the context of collaborative filtering, it is recognized that NMF via multiplicative updates can result in overfitting despite favorable convergence results.
The overfitting can be partially mitigated through regularization, but its out-of-sample performance remains low.
Bayesian optimization through the use of generative models, on the other hand, can effectively prevent overfitting in nonnegative matrix factorization
(
Brouwer et al. 2017
;
Lu and Ye 2022
;
Lu 2023
)
.
In the following sections, we introduce several methods for solving NMF problems and provide a brief discussion of their applications.

Algorithm 14

Projected Gradient Descent Method

1:

A function
f(\bm{x})
and a set
{\mathbb{S}}
;

2:

for

t=1,2,\ldots

do

3:

Pick a step size
\eta_{t}
;

4:

Set
\bm{x}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\bm{x}^{(t)}-\eta_{t}\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)}))
;

5:

end

for

6:

Output final
\bm{x}
;

15.2
NMF via Alternating Projected Gradient Descent (APGD)

The projected gradient descent (PGD, Algorithm
) addresses the minimization of a function over a set
{\mathbb{S}}
:

\mathop{\min}_{\bm{x}\in{\mathbb{S}}}f(\bm{x}).

The
orthogonal projection
is defined as

\mathcal{P}_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\bm{x})=\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{y}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\left\lVert\bm{y}-\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}.

When
{\mathbb{S}}
is the nonnegative orthant,
\mathcal{P}_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\bm{x})
simplifies to
\mathcal{P}_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\bm{x})=\max\{\mathbf{0},\bm{x}\}
, where the max operator is applied componentwise.

Therefore, the
alternating PGD (APGD)
approach for NMF updates the factored components iteratively by

\bm{Z}\leftarrow\max\bigg\{\mathbf{0},\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{Z}\in\real^{K\times N}}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}\bigg\}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{W}\leftarrow\max\bigg\{\mathbf{0},\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{W}\in\real^{M\times K}}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}\bigg\},

where each update can be solved using a least squares method followed by projection onto the nonnegative orthant.
Due to the projection, the solution may not be properly scaled. A closed-form scaling factor
\gamma
can improve the solution at each iteration:

\gamma^{*}=\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\gamma\geq 0}\left\lVert\gamma\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}=\frac{\langle\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z}\rangle}{\langle\bm{W}\bm{Z},\bm{W}\bm{Z}\rangle}=\frac{\langle\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top},\bm{W}\rangle}{\langle\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W},\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}\rangle}.

While it is generally not advised to use APGD due to its convergence challenges, APGD can be quite effective as an initialization method. This approach involves running a few iterations of APGD before switching to a different NMF algorithm, which is particularly beneficial for sparse matrices
(
Gillis 2014
)
.

15.3
NMF via Alternating Nonnegative Least Squares (ANLS)

The fundamental element of the ALS approach is the least squares problem (Lemma
).
For NMF, we focus on the
nonnegative least squares (NNLS)
problem:

\mathop{\min}_{\bm{x}\geq\mathbf{0}}f(\bm{x})=\mathop{\min}_{\bm{x}\geq\mathbf{0}}\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{M}\bm{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{with }\bm{M}\in\real^{m\times n},\bm{b}\in\real^{m},\bm{x}\in\real_{+}^{n}.

(15.2)

The KKT conditions imply the complementary slackness condition
\lambda_{i}x_{i}^{*}=0,\forall\,i
, where
\lambda_{i}
is the Lagrangian multiplier; and the optimal condition
\nabla f(\bm{x}^{*})-\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\bm{e}_{i}=\mathbf{0}
, where
\bm{x}^{*}
denotes the optimal solution of the NNLS problem.
Together, the complementary slackness and the optimal condition indicate that:

\nabla f(\bm{x}^{*})=\sum_{i:x_{i}^{*}=0}\lambda_{i}\bm{e}_{i}.

Therefore, we conclude that

(\textbf{KKT of NNLS})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{x}^{*}\geq\mathbf{0},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\nabla f(\bm{x}^{*})\geq 0,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,x_{i}^{*}(\nabla f(\bm{x}^{*}))_{i}=0,\,\forall\,i.

(15.3)

These conditions imply sparsity when the nonnegative constraint is applied, meaning the NNLS or NMF problem inherently imposes a
sparsity constraint
.

Assume we are given the inactive set
I\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
:

I=\left\{i\mid x_{i}^{*}>0,\,\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}\right\}.

The complement of
I
is the so-called
active set
, where the corresponding constraints are active. That is, the active set contains indices
i
such that
x_{i}^{*}=0
. The nonzero entries of
\bm{x}^{*}
can be determined by solving the following linear system:

\displaystyle{}_{I}=\mathbf{0}\,\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,\,[\bm{M}^{\top}(\bm{M}\bm{x}-\bm{b})]_{I}=\mathbf{0}\,\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,\,\bm{M}[:,I]^{\top}\bm{M}[:,I]\bm{x}[I]=\bm{M}[:,I]^{\top}\bm{b}.

These are the normal equations of the unconstrained least squares problem for
\bm{x}[I]
, that is,

\min_{\bm{x}[I]}\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{b}-\bm{M}[:,I]\bm{x}[I]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

This leads to the
active-set method
, which iteratively updates the active set through pivoting (that is, entering and removing variables from the active set) to ensure the objective function decreases
(
Lawson and Hanson 1995
)
; see Algorithm
.

Alternating nonnegative least squares (ANLS).

Once we have the active-set method for NNLS problems, NMF can be achieved by replacing OLS in ALS algorithms with NNLS, known as
alternating nonnegative least squares (ANLS)

(
Kim and Park 2011
)
.
Given a fixed
\bm{W}
, the NMF can be solved for each column of
\bm{Z}
separately:

\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{W}\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left\lVert\bm{a}_{n}-\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},

where each subproblem
\mathop{\min}_{\bm{z}_{n}\geq\mathbf{0}}\left\lVert\bm{a}_{n}-\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
can be solved using NNLS.
Since the NMF problem is symmetric:
\bm{A}=\bm{W}\bm{Z}
if and only if
\bm{A}^{\top}=\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\top}
such that
D(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})=D(\bm{A}^{\top},\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\top})
. The analysis of optimizing
\bm{W}
given
\bm{Z}
follows directly from the previous methodology.
We should also note that since the initial guess of
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
typically offers a poor approximation of
\bm{A}
, solving the NNLS subproblems exactly in the early stages of the alternating algorithms is often unnecessary.
Instead, it can be more efficient to use ANLS as a refinement step within a less computationally expensive NMF algorithm, such as APGD or MU (discussed in later sections)

Algorithm 15

Nonnegative Least Squares (NNLS) via Active-Set Method

1:

A real-valued matrix
\bm{M}\in\real^{m\times n}
, a real-valued vector
\bm{b}\in\real^{m}
;

2:

Initialize index sets
I=\emptyset
and
J=\{1,\ldots,n\}
;

3:

Initialize unknown
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
to an all-zero vector and let
\bm{w}\leftarrow\bm{M}^{\top}(\bm{b}-\bm{M}\bm{x})
;

4:

Let
\bm{w}[J]
denote the sub-vector with indices from
J
;

5:

Choose a stopping criterion on the approximation error
\delta
;

6:

Choose the maximal number of iterations
C
;

7:

iter=0
;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

8:

while

J\neq\emptyset
and
\max(\bm{w}[J])>\delta
and
iter<C

do

9:

iter=iter+1
;

10:

Let
j
in
J
be the index of
\max(\bm{w}[J])
in
\bm{w}
:
j=\mathop{\text{arg max}}_{j\in J}w_{j}
;

11:

Add
j
to
I
and remove
j
from
J
such that
I\cup J=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
;

12:

Let
\bm{M}[:,I]
be
\bm{M}
restricted to the variables/columns included in
I
;

13:

Let
\bm{s}
be vector of same length as
\bm{x}
;
Let
\bm{s}[I]
denote the sub-vector with indices from
I
, and let
\bm{s}[J]
denote the sub-vector with indices from
J
;

14:

Set
\bm{s}[I]\leftarrow((\bm{M}[:,I])^{\top}\bm{M}[:,I])^{-1}(\bm{M}[:,I])^{\top}\bm{b}
and
\bm{s}[J]
to zero;

15:

while

\min(\bm{s}[I])\leq 0

do

16:

Let
\alpha\leftarrow\min\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i}-s_{i}}
for
i
in
I
where
s_{i}\leq 0
;

17:

Set
\bm{x}\leftarrow\bm{x}+\alpha(\bm{s}-\bm{x})
;

18:

Move to
J
all indices
j
in
I
such that
x_{j}\leq 0
;

19:

Set
\bm{s}[I]\leftarrow((\bm{M}[:,I])^{\top}\bm{M}[:,I])^{-1}(\bm{M}[:,I])^{\top}\bm{b}
;

20:

end

while

21:

Set
\bm{s}[J]
to zero;

22:

Set
\bm{x}\leftarrow\bm{s}
;

23:

Set
\bm{w}\leftarrow\bm{M}^{\top}(\bm{b}-\bm{M}\bm{x})
;

24:

end

while

25:

Output
\bm{x}
;

15.4
NMF via Hierarchical Alternating Nonnegative Least Squares

Let
\bm{a},\bm{b}\in\real_{+}^{n}
be two nonnegative vectors, then the
univariate NNLS
problem can be formulated as

\mathop{\min}_{x\geq 0}\left\lVert\bm{a}-x\bm{b}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

The solution of
x
can be obtained in closed-form:
x=\max\big\{0,\frac{\bm{b}^{\top}\bm{a}}{\left\lVert\bm{b}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\big\}
if
\left\lVert\bm{b}\right\rVert_{2}\neq 0
.
Having this univariate NNLS solution in mind, we consider the
k
-th row of
\bm{Z}
for
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
, the subproblem in NMF is

\mathop{\min}_{\bm{Z}[k,:]\geq\mathbf{0}}\bigg\|\underbrace{\big(\bm{A}-\sum_{p\neq k}^{K}\bm{W}[:,p]\bm{Z}[p,:]\big)}_{=\bm{A}_{k}}-\bm{W}[:,k]\bm{Z}[k,:]\bigg\|_{F}^{2},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,k,\penalty\

(15.4)

which indicates the entries in a row of
\bm{Z}
do not interact (similarly, entries in a column of
\bm{W}
do not interact). Therefore, the optimization of each entry in a row of
\bm{Z}
can be decoupled.
Let
\bm{A}_{k}=\big(\bm{A}-\sum_{p\neq k}^{K}\bm{W}[:,p]\bm{Z}[p,:]\big)
, the NMF problem becomes a set of rank-one updates on
\bm{A}_{k}
, for
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
.
The solution is

\bm{Z}^{*}[k,:]=\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{Z}[k,:]\geq\mathbf{0}}\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}-\bm{W}[:,k]\bm{Z}[k,:]\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\max\left(\mathbf{0},\frac{\bm{W}[:,k]^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}}{\left\lVert\bm{W}[:,k]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,k,

where the max operator is applied componentwise.
This leads to the
hierarchical ANLS (Hi-ANLS)
solution for NMF problems, which iteratively solves a univariate NNLS problem.
The procedure is described in Algorithm
, where we note that
\bm{Z}[k,:]^{\top}=\bm{Z}^{\top}[:,k]
.
In the algorithm, we update the
k
-th row of
\bm{Z}
and
k
-th column of
\bm{W}
in an interleaved manner.
Gillis and Glineur 2012
show that updating
\bm{Z}
several times before updating
\bm{W}
can significantly improve the performance since this reuses the results of
\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}
and
\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}
.

Algorithm 16

NMF via Hierarchical Alternating Nonnegative Least Squares (Hi-ANLS)

1:

Matrix
\bm{A}\in\real_{+}^{M\times N}
;

2:

Initialize
\bm{W}\in\real_{++}^{M\times K}
,
\bm{Z}\in\real_{++}^{K\times N}
randomly with positive entries;

3:

Choose a stop criterion on the approximation error
\delta
;

4:

Choose maximal number of iterations
C
;

5:

iter=0
;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

6:

while

\left\lVert\bm{A}-(\bm{W}\bm{Z})\right\rVert_{F}^{2}>\delta
and
iter<C

do

7:

iter=iter+1
;

8:

for

k=1
to
K

do

9:

\bm{Z}[k,:]\leftarrow\max\left(\mathbf{0},\frac{\bm{W}[:,k]^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}}{\left\lVert\bm{W}[:,k]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right)
;
\triangleright

\bm{A}_{k}=\big(\bm{A}-\sum_{p\neq k}^{K}\bm{W}[:,p]\bm{Z}[p,:]\big)

10:

\bm{W}[:,k]\leftarrow\max\left(\mathbf{0},\frac{\bm{A}_{k}\bm{Z}[k,:]^{\top}}{\left\lVert\bm{Z}[k,:]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right)
;

11:

end

for

12:

end

while

13:

Output
\bm{W},\bm{Z}
;

15.5
NMF via Alternating Direction Methods of Multipliers (ADMM)

We briefly introduce the
alternating direction methods of multipliers (ADMM)
and then discuss its applications in matrix factorization and NMF.

ADMM.

ADMM addresses the following convex optimization problem:

\mathop{\min}_{\bm{x},\bm{z}}f(\bm{x})+g(\bm{z}),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{s.t.}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{D}\bm{x}+\bm{E}\bm{z}=\bm{f}.

(15.5)

Given a parameter
\rho>0
, the
augmented Lagrangian
of (
) is

L_{\rho}(\bm{x},\bm{z},\bm{l})=f(\bm{x})+g(\bm{z})+\langle\bm{l},\bm{D}\bm{x}+\bm{E}\bm{z}-\bm{f}\rangle+\frac{\rho}{2}\left\lVert\bm{D}\bm{x}+\bm{E}\bm{z}-\bm{f}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(15.6)

When
\rho=0
, the augmented Lagrangian reduces to the Lagrangian function; when
\rho>0
, the augmented Lagrangian function acts as a penalized version of the Lagrangian function.
The
augmented Lagrangian method
solves the problem by performing the following steps (at the
(t+1)
-th iteration):

\text{augmented Lagrangian:}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\left\{\begin{aligned} (\bm{x}^{(t+1)},\bm{z}^{(t+1)})&\in\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{x},\bm{z}}L_{\rho}(\bm{x},\bm{z},\bm{l});\\
\bm{l}^{(t+1)}&=\bm{l}^{(t)}+\rho(\bm{D}\bm{x}^{(t+1)}+\bm{E}\bm{z}^{(t+1)}-\bm{f}),\end{aligned}\right.

where the update on
\bm{l}^{(t+1)}
is derived from the
conjugate subgradient theorem
(see, for example,
Bach et al. 2011
), and
the symbol ‘
\in
’ indicates that the minimum points may not be uniquely determined.
One source of difficulty is the coupling term between the
\bm{x}
and
\bm{z}
variables, which is of the form
\rho(\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{D}^{\top}\bm{E}\bm{z})
.
ADMM tackles this difficulty by replacing the exact minimization of
(\bm{x},\bm{z})
with one iteration of the alternating minimization method.
To be more specific, for the
(t+1)
-iteration, the solution of ADMM takes the following form:

\text{ADMM:}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\left\{\begin{aligned} \bm{x}^{(t+1)}&\in\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{x}}\left\{f(\bm{x})+\frac{\rho}{2}\left\lVert\bm{D}\bm{x}+\bm{E}\bm{z}^{(t)}-\bm{f}+\frac{1}{\rho}\bm{l}^{(t)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\};\\
\bm{z}^{(t+1)}&\in\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{z}}\left\{g(\bm{z})+\frac{\rho}{2}\left\lVert\bm{D}\bm{x}^{(t+1)}+\bm{E}\bm{z}-\bm{f}+\frac{1}{\rho}\bm{l}^{(t)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\};\\
\bm{l}^{(t+1)}&=\bm{l}^{(t)}+\rho(\bm{D}\bm{x}^{(t+1)}+\bm{E}\bm{z}^{(t+1)}-\bm{f}).\end{aligned}\right.

(15.7)

Let
\widetilde{\bm{l}}=\frac{1}{\rho}\bm{l}
, this can be equivalently stated as (the one we will use in the sequel):

\text{ADMM:}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\left\{\begin{aligned} \bm{x}^{(t+1)}&\in\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{x}}\left\{f(\bm{x})+\frac{\rho}{2}\left\lVert\bm{D}\bm{x}+\bm{E}\bm{z}^{(t)}-\bm{f}+\widetilde{\bm{l}}^{(t)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\};\\
\bm{z}^{(t+1)}&\in\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{z}}\left\{g(\bm{z})+\frac{\rho}{2}\left\lVert\bm{D}\bm{x}^{(t+1)}+\bm{E}\bm{z}-\bm{f}+\widetilde{\bm{l}}^{(t)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\};\\
\widetilde{\bm{l}}^{(t+1)}&=\widetilde{\bm{l}}^{(t)}+(\bm{D}\bm{x}^{(t+1)}+\bm{E}\bm{z}^{(t+1)}-\bm{f}).\end{aligned}\right.

(15.8)

That is, iteratively updating
\bm{x},\bm{z}
, and
\bm{l}
.

ADMM applied to matrix factorization.

We return to the problem discussed in ALS (Equation (
), i.e., matrix factorization with Frobenius norm; not necessarily a NMF problem) together with a regularization function
r(\bm{Z})
:

\mathop{\min}_{\bm{Z}}\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{W}\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+r(\bm{Z}).

The problem can be equivalently stated with an auxiliary variable
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}\in\real^{K\times N}
:

\mathop{\min}_{\bm{Z}}\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{W}\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+r(\widetilde{\bm{Z}}),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{s.t.}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{Z}=\widetilde{\bm{Z}}.

(15.9)

Following (
), let a. {
\bm{x}\leftarrow\bm{Z}
,
\bm{z}\leftarrow\widetilde{\bm{Z}}
,
\widetilde{\bm{l}}\leftarrow\bm{L}
,
\bm{D}=-\bm{I}
,
\bm{E}=\bm{I}
} or b. {
\bm{x}\leftarrow\bm{Z}
,
\bm{z}\leftarrow\widetilde{\bm{Z}}
,
\widetilde{\bm{l}}\leftarrow\bm{L}
,
\bm{D}=\bm{I}
,
\bm{E}=-\bm{I}
},
the ADMM update for (
) is

\left\{\begin{aligned} \bm{Z}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leftarrow}}(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}+\rho\bm{I})^{-1}\left[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}+\rho(\widetilde{\bm{Z}}+\bm{L})\right]&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leftarrow}}&(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}+\rho\bm{I})^{-1}\left[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}+\rho(\widetilde{\bm{Z}}-\bm{L})\right];\\
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\widetilde{\bm{Z}}}r(\widetilde{\bm{Z}})+\frac{\rho}{2}\left\lVert-\bm{Z}+\widetilde{\bm{Z}}+\bm{L}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leftarrow}}&\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\widetilde{\bm{Z}}}r(\widetilde{\bm{Z}})+\frac{\rho}{2}\left\lVert\bm{Z}-\widetilde{\bm{Z}}+\bm{L}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\\
\bm{L}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leftarrow}}\bm{L}-\bm{Z}+\widetilde{\bm{Z}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leftarrow}}&\bm{L}+\bm{Z}-\widetilde{\bm{Z}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(15.10)

In practice, the Cholesky decomposition of
(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}+\rho\bm{I})
can be calculated such that the update can be obtained by forward and backward substitutions.
The update on
\bm{W}
can be obtained similarly due to symmetry.
We will consider the setting (a) in (
) for the following discussions.

ADMM applied to
\ell_{1}
regularization.

We may also consider the
\ell_{1}
regularization (see Section
):
r(\widetilde{\bm{Z}})=\lambda\|\widetilde{\bm{Z}}\|_{1}
. The update for each element
(k,n)
of
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}
is
\widetilde{z}_{kn}\leftarrow\max(0,1-\frac{\lambda}{\rho}\left\lvert h_{kn}\right\rvert^{-1})h_{kn}
for all
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
and
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
, where
h_{kn}=z_{kn}-l_{kn}
(i.e., the elements of
\bm{H}=\bm{Z}-\bm{L}
).

ADMM applied to smoothness/denoising regularization.

The smoothness regularization on
\bm{Z}
can be defined as
r(\widetilde{\bm{Z}})=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\bm{T}\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}
, where
\bm{T}
is an
N\times N
tridiagonal matrix with 2 on the main diagonal and
-1
on the superdiagonal and subdiagonal. This regularization ensures the proximal components in each row of
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}
is smooth (see Problem
). The update of
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}
becomes
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}\leftarrow\rho\bm{Z}(\lambda\bm{T}^{\top}\bm{T}+\rho\bm{I})^{-1}

(
Huang et al. 2016
)
.

ADMM applied to NMF.

The NMF with ADMM is achieved simply by replacing
r(\bm{Z})
with an indicator function.
The update on
\widetilde{\bm{Z}}
becomes
\max\left(\mathbf{0},\bm{Z}-\bm{L}\right)
, where the max operator is applied componentwise.
However, unlike the approaches introduced above and the MU approach in the next section, the updates from the ADMM approach are usually not monotonically nonincreasing.

15.6
NMF via Multiplicative Update (MU)

We further consider NMF via an alternative alternating update. The hidden features in
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
are modeled as nonnegative vectors in low-dimensional space. These latent vectors are randomly initialized and iteratively updated via an alternating multiplicative update rule to minimize the Frobenius norm distance between the observed and modeled matrices.
Following Section
, we consider the low-rank with
K
components; given
\bm{W}\in\real_{+}^{M\times K}
, we aim to update
\bm{Z}\in\real_{+}^{K\times N}
. The gradient with respect to
\bm{Z}
is given by Equation (
):

\begin{aligned} \nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})=\bm{W}^{\top}(\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})\in\real^{K\times N}.\end{aligned}

Applying the gradient descent idea discussed in Section
, the straightforward update for
\bm{Z}
can be achieved by

(\text{GD on $\bm{Z}$})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{Z}\leftarrow\bm{Z}-\eta\left(\nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})\right)=\bm{Z}-\eta\nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z}),

where
\eta
represents a small positive step size.

Multiplicative update (MU).

If we allow a different step size for each entry of
\bm{Z}
, the update can be written as:

(\text{GD${}^{\prime}$ on $\bm{Z}$})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\begin{aligned} z_{kn}&\leftarrow z_{kn}-\frac{\eta_{kn}}{2}\left(\nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})\right)_{kn}=z_{kn}-\eta_{kn}(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A})_{kn},\,\,\forall\,k,n,\end{aligned}

where
z_{kn}
denotes the
(k,n)
-th entry of
\bm{Z}
.
To proceed, we further rescale the step size:

\eta_{kn}=\frac{z_{kn}}{(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z})_{kn}}.

Then we obtain the update rule:

(\text{MU on $\bm{Z}$})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{Z}\leftarrow\bm{Z}\circledast\frac{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}]}{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z}]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{=}}\bm{Z}-\frac{[\bm{Z}]}{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z}]}\circledast\nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z}),

(15.11)

where
\frac{[\cdot]}{[\cdot]}
represents the componentwise division between two matrices. This is known as the
multiplicative update (MU)
, and is first developed in
Lee and Seung 2001
for NMF problems.
Analogously, the multiplicative update for
\bm{W}
can be obtained by

(\text{MU on $\bm{W}$})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{W}\leftarrow\bm{W}\circledast\frac{[\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top}]}{[\bm{W}\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{=}}\bm{W}-\frac{[\bm{W}]}{[\bm{W}\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}]}\circledast\nabla_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z}).

(15.12)

The factors
\frac{(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A})_{kn}}{(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z})_{kn}}
and
\frac{(\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top})_{mk}}{(\bm{W}\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top})_{mk}}
for all
m,k,n
in (
) and (
) are called
multiplicative factors
.
When
\bm{A}=\bm{W}\bm{Z}
, these multiplicative factors reduce to one, indicating that the corresponding gradients vanish.

MU vs gradient descent.

From the above construction, it is revealed that multiplication update algorithms are fundamentally similar to gradient descent algorithms, but they differ in their step size selection. With an appropriate choice of step size, a multiplication algorithm can transform the subtraction update rule of the standard gradient descent method into a multiplicative update rule.

In the gradient descent algorithm, a fixed or adaptive step length is typically used, and this step length is independent of the specific variable being updated. In other words, the step size may vary over time, but at any given update step, all entries of the variable matrix are updated using the same step size.
In contrast, the multiplication algorithm uses different step sizes (
\eta_{kn}
above) for different entries of the factor matrix. This means that the step length is adaptive to each matrix entry. This adaptability is a key reason why the multiplication algorithm can outperform the gradient descent algorithm.

KKT conditions for NMF with Frobenius norm.

The KKT conditions indicate that (see derivation in (
)):

\displaystyle\bm{Z}\geq\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbf{0},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\langle\bm{Z},\nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}_{K\times N};

(15.13)

\displaystyle\bm{W}\geq\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbf{0},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\langle\bm{W},\nabla_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}_{M\times K}.

This also implies

\displaystyle\min\{\bm{Z},\nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})\}=\mathbf{0}_{K\times N}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\min\{\bm{W},\nabla_{\bm{W}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})\}=\mathbf{0}_{M\times K},

(15.14)

where the min operator
\min\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is applied componentwise. Any pair
(\bm{W},\bm{Z})
satisfying the KKT conditions is a stationary point of the NMF problem in (
).

Problems in MU.

Equality (
*
) in (
) indicates a rescaled gradient descent update in the MU rules, which also implies

\frac{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}]_{kn}}{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z}]_{kn}}\geq 1\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,(\nabla_{\bm{Z}}L(\bm{W},\bm{Z}))_{kn}\leq 0,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,k,n.

Therefore, the MU algorithm induces three-fold rules: (i) Increase if its partial derivative is negative; (ii) Decrease it if its partial derivative is positive; (iii) Keep it unchanged if its partial derivative is zero.
However, if an element of
\bm{Z}
is equal to zero, the MU rule cannot modify it. Therefore, it is possible for an entry of
\bm{Z}
to be zero while its partial derivative is negative, which would not satisfy the KKT conditions in (
).
As a result, the iterates from the MU rule are not guaranteed to converge to a stationary point. There are several ways to address this issue, such as: (i) Using a small positive lower bound for the entries of
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{W}
(
Gillis and Glineur 2012
)
.
(ii) Using the MU rule while reinitializing zero entries of
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{W}
to a small positive constant when their partial derivatives become negative
(
Chi and Kolda 2012
)
.

We now prove the monotonicity of the MU rule for NMF problems.

Theorem 134
(Monotonically Nonincreasing of Multiplicative Update).

The loss
L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
remains nonincreasing under the following multiplicative update rules:

\displaystyle\bm{Z}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\bm{Z}\circledast\frac{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}]}{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z}]}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{W}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\bm{W}\circledast\frac{[\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top}]}{[\bm{W}\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}]},

where
\bm{A}\in\real_{+}^{M\times N},\bm{W}\in\real_{+}^{M\times K}
, and
\bm{Z}\in\real_{+}^{K\times N}
.
The operator
\frac{[\cdot]}{[\cdot]}
represents the componentwise division between two matrices, and
\circledast
denotes the Hadamard product between two matrices.

The MU update requires that
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{W}
should be initialized with positive (nonzero) entries; otherwise, the MU will not modify any entries due to the Hadamard product.

The MU approach for NMF sets a spark on NMF that has ignited a fire that has been growing steadily since then, becoming a cornerstone in the NMF literature for several reasons: (i) The update rules are extremely easy to implement; (ii) In practice, the convergence is relatively fast compared to many other methods; (iii) One major benefit of using MU rules is that the nonnegativity constraints are enforced automatically.
To prove the monotonicity of the MU rules, we need the following results.

Definition 135
(Auxiliary Function (Majorizer)).

G(\bm{x},\widetilde{\bm{x}})
is called an auxiliary function for
F(\bm{x})
(or a majorizer of
F
at
\widetilde{\bm{x}}
) if the conditions

G(\bm{x},\widetilde{\bm{x}})\geq F(\bm{x})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,G(\bm{x},\bm{x})=F(\bm{x})

are satisfied.
In other words, the auxiliary function
G(\bm{x},\widetilde{\bm{x}})
is an upper bound of
F(\bm{x})
and the bound is tight when
\widetilde{\bm{x}}=\bm{x}
.

Lemma 136
(Nonincreasing in Auxiliary Functions).

If
G
is an auxiliary function for
F
, then
F
is nonincreasing under the update

\bm{x}^{(t+1)}=\mathop{\text{arg min}}_{\bm{x}}\,G(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{(t)}).

(15.15)

Proof
[of Lemma
]
The definition of the auxiliary function indicates that
F(\bm{x}^{(t+1)})\leq G(\bm{x}^{(t+1)},\bm{x}^{(t)})\leq G(\bm{x}^{(t)},\bm{x}^{(t)})=F(\bm{x}^{(t)})
.

Note that
F(\bm{x}^{(t+1)})=F(\bm{x}^{(t)})
only if
\bm{x}^{(t)}
is a local minimum of
G(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{(t)})
w.r.t.
\bm{x}
. If the partial derivatives of
F
exist and are continuous in a small neighborhood of
\bm{x}^{(t)}
, this also implies that the gradient
\nabla F(\bm{x}^{(t)})=\mathbf{0}
. Thus, by iterating the update in (
), we obtain a sequence of estimates that converge to a local minimum
\bm{x}_{\min}=\text{arg min}_{\bm{x}}F(\bm{x})
of the objective function:

F(\bm{x}^{(0)})\geq F(\bm{x}^{(1)})\geq F(\bm{x}^{(2)})\geq\ldots\geq F(\bm{x}^{(t)})\geq F(\bm{x}^{(t+1)})\geq\ldots\geq F(\bm{x}_{\min}).

(15.16)

Definition
finds a majorizer
G
of
F
, and Lemma
shows the minimization property in
G
, hence the algorithm is often referred to as the
majorization-minimization (MM) framework
.
The update benefits when the global minimizer of
G
has a closed-form solution or can be computed efficiently.

Therefore, if we can find an appropriate auxiliary function
G(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{(t)})
for both variables in
\left\lVert\bm{A}-\bm{W}\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}
, the update rules in Theorem
follow from (
).
To apply the auxiliary function to the NMF problem, we consider a column in
\bm{A}
or
\bm{Z}
:
\bm{a}=\bm{a}_{n}
and
\bm{z}=\bm{z}_{n}
in the following lemma, where
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
.

Lemma 137
(Auxiliary Function for NMF).

Let
\bm{W}\in\real^{K\times N},\bm{a}\in\real^{M}
, and
\bm{z}\in\real^{K}
.
Let further
\bm{D}\in\real^{K\times K}
be a diagonal matrix with the
(k,k)
-th entry being
d_{kk}=\frac{(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{z})_{k}}{z_{k}}=\frac{\bm{w}_{k}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{z}}{z_{k}}=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{K}(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})_{kj}z_{j}}{z_{k}},\,\,\forall\,k\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
, where
\bm{w}_{k}
is the
k
-th column of
\bm{W}
and
z_{k}
is the
k
-th component of
\bm{z}
.
Then, the following function is an auxiliary function for
F(\bm{z})=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{a}-\bm{W}\bm{z}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
:

G(\bm{z},\bm{z}^{(t)})=F(\bm{z}^{(t)})+(\bm{z}-\bm{z}^{(t)})^{\top}\nabla F(\bm{z}^{(t)})+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\bm{z}^{(t)})^{\top}\bm{D}(\bm{z}-\bm{z}^{(t)}).

Proof
[of Lemma
]
Since the third-order partial derivatives of
F(\bm{z})
vanish (see Problem
),
F(\bm{z})
can be factored as

F(\bm{z})=F(\bm{z}^{(t)})+(\bm{z}-\bm{z}^{(t)})^{\top}\nabla F(\bm{z}^{(t)})+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{z}-\bm{z}^{(t)})^{\top}\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}(\bm{z}-\bm{z}^{(t)}).

Apparently,
G(\bm{z},\bm{z})=F(\bm{z})
. To complete the proof, we need to show that
G(\bm{z},\bm{z}^{(t)})\geq F(\bm{z})
; that is,
\bm{D}-\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}
is positive semidefinite.
To prove this, consider the matrix
\bm{M}\in\real^{K\times K}
whose entries are
m_{ij}=z_{i}(\bm{D}-\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})_{ij}z_{j}
for all
i,j\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
, which is a rescaling of the components of
\bm{D}-\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}
. Then
\bm{D}-\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}
is positive semidefinite if and only if
\bm{M}
is:

\displaystyle\bm{x}^{\top}

\displaystyle\bm{M}\bm{x}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{K,K}x_{i}m_{ij}x_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{=}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{K,K}\left\{(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})_{ij}z_{i}z_{j}x_{i}^{2}-(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})_{ij}z_{i}z_{j}x_{i}x_{j}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}}{{=}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{K,K}(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})_{ij}z_{i}z_{j}\left(\frac{1}{2}x_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}x_{j}^{2}-x_{i}x_{j}\right)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{K,K}(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})_{ij}z_{i}z_{j}\frac{1}{2}\left(x_{i}-x_{j}\right)^{2}\geq 0,

where equality
({\dagger})
follows from the symmetry of
\bm{M}
, and equality (
*
) follows from the diagonality of
\bm{D}
:

\sum_{i,j=1}^{K,K}x_{i}z_{i}d_{ij}z_{j}x_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{K}x_{i}z_{i}d_{ii}z_{i}x_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{K}x_{i}^{2}z_{i}^{2}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{K}(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})_{ij}z_{j}}{z_{i}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{K,K}(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W})_{ij}z_{i}z_{j}x_{i}^{2}.

This completes the proof.

The proof of Theorem
follows directly from the above lemmas.
Clearly, the approximations
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
remain nonnegative during the updates.
It is generally better to update
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
“simultaneously” rather than “sequentially,” i.e., updating each matrix completely before the other. In this case, after updating a row of
\bm{Z}
, we update the corresponding column of
\bm{W}
.
In the implementation, it is advisable to introduce a small positive quantity, say the square root of the machine precision, to the denominators in the approximations of
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}

at each iteration.
And a trivial value like
\epsilon=10^{-9}
suffices. The full procedure is shown in Algorithm
.
In practice, the algorithm can also be accelerated by updating
\bm{W}
several times before updating
\bm{Z}
, during which process we can reuse the result of
\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top}
and
\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}
, and vice versa.

Algorithm 17

NMF via Multiplicative Updates

1:

Matrix
\bm{A}\in\real_{+}^{M\times N}
;

2:

Initialize
\bm{W}\in\real_{++}^{M\times K}
,
\bm{Z}\in\real_{++}^{K\times N}
randomly with positive entries;

3:

Choose a stop criterion on the approximation error
\delta
;

4:

Choose maximal number of iterations
C
;

5:

iter=0
;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

6:

while

\left\lVert\bm{A}-(\bm{W}\bm{Z})\right\rVert_{F}^{2}>\delta
and
iter<C

do

7:

iter=iter+1
;

8:

\bm{Z}\leftarrow\bm{Z}\circledast\frac{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}]}{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z}]+\epsilon}
;

9:

\bm{W}\leftarrow\bm{W}\circledast\frac{[\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top}]}{[\bm{W}\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}]+\epsilon}
;

10:

end

while

11:

Output
\bm{W},\bm{Z}
;

15.6.1
Regularization

As mentioned in (
), the NNLS or NMF problem implicitly imposes a
sparsity constraint
.
On the other hand, similar to the ALS method with regularization discussed in Section
, recall that the regularization can help extend the applicability of ALS to general matrices.
Additionally, a regularization term can be incorporated into the NMF framework to enhance its performance:

L(\bm{W},\bm{Z})=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{w}\left\lVert\bm{W}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{z}\left\lVert\bm{Z}\right\rVert_{F}^{2},\qquad\lambda_{w}>0,\lambda_{z}>0,

where the employed matrix norm is still the Frobenius norm. The gradient with respect to
\bm{Z}
given
\bm{W}
is the same as that in Equation (
):

\displaystyle\frac{\partial L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})}{\partial\bm{Z}}=\bm{W}^{\top}(\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})+{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\lambda_{z}\bm{Z}}\in\real^{K\times N}.

The gradient descent update can be obtained by

(\text{GD on }\bm{Z})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{Z}\leftarrow\bm{Z}-\eta\left(\frac{\partial L(\bm{Z}|\bm{W})}{\partial\bm{Z}}\right)=\bm{Z}-\eta\left(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\lambda_{z}\bm{Z}}\right),

Analogously, if we assume a different step size for each entry of
\bm{Z}
, the update can be obtained by

(\text{GD${}^{\prime}$ on $\bm{Z}$})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\begin{aligned} z_{kn}&=z_{kn}-\eta_{kn}(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\lambda_{z}\bm{Z}})_{kn},\,\,\forall\,k,n.\end{aligned}

We again rescale the step size:

\eta_{kn}=\frac{z_{kn}}{(\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z})_{kn}}.

Then we obtain the MU rules for
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{W}
(due to symmetry):

\displaystyle\bm{Z}\leftarrow\bm{Z}\circledast\frac{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}-\lambda_{z}\bm{Z}]}{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z}]}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{W}\leftarrow\bm{W}\circledast\frac{[\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top}-\lambda_{w}\bm{W}]}{[\bm{W}\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}]}.

Modified MU.

Since the update for regularized NMF can result in negative values, a modified MU can be applied such that

\textbf{(MMU1):}\quad\bm{Z}\leftarrow\left[\bm{Z}\circledast\frac{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}-\lambda_{z}\bm{Z}]}{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z}]}\right]_{+};\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{W}\leftarrow\left[\bm{W}\circledast\frac{[\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top}-\lambda_{w}\bm{W}]}{[\bm{W}\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}]}\right]_{+},

where
[x]_{+}=\max\{x,\epsilon\}
. The parameter
\epsilon
is usually a very small positive number that prevents the emergence of negative update.
That is, we add a small lower bound for entries of
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
. Alternatively, another approach is:

\displaystyle\textbf{(MMU2):}\quad\bm{Z}\leftarrow\bm{Z}\circledast\frac{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{A}-\lambda_{z}\bm{Z}]_{+}}{[\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{W}\bm{Z}]}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{W}\leftarrow\bm{W}\circledast\frac{[\bm{A}\bm{Z}^{\top}-\lambda_{w}\bm{W}]_{+}}{[\bm{W}\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\top}]}.

15.7
NMF with Three Factors

The NMF method, when extended to incorporate three factor matrices, is referred to as
nonnegative matrix trifactorization (tri-NMF)
.
This approach introduces an additional factor:

\bm{A}\approx\bm{W}\bm{U}\bm{Z},

(15.17)

where
\bm{W}\in\real_{+}^{M\times K}
,
\bm{U}\in\real_{+}^{K\times J}
, and
\bm{Z}\in\real_{+}^{J\times N}
.
Consider the matrix-by-user matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}
, where each element is a binary number
\{0,1\}
(called
implicit data
; in contrast to
explicit data
used previously).
NMF on this matrix provides a sum of
K
rank-one matrices
\bm{A}\approx\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{W}[:,k]\bm{Z}[k,:]
.
In the context of implicit data, each rank-one matrix can be interpreted as finding a subset of users and a subset of items (e.g., movies) that interact strongly with each other.
The tri-NMF, on the other hand, can be approximated by

\bm{A}\approx\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\bm{W}[:,k]\bm{U}[k,j]\bm{Z}[j,:].

This formulation can be interpreted as identifying separately
J
subsets of movies that are watched together (the rows of
\bm{Z}
) and
K
subset of users that behave similarly (the columns of
\bm{W}
); while the matrix
\bm{U}
tells us how these subsets interact together.
If
u_{kj}>0
, then the
k
-th subset of users (corresponding to the positive entries of
\bm{W}[:,k]
) watches the movies from the
j
-th subset of movies (corresponding to the positive entries of
\bm{Z}[j,:]
).

In other words, tri-NMF identifies groups of users who exhibit similar behavior (by watching the same movies) and groups of movies that are similar (because they are watched by the same users), while connecting these groups through the nonnegative matrix
\bm{U}
. Another application of tri-NMF is in text mining, where it identifies subsets of documents that contain the same words (the columns of
\bm{W}
) and subsets of words that appear in the same documents (the rows of
\bm{Z}
), linking these subsets via the matrix
\bm{U}

(
Brouwer et al. 2017
;
Gillis 2020
;
Lu 2023
)
.

15.8
\beta
-Divergence, Alternative Perspectives of MU

As mentioned previously, the sum of squared loss, as given in (
) or (
), is convex when one of the factors is held constant, leading to a smooth optimization process.
This type of loss function falls under a broader class of distance/divergence estimators known as
\beta
-divergence
in the context of NMF.
Given two nonnegative scalars
x
and
y
, the
\beta
-divergence between
x
and
y
is defined as follows:

d_{\beta}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{x}{y}-\ln\frac{x}{y}-1,&\text{if }\beta=0;\\
&x\ln\frac{x}{y}-x+y,&\text{if }\beta=1;\\
&\frac{1}{\beta^{2}-\beta}(x^{\beta}+(\beta-1)y^{\beta}-\beta xy^{\beta-1}),&\text{otherwise}.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(15.18)

The
\beta
-divergence is continuous in
\beta
since
\mathop{\lim}_{\beta\rightarrow 0}(x^{\beta}-y^{\beta})/\beta=\ln(x/y)
.
When
\beta=0,1,
and
2
, the
\beta
-divergences are also known as the
Itakura-Saito (IS), KL, and Frobenius/Euclidean distances/divergences
, respectively.
The
\beta
-divergence between two matrices
\bm{B}
and
\bm{C}
is

D_{\beta}(\bm{B},\bm{C})=\sum_{j}d_{\beta}(\bm{b}_{j},\bm{c}_{j})=\sum_{i,j}d_{\beta}({b_{ij},c_{ij}}).

(15.19)

The analysis of
\beta
-divergence is complex. When the first argument is fixed at 1, smaller values are less penalized as the
\beta
value increases; however, when the first argument is
2
, smaller values are more penalized as the
\beta
value increases.
In both cases, larger values are more heavily penalized as the
\beta
value increases. See Figure
.

(a)

\beta
-divergence for
d_{\beta}(1,y)
.

(b)

\beta
-divergence for
d_{\beta}(2,y)
.

Figure 15.1:

The analysis of
\beta
-divergence is complex. When the first argument is fixed at 1, smaller values are less penalized as the
\beta
value increases; however, when the first argument is
2
, smaller values are more penalized as the
\beta
value increases.
In both cases, larger values are more heavily penalized as the
\beta
value increases.

Convexity of
\beta
-divergence.

When
\beta\in[1,2]
, the function
d_{\beta}(x,y)
is convex in the second argument
y
. This implies
D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})
is convex in
\bm{W}
when fixing
\bm{Z}
, and vice versa (Problem
).
Therefore, coordinate descent algorithms can be effectively applied for NMF using the
\beta
-divergence.

Scaling in arguments.

Let
\gamma>0
be a scale factor, then

d_{\beta}(\gamma x,\gamma y)=\gamma^{\beta}d_{\beta}(x,y).

(15.20)

This indicates that the larger the
\beta
, the more sensitive the
\beta
-divergence is to large values of
x
or
y
; on the contrary,
\beta
-divergence with small
\beta<0
values relies more heavily on the smallest data values.
However, when
\beta=0
(called
Itakura-Saito divergence, IS divergence
), the
\beta
-divergence is not related to the
\beta
value, and thus it is invariant to scaling.
What matters is the ratio between
x
and
y
; see (
).

Gradient.

Since we consider a nonnegative matrix
\bm{A}
for NMF problems, we should note that
d_{\beta}(x,\cdot)
for
x=0
is not defined for all values of
\beta
:

d_{\beta}(0,y)=\left\{\begin{aligned} \text{not defined},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,&\text{if $\beta\leq 0$};\\
\frac{1}{\beta}y^{\beta},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,&\text{if $\beta>0$},\end{aligned}\right.\,\,\,\implies\,\,\,d^{\prime}_{\beta}(0,y)=\left\{\begin{aligned} \text{not defined},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,&\text{if $\beta\leq 0$};\\
y^{\beta-1},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,&\text{if $\beta>0$},\end{aligned}\right.

where the derivative
d^{\prime}_{\beta}(0,y)
corresponds to the second variable
y
.
Therefore, the algorithm developed in the following sections can only be applied to positive matrices when
\beta\leq 0
.
Table
and Table
present the domains of
d_{\beta}(x,\cdot)
and
d^{\prime}_{\beta}(x,\cdot)
, respectively, for different values of
\beta
and
x
.

Table 15.1:

d_{\beta}(x,\cdot)

On the other hand, the gradients of
D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})
w.r.t.
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{W}
are given by (if exist)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{Z}}D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})

\displaystyle=\bm{W}^{\top}\big((\bm{W}\bm{Z})^{\beta-2}\circledast(\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})\big);

(15.21)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{W}}D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})

\displaystyle=\big((\bm{W}\bm{Z})^{\beta-2}\circledast(\bm{W}\bm{Z}-\bm{A})\big)\bm{Z}^{\top},

where
(\bm{W}\bm{Z})^{\beta-2}
denotes the componentwise exponent.
When
\beta=2
, the gradient reduces to the one in (
).

Decomposition of
\beta
-divergence.

The
\beta
-divergence can be divided into three parts: convex, concave, and constant terms.
We should note that this decomposition is not unique since any affine term is both convex and concave.
We follow the convention in
Févotte and Idier 2011
:

d_{\beta}(x,y)=\breve{d}_{\beta}(x,y)+\widehat{d}_{\beta}(x,y)+\bar{d}_{\beta}(x,y),

(15.22)

where
\breve{d}_{\beta}(x,y)
is convex in
y
,
\widehat{d}_{\beta}(x,y)
is concave in
y
, and
\bar{d}_{\beta}(x,y)
is constant in
y
; see Table
for different
\beta
values.

\breve{d}_{\beta}(x,y)/\breve{d}^{\prime}_{\beta}(x,y)
, convex

\widehat{d}_{\beta}(x,y)/\widehat{d}^{\prime}_{\beta}(x,y)
, concave

\bar{d}_{\beta}(x,y)
, constant

\beta<1,\beta\neq 0

-\frac{1}{\beta-1}xy^{\beta-1}/-xy^{\beta-2}

\frac{1}{\beta}y^{\beta}/y^{\beta-1}

\frac{1}{\beta(\beta-1)}x^{\beta}

\beta=0

xy^{-1}/-xy^{-2}

\ln y/y^{-1}

\beta>2

Scalar convex-concave-constant decomposition of
d_{\beta}(x,y)
with respect to the second variable

, and the corresponding derivatives with respect to the second variable
y
.

KKT conditions for NMF with
\beta
-divergence.

The KKT conditions indicate that (see derivation in (
)):

\displaystyle\bm{Z}\geq\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{Z}}D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbf{0},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\langle\bm{Z},\nabla_{\bm{Z}}D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}_{K\times N};

(15.23)

\displaystyle\bm{W}\geq\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{W}}D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbf{0},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\langle\bm{W},\nabla_{\bm{W}}D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}_{M\times K}.

This also implies

\displaystyle\min\{\bm{Z},\nabla_{\bm{Z}}D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})\}=\mathbf{0}_{K\times N}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\min\{\bm{W},\nabla_{\bm{W}}D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})\}=\mathbf{0}_{M\times K},

(15.24)

where the min operator
\min\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is applied componentwise.

15.8.1
MU for
\beta
-Divergence Obtained by Gradient Ratio Heuristic

We have shown that the MU update with Frobeius norm can be derived from rescaled gradient descent.
For brevity, let
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}=\nabla_{\bm{Z}}D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})=\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{+}-\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{-}
, where

\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{+}=\bm{W}^{\top}\big((\bm{W}\bm{Z})^{\beta-1}\big)\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{-}=\bm{W}^{\top}\big((\bm{W}\bm{Z})^{\beta-2}\circledast\bm{A}\big).

(15.25)

When
z_{kn}>0,\forall\,k,n
, the KKT conditions show that
(\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{+})_{kn}=(\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{-})_{kn}
. The rule from gradient descent (i.e.,
\bm{Z}^{(t+1)}=\bm{Z}^{(t)}-\eta\nabla_{\bm{Z}}
) indicates a small decrease (resp. increase) of
z_{kn}
will lead to a decrease of the loss function if
(\nabla_{\bm{Z}})_{kn}>0
(resp.
<0
).
Therefore, it is reasonable to update
z_{kn}
using the componentwise ratio between
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{-}
and
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{+}
:

\bm{Z}\leftarrow\bm{Z}\circledast\frac{[\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{-}]}{[\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{+}]},

(15.26)

where
\frac{[\cdot]}{[\cdot]}
represents the componentwise ratio of two matrices.
When
\beta=2
, the MU reduces to the case in Theorem
.
When
\beta=1
, the loss function becomes the KL divergence, and the update for
\bm{Z}
is

\textbf{($\beta=1$)}:\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{Z}\leftarrow\bm{Z}\circledast\frac{[\bm{W}^{\top}\frac{[\bm{A}]}{[\bm{W}\bm{Z}]}]}{[\bm{W}^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{M\times N}]}.

It can be shown that when
\beta\in[1,2]
, the MU algorithms will monotonically decrease
D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})
.

15.8.2
MU for
\beta
-Divergence Obtained by Rescaled PGD

As discussed in Section
,
the PGD approach involves projecting the gradient descent update onto the feasible set. Consider a standard GD update on
f(\bm{x})
:
\bm{x}^{(t+1)}=\bm{x}^{(t)}-\eta\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)})
, where
\eta
is a step size and
-\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)})
is a
descent direction
(
\bm{g}
is a descent direction if
\bm{g}^{\top}\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)})<0
.)
Consider further a diagonal
\bm{D}
such that
-\eta\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)})\rightarrow-\bm{D}\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)})
is also a descent direction (replacing the step size by a diagonal matrix) .
In this case, if the feasible set of
\bm{x}
is nonnegative, then the PGD is useful:
\bm{x}^{(t+1)}=\mathcal{P}(\bm{x}^{(t)}-\bm{D}\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)}))
, where
\mathcal{P}(x)=\max\{x,0\}
.
If we further decompose the gradient into positive and negative parts:
\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)})=\nabla^{+}f(\bm{x}^{(t)})-\nabla^{-}f(\bm{x}^{(t)})
with
\nabla^{+}f(\bm{x}^{(t)})>0
and
\nabla^{-}f(\bm{x}^{(t)})>0
, taking
\bm{D}=\mathrm{diag}\big(\frac{[\bm{x}^{(t)}]}{[\nabla^{+}f(\bm{x}^{(t)}))]}\big)
, the rescaled PGD update becomes a MU rule:

\bm{x}^{(t+1)}=\mathcal{P}\bigg(\bm{x}^{(t)}-\mathrm{diag}\big(\frac{[\bm{x}^{(t)}]}{[\nabla^{+}f(\bm{x}^{(t)})]}\big)\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)})\bigg)=\mathcal{P}\bigg(\bm{x}^{(t)}\circledast\frac{[\nabla^{-}f(\bm{x}^{(t)})]}{[\nabla^{+}f(\bm{x}^{(t)})]}\bigg).

(15.27)

If we use the decomposition of gradient in (
), the rescaled PGD becomes the MU update for NMF in (
).
If we further incorporate a step size
\eta
in the rescaled PGD update, it becomes

\bm{x}^{(t+1)}=\mathcal{P}\bigg((1-\eta)\bm{x}^{(t)}+\eta\bm{x}^{(t)}\circledast\frac{[\nabla^{-}f(\bm{x}^{(t)})]}{[\nabla^{+}f(\bm{x}^{(t)})]}\bigg).

(15.28)

Since
-\bm{D}\nabla f(\bm{x}^{(t)})
is a descent direction, the step size
\eta\in(0,1)
can ensure the update is monotonically nonincreasing.
Note that the projection operator can be omitted since all updates are nonnegative.

15.8.3
MU for
\beta
-Divergence Obtained by MM Framework

The
\beta
-divergence between two matrices can be defined columnwise (Equation (
)), and the
\beta
-divergence can be divided into three parts (convex, concave, and constant, Equation (
)). Thus, the loss function in NMF can be decomposed into (note the loss function can be further divided componentwise):

D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})=\sum_{n=1}^{N}d_{\beta}(\bm{a}_{n},\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n})=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left(\breve{d}_{\beta}(\bm{a}_{n},\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n})+\widehat{d}_{\beta}(\bm{a}_{n},\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n})+\bar{d}_{\beta}(\bm{a}_{n},\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n})\right).

For each column
n
, the MM framework involves finding auxiliary functions for the three components separately.
To see this, we need the following lemma:

Lemma 138
(Auxiliary Function By Parts).

Let
F(\bm{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}F_{i}(\bm{x})
, and let
G_{i}(\bm{x},\widetilde{\bm{x}})
be an auxiliary function for
F_{i}(\bm{x})
at
\widetilde{\bm{x}}
for all
i
.
Then,
G(\bm{x},\widetilde{\bm{x}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}(\bm{x},\widetilde{\bm{x}})
is an auxiliary function for
F(\bm{x})
at
\widetilde{\bm{x}}
.

This lemma indicates that if the auxiliary function is constructed separately for each component, it allows us to decouple the optimization.

Constant part.

There is no need to find an auxiliary function for the constant term
\bar{d}_{\beta}(\bm{a}_{n},\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n})
, since it does not influence the minimization of
d_{\beta}(\bm{a}_{n},\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n})
with respect to
\bm{z}_{n}
.

Concave part.

Any concave function can be upper-bounded using linearization (the tangent plane):

\widehat{d}_{\beta}(x,y)\leq\widehat{d}_{\beta}(x,\widetilde{y})+(y-\widetilde{y})\widehat{d}_{\beta}^{\prime}(x,\widetilde{y}),

where
\widehat{d}_{\beta}^{\prime}(x,\widetilde{y})
denotes the gradient of
\widehat{d}(x,\widetilde{y})
with respect to the second component
\widetilde{y}
.
Therefore, for any
\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n}\in\real^{K}
, the auxiliary function for the concave component
\widehat{d}_{\beta}(\bm{a}_{n},\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n})
can be constructed by

\widehat{G}(\bm{z}_{n},\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n})=\widehat{d}_{\beta}(\bm{a}_{n},\bm{W}\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n})+(\bm{W}\bm{z}_{n}-\bm{W}\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n})\circledast\widehat{d}_{\beta}^{\prime}(\bm{a}_{n},\bm{W}\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n}).

Convex part.

The auxiliary function for the convex part follows from the convexity inequality .
Construct a matrix
\bm{P}\in\real^{M\times K}
as follows:

p_{mk}=\frac{w_{mk}{\widetilde{z}}_{kn}}{\sum_{j}w_{mj}{\widetilde{z}}_{jn}}=\frac{w_{mk}{\widetilde{z}}_{kn}}{\bm{W}[m,:]\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\implies\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{P}\geq\mathbf{0}\text{ and }\bm{P}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}.

(15.29)

That is, each row of
\bm{P}
belongs to the unit simplex in
K
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\breve{d}_{\beta}(a_{mn},\bm{W}[m,:]\bm{z}_{n})

\displaystyle=\breve{d}_{\beta}\big(a_{mn},\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{mk}z_{kn}\big)=\breve{d}_{\beta}\big(a_{mn},\sum_{k=1}^{K}p_{mk}\frac{w_{mk}z_{kn}}{p_{mk}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}p_{mk}\breve{d}_{\beta}\big(a_{mn},\frac{w_{mk}z_{kn}}{p_{mk}}\big).

The decomposition finds the auxiliary function for
D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})
w.r.t.
\bm{Z}
.

Theorem 139
(Auxiliary Function for
D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})
w.r.t.
\bm{Z}
).

Let
\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{n}=\bm{W}\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n}
with
\widetilde{a}_{mn}=\bm{W}[m,:]\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n}
for all
m,n
, where
\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n}
is any vector in
K
. Then,
G(\bm{Z},\widetilde{\bm{Z}})=\sum_{n=1}^{N}G_{n}(\bm{z}_{n},\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n})=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{mn}
is an auxiliary function for
D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})
w.r.t.
\bm{Z}
, where

\displaystyle G_{mn}

\displaystyle=\bar{d}_{\beta}(a_{mn},\widetilde{a}_{mn})+\widehat{d}_{\beta}(a_{mn},\widetilde{a}_{mn})+\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{mk}(z_{kn}-\widetilde{z}_{kn})\widehat{d}_{\beta}^{\prime}(a_{mn},\widetilde{a}_{mn})+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{w_{mk}\widetilde{z}_{kn}}{\widetilde{a}_{mn}}\breve{d}_{\beta}\big(a_{mn},\frac{\widetilde{a}_{mn}z_{kn}}{\widetilde{z}_{kn}}\big).

Exercise
\thetheo
(Gradient and Hessian of Auxiliary Functions)

Consider the setting and notations in Theorem
.
Let
G_{n}(\bm{z}_{n},\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}G_{k}(\bm{z}_{kn},\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n})+C(\bm{z}_{n})
where
C(\bm{z}_{n})
is a constant w.r.t.
\bm{z}_{n}
.
That is,

G_{k}(\bm{z}_{kn},\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n})=\sum_{m=1}^{M}w_{mk}z_{kn}\widehat{d}_{\beta}^{\prime}(a_{mn},\widetilde{a}_{mn})+\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{w_{mk}\widetilde{z}_{kn}}{\widetilde{a}_{mn}}\breve{d}_{\beta}\big(a_{mn},\frac{\widetilde{a}_{mn}z_{kn}}{\widetilde{z}_{kn}}\big).

Show that gradient of the auxiliary function is

\nabla_{z_{kn}}G_{n}(\bm{z}_{n},\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n})=\sum_{m=1}^{M}w_{mk}\bigg(\widehat{d}_{\beta}^{\prime}(a_{mn},\widetilde{a}_{mn})+\breve{d}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(a_{mn},\frac{\widetilde{a}_{mn}z_{kn}}{\widetilde{z}_{kn}}\big)\bigg),

and the Hessian matrix is diagonal with entries

\nabla^{2}_{z_{kn}}G_{n}(\bm{z}_{n},\widetilde{\bm{z}}_{n})=\sum_{m=1}^{M}w_{mk}\frac{\widetilde{a}_{mn}}{\widetilde{z}_{kn}}\bigg(\breve{d}_{\beta}{{}^{\prime\prime}}\big(a_{mn},\frac{\widetilde{a}_{mn}z_{kn}}{\widetilde{z}_{kn}}\big)\bigg).

Note in all cases, the first-order derivative or the second-order derivative corresponds to the second argument of
d_{\beta}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

Since
\breve{d}_{\beta}(\cdot,\cdot)
is convex in the second argument, the Hessian is positive definite. Thus, the auxiliary function is convex.
These constructions result in the following theorem by minimizing the auxiliary function obtained in Theorem
.

Theorem 140
(Nonincreasing of MU for
\beta
-Divergence
(
Févotte and Idier 2011
;
Gillis 2020
)
).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real_{+}^{M\times N}
,
\bm{W}\in\real_{++}^{M\times K}
, and
\bm{Z}\in\real_{++}^{K\times N}
.
The loss
D_{\beta}(\bm{A},\bm{W}\bm{Z})
remains nonincreasing under the following multiplicative update rules:

\displaystyle\bm{Z}\leftarrow\bm{Z}\circledast\left(\frac{\left[\bm{W}^{\top}\left\{(\bm{W}\bm{Z})^{(\beta-2)}\circledast\bm{A}\right\}\right]}{[\bm{W}^{\top}(\bm{W}\bm{Z})^{(\beta-1)}]}\right)^{m(\beta)},\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\bm{W}\leftarrow\bm{W}\circledast\left(\frac{\left[\left\{(\bm{W}\bm{Z})^{(\beta-2)}\circledast\bm{A}\right\}\bm{Z}^{\top}\right]}{[(\bm{W}\bm{Z})^{(\beta-1)}\bm{Z}^{\top}]}\right)^{m(\beta)},

where

m(\beta)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{2-\beta},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,&\textit{if }&\beta<1;\\
&1,&\textit{if }&1\leq\beta\leq 2;\\
&\frac{1}{\beta-1},&\textit{if }&\beta>1.\end{aligned}\right.

When
\beta=2
, the result reduces to Theorem
.
When
1\leq\beta\leq 2
, the MU obtained via the MM framework coincides with heuristic described in Section
.

The update in Theorem
ensures nonnegativity of the parameter updates, provided they are initialized with positive values.

Choice of
\beta
for NMF.

The choice of
\beta
-divergence for NMF is problem-dependent.

Févotte et al. 2009
present results of decomposing a piano power spectrogram using
\beta=0
and demonstrate that components corresponding to very low residual noise and hammer strikes on the strings are extracted with great accuracy; these components are either ignored or severely degraded when using Euclidean or KL distances/divergences.

FitzGerald et al. 2009
show that
\beta=0.5
is optimal for music source separation problems.

Convergence.

An algorithm is said to be
convergent
if it produces a sequence of iterates
\{\bm{Z}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq 1}
or
\{\bm{W}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq 1}
that converges to a limit point
\bm{W}^{*}
or
\bm{Z}^{*}
satisfying the KKT conditions in (
). Monotonic nonincreasingness does not imply convergence in general, and neither is monotonicity necessary for convergence. Proving convergence of the MU methods is beyond the scope of this book; we refer the readers to
Gillis 2020
;
Févotte and Idier 2011
and references therein for more details.

15.9
Initialization

A significant challenge in NMF is the absence of guaranteed convergence to a global minimum.
Often, the convergence process is slow, and the algorithm may reach a suboptimal approximation.
In the preceding discussion, we initialized
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
randomly.
To mitigate this issue, there are also alternative strategies designed to obtain better initial estimates in the hope of converging more rapidly to a good solution
(
Boutsidis and Gallopoulos 2008
;
Gillis 2014
)
. We sketch the methods as follows for reference:

•

Clustering techniques.
Apply some clustering methods to the columns of
\bm{A}
, set the cluster means of the top
K
clusters as the columns of
\bm{W}
, and initialize
\bm{Z}
as a proper scaling of
the cluster indicator matrix (that is,
z_{kn}\neq 0
indicates that
\bm{a}_{n}
belongs to the
k
-th cluster);

•

Subset selection.
Pick
K
columns of
\bm{A}
, and set those as the initial columns for
\bm{W}
. And analogously,
K
rows of
\bm{A}
are selected to form the rows of
\bm{Z}
;

•

SVD-based approach.
Suppose the optimal rank-
K
approximation of
\bm{A}
is
\bm{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}
, where each factor
\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}
is a rank-one matrix with possible negative values in
\bm{u}_{i}
and
\bm{v}_{i}
, and nonnegative
\sigma_{i}
. Denote
[x]_{+}=\max(x,0)
, we notice

\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}=[\bm{u}_{i}]_{+}[\bm{v}_{i}]_{+}^{\top}+[-\bm{u}_{i}]_{+}[-\bm{v}_{i}]_{+}^{\top}-[-\bm{u}_{i}]_{+}[\bm{v}_{i}]_{+}^{\top}-[\bm{u}_{i}]_{+}[-\bm{v}_{i}]_{+}^{\top},

where the first two rank-one factors in this decomposition are nonnegative.
Then, either
[\bm{u}_{i}]_{+}[\bm{v}_{i}]_{+}^{\top}
or
[-\bm{u}_{i}]_{+}[-\bm{v}_{i}]_{+}^{\top}
can be selected to replace the factor
\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}
.
Boutsidis and Gallopoulos 2008
suggest to replace each rank-one factor in
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}
with either
[\bm{u}_{i}]_{+}[\bm{v}_{i}]_{+}^{\top}
or
[-\bm{u}_{i}]_{+}[-\bm{v}_{i}]_{+}^{\top}
, selecting the one with the larger norm and scaling it properly.
In other words, if we select
[\bm{u}_{i}]_{+}[\bm{v}_{i}]_{+}^{\top}
, then
\sigma_{i}\cdot[\bm{u}_{i}]_{+}
can be initialized as the
i
-th column of
\bm{W}
, and
[\bm{v}_{i}]_{+}^{\top}
can be chosen as the
i
-th row of
\bm{Z}
.

However, these techniques are not guaranteed to yield better performance theoretically.
We recommend referring to the aforementioned papers for more detailed information.

15.10
Movie Recommender Context

Both NMF and ALS methods approximate a matrix and reconstruct its entries using a set of basis/template vectors.
The key difference lies in the nature of these basis vectors and how the approximation is carried out.
The basis in NMF is composed of vectors with nonnegative elements while the basis vectors in ALS can have positive or negative values.
In NMF, each vector is reconstructed as a nonnegative summation of the basis vectors with “relatively” small components in the direction of each basis vector.
In contrast, in the ALS approximation, the data is modeled as a linear combination of the basis vector such that we can add or subtract vectors as needed; and the components in the direction of each basis vector can be large positive values or negative values. Therefore, depending on the application, one or the other factorization can be utilized to describe the data with different meanings.

Movie recommender context.

In the context of a movie recommender system, the rows of
\bm{W}
represent the hidden features of movies, while the columns of
\bm{Z}
represent the hidden features of users.
For example, in NMF, a movie might be described as 0.5 comedy, 0.002 action, and 0.09 romantic. However, in the ALS approach, we can get combinations such as 4 comedy,
-0.05
action, and
-3
drama, indicating positive or negative contributions to each feature.

Implicit hierarchy.

Both ALS and NMF do not rank the importance of each basis vector hierarchically. In contrast, singular value decomposition (SVD) ranks the importance of each basis vector based on the corresponding singular value.
In the SVD representation of
\bm{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}
,
this usually means that the reconstruction
\sigma_{1}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}
via the first set of basis vectors dominates and is the most used set to reconstruct data, followed by the second set, and so on.
This creates an implicit hierarchy in the SVD basis that doesn’t happen in the ALS or the NMF approach.

Interpretability of basis vectors.

In SVD, the basis vectors can be statistically interpreted as the directions of maximum variance, but many of these directions lack a clear visual or intuitive interpretation due to the presence of zero, positive, and negative entries.
When these basis vectors are used in a linear combination, the combination involves complex cancellations of positive and negative values, which can obscure the intuitive physical meaning of individual basis vectors. As a result, many basis vectors do not provide a meaningful explanation for nonnegative data, such as pixel values in a color image.
On one hand, the entries of a nonnegative pattern vector should all be nonnegative values. On the other hand, mutually orthogonal singular vectors must contain negative entries. For example, if all entries of the singular vector
\bm{u}_{1}
corresponding to the maximum singular value are nonnegative, then any other singular vector orthogonal to
\bm{u}_{1}
must contain at least one negative entry; otherwise, the orthogonality condition
\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}=0
for
j\neq 1
cannot be satisfied. This indicates that mutually orthogonal singular vectors are not suitable as pattern vectors or basis vectors in nonnegative data analysis.

15.11
Other Applications

Music spectral reconstruction.

To illustrate the application of NMF, we demonstrate how this technique can decompose a spectrogram of a music recording into components that carry musical significance
(
Müller 2015
)
. As an example, let’s examine the opening measures of
Frédéric Chopin’s Prélude Op. 28, No. 4
. Figure
presents the musical notation alongside a piano-roll visualization that is synchronized with an audio recording of the piece. For clarity, all information pertaining to the note numbered
p=71
are emphasized with red rectangular frames.

Figure 15.2:
Musical score and piano-roll representation. Figure is adapted from
Müller 2015
.

Regarding the original data matrix
\bm{A}
, we utilize the magnitude STFT (see, for example,
López-Serrano et al. 2019
), which consists of a series of spectral vectors. By applying NMF, this matrix can be decomposed into two nonnegative matrices,
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
. Ideally,
\bm{W}
encapsulates the spectral patterns corresponding to the pitches of the notes present in the musical piece, whereas
\bm{Z}
indicates the temporal points at which these patterns appear in the audio recording. Figure
illustrates such a decomposition applied to the Chopin prelude.

Figure 15.3:
Ideal NMF of the spectrogram using NMF. Figure is adapted from
Müller 2015
.

In this scenario, each template represented by the matrix
\bm{W}
corresponds to the spectral manifestation of a specific pitch within
\bm{A}
, and the activation matrix
\bm{Z}
resembles the piano-roll representation of the musical score.
Therefore, the advantages of NMF over general matrix factorization are evident:

•

Nonnegativity constraint.
NMF enforces nonnegativity on both the factorization matrices
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
. This constraint makes the resulting matrices more interpretable because they can be directly related to physical or perceptual quantities in the domain of interest. In the case of music, the nonnegative factors correspond to meaningful musical elements like notes or chords.

•

Interpretability.
In NMF, the matrix
\bm{W}
represents the spectral patterns (timbres) of the notes present in the music piece, and
\bm{Z}
indicates the temporal activations of these patterns. This leads to a more interpretable decomposition compared to unconstrained matrix factorization methods, where the factors might not have a clear physical or musical interpretation.

Figure 15.4:
NMF of the CBCL face database with
K=49
. The basis vectors in
\bm{W}
are reshaped into
19\times 19
images. Facial features can be observed from these reshaped basis vectors, e.g., eyes, noses, nasolabial folds, and lips. Figure is adapted from
Lee and Seung 1999
;
Gillis 2014
.

Facial feature extraction and reconstruction.

Suppose each column of the data matrix
\bm{A}\in\real_{+}^{M\times N}
represents a vectorized grayscale image of a face, where the
(m,n)
-th entry of
\bm{A}
corresponds to the intensity of the
m
-th pixel in the
n
-th face. NMF decomposes
\bm{A}
into two nonnegative matrices
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
such that each image
\bm{a}_{n}
can be approximated by a linear combination of the columns of
\bm{W}
. Since
\bm{W}
is nonnegative, its columns can be interpreted as images, referred to as template images, which are vectors of pixel intensities. The nonnegative weights in
\bm{Z}
ensure that these template images are combined additively to reconstruct each original face image. Given that the number of basis images
K
is typically much smaller than the number of images
N
, the basis images should capture localized and sparse features that are common across multiple images. For facial images, these basis images often represent features such as eyes, noses, nasolabial folds, and lips (see Figure
, NMF for the CBCL face data ), while the columns of
\bm{Z}
indicate the presence of these features in each image
(
Lee and Seung 1999
;
Gillis 2014
)
.

On the other hand, if each column of
\bm{A}
indicates a facial image of a single person, the NMF approach can be utilized for face recognition. NMF has been shown to be more robust to occlusions compared to PCA or ALS, which generates dense factors. For instance, if a new face with occlusions (e.g., wearing sunglasses or distortions) needs to be mapped into the NMF basis, the non-occluded parts (e.g., the lips or the noise) can still be accurately approximated
(
Jain et al. 2017
)
.

Topic recovery.

As mentioned at the very beginning of this chapter, NMF can be effectively applied to topic recovery problems.
Typically, this application involves constructing a term-document matrix
\bm{A}
, where the rows correspond to terms (words or phrases) and the columns correspond to documents. Each entry
a_{mn}
in the matrix represents the weight or importance of term
m
in document
n
.
This weight could be binary (presence/absence),
term frequency (TF)
, or
term frequency-inverse document frequency (TF-IDF)

(
Shahnaz et al. 2006
)
.
In this framework, each column of
\bm{W}
can be interpreted as a topic, while each column of
\bm{Z}
indicates the positive weight of each topic for the given document; NMF in this context aligns with a soft clustering approach where each data point can belong to multiple clusters.
NMF is particularly well-suited for topic recovery because it captures the additive nature of topics in text data and produces interpretable results. However, the choice of the number of topics
K
and the initialization of
\bm{W}
and
\bm{Z}
can significantly affect the quality of the results. Additionally, NMF is sensitive to the scaling of the input data, so it’s important to preprocess the term-document matrix appropriately.

L
-strongly smooth and PGD in Hi-ANLS problems.
A function
f(\bm{x}):\real^{n}\rightarrow\real
is called an
L
-Lipschitz gradient continuous function (a.k.a., a
L
-strongly smooth function) if it satisfies that
\left\lVert\nabla f(\bm{x})-\nabla f(\bm{y})\right\rVert_{2}\leq L\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}
for all
\bm{x},\bm{y}
. Show that the subproblem (
) in Hi-ANLS is
L
-strongly smooth with constant
L=\left\lVert\bm{W}[:,k]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
. Therefore, the subproblem can be treated as a
projected gradient descent (PGD)
update with a step size
\eta=\frac{1}{L}
, i.e., using gradient descent update with a step size
\eta=\frac{1}{L}
first and projecting the update onto the nonnegative orthant afterwards.

Descent lemma for

-strongly smooth functions.

be a function defined over a convex set
{\mathbb{S}}
such that

and

Show that

Hint: Use fundamental theorem of calculus (
\nabla f(\bm{x}+\alpha\bm{d})-\nabla f(\bm{x})=\int_{0}^{\alpha}\nabla^{2}f(\bm{x}+t\bm{d})\bm{d}dt
).

15.20.

AB divergence
(
Amari and Nagaoka 2000
)
.
Let the
\alpha
-
\beta
(AB) divergence
be given as follows:

\displaystyle d_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{\alpha\beta}(x^{\alpha}y^{\beta}-\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta}x^{\alpha+\beta}-\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}y^{\alpha+\beta}),&\alpha,\beta,\alpha+\beta\neq 0;\\
\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}(x^{\alpha}\ln(\frac{x^{\alpha}}{y^{\alpha}})-x^{\alpha}+y^{\alpha}),&\alpha\neq 0,\beta=0;\\
\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}(\ln(\frac{y^{\alpha}}{x^{\alpha}})+(\frac{y^{\alpha}}{x^{\alpha}})^{-1}-1),&\alpha=-\beta\neq 0;\\
\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}(y^{\beta}\ln(\frac{y^{\beta}}{x^{\beta}})-y^{\beta}+x^{\beta}),&\alpha=0,\beta\neq 0;\\
\frac{1}{2}(\ln(x)-\ln(y))^{2},&\alpha=0,\beta=0.\end{cases}

When
\alpha+\beta=1
, it is called the
\alpha
-divergence
. Discuss when it reduces to the
\beta
-divergence.
Show that
d_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)\geq 0
and the equality holds if and only if
x=y
.
