15.20.

Nonnegative algebra.
A bounty of results can be harvested from nonnegative conditions. We investigate several of them in this problem. Given square matrices
\bm{A},\bm{B},\bm{C},\bm{D}\in\real^{n\times n}
, show that

•

Triangle inequality.

\left\lvert\bm{A}\bm{B}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\bm{A}\right\rvert\left\lvert\bm{B}\right\rvert
.

•

Nonexpansiveness.

\left\lvert\bm{A}^{k}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\bm{A}\right\rvert^{k}
, for all
m=\{1,2,\ldots\}
.

•

Equal norm.

\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}=\left\lVert\left\lvert\bm{A}\right\rvert\right\rVert_{F}
.

•

If
\left\lvert\bm{B}\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\bm{A}\right\rvert
, then
\left\lVert\bm{B}\right\rVert_{F}\geq\left\lVert\bm{A}\right\rVert_{F}
.

•

If
\bm{B}\geq\bm{A}\geq\mathbf{0}
and
\bm{D}\geq\bm{C}\geq\mathbf{0}
, then
\bm{B}\bm{D}\geq\bm{A}\bm{C}\geq\mathbf{0}
.

•

If
\bm{B}\geq\bm{A}\geq\mathbf{0}
, then
\bm{B}^{k}\geq\bm{A}^{k}\geq\mathbf{0}
, for all
m=\{1,2,\ldots\}
,

where
\bm{B}\geq\bm{A}
indicates that
\bm{B}-\bm{A}
is a nonnegative matrix.
Given rectangular matrices
\bm{A},\bm{B}\in\real^{m\times n}
, show that

•

\left\lvert\bm{A}+\bm{B}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\bm{A}\right\rvert+\left\lvert\bm{B}\right\rvert
.

15.20.

Let
\bm{A}\in\real_{+}^{n\times n}
be nonnegative. Show that

\displaystyle\text{Row sum: }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathop{\min}_{1\leq i\leq n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{ij}

\displaystyle\leq\rho(\bm{A})\leq\mathop{\max}_{1\leq i\leq n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{ij};

\displaystyle\text{Column sum: }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathop{\min}_{1\leq j\leq n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{ij}

\displaystyle\leq\rho(\bm{A})\leq\mathop{\max}_{1\leq j\leq n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{ij}.

Chapter 16
Biconjugate Decomposition

16.1
Existence of the Biconjugate Decomposition

The concept of biconjugate decomposition was introduced by
Chu et al. 1995
, while the rank-diminishing operator, on the other hand, has roots in the work of
Egerváry 1960
;
Householder 1964
;
Stewart 1973
.
A variety of matrix decomposition methods can be unified through this biconjugate decomposition.
In Section
, biconjugate decomposition is put into perspective by providing connections with standard decompositional methods, namely LDU, Cholesky, QR, and SVD decompositions.
The existence of the biconjugate decomposition is supported by the rank-one reduction theorem, as presented below.

Theorem 141
((Wedderburn’s) Rank-One Reduction).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be an
m\times n
matrix with rank
r
, and let
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
and
\bm{y}\in\real^{m}
be a pair of vectors such that
w=\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}\neq 0
. Then the matrix

\bm{B}=\bm{A}-w^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{x}\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{A}

(16.1)

has a rank of
r-1
, which is exactly one less than the rank of
\bm{A}
:
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{B})=\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})-1
.
A generalization of the rank-one reduction is discussed in Problem
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
To prove the theorem, it suffices to show that the dimension of the null space of
\bm{B}
is one greater than that of
\bm{A}
, indicating that
\bm{B}
has a rank exactly one less than the rank of
\bm{A}
.

For any vector
\bm{n}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
, i.e.,
\bm{A}\bm{n}=\mathbf{0}
, we have
\bm{B}\bm{n}=\bm{A}\bm{n}-w^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{x}\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{n}=\mathbf{0}
, implying that
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})\subseteq\mathcal{N}(\bm{B})
.

Now, consider any vector
\bm{m}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{B})
, i.e.,
\bm{B}\bm{m}=\mathbf{0}
. We have
\bm{B}\bm{m}=\bm{A}\bm{m}-w^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{x}\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{m}=\mathbf{0}
.

Let
k=w^{-1}\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{m}
, which is a scalar.
Therefore,
\bm{B}\bm{m}=\bm{A}(\bm{m}-k\bm{x})=\mathbf{0}
, i.e., for any vector
\bm{n}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
, we could find a vector
\bm{m}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{B})
such that
\bm{n}=(\bm{m}-k\bm{x})\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
.
Note that
\bm{A}\bm{x}\neq\mathbf{0}
based on the definition of
w
. Thus, the null space of
\bm{B}
is therefore obtained from the null space of
\bm{A}
by adding
\bm{x}
to its basis, which will increase the order of the space by one.
Consequently, the dimension of
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A})
is smaller than the dimension of
\mathcal{N}(\bm{B})
by one, which completes the proof.

The converse of the above theorem is also true, as shown in the following corollary.

Corollary 142
(Rank-One Reduction,
(
Egerváry 1960
)
).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any
m\times n
matrix, and let
\bm{u}\in\real^{m}
and
\bm{v}\in\real^{n}
be two vectors.
Then, the rank of matrix
\bm{B}=\bm{A}-\sigma^{-1}\bm{u}\bm{v}^{\top}
is less than that of
\bm{A}
if and only if there exist vectors
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
and
\bm{y}\in\real^{m}
such that
\bm{u}=\bm{A}\bm{x},\bm{v}=\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{y}
, and
\sigma=\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}\neq 0
. In this case, it holds that
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{B})=\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})-1
.

More generally, the rank-one reduction can be extended to the case where a matrix of rank possibly greater than one is subtracted.

Corollary 143
(Rank-
k
Reduction,
(
Cline and Funderlic 1979
)
).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any
m\times n
matrix. Let further
\bm{P}\in\real^{m\times k}
,
\bm{U}\in\real^{k\times k}
be nonsingular, and
\bm{Q}\in\real^{n\times k}
. Then,

\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}-\bm{P}\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{Q}^{\top})=\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})-\mathrm{rank}(\bm{P}\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{Q}^{\top})

if and only if there exist
\bm{X}\in\real^{n\times k}
and
\bm{Y}\in\real^{m\times k}
such that

\bm{P}=\bm{A}\bm{X},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{Q}=\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{Y},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{U}=\bm{Y}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}.

Suppose a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
has rank
r
. We can define a rank-reducing process to generate a sequence of
Wedderburn matrices

\{\bm{A}_{k}\}
:

\bm{A}_{1}=\bm{A}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{A}_{k+1}=\bm{A}_{k}-w_{k}^{-1}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k},\quad\forall\,k\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\},

(16.2)

where
\bm{x}_{k}\in\real^{n}
and
\bm{y}_{k}\in\real^{m}
are any vectors satisfying
w_{k}=\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{k}\neq 0
.
The operator in Equation (
) is known as a
rank-diminishing operator
, and the process described by Equation (
) is referred to the
rank-reducing process
.
And the sets
\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{r}\}
and
\{\bm{y}_{1},\bm{y}_{2},\ldots,\bm{y}_{r}\}
are called the
vectors associated with the rank-reducing process
. Alternatively, if we let
\bm{X}=[\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{r}]
and
\bm{Y}=[\bm{y}_{1},\bm{y}_{2},\ldots,\bm{y}_{r}]
, then (
\bm{X},\bm{Y}
) is said to
effect a rank-reducing process
for
\bm{A}
.
The sequence will terminate after
r
steps since the rank of
\bm{A}_{k}
decreases by exactly one at each step.
The sequence can be written out as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bm{A},

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{1}-\bm{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=w_{1}^{-1}\bm{A}_{1}\bm{x}_{1}\bm{y}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}_{1},

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{2}-\bm{A}_{3}

\displaystyle=w_{2}^{-1}\bm{A}_{2}\bm{x}_{2}\bm{y}_{2}^{\top}\bm{A}_{2},

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{3}-\bm{A}_{4}

\displaystyle=w_{3}^{-1}\bm{A}_{3}\bm{x}_{3}\bm{y}_{3}^{\top}\bm{A}_{3},

\displaystyle=\vdots

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{r-1}-\bm{A}_{r}

\displaystyle=w_{r-1}^{-1}\bm{A}_{r-1}\bm{x}_{r-1}\bm{y}_{r-1}^{\top}\bm{A}_{r-1},

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{r}-\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle=w_{r}^{-1}\bm{A}_{r}\bm{x}_{r}\bm{y}_{r}^{\top}\bm{A}_{r}.

By summing up the sequence, we obtain

\displaystyle\textbf{(Rank-reducing)}:

\displaystyle(\bm{A}_{1}-\bm{A}_{2})+(\bm{A}_{2}-\bm{A}_{3})+\ldots+(\bm{A}_{r-1}-\bm{A}_{r})+(\bm{A}_{r}-\mathbf{0})

(16.3)

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{1}=\bm{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}_{i}\bm{x}_{i}\bm{y}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}_{i}.

Therefore, we can derive the following decomposition directly from this rank-reducing process.

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
any matrix with rank
r
.
This equality (
), which results from the rank-reducing process, implies the following matrix decomposition

\bm{A}=\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{\top},

where
\boldsymbol{\Omega}=\mathrm{diag}(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{r})
,
\boldsymbol{\Phi}=[\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\phi}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\phi}_{r}]\in\real^{m\times r}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}=[\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{r}]
with

\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}=\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}=\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{y}_{k},\qquad\forall\,k\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}.

Thus, different choices of
\bm{x}_{k}
’s and
\bm{y}_{k}
’s will result in different biconjugate factorizations, making this factorization quite general and versatile. In the following sections, we will demonstrate its connection with some well-known decomposition methods.

Remark 144
.

Regarding the vectors
\bm{x}_{k}
and
\bm{y}_{k}
in the Wedderburn sequence, the following property holds:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{k}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}_{k+1})\quad\implies\quad\bm{x}_{k}\bot\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}_{k+1}^{\top}),

\displaystyle\bm{y}_{k}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}_{k+1}^{\top})\quad\implies\quad\bm{y}_{k}\bot\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}_{k+1}).

To see why, consider the following:

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{k+1}\bm{x}_{k}

\displaystyle=(\bm{A}_{k}-w_{k}^{-1}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k})\bm{x}_{k}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{k}(\bm{x}_{k}-\frac{\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}}{\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}}\bm{x}_{k})=\mathbf{0}.

Similarly, it can be shown that
\bm{A}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{y}_{k}=\mathbf{0}
.

Lemma 145
(General Term Formula of Wedderburn Sequence: V1).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any matrix with rank
r
, and let
\bm{A}_{1}=\bm{A}
.
For each matrix in the sequence defined by
\bm{A}_{k+1}=\bm{A}_{k}-w_{k}^{-1}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}
(
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,r-1\}
), the matrix
\bm{A}_{k+1}
can be expressed as

\bm{A}_{k+1}=\bm{A}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A},\qquad\forall\,k\in\{1,2,\ldots,r-1\},

where

\bm{u}_{k}=\bm{x}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{k}}{w_{i}}\bm{u}_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{v}_{k}=\bm{y}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}}{w_{i}}\bm{v}_{i}.

Let
\bm{X}=[\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{r}]
,
\bm{Y}=[\bm{y}_{1},\bm{y}_{2},\ldots,\bm{y}_{r}]
,
\bm{U}=[\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{r}]
, and
\bm{V}=[\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{r}]
be the column partitions for each set of vectors.
Then, the rank-reducing process can be viewed as transforming the matrix pair
(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
into
(\bm{U},\bm{V})
.

The proof of this lemma is deferred to Section
. We notice that
w_{i}=\bm{y}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}_{i}\bm{x}_{i}
in the general term formula is related to
\bm{A}_{i}
, which means it is not the true general term formula.
We will later reformulate
w_{i}
to be related to
\bm{A}
rather than
\bm{A}_{i}
.
From the general term formula of the Wedderburn sequence, we have:

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A},

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{k}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}.

Thus, we obtain
\bm{A}_{k+1}-\bm{A}_{k}=-w_{k}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{k}\bm{v}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}
.
Since the sequence is defined as
\bm{A}_{k+1}=\bm{A}_{k}-w_{k}^{-1}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}
, we can deduce that
w_{k}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{k}\bm{v}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}=w_{k}^{-1}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}
.
Consequently, it follows that

\displaystyle\bm{A}\bm{u}_{k}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k},

(16.4)

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}.

Let
z_{k,i}=\frac{\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{k}}{w_{i}}
, which is a scalar. Referring to the definitions of
\bm{u}_{k}
and
\bm{v}_{k}
in the lemma above, we can express them explicitly as follows:

•

\bm{u}_{1}=\bm{x}_{1}
;

•

\bm{u}_{2}=\bm{x}_{2}-z_{2,1}\bm{u}_{1}\implies

\bm{x}_{2}
is a linear combination of
\bm{u}_{1}
and
\bm{u}_{2}
;

•

\bm{u}_{3}=\bm{x}_{3}-z_{3,1}\bm{u}_{1}-z_{3,2}\bm{u}_{2}\implies

\bm{x}_{3}
is a linear combination of
\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}
, and
\bm{u}_{3}
;

•

\ldots
.

Each
z_{k,i}
(
i<k
) encodes the component of
\bm{x}_{k}
in that of
\bm{u}_{i}
.
This process bears resemblance to the Gram-Schmidt process (Section
).
However, in this process, we do not perform an
orthogonal projection
of
\bm{x}_{2}
onto
\bm{x}_{1}
to find the vector component of
\bm{x}_{2}
along
\bm{x}_{1}
, as we would do in an orthogonal projection (Section
).
Instead, the vector of
\bm{x}_{2}
along
\bm{x}_{1}
is now defined by
z_{2,1}
(i.e., an
oblique projection
; Section
).
This process is illustrated in Figure
.
In Figure
,
\bm{u}_{2}
is not perpendicular to
\bm{u}_{1}
(in the Gram-Schmidt process,
\bm{u}_{2}
would be perpendicular to
\bm{u}_{1}
via orthogonal projections).
Nevertheless,
\bm{u}_{2}
does not lie on the same line as
\bm{u}_{1}
, so
\{\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\}
can still span a
2
subspace. Similarly, in Figure
,
\bm{u}_{3}=\bm{x}_{3}-z_{3,1}\bm{u}_{1}-z_{3,2}\bm{u}_{2}
does not lie in the subspace spanned by
\{\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\}
, allowing
\{\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\bm{u}_{3}\}
to span a
3
subspace.

A moment of reflexion would reveal that the span of
\{\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1}\}
is the same as the span of
\{\bm{u}_{2},\bm{u}_{1}\}
. This equivalence extends to the
\bm{v}_{i}
vectors and
\bm{y}_{i}
vectors as well.
We can express this property as follows:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathrm{span}\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{j}\}&=\mathrm{span}\{\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{j}\},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,j\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\};\\
\mathrm{span}\{\bm{y}_{1},\bm{y}_{2},\ldots,\bm{y}_{j}\}&=\mathrm{span}\{\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},\ldots,\bm{v}_{j}\},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,j\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(16.5)

(a)

“Project” onto a line.

(b)

“Project” onto a space.

Figure 16.1:
“Project” a vector onto a line and onto a space. Compare to the Gram-Schmidt process in Figure
.

Furthermore, based on the rank-reducing property in the Wedderburn sequence, we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{C}(\bm{A}_{1})&\supset\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}_{2})\supset\mathcal{C}(\bm{A}_{3})\supset\ldots;\\
\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}_{1}^{\top})&\subset\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}_{2}^{\top})\subset\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}_{3}^{\top})\subset\ldots.\end{aligned}\right.

Since
\bm{y}_{k}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}_{k+1}^{\top})
, it then follows that
\bm{y}_{j}\in\mathcal{N}(\bm{A}_{k+1}^{\top})
for all
j<k+1
, i.e.,
\bm{A}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{y}_{j}=\mathbf{0}
for all
j<k+1
.
This also holds true for
\bm{x}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{y}_{j}=0
for all
j<k+1
. From Equation (
), we also have
\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{y}_{j}=0
for all
j<k+1
. Following Equation (
), we obtain

\bm{x}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{y}_{j}=0\,\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equation:wedderburn-au-akxk}}}{{\implies}}\,\,\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{y}_{j}=0\,\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equation:wedderburn-span-same}}}{{\implies}}\,\,\bm{v}_{j}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{k+1}=0\quad\text{ for all }j<k+1.

(16.6)

Similarly, we can prove

\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{j}=0\quad\text{ for all }j<k+1.

(16.7)

Moreover, since
w_{k}=\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}
, according to Equation (
), we can express
w_{k}
as:

\displaystyle w_{k}

\displaystyle=\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}=\bm{v}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{k}

\displaystyle=\bm{v}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}(\bm{u}_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{k}}{w_{i}}\bm{u}_{i})\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{by the definition of }\bm{u}_{k}\text{ in Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lemma:wedderburn-sequence-general}})

\displaystyle=\bm{v}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{k},\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{by }\bm{v}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{j}=0\text{ for all }j<k)

which can be utilized to substitute the value of
w_{k}
in Lemma
. We then have the full version of the general term formula of the Wedderburn sequence.
In this form, the formula no longer depends on
\bm{A}_{k}
’s (in the form of
w_{k}
’s):

\bm{u}_{k}=\bm{x}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{k}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}}}\bm{u}_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{v}_{k}=\bm{y}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}}}\bm{v}_{i}.

(16.8)

Gram-Schmidt process from Wedderburn sequence.

Suppose the matrices
\bm{X}=[\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{r}]\in\real^{n\times r}
and
\bm{Y}=[\bm{y}_{1},\bm{y}_{2},\ldots,\bm{y}_{r}]\in\real^{n\times r}
effect a rank-reducing process for
\bm{A}
.
If
\bm{A}=\bm{I}\in\real^{n\times n}
is the identity matrix and
(\bm{X}=\bm{Y})
are identical, containing the vectors for which an orthogonal basis is desired, i.e., we aim to obtain an orthogonal basis spanning the same column space of
\bm{X}=\bm{Y}
, then the result of the rank-reducing process,
(\bm{U}=\bm{V})
, gives the resultant orthogonal basis (but not an orthonormal basis).

To see this, we follow the computation of the Gram-Schmidt process as given in Equation (
), where we replace the data matrix by
\bm{X}=\bm{Y}\in\real^{m\times n}
(assuming
\bm{X}
has full rank for simplicity), and we use matrix
\bm{Q}=[\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\ldots,\bm{q}_{n}]
to denote the orthonormal basis.
Then, for any
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, the Gram-Schmidt process admits

\text{Gram-Schmidt process}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \bm{x}_{k}^{\perp}&=\left(\bm{I}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\bm{q}_{i}\bm{q}_{i}^{\top}\right)\bm{x}_{k};\\
\bm{q}_{k}&=\frac{\bm{x}_{k}^{\perp}}{\left\lVert\bm{x}_{k}^{\perp}\right\rVert}.\end{aligned}\right.

(16.9)

Considering the rank-reducing process with
\bm{A}=\bm{I}
,
\bm{X}=\bm{Y}
. Since
\bm{A}
is symmetric and
\bm{X}=\bm{Y}
, we obtain
\bm{U}=\bm{V}
. We can thus focus on the analysis of
\bm{U}
. For any
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, the rank-reducing process yields

\text{Rank-reducing process}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \bm{u}_{k}&=\bm{x}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{k}}{\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}}\bm{u}_{i}=\left(\bm{I}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\bm{u}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}}{\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\bm{u}_{i}}\bm{u}_{i}\right)\bm{x}_{k};\\
\widetilde{\bm{u}}_{k}&=\frac{\bm{u}_{k}}{\left\lVert\bm{u}_{k}\right\rVert}.\end{aligned}\right.

(16.10)

Therefore, by comparing Equations (
) and (
), we can find the equivalence between
\bm{q}_{k}
and
\widetilde{\bm{u}}_{k}
for
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. Thus, the Wedderburn sequence effects a Gram-Schmidt process when
\bm{X}=\bm{Y}
is the data matrix and
\bm{A}=\bm{I}
.

The expressions for
\bm{u}_{k}
and
\bm{v}_{k}
in Equation (
) closely resemble the projection onto the perpendicular space in the Gram-Schmidt process, as shown in Equation (
). We then define the bilinear form
<\bm{x},\bm{y}>=\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}
to explicitly mimic the form of projection in Equation (
).

We consolidate the results obtained so far into the following lemma, which provides a concise overview of what we have been working on. These results will be extensively utilized in the sequel.

Lemma 146
(Properties of Wedderburn Sequence).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any matrix with rank
r
, and let
\bm{A}_{1}=\bm{A}
.
For each matrix in the sequence defined by
\bm{A}_{k+1}=\bm{A}_{k}-w_{k}^{-1}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}
(
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,r-1\}
), the matrix
\bm{A}_{k+1}
can be expressed as

\bm{A}_{k+1}=\bm{A}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A},

where

\bm{u}_{k}=\bm{x}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}<\bm{x}_{k},\bm{v}_{i}>}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}<\bm{u}_{i},\bm{v}_{i}>}}\bm{u}_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{v}_{k}=\bm{y}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}<\bm{u}_{i},\bm{y}_{k}>}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}<\bm{u}_{i},\bm{v}_{i}>}}\bm{v}_{i}.

(16.11)

Furthermore, we can observe the following properties:

\displaystyle\bm{A}\bm{u}_{k}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k};

(16.12)

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k};

<\bm{u}_{k},\bm{v}_{j}>=<\bm{u}_{j},\bm{v}_{k}>=0\text{ for all }j<k;

(16.13)

w_{k}=\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}=<\bm{u}_{k},\bm{v}_{k}>.

(16.14)

By substituting Equation (
) into Form 1 of the biconjugate decomposition and using Equation (
), which implies
w_{k}=\bm{v}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{k}
, we obtain the Form 2 and Form 3 of this decomposition:

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any matrix with rank
r
.
This equality (
), which results from the rank-reducing process, implies the following matrix decomposition:

\bm{A}=\bm{A}\bm{U}_{r}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{r}^{-1}\bm{V}_{r}^{\top}\bm{A},

(16.15)

where
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{r}=\mathrm{diag}(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{r})
,
\bm{U}_{r}=[\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{r}]\in\real^{n\times r}
, and
\bm{V}_{r}=[\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},

\ldots,

\bm{v}_{r}]\in\real^{m\times r}
with

\bm{u}_{k}=\bm{x}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{<\bm{x}_{k},\bm{v}_{i}>}{<\bm{u}_{i},\bm{v}_{i}>}\bm{u}_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{v}_{k}=\bm{y}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{<\bm{u}_{i},\bm{y}_{k}>}{<\bm{u}_{i},\bm{v}_{i}>}\bm{v}_{i}.

(16.16)

Additionally, the following decomposition holds:

\bm{V}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{U}_{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\gamma},

(16.17)

where
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{diag}(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{\gamma})
,
\bm{U}_{\gamma}=[\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2},\ldots,\bm{u}_{\gamma}]\in\real^{n\times\gamma}
, and
\bm{V}_{\gamma}=[\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2},

\ldots,

\bm{v}_{\gamma}]\in\real^{m\times\gamma}
. Note the difference between the subscripts
r
and
\gamma
employed here, with
\gamma\leq r
.

Note that Equation (
) is derived from (
),
and Equation (
) comes from (
).
We observe that, in the above two forms of the biconjugate decomposition, they no longer depend on the Wedderburn matrices
\{\bm{A}_{k}\}
.

A word on the notation.

We will employ subscripts to indicate the dimension of the matrix to prevent confusion in the following discussion, e.g., the
r,\gamma
notations used in the preceding theorem.

16.2
Properties of the Biconjugate Decomposition

Corollary 147
(Connection of
\bm{U}_{\gamma}
and
\bm{X}_{\gamma}
).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any matrix with rank
r\geq\gamma
.
If
(\bm{X}_{\gamma},\bm{Y}_{\gamma})\in\real^{n\times\gamma}\times\real^{m\times\gamma}
effects a rank-reducing process for
\bm{A}
, then there exist unique unit upper triangular matrices
\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}\in\real^{\gamma\times\gamma}
and
\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)}\in\real^{\gamma\times\gamma}
such that

\bm{X}_{\gamma}=\bm{U}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{Y}_{\gamma}=\bm{V}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)},

where
\bm{U}_{\gamma}
and
\bm{V}_{\gamma}
are matrices with columns derived from the Wedderburn sequence, as described in Equation (
).

Proof
[of Corollary
]
The proof follows directly from the definitions of
\bm{u}_{k}
and
\bm{v}_{k}
in Equation (
) or Equation (
). We set the
j
-th column of
\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}
and
\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)}
as follows:

\left[\frac{<\bm{x}_{j},\bm{v}_{1}>}{<\bm{u}_{1},\bm{v}_{1}>},\frac{<\bm{x}_{j},\bm{v}_{2}>}{<\bm{u}_{2},\bm{v}_{2}>},\ldots,\frac{<\bm{x}_{j},\bm{v}_{j-1}>}{<\bm{u}_{j-1},\bm{v}_{j-1}>},1,0,0,\ldots,0\right]^{\top},

and

\left[\frac{<\bm{u}_{1},\bm{y}_{j}>}{<\bm{u}_{1},\bm{v}_{1}>},\frac{<\bm{u}_{2},\bm{y}_{j}>}{<\bm{u}_{2},\bm{v}_{2}>},\ldots,\frac{<\bm{u}_{j-1},\bm{y}_{j}>}{<\bm{u}_{j-1},\bm{v}_{j-1}>},1,0,0,\ldots,0\right]^{\top}.

And the uniqueness stems from the fact that the matrices
\bm{U}_{\gamma}
and
\bm{V}_{\gamma}
have independent columns from the rank-reducing process.
This completes the proof.

The pair
(\bm{U}_{\gamma},\bm{V}_{\gamma})\in\real^{m\times\gamma}\times\real^{n\times\gamma}
in Theorem
is called a
biconjugate pair
with respect to
\bm{A}
if
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\gamma}
is nonsingular and diagonal.
Furthermore, suppose the pair
(\bm{X}_{\gamma},\bm{Y}_{\gamma})\in\real^{n\times\gamma}\times\real^{m\times\gamma}
effects a rank-reducing process for
\bm{A}
.
Then, the pair
(\bm{X}_{\gamma},\bm{Y}_{\gamma})
is said to be
biconjugatable
and can be
biconjugated into a biconjugate pair
of matrices
(\bm{U}_{\gamma},\bm{V}_{\gamma})
, if there exist unit upper triangular matrices
\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}
and
\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)}
such that
\bm{X}_{\gamma}=\bm{U}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}
and
\bm{Y}_{\gamma}=\bm{V}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)}
.

16.3
Connection to Well-Known Decomposition Methods

In this section, we demonstrate how biconjugate decomposition can be related to well-known decompositional methods.

16.3.1
LDU Decomposition

Theorem 148
(LDU,
Chu et al. 1995
Theorem 2.4).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any matrix with rank
r\geq\gamma
.
Let further
(\bm{X}_{\gamma},\bm{Y}_{\gamma})\in\real^{n\times\gamma}\times\real^{m\times\gamma}

with
\gamma
in
\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
. Then, the pair
(\bm{X}_{\gamma},\bm{Y}_{\gamma})
can be biconjugated if and only if
\bm{Y}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{\gamma}
has an LDU decomposition.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Suppose
\bm{X}_{\gamma}
and
\bm{Y}_{\gamma}
are biconjugatable; then there exist unit upper triangular matrices
\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}
and
\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)}
such that
\bm{X}_{\gamma}=\bm{U}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}
,
\bm{Y}_{\gamma}=\bm{V}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)}
, and
\bm{V}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{U}_{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\gamma}
is a nonsingular diagonal matrix. Then it follows that

\bm{Y}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{\gamma}=\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)\top}\bm{V}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{U}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}=\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}

is the unique LDU decomposition of
\bm{Y}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{\gamma}
. This expression can be regarded as the
fourth form of biconjugate decomposition
.

Conversely, suppose
\bm{Y}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{\gamma}=\bm{R}_{2}^{\top}\bm{D}\bm{R}_{1}
is an LDU decomposition, with both
\bm{R}_{1}
and
\bm{R}_{2}
being unit upper triangular matrices. Since
\bm{R}_{1}^{-1}
and
\bm{R}_{2}^{-1}
are also unit upper triangular matrices,
(\bm{X}_{\gamma},\bm{Y}_{\gamma})
biconjugates into
(\bm{X}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{1}^{-1},\bm{Y}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{2}^{-1})
.

Corollary 149
(Determinant).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any matrix with rank
r\geq\gamma
.
Suppose the pair
(\bm{X}_{\gamma},\bm{Y}_{\gamma})\in\real^{n\times\gamma}\times\real^{m\times\gamma}
can be biconjugated into
(\bm{U}_{\gamma},\bm{V}_{\gamma})
such that
\bm{V}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{U}_{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{diag}(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{\gamma})
is a nonsingular diagonal matrix.
Then it follows that

\det(\bm{Y}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{\gamma})=\prod_{i=1}^{\gamma}w_{i}.

Proof
[of Corollary
]
By Theorem
, since
(\bm{X}_{\gamma},\bm{Y}_{\gamma})
are biconjugatable, then there exist unit upper triangular matrices
\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}
and
\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)}
such that
\bm{Y}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{\gamma}=\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}
. The determinant is simply the product of the diagonal elements.

Lemma 150
(Biconjugatable in Principal Minors).

Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
be any matrix with rank
r\geq\gamma
.
In the Wedderburn sequence, we choose
\bm{x}_{i}
as the
i
-th standard basis in
n
for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,\gamma\}
(i.e.,
\bm{x}_{i}=\bm{e}_{i}\in\real^{n}
), and
\bm{y}_{i}
as the
i
-th standard basis in
m
for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,\gamma\}
(i.e.,
\bm{y}_{i}=\bm{e}_{i}\in\real^{m}
).
That is,
\bm{Y}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{\gamma}
corresponds to the leading principal submatrix of
\bm{A}
, i.e.,
\bm{Y}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{\gamma}=\bm{A}[1:\gamma,1:\gamma]
.
Then,
(\bm{X}_{\gamma},\bm{Y}_{\gamma})
is biconjugatable into
(\bm{U}_{\gamma},\bm{V}_{\gamma})
such that
\bm{V}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{U}_{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{diag}(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{\gamma})
is a nonsingular diagonal matrix
if and only if the
\gamma
-th leading principal minor of
\bm{A}
is nonzero:
\det(\bm{A}[1:\gamma,1:\gamma])\neq 0
. In this case, the
\gamma
-th leading principal minor of
\bm{A}
is given by
\prod_{i=1}^{\gamma}w_{i}
.

Proof
[of Lemma
]
The proof is straightforward that the
\gamma
-th leading principal minor of
\bm{A}
being nonzero will imply that
w_{i}\neq 0
for all
i\leq\gamma
. Thus, the Wedderburn sequence can be successfully obtained. The converse holds because Corollary
implies that
\det(\bm{Y}_{\gamma}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{\gamma})
is nonzero.

We have now arrived at the LDU decomposition for square matrices.

Theorem 151
(LDU: Biconjugate Decomposition for Square Matrices).

For any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, the pair
(\bm{I}_{n},\bm{I}_{n})
is biconjugatable if and only if all the leading principal minors of
\bm{A}
are nonzero. In this case,
\bm{A}
can be factored as

\bm{A}=\bm{V}_{n}^{-\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}\bm{U}_{n}^{-1}=\bm{L}\bm{D}\bm{U},

where
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}=\bm{D}
is a diagonal matrix with nonzero values along its diagonal,
\bm{V}_{n}^{-\top}=\bm{L}
is a unit lower triangular matrix, and
\bm{U}_{n}^{-1}=\bm{U}
is a unit upper triangular matrix.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
As per Lemma
, it is evident that the pair
(\bm{I}_{n},\bm{I}_{n})
is biconjugatable.
Based on Corollary
, we have
\bm{U}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}=\bm{I}_{n}
and
\bm{I}_{n}=\bm{V}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)}
. Thus,
\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}=\bm{U}_{n}^{-1}
and
\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)}=\bm{V}_{n}^{-1}
are well defined. This completes the proof.

16.3.2
Cholesky Decomposition

For symmetric and positive definite matrices, the leading principal minors are always positive.
The proof for this statement can be found in Section
.
The following theorem describes the biconjugate decomposition for positive definite matrices.

Theorem 152
(Cholesky: Biconjugate Decomposition for PD Matrices).

For any symmetric and positive definite matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, the Cholesky decomposition of
\bm{A}
can be derived from the Wedderburn sequence by setting
(\bm{X}_{n},\bm{Y}_{n})
as the pair
(\bm{I}_{n},\bm{I}_{n})
.
In this case,
\bm{A}
can be factored as

\bm{A}=\bm{U}_{n}^{-\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}\bm{U}_{n}^{-1}=(\bm{U}_{n}^{-\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2})(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}\bm{U}_{n}^{-1})=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R},

where
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}
is a diagonal matrix with positive values along the diagonal, and
\bm{U}_{n}^{-1}
is a unit upper triangular matrix.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Given that the leading principal minors of positive definite matrices are positive,
w_{i}>0
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.
It follows from the LDU factorization via biconjugation and the symmetry of
\bm{A}
that
\bm{A}=\bm{U}_{n}^{-\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}\bm{U}_{n}^{-1}
.
Since
w_{i}
’s are positive,
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}
is positive definite and can be factored as
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}
. This implies that
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}\bm{U}_{n}^{-1}
is the Cholesky factor.

16.3.3
QR Decomposition

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
has full rank, which allows for the QR decomposition:
\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R}
, where
\bm{Q}\in\real^{n\times n}
is orthogonal, and
\bm{R}\in\real^{n\times n}
is upper triangular with full rank and positive diagonal values. We then have the following result.

Theorem 153
(QR: Biconjugate Decomposition for Nonsingular Matrices).

For any nonsingular matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, the QR decomposition of
\bm{A}
can be obtained from the Wedderburn sequence by setting
(\bm{X}_{n},\bm{Y}_{n})
as
(\bm{I}_{n},\bm{A})
.
Thus,
\bm{A}
can be factored as

\bm{A}=\bm{Q}\bm{R},

where
\bm{Q}=\bm{V}_{n}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{-1/2}
is an orthogonal matrix and
\bm{R}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}
is an upper triangular matrix, according to the
Form 4
in Theorem
, with
\gamma=n
:

\bm{Y}_{n}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{n}=\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)\top}\bm{V}_{n}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{U}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}=\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)},

where we set
\gamma=n
because
\gamma
is any value such that
\gamma\leq r
and the rank
r=n
.

Proof
[of Theorem
]
Since
(\bm{X}_{n},\bm{Y}_{n})=(\bm{I}_{n},\bm{A})
, applying Theorem
, we have the decomposition

\bm{Y}_{n}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{n}=\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)\top}\bm{V}_{n}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{U}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}=\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}.

Substituting
(\bm{X}_{n},\bm{Y}_{n})=(\bm{I}_{n},\bm{A})
into the decomposition above, we obtain:

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{n}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{n}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)\top}\bm{V}_{n}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{U}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}=\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)};

(16.18)

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)};

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}\bm{R}_{1};\qquad(\text{$\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}$ is symmetric and let $\bm{R}_{1}=\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}=\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)}$})

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}

\displaystyle=(\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2\top})(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}\bm{R}_{1});

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad(\text{let $\bm{R}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}\bm{R}_{1}$})

To see why
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}
can be factored as
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}
, we consider the following steps.
Suppose
\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}]
is the column partition of
\bm{A}
. We obtain
w_{i}=\bm{y}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{i}=\bm{a}_{i}^{\top}\bm{a}_{i}>0
since
\bm{A}
is nonsingular. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}=\mathrm{diag}(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{n})
is positive definite and it can be factored as

\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}.

(16.19)

By
\bm{X}_{\gamma}=\bm{U}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(x)}
in Theorem
for all
\gamma\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, we have

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{n}

\displaystyle=\bm{U}_{n}\bm{R}_{1};

\displaystyle\bm{I}_{n}

\displaystyle=\bm{U}_{n}\bm{R}_{1};\qquad(\text{Since $\bm{X}_{n}=\bm{I}_{n}$})

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{n}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{1}^{-1}.

By
\bm{Y}_{\gamma}=\bm{V}_{\gamma}\bm{R}_{\gamma}^{(y)}
in Theorem
for all
\gamma\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, we have

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{n}

\displaystyle=\bm{V}_{n}\bm{R}_{1};

(16.20)

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{V}_{n}\bm{R}_{1};\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{$\bm{A}=\bm{Y}_{n}$})

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\bm{V}_{n}^{\top}\bm{V}_{n}\bm{R}_{1};

\displaystyle\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}\bm{R}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\bm{V}_{n}^{\top}\bm{V}_{n}\bm{R}_{1};\qquad

\displaystyle(\text{Equation\penalty\ \eqref{equation:biconjugate-qr-ata1}})

\displaystyle(\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2\top})(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}\bm{R}_{1})

\displaystyle=(\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{-1/2\top})\bm{V}_{n}^{\top}\bm{V}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{1/2}\bm{R}_{1});\qquad

\displaystyle\text{(Equation\penalty\ \eqref{equation:omega-half-qr})}

\displaystyle\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{-1/2\top}\bm{V}_{n}^{\top})(\bm{V}_{n}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{-1/2})\bm{R}.

Thus,
\bm{Q}=\bm{V}_{n}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}^{-1/2}
is an orthogonal matrix.

16.3.4
SVD

To explore the SVD of a square matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
within the biconjugation decomposition, we introduce the following notation: let
\bm{A}=\bm{U}^{\mathrm{svd}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{svd}}\bm{V}^{\mathrm{svd}\top}
be the SVD of
\bm{A}
, where
\bm{U}^{\mathrm{svd}}=[\bm{u}_{1}^{\mathrm{svd}},\bm{u}_{2}^{\mathrm{svd}},\ldots,\bm{u}_{n}^{\mathrm{svd}}]
is orthogonal,
\bm{V}^{\mathrm{svd}}=[\bm{v}_{1}^{\mathrm{svd}},\bm{v}_{2}^{\mathrm{svd}},\ldots,\bm{v}_{n}^{\mathrm{svd}}]
is orthogonal, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{svd}}=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{n})
is diagonal. Without loss of generality, we assume
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
and
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})=n
. Readers can verify the equivalence for a general matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
.

If the pair (
\bm{X}_{n}=\bm{V}^{\mathrm{svd}}
,
\bm{Y}_{n}=\bm{U}^{\mathrm{svd}}
) effects a rank-reducing process for
\bm{A}
.
From the definitions of
\bm{u}_{k}
and
\bm{v}_{k}
in Equation (
) or Equation (
), we have

\bm{u}_{k}=\bm{v}_{k}^{\mathrm{svd}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{v}_{k}=\bm{u}_{k}^{\mathrm{svd}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad w_{k}=\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{k}=\sigma_{k}.

This implies
\bm{V}_{n}=\bm{U}^{\mathrm{svd}}
,
\bm{U}_{n}=\bm{V}^{\mathrm{svd}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{svd}}
, where we set
\gamma=n
because
\gamma
can be any value such that
\gamma\leq r
and the rank
r=n
.

By
\bm{X}_{n}=\bm{U}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}
in Theorem
, we have

\bm{X}_{n}=\bm{U}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}\quad\implies\quad\bm{V}^{\mathrm{svd}}=\bm{V}^{\mathrm{svd}}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}\quad\implies\quad\bm{I}_{n}=\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}.

By
\bm{Y}_{n}=\bm{V}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)}
in Theorem
, we have

\bm{Y}_{n}=\bm{V}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)}\quad\implies\quad\bm{U}^{\mathrm{svd}}=\bm{U}^{\mathrm{svd}}\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)}\quad\implies\quad\bm{I}_{n}=\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)}.

Applying Theorem
again and setting
\gamma=n
, we have

\bm{Y}_{n}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}_{n}=\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)\top}\bm{V}_{n}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{U}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}=\bm{R}_{n}^{(y)\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}\bm{R}_{n}^{(x)}.

This simplifies to

\bm{U}^{\mathrm{svd}\top}\bm{A}\bm{V}^{\mathrm{svd}}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{svd}},

which corresponds precisely to the form of a SVD. This demonstrates the equivalence between the SVD and the biconjugate decomposition when the Wedderburn sequence is applied with
(\bm{V}^{\mathrm{svd}},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{svd}})
as
(\bm{X}_{n},\bm{Y}_{n})
.

16.4
Proof: General Term Formula of Wedderburn Sequence

In Lemma
, we present the general term formula of the Wedderburn sequence.
Given any matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
, the Wedderburn sequence of
\bm{A}
is defined by
\bm{A}_{k+1}=\bm{A}_{k}-w_{k}^{-1}\bm{A}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}
with
\bm{A}_{1}=\bm{A}
. The proof of the general term formula for this sequence is as follows:

Proof
[of Lemma
]
For
\bm{A}_{2}
, let
\bm{u}_{1}=\bm{x}_{1}
and
\bm{v}_{1}=\bm{y}_{1}
. We have:

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{1}-w_{1}^{-1}\bm{A}_{1}\bm{x}_{1}\bm{y}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}_{1}=\bm{A}-w_{1}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}.

For
\bm{A}_{3}
, we can write out the equation as:

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{3}=\bm{A}_{2}-w_{2}^{-1}\bm{A}_{2}\bm{x}_{2}\bm{y}_{2}^{\top}\bm{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=(\bm{A}-w_{1}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A})-w_{2}^{-1}(\bm{A}-w_{1}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A})\bm{x}_{2}\bm{y}_{2}^{\top}(\bm{A}-w_{1}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A})\hskip 17.00024pt

\displaystyle\text{(substitute $\bm{A}_{2}$)}

\displaystyle=(\bm{A}-w_{1}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A})-w_{2}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{A}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{x}_{2}}-w_{1}^{-1}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{x}_{2}})({\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{y}_{2}^{\top}}-w_{1}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{y}_{2}^{\top}}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}){\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{A}}\hskip 17.00024pt

\displaystyle\text{(factor out $\bm{A}$)}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}-w_{1}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}-w_{2}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{2}\bm{v}_{2}^{\top}\bm{A}=\bm{A}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A},

where
\bm{u}_{2}=\bm{x}_{2}-w_{1}^{-1}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{2}=\bm{x}_{2}-\frac{\bm{v}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{2}}{w_{1}}\bm{u}_{1}
, and
\bm{v}_{2}=\bm{y}_{2}-w_{1}^{-1}\bm{y}_{2}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{1}\bm{v}_{1}=\bm{y}_{2}-\frac{\bm{y}_{2}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{1}}{w_{1}}\bm{v}_{1}
.
Similarly, we can find the expression of
\bm{A}_{4}
by
\bm{A}
:

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{4}=\bm{A}_{3}-w_{3}^{-1}\bm{A}_{3}\bm{x}_{3}\bm{y}_{3}^{\top}\bm{A}_{3}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}-w_{3}^{-1}\big(\bm{A}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\big)\bm{x}_{3}\bm{y}_{3}^{\top}\big(\bm{A}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\big)\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,

\displaystyle\text{{(substitute $\bm{A}_{3}$)}}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}-w_{3}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{A}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{x}_{3}}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{x}_{3}}\big)\big({\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{y}_{3}^{\top}}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{y}_{3}^{\top}}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\big){\color[rgb]{0,0.08,0.45}\bm{A}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,

\displaystyle\text{(factor out $\bm{A}$)}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}-w_{3}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{3}\bm{v}_{3}^{\top}\bm{A}=\bm{A}-\sum_{i=1}^{3}w_{i}^{-1}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A},

where
\bm{u}_{3}=\bm{x}_{3}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{3}}{w_{i}}\bm{u}_{i}
, and
\bm{v}_{3}=\bm{y}_{3}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{\bm{y}_{3}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}}{w_{i}}\bm{v}_{i}
.
Continuing this process, we can define

\bm{u}_{k}=\bm{x}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\bm{v}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}_{k}}{w_{i}}\bm{u}_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{v}_{k}=\bm{y}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\bm{y}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{u}_{i}}{w_{i}}\bm{v}_{i},

and the general term of the Wedderburn sequence can be proved by induction.

16.18.

Rank reduction theorem.
Let
\bm{A}\in\real^{m\times n}
,
\bm{X}\in\real^{n\times k}
, and
\bm{Y}\in\real^{m\times k}
such that
\bm{W}=\bm{Y}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{X}
is nonsingular. Show that

\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}-\bm{A}\bm{X}\bm{W}^{-1}\bm{Y}^{\top}\bm{A})=\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A})-\mathrm{rank}(\bm{A}\bm{X}\bm{W}^{-1}\bm{Y}^{\top}\bm{A}).

When
k=1
, this is the rank-one reduction (Theorem
).
Discuss its connection to Corollary
.

is symmetric and
\bm{X}=\bm{Y}
, then
\bm{U}=\bm{V}
in Lemma
.
